# Preface: How to use this book

Immunology is a fascinating subject, the study of which has led to enormous improvements in the understanding and treatment of disease. This electronic textbook aims to introduce and explain each of the major aspects of the human immune system that are likely to be covered during undergraduate courses in immunology. It should be of most benefit to students of biology, medicine, microbiology and, of course, immunology. The different topics are introduced in an order that can be followed from the most basic concepts of immunology in the early chapters, through to quite advanced topics later in the book. Each chapter begins with a summary of the information leading up to the chapter, and ends with a list of revision points summarising each key new item introduced in the chapter. For those wishing to explore particular topics in more depth, suggestions for further reading are offered at the end of each chapter, with links to external websites and articles that are free (open access) to readers with internet connections. At the end of the book there are self-test questions and answers to aid revision and also to help gauge understanding of the material presented in each chapter.

The book has been written to be clearly displayed on tablets and other mobile devices, such as cell phones or mobile phones. In particular, the text and images are optimised to minimise file sizes in order to enable quick download and to be easily accommodated and read on smaller devices. The book is also enabled for electronic text-to-speech conversion, so if your device offers this function, it will be possible to listen to the book as an aid to revision. To limit the requirements for space and memory, there is no separate index section. However, readers will be able to use the ‘Find’ or ‘Search’ function of their device to search and jump to any section in the book using this feature.

# Chapter 1: Introduction to immunology, the challenges and the requirements

The challenges

Every day, our bodies repel countless attempted invasions by micro-organisms. To these invaders, we represent an exceptionally rich source of nutrients, water, warmth and minerals, all separated from their world by barriers less than one millimetre in thickness. Over the course of a lifetime, we are exposed to millions of such attempts to colonise our bodies. The vast majority of these challenges represent trivial infections by harmless organisms that are easily repelled without consequence, and no lasting colonisation or overt symptoms of infection will occur. Occasionally, however, are we faced with more serious threats, and without any form of defence, infection by these organisms would be fatal. This constant battle for survival between microbe and host has resulted in an immense selective pressure which, over the past 500 million years or so, has driven the evolution of a powerful and complex means of defence against infection - namely the mammalian immune system.

The challenges faced by the immune system are enormous. To begin with, the types of threat posed by different organisms cover a vast range of physical sizes, ranging all the way from toxins, which can be less than 1 nm in diameter, through to viruses (5 - 500 nm), bacteria (500 - 10,000 nm), and eukaryotic invaders, such as fungi (2 - 20,000 μm), protozoa (1 - 300 μm) and parasitic worms (1 mm - 20 m) (Figure 1.1). There are also other challenges to face beyond infectious threats. For example, our health is dependent on the large number of commensal bacteria in our intestines which help us to digest food and create vitamins, so mechanisms are required to avoid triggering inappropriate immune responses to these organisms. We also need to maintain surveillance of potential damage to our own cells, which accumulate mutations as we age, and some of these which may give rise to tumours. We also need mechanisms to remember which organisms we have been exposed to in the past, and of these, which were innocuous and which were dangerous. We need to be able to initiate the correct type of immune response to eliminate specific types of organism in the most effective manner. And finally, given the enormous potential of the immune system to wage destructive war in the tissues, systems must be in place to guard against accidental or collateral damage to self cells and tissues.

Remarkably, the body has a defensive response to meet all of these challenges, and collectively the cells and tissues of the body which provide the different elements of this response are referred to as the immune system. The aim of this book is to discuss each of the different arms of the immune system, with an emphasis placed on the molecules and cells that underpin the mechanisms of each different type of response.

![](image_rsrc4AY.jpg)

Cells are at the heart of immune function

An effective immune response is generated by co-operation between the specialised cells, tissues and mediators of the immune system, and the many other cell-types of the body they exist to protect. A good understanding of immunity is therefore dependent on a solid understanding of four key aspects of cell biology, namely: (i) how different types of cell originate from precursor cells, (ii) the concept of clonal selection, (iii) how cells communicate with each other and (iv) how proteins interact with other proteins.

The human body consists of approximately 10 trillion cells, all of which are descended from a single fertilised egg cell, through multiple rounds of division. Although each of these cells is derived from a precursor containing exactly the same genetic information as was contained in the fertilised egg, they each receive a carefully timed and co-ordinated set of programming cues during development which results in the formation of approximately 250 different types of cell, all of which are required to perform the physiological functions of the body. This process, by which a less specialised cell becomes a more specialised type of cell, is called differentiation, and is discussed in Chapter 2.

The communication between cells of the immune system and the cells and tissues they protect is also critical to understanding of immune function. All cells of the body are in constant communication, both with their immediate neighbours and with cells in distant tissues, through the use of diverse molecules and mediators. The major mechanisms by which cells transmit signals to each other, and how these signals are conveyed from the cell membrane to the nucleus to modulate gene expression, referred to as cell signalling, are also covered in Chapter 2.

The functions of cells of the immune system are also dependent on a diverse array of secreted and membrane-bound mediators, most of which are proteins. For example, pattern recognition receptors are required to bind to microbes to enable their detection and elimination. Adhesion molecules and chemokine receptors are required to help immune cells migrate into the correct tissues. Cytokines enable immune cells to send signals to each other and a subset of these, termed chemokines, are required to recruit cells to a particular location. The major mechanisms by which such proteins are produced, and how they bind to and affect their targets, are discussed throughout the book.

There are two major arms of the immune response

The immune defences of early forms of multi-cellular life were relatively unsophisticated. Their defences depended on simple deterrents to infection, such as strong physical barriers at the interface between the organism and the environment, the secretion of broadly active anti-microbial substances, and the detection of a small number of highly conserved molecular patterns associated with most micro-organisms, using invariant receptors that evolve very slowly over thousands of generations. This type of immunity is still present in all multi-cellular organisms today, including man, and is referred to as innate immunity (or natural immunity). This quick-acting and powerful response is based on the recognition of key molecules commonly associated with many different types of invader using receptors that are encoded by the germline DNA, and only change very slowly over millennia by evolution. It is a powerful system that is sufficient to protect us from the majority of potential pathogens.

However, as multicellular organisms became more complex, the methods employed by microbes to infect their hosts also became more sophisticated. This powerful selective pressure led some organisms to evolve mechanisms by which they could evade the innate immune response. A new, more powerful type of immune response was therefore required. This new type of response, called the adaptive immune response, (or acquired immune response) first emerged about 500 million years ago in the jawed fishes, and has since been inherited by all vertebrate animals.

Unlike innate immune responses, adaptive responses are highly specific to particular species or strains of invader. Moreover, the adaptive immune system comprises a form of memory, by which exposure to one organism leads to enhanced defence against that same organism if it is encountered again. This state of improved defensive capacity due to memory of an earlier challenge is called immunity, and organisms are said to be immune to a certain pathogen if they possess this memory. Today, only vertebrates have this sophisticated type of immune response, and all lower animals make do solely with innate immunity. However, as will be discussed in the remainder of this book, there is constant crosstalk between the innate and adaptive arms of the immune system in man, and we need both of them to survive.

The key cells and mediators of innate immunity

The innate immune system is composed of a variety of passive and active defence mechanisms. Passive defences include physical barriers to colonisation, such as the tough outer layers of the skin, the acidic pH in the stomach, and the secretion of anti-microbial proteins and peptides onto epithelial surfaces and into secretions such as tears and saliva. If organisms breach these defences and reach the internal tissues, they may then be acted upon by a range of proteins that detect conserved molecules on the surface of the organisms. These proteins, which include complement, collectins and ficolins, bind to the invaders, causing damage to their membranes, and also labelling them for clearance or destruction.

Should these further defences not repel the invader, the cellular arm of innate immunity may then be called in. Cells of the innate immune system include phagocytic cells, such as macrophages, neutrophils and dendritic cells, which can sense micro-organisms and ingest them for killing and degradation.

Once pathogens are detected by the innate immune system, two major types of response may be induced: an anti-viral response or an inflammatory response. Generally speaking, the anti-viral response depends largely on signals that encourage nearby cells to shut down protein synthesis and switch on their own cellular defences against viral infection. By contrast, inflammation is the process of recruitment of white blood cells (leukocytes) and plasma proteins from the blood into the tissues to combat infection. The latter process is associated with redness, heat and swelling of the affected area, and is typically triggered in response to so-called danger signals, which are molecular motifs recognised by ancient, highly conserved receptors expressed on many different types of cell, including many cells not associated with the immune system.

The process of inflammation can be quite destructive to tissues, and therefore it must be carefully regulated to avoid unnecessary damage to the host. A key role played by the innate immune system is therefore distinguishing between those molecules which belong to the host (and which are therefore safe), or are otherwise innocuous, and those which are derived from infectious organisms, and therefore require a defensive response. This is achieved by the detection of a relatively small number of molecules termed pathogen-associated molecular patterns (PAMPs), by the use of so-called pattern recognition receptors (or PRRs), such as Toll-like receptors and NOD-like receptors, which will be discussed in more detail in Chapter 4.

The key limitation of the innate immune system it that no memory is generated by exposure to a particular type of organism. This means that innate responses do not improve in accuracy or strength each time the same microbe is encountered. However, innate immunity is quick acting (responses to microbes can be generated within minutes), and is therefore relied upon to limit the spread of infections in the early phase of infection (up to 2 weeks) until the adaptive immune system is able to respond and clear the infection (Figure 1.2). The key cells and mediators of the innate immune system are summarised in Table 1.1.

Table 1.1: Key cells and mediators of the innate immune system

![](image_rsrc4AZ.jpg)

![](image_rsrc4B0.jpg)

There are two key arms of the adaptive immune response

As organisms evolved and became more complex, many of the pathogens they encountered evolved mechanisms to resist clearance by innate immunity. Defence against such organisms required a new form of response which addressed the shortcomings of innate immunity, most notably the lack of memory, the poor specificity of responses, the failure to eliminate some intracellular pathogens and the limited potential to distinguish between self and non-self. Remarkably, a system with the capacity to deal with each of these short-comings evolved twice: once in the jawless fishes, and a slightly more advanced system in jawed vertebrates, including all mammals, which is the form we will discuss in this book. This new form of immunity, termed adaptive immunity, is based on two key functional arms, namely:

(i) Humoral immunity, which is mediated by proteins called antibodies that bind to and neutralise microbes, and are produced by cells called B-lymphocytes

(ii) Cell-mediated immunity, which is mediated by T-lymphocytes, serves to kill virally infected cells and also to control and regulate the immune responses of other cells

Unlike innate immunity, adaptive immunity shows high specificity for target molecules and the ability to ‘remember’ previous encounters with particular microbes. This means that adaptive immune responses are initiated more quickly and become more effective each time the same microbe is encountered (Table 1.2). This is achieved through the use of a completely different system of pathogen recognition compared to innate immunity. Whereas innate immunity depends on a small number of receptors that detect highly conserved molecules present in a wide range of organisms, the adaptive immune system depends on an array of millions of different receptors, each with specificity for a different target molecule that might be expressed by an invading microbe. These highly diverse target molecules are termed antigens. An enormous diversity of receptors is required to detect antigens, since there is vast diversity of antigens we may encounter over a lifetime. As there simply isn’t enough DNA in the human genome to code for the millions of possible receptors that are required for adaptive immunity to work, they must be prepared through the process of DNA recombination, which is described in more depth in Chapter 10.

Table 1.2: Key differences between innate and adaptive immunity

![](image_rsrc4B1.jpg)

This process results in the generation of millions of different lymphocytes, which start out as single cells each with a receptor specific for a different target antigen. The vast majority of these lymphocytes will never encounter an antigen that matches their receptor. However, during an infection, a very small proportion of the available lymphocytes, perhaps just one per million lymphocytes present in the blood, will be able to recognise a relevant antigen on that microbe and respond effectively. Various means of concentrating antigen, and specialised tissue locations which allow antigens and lymphocytes to meet, are therefore present in the body as will be discussed later. When lymphocytes encounter their antigen for the first time (they are said to be naïve up until that point), and the correct co-stimulatory signals are received, they begin to proliferate very quickly. In this way, a small number of reactive lymphocytes which are very rare at the start of the immune response, can be expanded to millions of cells, all with the same receptor and specificity for a particular antigen. This very powerful process of selection is referred to as clonal expansion (Figure 1.3).

![](image_rsrc4B2.jpg)

Once a large number of lymphocytes reactive to a specific microbe is generated and the infection is cleared with help from effector cells (the term for the various cells that ultimately clear the infection), the levels of antigen diminish. This causes most of the responsive lymphocytes to die by apoptosis, a process referred to as contraction. However, a proportion of these cells differentiate into long-lived memory cells, which retain the same receptor specificity for that antigen. These cells are therefore ready to respond quickly the next time the same pathogen is encountered and is the basis of immunological memory (as will be discussed in Chapter 13).

In addition to defending against infectious organisms, the adaptive immune system also has mechanisms to detect damaged cells, and to prevent inappropriate responses to self molecules. When these mechanisms break down, auto-immunity can occur, as discussed in Chapter 14. We will now discuss the major cell types involved in the adaptive response, namely B-lymphocytes, T-lymphocytes and antigen-presenting cells, the key functions of which are summarised in Table 1.3.

Table 1.3: Key cells of the adaptive immune system

![](image_rsrc4B3.jpg)

B-lymphocytes produce antibodies which binds to antigen

The term antigen refers to any molecule that may be recognised by the receptors of the adaptive immune system. They can be composed of protein, lipid, carbohydrate, or any combination of these. The part of the molecule that is recognised by B- or T-lymphocyte receptors is called the epitope, (or determinant), and there may often be more than one epitope on the surface of a single antigen.

Antibodies, or immunoglobulins (often shortened to Ig), are proteins produced by B-lymphocytes which can bind to specific epitopes on the surface of antigens with very high affinity and specificity. They are expressed in a membrane bound form on the surface of B-lymphocytes, serving as their receptor for antigen, and also in a soluble form in plasma and mucosal secretions. The binding of antibody to antigens on the surface of microbes serves to accomplish 4 main tasks:

(i) inhibit invasion by clumping the organism into large complexes

(ii) prevent proper function of surface molecules used by the organism

(iii) label the organism for rapid uptake and degradation by phagocytic cells

(iv) label the organism for targeting by proteins of the complement system, resulting in lysis

Just as we are exposed to different types of pathogen, so there are different classes of antibody with different properties to deal with these different types of microbe. These are referred to as IgA, IgG, IgD, IgM and IgE. For example, IgE is useful in the defence against parasites, and IgA defends mucosal barriers from invasion by pathogens. The process by which a B-lymphocyte changes production of antibody from one class to another is referred to as class switching, and will be discussed further in Chapter 8.

B-lymphocytes are generated by haematopoiesis in the bone marrow (described in Chapter 7). The mature, naïve B-cells that result from a circulating population of millions of individual cells each with a different receptor expressed on their surface. When an individual B-lymphocyte encounters antigen for the first time, and if the antibody binds strongly to the antigen, it may become activated to proliferate and differentiate. This single activated cell therefore becomes a B-lymphocyte clone, which means many cells expressing the same receptor derived from multiple rounds of cell division from a single precursor cell.

Together with signals from T-helper lymphocytes, the B-lymphocyte can produce antibody of the most appropriate class for defence against that particular antigen. Some of these cells differentiate into plasma cells, which are effectively cellular factories devoted to secreting large quantities of antibodies. Plasma levels of antibodies for specific antigens therefore begin to accumulate and eventually a point is reached where the microbe is cleared. At this point, the antigen is eliminated and most of the effector B-cells die by apoptosis (contraction). However, some of the B-cells differentiate into memory cells, and a small quantity of antibody remains secreted into plasma over the longer term. The levels of antibody for the specific antigens are at this stage lower than they were at the peak of the immune response, but are still generally high enough to confer immunity from re-infection with the same organism. Achieving this low-level production of protective antibodies is one of the main aims of vaccination, in which deliberate exposure of an individual to a weakened organism or antigens from a target organism (termed vaccines) confer subsequent protection from such organisms (as will be discussed in Chapter 13). In another therapeutic application, protective antibodies can be given from one individual to another by the transfer of serum. This approach is called passive immunity. This type of immunity does not last long and offers no lasting memory to the recipient, but is a useful therapy in the short-term treatment of, for example, toxins and snake bites.

T-lymphocytes detect antigen displayed by antigen presentation cells

Although antibodies offer an excellent strategy to detect and bind to antigens on the surface of microbes, they are not able to target antigens which are present inside the microbe. Moreover, many pathogens have evolved means of hiding within host cells where antibodies cannot penetrate. These problems are dealt with by T-lymphocytes. The major roles of T-lymphocytes are to:

(i) provide signals to phagocytes to help them destroy intracellular micro-organisms

(ii) kill cells which are harbouring intracellular organisms

(iii) help B-cells switch the type of antibody they are producing (class-switching)

(iv) inhibit immune responses to antigens that do not require an immune response

Different classes of T-lymphocyte perform these different functions, and these may be distinguished by the expression of specific markers on the cell surface. For example, cytotoxic T-cells (Tc) express the marker CD8, and are referred to as CD8 positive, or CD8+. By contrast, the T-helper (Th) lymphocytes which ‘help’ phagocytes to kill intracellular bacteria, or B-lymphocytes to class switch, express CD4, but not CD8. Regulatory T-cells (Treg), which inhibit immune responses to specific antigens, also express CD4 but not CD8.

Unlike B-lymphocytes, T-lymphocytes cannot detect intact antigen. Instead, their receptor (the T-cell receptor, or TCR), detects short fragments of protein antigens, termed peptides, which must be presented to them on the surface of a molecule called the major histocompatibility complex (MHC). The cells which express MHC and display peptides to T-lymphocytes are called antigen presenting cells (APC). While most cells have some capacity to present antigen to T-cells, dendritic cells (DCs) are by far the most proficient at displaying peptide to and activating naïve CD4+ and CD8+ T-cells. DCs are adept at capturing and digesting protein antigens to generate peptides for display to T-cells. They are distributed throughout the tissues of the body, but on encountering antigen they become activated and migrate to specialised tissues termed lymph nodes, where they wait to present peptide to passing T-cells. A crucial role of the APC is to offer a co-stimulatory signal to the T-lymphocyte. This signal informs the T-cell of the level of threat associated with the antigen (decided largely by innate immune sensors of the APC), and helps to avoid generating inappropriate or unnecessary T-lymphocyte responses to harmless substances.

Cytokines

The proper functioning and co-ordination of the immune system requires constant communication between each of the different types of immune cell, and the tissues they patrol. Although these signals can be mediated by lipids and other small molecules, the majority of the signals transferred between immune cells are via small, secreted proteins called cytokines. Over 150 different cytokines are thought to exist, and they can have multiple effects on multiple different types of target cell (a property referred to as pleiotropy). Some cytokines have very specific and unique functions. Others relay signals which are very similar to those of other cytokines (a property referred to as redundancy). Cytokines are generally not stored within the cell, but synthesised rapidly following activation and transcription of the gene, in response to a particular stimulus. Their production is often quite transient, and they usually act locally, changing patterns of gene expression either in the cell producing the cytokines itself (autocrine signalling) or cells nearby to the producing cell (paracrine signalling). However, a small number of cytokines, such as tumour necrosis factor (TNF)-α, interleukin (IL)-1 and IL-6 can reach levels in the circulation that are sufficient to exert systemic effects in the host, such as fever or the acute phase response, which confer additional protection from infection.

Lymphoid organs

The cells of the adaptive immune response require direct cell-cell contact with each other to function properly, and therefore require mechanisms, and specific locations, in which to meet. Such meetings occur in the secondary lymphoid organs, which include the lymph nodes, gut-associated lymphoid tissue and spleen. Immune cells migrate from the tissues to the lymph nodes by travelling through lymphatic drainage vessels, which permeate the tissues to drain them of interstitial fluid. Microbes or antigens entering from almost anywhere on the body will ultimately drain to a lymph node through these vessels. It is here that the antigens are detected, and the adaptive immune response initiated. The cells and tissues of the lymphatic system will be discussed in more detail in Chapter 3.

Summary

1) There are two arms of the immune response, innate immunity which is quick acting and provides defence during the early phase of infections, and adaptive immunity which takes time to develop and deals with the later phase of infection clearance

2) Innate immunity is based on the recognition of highly conserved PAMPs using a small number of pattern recognition receptors which remain the same throughout life

3) Adaptive immunity is based on the recognition of highly variable antigens using a very large number of highly diverse receptors generated by DNA recombination

4) The two key arms of adaptive immunity are humoral immunity (mediated by antibodies) and cell mediated immunity (mediated by T-lymphocytes)

5) Antibody, produced by B-lymphocytes, binds to antigens of infectious organisms to slow their progress and label them for destruction

6) Different types of T-lymphocyte are required to help phagocytes kill bacteria (T-helper cells), down-regulate immune responses to harmless antigens (T-regulatory cells), and kill cells infected with intracellular pathogens (cytotoxic T-cells)

7) T-cell subtypes can be distinguished by the expression of the cell surface markers CD4 and CD8

8) T-cells cannot recognise intact antigen, only peptide fragments of protein antigens presented to them by antigen presenting cells (APC)

9) The process of amplification of specific clones of B- or T-lymphocytes expressing the best receptor to deal with a particular antigen by selective proliferation is called clonal selection

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immune_system>

Smith KA. A Living History of Immunology. Front Immunol 6:502 (2015) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4586498/>

# Chapter 2: Introduction to cellular and molecular biology

A good understanding of the mammalian immune system requires familiarity with several key principles of molecular and cellular biology. This chapter will serve as a brief revision of these key principles, with a specific emphasis on how they relate to the functions of the immune system. Readers who are already familiar with concepts such as transcription, translation, differentiation and cell signalling, may prefer to proceed directly to Chapter 3.

Different types of cell mediate different aspects of immunity

Just as the range of potential threats we may be exposed to is extensive, so an equally broad range of defences exists to respond appropriately to each of these threats. These defences are mediated largely by a network of different types of highly specialised immune cell, each possessing unique capabilities and functions to help deal with specific threats. In order for these cells to respond effectively, they must be able to sense their environment and provide the right type of response. They must also be able to communicate with each other, and the cells of the body, to co-ordinate the complex multi-cellular responses required to eliminate pathogens. Remarkably, although the different cell types that make up the immune system vary enormously in their function and capabilities, they are produced and replaced continually by the division of the same pool of stem cells in the bone marrow throughout life. Understanding how these different cells arise from common precursors, and how they respond to signals from the host and infectious agents to deal with specific threats is therefore key to understanding immunology.

Cellular differentiation is key to immune cell function

The human body is composed of approximately 10 trillion cells, all of which are descended from a single fertilised egg cell. The process by which this single type of cell gives rise to the 250 or so different types of cell that make up the adult body is called cellular differentiation. This process, by which one cell changes its gene expression programme to express an altered set of proteins, imparts on the daughter cells new properties and functions. For example, nerve cells must conduct electrical signals, enterocytes absorb nutrients from the gut, myocytes enable muscles to contract and epithelial cells form sheets and barriers to separate the organs. This remarkable feat is achieved despite the fact that the majority of the cells in the body contain exactly the same basic genetic information that was present in the fertilised egg cell, namely 3 billion base pairs of DNA coding for ~20,000 protein coding genes. What changes when cells differentiate is that the expression of some of these genes is turned up (or on), and some of these genes are turned down (or off). Which types of gene, and hence which proteins, are expressed by the different types of cell dictate all of their properties and functions.

As our bodies develop, only a small number of cells retain the ability to continue dividing indefinitely. Those that retain this ability, which is termed self-renewal, are called stem cells. Another common feature of stem cells is that they remain in a relatively non-differentiated state, and do not express many markers of mature cells. However, when stem cells divide, one of the two resulting cells remains a stem cell, and the other (the daughter cell) generally enters a programme of differentiation to become a more specialised type of cell.

Stem cells can be broadly categorised according to how many different types of cell they are capable of giving rise to. For example, pluripotent stem cells are those which can differentiate into almost any type of cell in the body, multipotent stem cells can give rise to only cell types that belong to a small number of related families, and unipotent stem cells can give rise to only one type of cell. Notably, all of the circulating cells of the human immune system descend from haematopoietic stem cells (HSCs). These cells reside in the bone marrow and divide throughout life to yield precursors for each of the different blood cells (also called leukocytes) in a process called haematopoiesis. Depending on the different cues they encounter as they divide, these precursor cells (which are no longer self-renewing), can become committed to particular paths of future development. Major cell types arising from this pathway include monocytes, neutrophils, T-lymphocytes and B-lymphocytes (Figure 2.1). Once a cell has completed its particular pathway of development, it is said to be terminally differentiated, which means it can no longer change into another type of cell.

![](image_rsrc4B4.jpg)

The changes in gene expression that occur when a cell commits to a particular developmental pathway can often be used to identify that type of cell. In particular, the specific proteins expressed at the cell surface often change during differentiation and can be used to identify particular lineages. Cell surface proteins that mark specific groups of cells are called cluster of differentiation, or CD, markers. These CD markers, of which there are currently more than 300, are very useful therapeutically and for immunological research, as their detection may be used to identify particular types of cell. For example, CD3 is a useful marker of T-lymphocytes, CD14 is present on the surface of monocytes, and CD19 identifies B-lymphocytes.

However, it should also be noted that in addition to the major types of cell that result from differentiation, similar processes can also alter the phenotype of terminally differentiated cells to subtly alter their functions. These different programmes of gene expression are often referred to as altered states of activation, or polarisation. For example, macrophages can be driven towards a so-called M1 or M2 state of activation depending on their exposure to particular cytokines. Sometimes even quite subtle alterations to gene expression, resulting in changes to only a small number of genes, can have a marked effect on a cell’s state of activation and the immune functions it may exert.

Transcription factors dictate cell type and function

As mentioned above, cell type is determined by which proteins are expressed by that cell. This is in turn is dictated by which genes are expressed, and to what extent, from the chromosomal DNA in the nucleus. At any one time, of the ~20,000 genes which a cell could call on to express, only ~30% to ~80% of these may actually be switched on. The mechanism by which a cell switches on and off gene expression is therefore the key to cellular differentiation, and also to immune cell function.

For gene expression to occur, an enzyme complex called RNA polymerase assembles at the start of the gene. This enzyme complex then makes a copy of the DNA sequence by creating a molecule called messenger RNA (mRNA), in a process called transcription. This mRNA then leaves the nucleus to enter the cytoplasm, where it is translated into protein by large multiprotein complexes called ribosomes. The proteins resulting from translation are then folded and glycosylated correctly by various means and directed towards their required location in the cell (Figure 2.2).

![](image_rsrc4B5.jpg)

Many genes are regulated by how frequently the process of transcription occurs for that gene. This in turn is regulated largely by proteins called transcription factors. These are proteins which can bind to DNA in regions close to genes (termed promoters and enhancers) to modulate the expression of those genes. There are approximately 2,000 different types of transcription factor in the human genome, and those which are chosen to be expressed by a cell therefore dictate the expression of all the other genes in the cell. One type of transcription factor can regulate anywhere from just one to hundreds of different genes. This is why changes to expression of a small number of transcription factors, often just one transcription factor, can have a large impact on gene expression and therefore the overall functions of a cell.

Many transcription factors can be activated by signals received from outside the cell, causing increased or decreased expression of the sets of genes they govern. In this way, a cell can sense signals from its environment, for example hormones or signalling molecules released from adjacent cells, and react accordingly by changing its gene expression programme. Some transcription factors can compete with other transcription factors for binding sites on the DNA regions that regulate genes, and therefore inhibit the function of each other. Others may co-operate to help switch on gene expression, and some switch on the expression of key transcription factors that dictate cell type. By these means, stable networks of gene expression are often formed, which help the cell remain the type of cell it was instructed to become during the differentiation process. The expression levels of specific transcription factors are therefore key to regulating immune responses.

Common mechanisms of signal transduction

All cells must be able to receive signals from the outside world and respond accordingly. This process begins with the expression of receptors, which bind to signalling molecules, termed ligands, to cause activation of these receptors. Examples of such ligands include cytokines, chemokines and hormones, such as insulin. The activation of receptors by their ligands can cause changes to cell function either by changing the expression of genes, or by altering the activity of proteins already present in the cell. In either case, a series of intermediate molecules usually help to transmit the signal from the receptor to the nucleus, where gene expression is altered, or to its target protein inside the cell.

While there are many dozens of different cell signalling pathways used by human cells, a small number of key pathways mediate a great deal of immune cell signalling. These pathways include, for example, receptor tyrosine kinase signalling, mitogen activated protein (MAP) kinase cascades, G-protein coupled receptor signalling, phosphatidyl-inositol (PI)-3 kinase (PI3K) signalling and calcium ion (Ca2+)-dependent protein activation. The specific details of each of these pathways will be discussed individually in later chapters. At the most basic level, however, it may be helpful to recall that cell signalling often involves one or more of the following key processes: (i) the phosphorylation or dephosphorylation of tyrosine, serine or threonine residues on specific proteins, (ii) the proteolysis of certain proteins, (iii) changes to cytosolic calcium ion levels (iv) changes to levels of cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP) (v) the production of inositol triphosphate (IP3) by PI3K and (vi) the activation of G-proteins. Figure 2.3 gives an overview of the major components of each of these pathways.

![](image_rsrc4B6.jpg)

Proteins are chains of covalently linked amino acids that are typically tightly folded

Proteins are the fundamental units that form the structure and machinery of every cell. They are composed of long, linear chains of amino acids, each joined to the other by covalent bonds. Proteins are built from constituent amino acids by the ribosome, which translates the code presented to it on mRNA molecules. There are 20 different amino acids, each coded for by a specific group of three DNA nucleotides, which may be A, T, C or G (adenine, cytosine, thymine or guanine, respectively). For example, the DNA sequence ATG CAA ACT TAG codes for methionine (and also signals the start of the protein), then the two amino acids glutamine and threonine, and then the stop signal (TAG), which marks the end of the protein.

As each triplet of nucleotides, referred to as a codon, is read from the mRNA by the ribosome, a single amino acid is joined to the existing chain. This process is repeated along the entire length of the mRNA, and a linear chain of linked amino acids, or peptide, slowly emerges one amino acid at a time, from the ribosome. Certain signals associated with the protein may at this stage direct it into the lumen of the endoplasmic reticulum to become a soluble, secreted protein, into the cytosol to become a cytoplasmic protein or to straddle the ER membrane to become a transmembrane protein (i.e. a protein in which part of the molecule is on the cytosolic side of the membrane and part on the outside of the membrane). In each of these cases, the long peptide chain folds around itself to adopt a very particular structure which imparts the function of the protein.

Some of the most critical mechanisms of immunity depend on the strength of the binding (affinity) of proteins to other proteins or peptides. For example, the effectiveness of an antibody is directly related to the affinity it has for its target antigen. Likewise, the specificity of the T-cell receptor (TCR) is entirely dependent on the strength of the interaction between the TCR and the peptide presented on MHC. As a general rule, for proteins to bind well to each other, they must first have complementary shape. In particular, the binding surfaces of antibodies often map very closely to the shape of the epitope on the antigen they bind to. Next, amino acids possess certain properties that may promote or inhibit binding between partner proteins. These potential means by which amino acids my promote interaction are generally speaking related to (i) charge, (ii) hydrogen bonding (iii) hydrophobic interactions, and (iv) van der Waals interactions. Figure 2.4 shows some of the key means by which binding between proteins may be enhanced by complementary amino acids.

![](image_rsrc4B7.jpg)

Vesicle trafficking and phagocytosis

The cell membrane, also called the plasma membrane, is composed of two layers of phospholipid. This bilayer is strengthened and supported by the protein cytoskeleton inside the cell. The majority of molecules, and certainly all large proteins, cannot pass easily through this barrier, and therefore the cell requires mechanisms to take molecules into its interior, and also to release its products into the exterior environment. Within every cell are many vesicles and other structures which are also surrounded by phospholipid bilayers. These internal bilayers form the boundaries of the many different functional compartments within the cell, such as the endoplasmic reticulum, golgi apparatus, endocytic vesicles and the lysosome.

With help from proteins attached to the cytoskeleton, these vesicles and their cargoes can be moved around the cell in defined directions. Moreover, there are mechanisms by which certain membranes can be directed to fuse (join) together to form larger vesicles and mix their cargoes; or to split apart and send some cargo in a different direction.

This internal movement of vesicles is essential for the uptake of antigens into dendritic cells, for example, and for the subsequent presentation of processed peptide on MHC. It is also required for macrophages and other phagocytic cells to ingest pathogens and transfer them to the lysosome for degradation, and for antibody to be secreted from B-lymphocytes. Figure 2.5 shows the common modes of vesicle trafficking. At this stage, however, the key point to bear in mind is that the contents and conditions within vesicles can be very different from that in the cytosol or outside the cell.

![](image_rsrc4B8.jpg)

Summary

1) The immune system is dependent on many different types of cell, each of which has highly specialised functions

2) These functions are dependent on which genes are expressed by each type of cell

3) Gene expression is mediated by copying the DNA code from the nucleus to messenger RNA (transcription), and the mRNA is then copied to protein (translation)

4) Transcription of genes is regulated by transcription factors, which bind to specific DNA sequences near each gene

5) Transcription factors dictate which genes are switched on or off, and therefore all of the properties and functions of a cell

6) Transcription factors can themselves be switched on and off in response to signals from outside the cell

7) Some individual transcription factors can have a large impact on immune cell function

8) All white blood cells (leukocytes) are descended from one type of self-renewing cell, called haematopoietic stem-cells, in the bone marrow

9) Different types of immune cell can often be distinguished by cell surface proteins called CD markers

10) A small number of shared cell signalling pathways regulate many major mechanisms of immunity

11) Phospholipid bilayer membranes form barriers to most substances outside the cell and are also used to create functional compartments within the cell

12) Vesicle trafficking allows cells to take up molecules for digestion, and also to secrete soluble molecules into the extracellular fluid, or to display membrane-bound molecules on the surface of the cell

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cell_biology>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_biology>

Uings IJ, Farrow SN. Cell receptors and cell signalling. Mol Pathol 53:295-9 (2000) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC1186983/>

Napetschnig J, Wu H. The Molecular basis of NF-κB signalling. Annu Rev Biophys 42:443-68 (2013) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3678348/>

# Chapter 3: Cells and tissues of the immune response

In this chapter we will introduce the major types of cell which are responsible for each different aspect of immune defence, and also the specialised tissues in which cells of the immune system meet and co-ordinate responses.

Overview of key cells and tissues of the immune system

While the majority of cells in the body possess some capacity to detect and respond to infection, they do not possess the capabilities required to eliminate microbes. These functions are provided by effector cells, derived from several different populations of highly specialised cells which form the cellular component of the immune system. The major cell types required to deliver effective defence include phagocytes, which ingest and destroy microbes and other foreign bodies, lymphocytes, which offer the great receptor diversity required to recognise and combat diverse antigens, antigen presenting cells, which present antigen to lymphocytes and enable them to become activated, and various granule containing cells (or granulocytes), which serve to combat a variety of other threats. While some of these cells spend most of their time in a relatively fixed sentinel position in the tissues, others are constantly in motion, patrolling the tissues throughout the body by circulating in the blood and in the lymphatic vessels which drain the tissues.

Many of these cells have a relatively short lifespan (some <1 week) and require to be replaced on a daily basis throughout life. This is achieved through the process of haematopoiesis, which occurs in the bone marrow in adults and generates all white blood cells (leukocytes). Some classes of leukocyte are highly numerous (such as neutrophils, constituting ~50-70 % of leukocytes in health), and others are rare (such as basophils, which constitute ~0.2-0.5 % of leukocytes in health). The key properties of major types of leukocyte are summarised in Figure 3.1 and Figure 3.2.

![](image_rsrc4B9.jpg)

![](image_rsrc4BA.jpg)

Leukocytes are generated in so-called primary (or generative) lymphoid organs. These include the bone marrow (where haematopoiesis occurs) and the thymus, a small organ close to the heart through which T-lymphocytes must transit in order to mature.

One of the key challenges faced by the immune system is that antigens may enter the body in any anatomical location, yet the number of lymphocytes responsive to that antigen is likely to be very low (perhaps only one in 1 million cells). A mechanism is therefore required to capture antigens wherever they may breach the outer defences, and channel those antigens towards concentrated populations of lymphocytes. This feat is achieved by the drainage of tissue fluids through lymphatic vessels to secondary (or peripheral) lymphoid organs, where lymphocyte responses to antigen are first initiated. Key secondary lymphoid organs include the lymph nodes, gut-associated lymphoid tissues and spleen, as will be discussed later.

Neutrophils

Neutrophils, also called polymorphonuclear leukocytes (PMN), are the most abundant type of leukocyte in human blood. These short-lived cells are efficient phagocytes, being particularly good at pursuing and ingesting bacteria and fungi. They contain two types of intracellular granule: specific granules and azurophilic granules, each of which contains antimicrobial enzymes and peptides and confers on the neutrophil powerful means of killing bacteria and fungi. Neutrophils are particularly sensitive to the early signs of inflammation expressed by endothelial cells, and are the first to enter inflammed tissues (discussed in more depth in Chapter 5). Within minutes of endothelial expression of proinflammatory adhesion molecules, particularly E-selectin, neutrophils enter the tissues by migrating between the endothelial cells that line the blood vessels. They then follow chemotactic signals to home in on micro-organisms and ingest them. After leaving the bone marrow and entering the blood, the half-life of neutrophils is about 5 days. If they do not encounter a site of inflammation in that time, they undergo apoptosis and are then removed from the blood by the liver and spleen.

Monocytes and macrophages

Monocytes are an incompletely differentiated cell that circulates in the blood at a frequency of ~5-10% of the leukocyte population. Their principle role is to eventually leave the blood and migrate into almost any of the tissues of the body to differentiate into macrophages. This may be done in the absence of inflammation to cover the day-to-day replacement of ageing tissue macrophages. Monocytes can also be recruited to sites of inflammation to provide large numbers of macrophages to help deal with tissue injury or infection. There are at least two different types of monocyte based on their sensitivity to different chemokines and expression of the CD markers CD14 and CD16. In man, CD14lo CD16- monocytes are considered to be associated with pro-inflammatory responses, and CD14hi CD16- monocytes are considered to be associated with tissue repair. Similar monocytes subsets exist in mice, but the patterns of surface markers are a little different.

All tissues of the body contain resident macrophages, which serve many essential functions. Chief among these, macrophages are efficient phagocytes, and readily engulf and digest not only microbes, but also dead host cells and other extracellular debris. They are efficient killers of phagocytosed microbes via lysosomal expression of reactive oxygen species, proteases and other molecules that are toxic to microbes. Macrophages express a wide array of innate immune receptors, and serve as sentinels for the recognition of infection through detection of PAMPs (discussed in more depth in Chapter 4). They often initiate and subsequently control inflammatory responses by the secretion of cytokines and chemokines. The cytokines and other factors produced by macrophages can also direct the wound healing process and tissue remodelling after injury or inflammation. Slightly different forms of macrophage are present in different tissues. For example, macrophages in the liver are called Kupffer cells, in the lungs alveolar macrophages, and microglial cells in the central nervous system. Macrophages also have the ability to express MHC-II and present antigen to T-cells. Not all tissue macrophages stem from the recruitment of monocytes - many populate the tissues early in development from precursors that arise in the yolk sac and foetal liver.

Macrophages can ingest microbes by the binding of conserved PAMPs to membrane receptors, or via opsonins, which are molecules such as antibody and complement that coat microbial surfaces and target them for phagocytosis. As will be discussed in later chapters, macrophages can respond to different signals to be activated in different ways. For example, bacterial PAMPs and the cytokine IFN-γ promote classical activation, which heightens their capacity to kill intracellular bacteria, whereas cytokines such as IL-4 promote an alternative state of activation, which promotes tissue remodelling, fibrosis, blood vessel formation and wound healing.

Macrophages are different from neutrophils in a number of key ways. They are much longer lived, often surviving for many months before being replaced, and can become a hallmark cell of chronic inflammatory lesions. They may also present antigen to T-cells and are not terminally differentiated, which means they retain the ability to undergo cell division and proliferate at their site of recruitment if required.

Mast cells, basophils and eosinophils

Like neutrophils, mast cells, basophils and eosinophils contain many cytoplasmic granules that contain pro-inflammatory and anti-microbial substances, and are therefore also referred to as granulocytes. The mediators produced by these three types of cell are similar, and are thought to be involved in host defence against parasitic worms (helminths), and also in allergic diseases (discussed in Chapter 17). Mature mast cells are not normally found in the blood (and are strictly speaking not leukocytes), but reside in the tissues, particularly the skin and mucosal epithelium since these are common points of microbe entry. Their granules contain pro-inflammatory cytokines and histamine, and also acidic proteins which bind to basic dyes. The mast cell membrane is typically decorated with IgE antibodies, which bind to specific membrane receptors expressed by these cells. When these antibodies encounter their respective antigen, this activates the mast cell, causing it to release the contents of its granules, resulting in inflammation and other changes to the tissues. Mast cells play a key role in allergic disease because IgE produced in response to allergens, such as pollen, is a key trigger of mast cell activation in allergy.

Basophils are similar to mast cells in appearance and function, binding basic dyes, expressing IgG and IgE receptors, and releasing similar products when activated via these receptors. However, rather than populating the tissues, basophils generally remain in the blood, albeit at low frequency (0.2-0.5%).

The granules of eosinophils are distinct from those of mast cells and basophils, in that they contain basic proteins that bind acidic dyes, and therefore stain different colours when examined histologically. Eosinophils are mostly present in the mucosal tissues, such as the gut, respiratory and genitourinary tracts. These cells are thought to play a role in defence against parasites, releasing mediators when activated that are toxic to eukaryotic cells.

Dendritic cells

As explained in chapter 1, T-lymphocytes cannot be activated by antigen directly, as they require antigen to be presented to them on MHC molecules expressed on the surface of antigen-presenting cells (APC). Almost all cells of the body are able to express MHC-I, and therefore have some capacity to present self and viral peptides to CD8+ T-cells. However, MHC-II is required to activate CD4+ helper T-cells, and this is expressed by a much smaller range of APCs. Such MHC-II expressing cells include macrophages and, to some extent, B-cells. However, by far the most efficient APC with the greatest capacity to activate and modulate T-cell function is the dendritic cell, which is specialised for this purpose.

The majority of dendritic cells (DCs) are referred to as conventional DCs, and these are derived from the myeloid lineage during haematopoiesis. They share some properties with other myeloid cells, such as their propensity for phagocytosis and degradation of microbes, expression of a wide array of innate immune PRRs and secretion of immunomodulatory cytokines and chemokines. However, additional features include the ability to express very large numbers of MHC on their surface, and to expand the surface area of their cell membranes dramatically by the formation of hundreds of long, cytoplasmic projections. Such DCs typically adopt a sentinel position in the tissues, and are most numerous in the skin and mucosa, since these two barriers are at most risk of microbial invasion. Here, they remain stationary until activated directly or indirectly by the presence of PAMPs. Such signals cause the DCs to become mobile, and they migrate towards the lymph nodes, increasing their expression of presentation molecules and maturing along the way. This allows antigen to be captured and processed in the periphery and presented to T-cells waiting in the lymph nodes, as will be discussed in Chapter 9.

Two other types of DC play roles which are quite distinct from conventional DCs. Plasmacytoid DCs circulate in the blood, originate from a different lineage during haematopoiesis, and serve as major sensors of viral nucleic acids. Their role appears to be to secrete large quantities of type I interferons on activation, and thereby contribute to host defence against viruses. Follicular dendritic cells present intact antigens captured by complement molecules or antibody, to B-cells in the lymph nodes and spleen. These unusual cells do not arise from haematopoiesis or the bone marrow.

B-lymphocytes

B-lymphocytes, also frequently referred to more simply as B-cells, are the cells responsible for the production of antibody. They are so-called on the discovery that they can mature in the bursa of birds. However, in mammals, they are generated from common lymphoid progenitors during haematopoiesis in the bone marrow, which is also where they mature. There are three major subsets of B-cells: follicular B-cells, marginal zone B-cells and B-1 B-cells. While follicular B-cells express highly diverse receptors and can therefore recognise a wide variety of potential antigens, marginal zone and B-1 B-cells express relatively limited receptor diversity, and respond to a much narrower range of potential antigens. Each of these cell types performs a particular role in a particular location within the secondary lymphoid organs.

Mature B-cells which leave the bone marrow are said to be naïve, since they have not yet encountered antigen. Morphologically, naïve B-cells are similar to all lymphocytes, being small (8-10 μm) and largely composed of a round nucleus with little cytoplasm. These naïve cells circulate in the blood briefly before migrating into the peripheral lymphoid organs, where they wait to meet antigen. If they do not encounter antigen, they may recirculate through the blood to sample other lymphoid organs several times, or they will eventually die by apoptosis to be replaced by new naïve cells released from the bone marrow throughout life. Naïve B-cells express the IgM and IgD types of antibody, but not IgA, IgG or IgE.

However, if naïve B-cells are activated by antigen for which their receptor is specific, they can develop into either effector or memory B-cells. The effector B-cells are adapted to synthesise and secrete large quantities of antibody into the plasma and are hence termed plasma cells. The golgi apparatus and endoplasmic reticulum of plasma cells can show a characteristic swelling that expands the cytoplasm outwards as the cells become high output protein factories secreting large quantities of soluble antibody. Plasma cells have often class-switched to express IgG, IgA or IgE, and can migrate back to the bone marrow to synthesise relatively high levels of antibody for some time after the antigenic stimulus has been cleared.

Memory B-cells are much longer lived than plasma cells, remaining in a quiescent form, (which means their rate of proliferation is very low), for several years. They do, however, still secrete low levels of antibody, and this is often sufficient to provide effective defence against microbes expressing the target antigen. They remain in this state until encountering antigen once again, at which point they proliferate rapidly to aid clearance of the infection before it takes hold.

T-lymphocytes

T-lymphocytes, unlike B-lymphocytes, do not mature fully in the bone marrow. Instead, they leave the bone marrow in an immature form, migrating via the blood to the thymus, which is a specialised primary lymphoid organ located behind the heart. Once T-lymphocytes (also called T-cells) have completed their maturation in this organ they exit as mature, naïve T-cells (the ‘T’ in T-cell refers to the thymus).

On leaving the thymus, naïve T-cells migrate to the secondary lymphoid tissues (such as lymph nodes, spleen, or intestinal mucosa) via the blood, and may then repeatedly pass through these tissues via recirculation several times to patrol for their corresponding antigen. Should a T-cell encounter peptide that matches its receptor presented to it on an MHC molecule with appropriate co-stimulatory signals, it will become activated and proliferate rapidly. From this point onwards, most of the progeny of the reactive T-cell will differentiate into effector T-cells, and a small proportion into memory T-cells. Effector T-cells often express the marker CD25, and preferentially migrate towards inflammed tissues. The small number of memory T-cells induced by an immune response migrate preferentially to inflammed tissue and also the mucosal tissues, since the latter are frequent sites of pathogen entry. Like memory B-cells, memory T-cells are long-lived, remain in a quiescent state, (which means they proliferate very slowly or not at all), and remain ready to proliferate quickly should the antigen they are responsive to appears in the body once again.

T-cells, like B-cells, express highly diverse receptors generated by somatic DNA recombination (described in Chapter 10). However, while B-cell receptors can be secreted from B-cells in a soluble form (called antibody), T-cell receptors remain anchored in the T-cell outer membrane. The only purpose of this receptor is to enable the detection and discrimination of short sections of antigens presented on MHC (or equivalent) molecules. There are two main types of TCR: αβ and γδ. TCRs made from αβ chains show high receptor diversity, while γδ TCRs show limited receptor diversity and may therefore be considered to play a role in innate immunity. T-cells can also be classified by the expression of two surface molecules called CD4 and CD8, and these reflect different functions of the cells expressing these receptors.

CD4+ helper T-cells promote B-cell differentiation, the activation of macrophages and the secretion of cytokines to modulate immune responses, such as inflammation. A second type of CD4+ T-cell, the regulatory T-cell (T-reg), inhibits the responses of other T-cells and tends to dampen immune responses. It is thought that T-regs play a key role in preventing accidental immune responses to self molecules. T-cells expressing the CD8 receptor are cytotoxic (Tc) cells, that serve to kill host cells infected with intracellular pathogens, such as viruses, and also cells expressing MHC that is different from normal host MHC. The fourth major type of T-cell is the γδ T-cell, which shows limited receptor diversity and appears to be responsive to both peptide and non-peptide antigens.

NK cells and NKT cells

Natural killer (NK) cells are lymphocytes that do not express antigen receptors like those expressed by T-cells or B-cells. Instead, all NK cells express the same germ-line encoded receptors with no recombination-induced diversity and are therefore part of the innate immune system. Their primary role is to detect, and kill, host cells that are infected with pathogens via recognition of specific markers on the surface of infected cells. Activated NK cells also secrete the cytokine IFN-γ, which enhances the capacity of macrophages to kill intracellular pathogens. The mechanisms of NK cell function will be discussed in Chapter 4.

Natural killer T (NKT)-cells are a subset of T-lymphocytes which also express a key NK cell marker; CD161. They express αβ type T-cell receptors generated by genetic recombination, but are unusual in that their receptors show low diversity. Their main function is to detect and respond to lipid antigens, which contrasts with the requirement of other αβ receptor expressing T-cell subsets which can only recognise peptide.

The bone marrow is the site of leukocyte generation in adults

Most of the cells described above have a limited lifespan in the circulation and must therefore be continually replaced throughout life. This is achieved by the process of haematopoiesis, which is the production of new blood cells by the proliferation of stem cells and their subsequent differentiation into each of the different types of white cell, red cell and platelet, in the blood. In adult mammals, all leukocytes are normally generated in the bone marrow, which is a soft, highly vascularised spongy tissue found in the centre of certain bones. All circulating granulocytes, monocytes, lymphocytes, red cells and platelets are derived from the proliferation and differentiation of haematopoietic stem cells (HSC), which reside and replicate in the bone marrow.

HSCs are pluripotent stem cells, which means they can continually divide throughout life (a property termed self-renewal), and yet their daughter cells retain the potential to become any of the different types of blood cell. This is achieved by asymmetric division, a process by which one of the daughter cells remains an HSC, and the other begins a stepwise path of differentiation towards one of the leukocyte phenotypes.

Which type of cell an HSC daughter cell becomes depends on the signals it receives from neighbouring cells, particularly stromal cells, and cytokines derived locally or from elsewhere in the body. The first major branch in this process of differentiation comes from signals which push the cell to a decision to become either a common lymphoid progenitor or a common myeloid progenitor. Notably, these progenitors can no longer divide indefinitely but can still give rise to several different types of cell. Once this decision is made, the cell can only differentiate further down that pathway and cannot differentiate along the parallel pathway. It is said to be committed to that particular lineage. Thus, the common lymphoid progenitors give rise to the lymphocytes including B-cells, T-cells and NK-cells, and the common myeloid progenitors give rise to granulocytes, monocytes, most DCs, neutrophils, red blood cells and platelets. The major branches and points of lineage commitment of haematopoiesis are summarised in Figure 3.3.

Although most of the signals required to differentiate along particular lineages are derived locally, cytokines and other signals from elsewhere in the body can also impact on proliferation rates and lineage choices, thereby altering the production rates of certain types of cell, particularly during infection. During severe infection or injury, enhanced leukocyte production may be required, and on these occasions leukocytes can also be generated in the liver and spleen, through a process termed extramedullary haematopoiesis.

![](image_rsrc4BB.jpg)

The thymus is the site of T-cell maturation

The thymus is a capsulated primary lymphoid organ situated close to the heart. Immature T-cells from the bone marrow migrate to the thymus to complete their process of maturation. The thymus is permeated by arterial and venous blood vessels and is separated into numerous lobules by fibrous barrier-like structures termed septa (the plural of septum). Each of these lobules contains two distinct types of tissue, the medulla, which is in the centre, and the cortex, which surrounds the medulla (Figure 3.4). The outer cortex is densely packed with immature T-cells (termed thymocytes for as long as they remain in the thymus), which begin their process of maturation in this location. As these cells mature, they migrate slowly towards the medulla in the centre of the lobule. Here, highly specialised cells termed thymic medullary epithelial cells (TMEC) present self-antigens to T-cells and play a key role in preventing autoimmunity (as will be discussed in Chapter 14). Only when the T-cells have completed their maturation are they able to leave the thymus to begin their search for antigen in the secondary lymphoid organs.

![](image_rsrc4BC.jpg)

The lymphatic system collects antigen from the tissues

As mentioned above, one of the key challenges faced by the immune system is that while antigen can enter anywhere in the body, the naïve cells expressing receptors that may recognise or bind to that antigen are rare, and very unlikely to be at the site of entry. A system is therefore required to capture antigen from the diverse tissues of the body and concentrate it into a small volume containing large numbers of potentially reactive lymphocytes. This function is carried out by the lymphatic system, which depends on a network of vessels similar to blood vessels that permeate most tissues of the body (Figure 3.5).

![](image_rsrc4BD.jpg)

Blood is pumped by the heart through the arteries, which branch into smaller vessels and eventually the capillaries to deliver oxygen and nutrients to the tissues. Most of this fluid then returns to the heart in the blood via the venules and veins. However, a small proportion of this fluid leaks out of the smaller vessels into the surrounding tissues. This results in a slow accumulation of fluid (termed extracellular, or interstitial, fluid) which must be drained away, and this is achieved by lymphatic vessels. These vessels are much more permeable than blood vessels, and serve to collect and channel tissue fluid (which is now called lymph), through progressively larger lymphatic vessels which eventually converge to return the tissue fluid to the blood by emptying into the vena cava. Punctuated along the length of lymph vessels are numerous small encapsulated secondary lymphoid organs termed lymph nodes. In these lymph nodes, lymphocytes and other cells continually sample the lymphatic fluid, and anything carried in it, to determine if an immune response is required. The channelling of tissue fluids by lymphatic vessels through a series of lymph nodes therefore serves to concentrate both antigen and lymphocytes into a small space, dramatically improving the potential for antigen to be recognised by responsive lymphocytes.

Lymph nodes

Lymph enters each lymph node via the afferent lymphatic vessels, permeates through the lymph node to be filtered and sampled by lymphocytes, and then exits via the efferent lymphatic vessels. An easy way to remember which vessel is which is that lymph approaches in the afferent vessel and exits via the efferent vessel. The internal structure of the lymph node is highly organised, and different types of cell performing different jobs remain in specific locations within the lymph node. In particular, the outer region of the lymph node, or cortex, contains many B-cell zones called primary follicles. Some of these contain germinal centres, composed of B-cells responding to antigen, and are called secondary follicles. The follicles also contain follicular dendritic cells (FDCs). T-cells, however, generally remain outwith the follicles, in nearby T-cell zones which also contain conventional DCs (Figure 3.6). Lymph nodes are also perfused with blood vessels, and the high endothelial venules are the site naïve T-cells use to enter the lymph node. Chemokines released from stromal cells specific to the lymph node attract and retain the different classes of lymphocyte in their correct locations. Maintaining T-cells close to cDCs and B-cells close to FDCs enables efficient presentation of antigen to each cell type. However, on activation by antigen, T-cells and B-cells can migrate away from these locations to perform effector functions.

![](image_rsrc4BE.jpg)

Spleen

The spleen is a large organ in the abdomen that serves the principle purpose of removing dead red blood cells and antibody complexes from the circulation. However, the spleen also performs a function similar to that of lymph nodes, enabling lymphocytes to sample circulating blood rather than lymph. The tissues inside the spleen are differentiated into the red pulp, which is highly vascularised, and the white pulp which contains large numbers of lymphocytes. Macrophages in the red pulp help to clear senescent red blood cells (erythrocytes), microbes and immune complexes from the circulation. The white pulp surrounds the smaller arterial branches, and just as in the lymph nodes, the T-cell zones and B-cell zones (follicles) are ordered in distinct locations in the white pulp (Figure 3.7). Marginal zone B-cells are different from follicular B-cells, in that they display low receptor diversity.

![](image_rsrc4BF.jpg)

Other lymphoid tissues

In addition to the highly organised primary and secondary lymphoid organs described above, there are also less organised collections of lymphocytes in the body which serve key roles in defence, particularly adjacent to the skin and mucosal surfaces. For example, Peyer’s patches in the intestine promote lymphocyte development in the absence of an encapsulating organ, as will be discussed in more depth in Chapter 15.

Immune privileged tissues

Certain tissues of the body are so critical to the health and reproductive success of the host that immune responses are prevented from occurring in these tissues. This is because in these very sensitive tissues, the immune response itself could be damaging or even life-threatening. Such tissues are referred to as immune privileged tissues, and include the brain, eye, testis, placenta and foetus. Antigens which enter these locations generally do not elicit immune responses.

Summary

1) Many different types of highly specialised cell are required for effective immune responses

2) Highly organised anatomical structures, the lymphoid organs and lymphatic vessels, are also required for effective immunity

3) All white blood cells (leukocytes) are produced and replaced continually by haematopoiesis, which occurs in the bone marrow and depends on the division of haematopoietic stem cells and their subsequent differentiation into each type of immune cell

4) Major classes of leukocyte include: granulocytes (neutrophils, basophils and eosinophils), monocytes, T-lymphocytes, B-lymphocytes, NK cells and plasmacytoid dendritic cells

5) Of the specialised immune cells, some remain resident in the tissues for most of the time, and others circulate constantly around the body via the blood, lymphatics and the tissues

6) Phagocytes, which ingest and destroy microbes, include neutrophils, monocytes, macrophages and dendritic cells

7) Antigen presenting cells include conventional dendritic cells and macrophages (which present peptide to T-cells), and follicular dendritic cells (which present intact antigen to B-cells)

8) Leukocytes can be identified by their morphology and also by cell surface markers called CD molecules (for example, CD14 is marker of monocytes)

9) The precursors of all lymphocytes are generated in the bone marrow, but while B-lymphocytes mature in the bone marrow, T-lymphocytes exit via the circulation to complete their maturation in the thymus

10) Mature B- and T-lymphocytes that have not yet encountered antigen are referred to as naïve cells, which may then become either effector cells (producing mediators to deal with the infection) or memory cells (which are long-lived, quiescent cells that respond quickly should the same antigen be encountered again)

11) The primary (or generative) lymphoid organs are where lymphocytes are generated and/or mature, namely the bone marrow and thymus

12) The secondary (or peripheral) lymphoid organs are where naïve lymphocytes encounter antigen to be activated, and include lymph nodes and spleen

13) Lymphatic vessels throughout the body collect extracellular fluid from the tissues, and channel it towards lymph nodes which are encapsulated filtering organs placed at intervals along lymphatic vessels. This is required to concentrate antigen and DCs collected from the tissues with dense populations of lymphocytes to maximise the potential for rare, responsive lymphocytes to meet their corresponding antigen

14) The two main functions of the spleen are to support lymphocyte responses to blood-borne antigens, and also to remove old or damaged red blood cells from the circulation

15) In both lymph nodes and in the white pulp of the spleen, B-cells aggregate together in zones termed follicles, where follicular dendritic cells reside to present antigen to B-cells

16) Lymph nodes and spleen also contain T-cell zones, where mature conventional dendritic cells reside to present peptide to T-lymphocytes

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunology>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thymus>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bone_marrow>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hematopoesis>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lymphatic_system>

Randolph GJ, Ivanov S, Zinselmeyer BH, Scallan JP. The Lymphatic System: Integral Roles in Immunity. Annu Rev Immunol 35:31-52 (2017) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5551392/>
