# Chapter 4: Innate immunity and pattern recognition

As described in Chapter 1, the mammalian immune system is composed of two arms: the innate immune system, which is quick acting but lacks memory, and the adaptive immune system, which takes longer to develop but is more specific, more powerful and has the ability to generate memory of previous encounters with antigens. In this chapter we will discuss the key functions and mechanisms of the human innate immune system.

Introduction to innate immunity

One of the key challenges faced by the immune system is the incredible rate of multiplication of many infectious organisms. Some bacteria can double in number every twenty minutes, and some viruses can release thousands of progeny within hours of infecting a single host cell. If allowed to multiply unchecked, such organisms could easily overwhelm the entire body within days. Moreover, several of these organisms can only be cleared by the more potent and specific responses of the adaptive immune system. However, it can often take 10-15 days before an effective adaptive response (mediated by antibodies and effector T-cells) can be generated. The innate immune system is therefore required to protect the host during the early stages of infection, and either eliminate, or hold back the progress of infection to give the adaptive immune system sufficient time to generate the necessary antigen-specific responses.

A key requirement of the innate immune system is that it must be able to detect the presence of infection rapidly. The lengthy process of DNA recombination and clonal selection is therefore not employed in innate immunity. Instead, a collection of germ-line encoded receptors, termed pattern recognition receptors (PRRs), are used to detect highly conserved molecular features that are present in the molecules of pathogens but which are not present in mammalian cells. The molecules which are detected by PRRs are called pathogen associated molecular patterns, or PAMPs. Some PRRs are present in cells, and others are soluble molecules in the circulation and in secretions. The detection of PAMPs by PRRs can result in the induction of either or both of two major effector mechanisms of the innate immune system, namely:

1) The inflammatory response, which is the recruitment of plasma and leukocytes into affected tissues, or

2) The anti-viral response, which limits the spread of viruses from cell to cell

As both of these responses can be damaging to tissues, they are only initiated in response to dangerous stimuli, such as infections, and not to molecules normally present in healthy tissues. The mechanisms by which the innate immune system discriminates between so-called self- and non-self molecules, and how this promotes anti-microbial defence, will now be discussed.

Key molecular features of PAMPs and DAMPs

PRRs have evolved to detect PAMPs which display the following characteristics - they should be:

1) Highly conserved molecules expressed not just by one species, but by whole classes of microbes (such as Gram-negative bacteria or RNA viruses)

2) Required for the survival, or at least the pathogenicity, of such organisms

3) Not present in mammalian cells

By focussing on such molecules, the innate immune system achieves its aim of not targeting healthy tissues, and it also ensures that it is very difficult for microbes to mutate or reduce expression of molecules in order to evade the immune system. The consequence of these relatively strict requirements, however, is that only a small number of molecules meet these criteria and may be recognised as PAMPs by mammalian PRRs. Table 4.1 and Figure 4.1 summarise the properties of several key PAMPs recognised by the human innate immune system.

Table 4.1: Properties of major PAMPs

![](image_rsrc4BG.jpg)

![](image_rsrc4BH.jpg)

Although the recognition of PAMPs by the innate immune system is tailored to prevent responses in healthy, uninfected tissues, certain molecules of host cell origin can also be produced by damaged cells or tissues to stimulate the innate immune system. These molecules are referred to as damage associated molecular patterns, or DAMPs. Examples include mitochondrial DNA and proteins (which are normally sequestered from PRRs in healthy cells), ATP (when it is present in the extracellular space) and the chromatin binding protein high-mobility group box 1. While there is some evidence that many of these molecules interact directly with PRRs, evidence also exists to suggest that some proposed DAMPs do not act on PRRs directly, but rather increase cellular sensitivity to PAMPs and other inflammatory stimuli.

Physical and chemical barriers also contribute to innate immunity

In addition to the responses initiated by PRR detection of PAMPs, other, less active mechanisms of defence can also be considered to be a part of the innate immune system. For example, the epithelial cells of the skin and mucosal surfaces serve an essential defensive role by forming a continuous physical barrier to separate the tissues from the external environment. The strength of this barrier is maintained by the expression of tight junction proteins, which join each epithelial cell tightly to its neighbour, preventing the formation of gaps that might be penetrated by microbes. Thick layers of keratin protein in the skin serve a similar purpose.

Various secretions, such as saliva and sebum, also contain antimicrobial proteins and other substances which damage or limit the growth of microbes. An example of such proteins is the enzyme lysozyme, which degrades the cell wall of many types of bacteria, and is present in saliva and tears. Ingested organisms pass through the stomach, where they are subjected to highly acidic conditions (pH 1.3 - 3.5). which, combined with the actions of digestive enzymes, is toxic to most micro-organisms. Beyond the stomach, bile acids in the intestine disrupt the membrane lipids of many different types of bacteria. The peristaltic motion of the small intestine keeps ingested material moving quickly towards the colon, so limiting the opportunities for pathogens to invade. In an interesting twist on evolution, the commensal microbes which colonise our skin and mucosal surfaces also play a key role in host defence. The provision of a conducive environment for a carefully selected array of benign, non-pathogenic organisms to replicate and remain resident by the host makes it difficult for pathogenic organisms to take hold, since they are often outcompeted by the commensal organisms. Mucous, secreted onto certain epithelial surfaces, traps and slows the progress of micro-organisms, allowing them to be expelled by peristalsis or ciliary action.

A further mechanism which limits the invasion of micro-organisms is the expression of anti-microbial peptides by epithelial cells, most notably defensins and cathelicidins. Defensins are short peptides (29-34 amino acid long) with a net positive charge (hence the term ‘cationic peptide’, is often used) that are toxic to bacteria and fungi. There are two main types of defensin in humans, α and β, which are distinguished by the location of their internal di-sulphide bonds. A specialised type of epithelial cell in the intestine, called the Paneth cell, is a major producer of α-defensins and the production of these peptides by Paneth cells plays a key role in the maintenance of sterility in the intestinal crypts of the small intestine. Certain leukocytes, including neutrophils and NK-cells, can also release defensins on degranulation. Cathelicidins are created by the proteolytic cleavage of an 18 kD precursor protein into two peptides, which are toxic to micro-organisms. These peptides are produced by neutrophils and by epithelial cells of the skin and the gastrointestinal tract.

Soluble PRRs promote the opsonisation and clearance of microbes

Micro-organisms that breach epithelial defences to reach the circulation are acted upon by soluble PRRs. These soluble PRRs generally target PAMPs on the outer surface of the microbes. Their roles are to (i) label the microbes as targets for ingestion and clearance by phagocytes (a process termed opsonisation, Figure 4.2), (ii) cause damage to the outer membrane of micro-organisms and (iii) promote recruitment of leukocytes to the site of infection. There are 4 main classes of soluble PRR that will be discussed in this chapter: complement proteins, pentraxins, collectins and natural antibodies.

![](image_rsrc4BJ.jpg)

Proteolytic cascades are central to soluble PRR effector functions

A key mechanism shared by many circulating PRRs is the ability to rapidly amplify responses on activation through the use of proteolytic cascades. Plasma contains many so-called zymogens, which are enzymes that remain inactive until they are cleaved by a protease. Some of these zymogens are also proteases themselves, and the activation of one zymogen can result in activation of many further zymogens downstream, each activating the next by proteolytic cleavage. The final products of such cascades generally have inflammatory and anti-microbial properties.

There are three major pathways of complement activation

The complement system is a family of circulating proteins that represent a good example of such a cascade. The central mediator of this system is a plasma protein called C3. When cleaved, C3 gives rise to two smaller products: C3a and C3b. C3a serves to promote leukocyte recruitment and C3b binds to the microbial surface to promote opsonisation and, later, the recruitment of further proteins to damage the microbial membrane. There are three major pathways which lead to the activation of C3: the classical, alternative and lectin pathways of complement activation (Figure 4.3).

The classical pathway is activated by the binding of a protein called C1q to antibodies bound to a microbial surface. This hexameric protein carries with it the helper proteins C1r and C1s, which are proteases that are activated when C1q binds the Fc portions of antibody (most efficiently IgM), resulting in activation of the complement cascade. Notably, it is only activated by antibodies bound to a fixed surface, and not by free, soluble antibodies.

The alternative pathway is triggered by C3 itself. C3 is slightly unstable and tends to become activated spontaneously on binding to membranes. To prevent damage being caused to host cells, they express proteins on their surface which inhibit the actions of C3. Since bacteria lack these inhibitors, they become labelled by C3. Some bacterial cell wall molecules, such as LPS, also enhance C3 activation.

The lectin pathway is mediated by mannose binding lectin (MBL), which is a soluble PRR similar to C1q. However, unlike C1q it does not bind to antibody but to terminal mannose sugars on microbial proteins and lipids. Also, it does not use C1r and C1s to initiate the complement protease cascade. Instead, two associated proteins called mannan-binding protein associated serine protease (MASP)-1 and MASP-2 are employed.

The protease cascades initiated by each of these pathways results in the cleavage of C3 to yield C3a and C3b. C3b binds covalently, and therefore irreversibly, to the target membrane and is recognised by receptors on phagocytes to promote ingestion and degradation of the pathogen. C3b also enables the activation of C5 convertase, which yields C5a and C5b. C5a is, like C3a, a chemoattractant for leukocytes. C5b initiates the formation of the so-called membrane attack complex, which generates a pore structure composed of many complement proteins that together punch a hole in the microbial membrane, promoting lysis of the microbe.

![](image_rsrc4BK.jpg)

Pentraxins, collectins and ficolins act as opsonins and activators of complement

Pentraxins are multimeric plasma proteins that bind to microbial surfaces and activate the classical pathway of complement activation, activating C1q in much the same way as bound antibody does. Prominent members of this family include C-reactive protein (CRP) and serum amyloid P (SAP). CRP and SAP tend to recognise phospholipid head-group features associated with both microbial membranes and damaged or dying host cells.

Collectins also act as opsonins and include MBL and the pulmonary surfactant proteins SP-A and SP-D. Structurally similar to these, ficolin also employs MASP1 and MASP2 for the activation of complement. The PAMPs recognised and bound by ficolin are N-acetylglucosamine and lipoteichoic acid molecules, which are present in the cell walls of certain types of bacteria.

Natural antibodies show limited diversity and are part of the innate immune system

While the adaptive immune system is proficient at producing antibodies with high levels of diversity and antigen specificity, another type of antibody is also produced by certain types of B-cell that displays much lower receptor diversity, and is considered to be part of the innate immune system. These so-called natural antibodies are generated by recombination of a limited set of DNA segments (see Chapter 10), and generally target carbohydrate or lipid antigens associated with microbial and damaged host cell membranes. Natural antibodies are produced before exposure to microbes, and tend to be of the IgM class, which is the most efficient activator of complement.

Transmembrane PRRs can promote defence by initiating cell signalling

A number of PRRs expressed by cells are trans-membrane receptors capable of initiating downstream signalling and activating defensive programmes of gene expression (Figure 4.4). These receptors play various key roles in innate immunity, from encouraging the phagocytosis of microbes and debris, through to directing chemotaxis of leukocytes towards sites of infection, the activation of the inflammatory cytokine cascade and the stimulation of subsequent adaptive immune responses. We will now discuss several major families of transmembrane PRRs.

![](image_rsrc4BM.jpg)

There are 10 Toll-like receptors in the human genome

In 1997, it was discovered that deficiency in a single gene, termed ‘Toll’, resulted in dramatic loss of innate immune function and capacity to defend against infection in the fruit fly Drosophila. Since then, ten similar homologues of Toll have been identified in the human genome, and these are referred to as Toll-like receptors.

Just as in the fruit fly, the mammalian equivalents of these receptors play a key role in the recognition of microbes. They are type I single transmembrane receptors with an intracellular Toll/IL-1 receptor (TIR) domain, and an extracellular domain composed of leucine-rich repeats (LRRs). In common with several other PRRs, the LRR domain serves as the site for the recognition of PAMPs, often by binding directly to them. In the absence of infection, TLRs diffuse freely in the cell membrane in a non-activated form. However, the binding of PAMP to the extracellular domain of a TLR promotes the dimerisation of the receptor, which brings two TIR domains close together inside the cytoplasm. Thus, a new surface is formed inside the cell and this causes the recruitment of specific signalling adaptors, which relay pro-inflammatory signals to the nucleus to modify gene transcription.

Although most types of cell express at least one type of TLR (e.g., often TLR3), professional immune cells, particularly macrophages and dendritic cells, express the widest variety of TLRs (see Table 4.2). Several of the TLRs (including TLR2, TLR4 and TLR5) are generally expressed on the outer cell membrane, and therefore serve to detect PAMPs in the extracellular fluid. However, other members, such as TLRs 3, 7, 8 and 9, are expressed in endosomal compartments, and sample phagocytosed materials (Figure 4.4). By sensing the contents of the endosome, TLRs can also be used to detect molecules that are quite similar between host and microbes, such as DNA and single stranded RNA, since these molecules are not normally present in the endosomes of undamaged cells. This detection of the incorrect compartmentalisation of molecules caused by damage to cells is common to some other mechanisms of innate immune detection of tissue damage or infection.

Most TLRs detect their PAMP directly, but LPS requires the accessory extracellular protein MD-2, which remains bound to TLR4 and undergoes a conformational alteration when LPS molecules bind to a pocket in the MD-2 molecule. The movement of the lipophilic PAMPs LPS and lipopeptide through the hydrophilic extracellular fluid to their receptors on the cell surface is catalysed by the serum proteins LPS-binding protein (LBP) and soluble CD14 (sCD14). CD14 also exists as a GPI-anchored membrane bound form, which is highly expressed by monocytes and macrophages and markedly enhances their sensitivity to LPS.

Table 4.2: TLRs, their specific ligands and patterns of expression

![](image_rsrc4BN.jpg)

All TLRs stimulate multiple intracellular signalling pathways simultaneously to induce expression of target genes. The three major pathways stimulated by TLR-signalling are the nuclear factor (NF)-kB cascade, the mitogen-activated protein (MAP)-kinase cascade, and certain interferon regulatory factor (IRF) signalling pathways. Which of these pathways is induced by any particular TLR depends on the type of adaptor proteins they recruit when dimerised, and this is in turn dictated by the structure of their TIR domain, which is slightly different for each TLR (Figure 4.5).

![](image_rsrc4BP.jpg)

All of the TLRs except TLR3 recruit the adaptor protein myeloid differentiation factor-88 (MyD88). MyD88 recruitment causes activation of certain enzymes, termed downstream signalling intermediates, to become active kinases. Kinases are enzymes which add a phosphate group to other proteins. During many forms of cellular signalling, the phosphorylation of proteins is used to convert these target proteins into active kinases themselves, and these may then act on further target proteins downstream, hence the term kinase cascade. Downstream of MyD88 activation, a protein called IkBα is ubiquitinylated and degraded by the proteasome, releasing its cargo, the active transcription factor NF-kB, to the nucleus where it activates expression of inflammatory genes. The upregulation of these genes is aided by the activation of other transcription factors by other pathways, including Erk, Jnk and p38 MAPK, which are triggered downstream of the MAPK cascade.

TLR3 does not recruit MyD88, but instead recruits an adaptor called TRIF (TIR-domain containing adaptor inducing interferon-β). The key downstream target of TRIF activation is IRF3, a transcription factor that promotes the expression of anti-viral response genes, particularly type I interferons. The NF-kB cascade is also activated by TRIF, but only modestly and after some delay relative to the MyD88 pathway. TLR4 is the only other TLR which can recruit and utilise TRIF. It is unique in being able to use both MyD88 and TRIF at the same time.

A slightly different signalling pathway occurs downstream of the endosomal TLRs 7, 8 and 9. These utilise MyD88, but not TRIF, and other adaptors to stimulate the activation of IRF3 and IRF7, which co-operate to potently induce expression of type I interferons. Thus, cell surface TLRs tend to be good activators of the inflammatory response, and endosomal TLRs tend to be good activators of anti-viral responses.

Formyl peptide receptors

The first amino acid residue of bacterial proteins is a methionine modified by the attachment of a formyl chemical group. This molecular signature is recognised by formyl peptide receptors on macrophages and neutrophils to promote the movement of these cells towards the source of the molecule in a chemotactic manner. Unlike most other PAMPs, formyl peptide does not induce robust inflammatory cytokine production. This may be because while this signature is not present on most mammalian proteins, it is part of mitochondrial proteins, since these organelles are effectively miniature bacteria lacking most, but not all, PAMPs (specifically, they retain formyl peptides and CpG DNA). Because mitochondrial proteins are compartmentalised away from PRRs in undamaged cells, mitochondrial formyl peptide and mitochondrial DNA can both be considered to be examples of DAMPs. Formyl peptide receptors are 7-transmembrane G-protein coupled receptors (GPCRs) that signal via cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP).

Dectins

Dectins belong to a family of proteins called C-type lectins. Lectins are proteins that bind to carbohydrate groups, and the ‘C’ indicates that these lectins require calcium ions for binding. Dectin-1 binds β-glucan, which is abundant in the cell walls of yeast, and dectin-2 binds mannose sugars on the hyphal form of the common pathogenic fungus Candida albicans. Recognition of these PAMPs by dectin-1 or dectin-2 promotes intracellular signalling cascades that result in the expression of pro-inflammatory cytokine production by dendritic cells.

The mannose receptor

The mannose receptor also belongs to the C-type lectin family of proteins, but unlike dectins or TLRs, does not appear to induce intracellular signalling associated with inflammatory responses. Its chief purpose is to detect proteins modified by terminal D-mannose, L-fucose or N-acetyl-D-glucosamine sugar residues, which are common in microbes but rare in mammalian proteins. Binding of these PAMPs facilitates the phagocytosis of micro-organisms.

Scavenger receptors

The scavenger receptors are a class of structurally diverse cell surface proteins that serve to enhance the binding and subsequent phagocytosis of extracellular molecules. The range of ligands bound by the various scavenger receptors is very broad, including many PAMPs, such as lipoteichoic acid, LPS and β-glucan, but also DAMPs, such as damaged host lipids and apoptotic cell debris. Like the mannose receptor, the chief role of scavenger receptors appears to be to aid phagocytosis and clearance of molecules, rather than the induction of pro-inflammatory signalling pathways.

Cytosolic PRRs detect intracellular PAMPs or damage to cellular membranes

Because some infectious organisms can breach the cell’s membranes to enter the cytosol, many different types of cell express PRRs in the cytoplasm to detect the PAMPs of such organisms (Figure 4.6). The NOD-like receptors and RIG-like receptors (NLRs, nucleotide oligomerisation domain contain protein, and RLRs, retinoic acid inducible gene, respectively), are two major families of cytosolic PRRs. Most cytosolic PRRs detect PAMPs directly, but others sense damage to the membrane integrity of cellular compartments, that is commonly associated with invasion by intracellular microbes.

![](image_rsrc4BR.jpg)

RIG-like receptors

RIG-like receptors (RLRs) serve to induce expression of antiviral genes in response to the detection of viral RNA motifs. They appear to be able to differentiate between host and viral RNA, at least partly on the basis that host RNAs in the cytosol are generally modified to remove the 5’ triphosphate that often remains present in viral RNA. The major downstream transcription factors activated by RLR signalling include IRF3 and IRF7, which together are potent inducers of the anti-viral type I interferon genes.

NOD-like receptors

There are at least 20 different NLRs, many of which have been found to have key roles in intracellular PAMP detection. They are typically composed of three functional domains: (i) a leucine-rich repeat domain which senses PAMPs, (ii) a NACHT domain which enables individual NLRs to bind to each other, and (iii) a cell signalling domain that serves to recruit adaptors and initiate signalling. The cell signalling domain can be either a CARD, Pyrin or BIR domain, and each of these domains promotes a different type of cell signalling and gene expression response.

CARD domain containing NLRs, such as NOD1 and NOD2, initiate signalling by oligomerising in response to the binding of PAMP, which means that they bind to each other to form large complexes. This causes the recruitment and activation of a kinase called RIP2, which then results in the activation of NF-kB and the expression of inflammatory genes in macrophages, and barrier-protective genes in epithelial cells (Figure 4.6). Fragments of the peptidoglycan from Gram-negative bacteria are sensed by NOD1, and peptidoglycan fragments found in both Gram-negative and Gram-positive bacteria are sensed by NOD2. NOD1 and NOD2 play key roles in the maintenance of epithelial barrier integrity and immune responses in the gut. Mutations in NOD2 are associated with risk of developing Crohn’s disease, which is a chronic inflammatory disease of the intestine.

Inflammasomes trigger the activation and release of interleukin-1β

Inflammasomes are cytosolic multi-protein complexes that play a key role in the activation and release of the cytokines interleukin-1β (IL-1β) and IL-18. Because these cytokines are among the most potent inducers of inflammation in the body, their synthesis and release must be tightly controlled, otherwise unnecessary damage to tissues could occur. A two-step activation process is therefore utilised to control their release.

Resting macrophages contain very little IL-1β mRNA or protein. However, if the macrophage receives a ‘priming stimulus’, such as TLR-signalling in response to extracellular LPS, the IL-1β gene is transcriptionally activated, causing more IL-1β mRNA and protein to accumulate in the cytosol. At this stage, the IL-1β is present in an inactive long version, called pro-IL-1β. It also lacks a signal sequence that would allow it to leave the cell. Together, these mechanisms prevent IL-1β from being accidentally activated or released from the cell, in response to just one signal alone.

For active IL-1β to be formed, it must first be cleaved by a protease called caspase-1. Capase-1 itself is also normally expressed in the cell in an inactive, long form. In order to gain its activity, it must cluster close together with other caspase-1 molecules, resulting in autoprocessing. This clustering is achieved by the formation of large, multiprotein inflammasome complexes, which occurs in response to the detection of certain PAMPs or some forms of cell damage (the ‘second signal’). In addition to cleaving IL-1β, active caspase-1 also cleaves a protein called gasdermin D. The N-terminal fragment of cleaved gasdermin D migrates to the plasma membrane, where it forms pores that allow the release of the active IL-1β (and IL-18) out of the cell. In this way, two different, consecutive signals are generally required for the activation and release of IL-1β and IL-18.

There are several different types of inflammasome, each generally consisting of a sensor protein from the AIM2 (absent in melanoma) or NOD families, an adaptor protein (ASC), and caspase-1. ASC contains both a pyrin domain (PYD) and a caspase recruitment domain (CARD), which enable the sensor protein to connect to and cluster the caspase-1 enzyme (Figure 4.7). In unstimulated cells, these components of inflammasomes are not clustered together. They are only brought together when the sensor protein detects either their specific PAMP, or certain forms of cell damage.

The range of stimuli that may promote the activation of different inflammasomes is quite diverse. For example, NLRP1 detects the ‘lethal factor’ toxin from the bacterium Bacillus anthracis. NLRC4 detects products of bacterial type 3 secretion systems. Aim2 detects cytosolic DNA and pyrin disturbances of cytoskeleton dynamics that may reflect cellular invasion. NLRP3 inflammasomes are unusual, in that they appear to be activated by many different stimuli via common mechanisms related to cellular damage. For example, a number of different infectious or damaging agents can cause pore formation in the various membranes of the cell, resulting in characteristic changes to intracellular cathepsin and potassium ion concentrations. Membrane damage can be caused by the phagocytosis of crystals, such as asbestos or monosodium urate, that may pierce intracellular compartments and cause leakage of their constituents (e.g. cathepsin) into the cytosol. Certain pathogens may also escape from the phagolysosome into the cytosol as part of their life-cycle, or release toxins that puncture the outer cell membrane. All of these events result in similar activation of NLRP3 inflammasomes.

Not all inflammasomes require ASC. An unusual inflammasome (called the ‘non-canonical inflammasome’), forms through the clustering of caspase-4 or caspase-5 (caspase-11 in the mouse), without requirement for ASC. These proteins bind cytosolic LPS directly, resulting in the cleavage of gasdermin D and also a protein called pannexin-1. This results in the indirect activation of the NLRP3 inflammasome so caspase-1 is then activated to cleave IL-1β.

Notably, the formation of pores in the membrane by activated inflammasomes serves another key process beyond release of IL-1β. If a strong stimulus is received, the pores remain open for a long time, causing the cell to swell and die in a process called pyroptosis. It is thought that this allows cytosolic pathogens to be released for attack by other elements of the immune system.

![](image_rsrc4BS.jpg)

Cells of innate immune system

Every cell in the body is endowed with at least some form of innate immune function. For example, all cells have the ability to detect viral infection and to alter their gene expression accordingly to limit viral replication. However, other cell types are adapted to play more specific or pro-active roles in the detection of microbes or defensive responses, and these will now be discussed.

Phagocytes

Phagocytes are cells required by the body to ingest particulate matter for degradation and removal from the tissues. This process therefore plays a key housekeeping role in the body, for example by enabling the clearance of dead cells and debris. However, phagocytosis is also required for the defence against most infectious organisms. The two major cell types involved in this process are neutrophils and macrophages, both of which are well adapted to the ingestion and killing of micro-organisms through the expression of diverse cell-surface PRRs and the specialised enzymes which are required to degrade microbes within phagolysosomes. A second function of phagocytic cells, particularly macrophages, is to release cytokines following the detection of bacteria to promote broader anti-microbial responses, most notably inflammation. By expressing a wide array of PRRs, macrophages therefore serve as efficient detectors of the early signs of infection.

The binding of microbes to phagocytes is partly mediated by PRRs expressed on the cell surface. However, the process is markedly enhanced by the presence of opsonins, which bind to the surface of pathogens and mark them for phagocytosis. Efficient opsonins include complement and, particularly, antibodies of the IgG class.

Phagocytes contain intracellular compartments termed lysosomes which in turn contain enzymes and other mediators which are highly toxic to microbes. After the pathogen is engulfed, the resulting vesicle (termed a phagosome) is fused (or in other words, merged), with one or more lysosomes. The resulting intracellular vesicle is called a phagolysosome, and it is in this location that the organism is killed and its molecules digested. Some organisms are resistant to killing in the phagolysosome under normal conditions, and the phagocyte is sometimes required to be further activated to enhance its capacity to kill microbes in the phagolysosome. This activation can be induced by detection of PAMPs, or by the cytokine IFN-γ released from nearby cells, or particularly by both of these signals working together. Within the membrane of the phagolysosome, an enzyme called phagocyte oxidase converts molecular oxygen (O2) into so-called ‘reactive oxygen species’ (ROS), which are highly damaging to both host and microbial molecules. Nitric oxide, produced by the enzyme inducible nitric oxide synthase (iNOS), combines with these ROS to form further microbe damaging molecules including peroxynitrite. Powerful proteases, such as elastase and cathepsin G, are also present in the phagolysosome. Since each of these agents are toxic to nearby cells and the phagocytes themselves, full activation of the lysosomal machinery is only triggered when the necessary activating signals are present, as they are during an infection.

Dendritic cells

Although macrophages express MHC-II and can present peptides to T-cells, they are not able to activate naïve T-cells to stimulate adaptive responses to a new antigen. This requires the action of a specialised set of cells called dendritic cells (DCs). DCs are positioned throughout the body, particularly at sites at risk of infection such as the epithelial surfaces, where they serve to collect antigen and eventually migrate to lymph nodes to display processed antigen to T-cells. In addition to this function, DCs express the widest array of PRRs of any cell type and therefore serve as key sentinels for the detection of invading microbes by their ability to recognise a wide variety of potential pathogens. These cells also form a key functional bridge between the innate and adaptive immune systems. Each different type of pathogen that is encountered requires a different type of T-cell response to deal with it effectively. For example, intracellular bacteria are best dealt with by Th1 cells, and fungal infections by Th17 cells. Which type of T-cell a naïve T-cell becomes when it encounters antigen for the first time is largely decided by the DC which presents the antigen to it. The DC makes this decision based on the types of PAMPs it has detected which are associated with that particular pathogen. Indeed, efficient activation of naïve T-cells by DCs is dependent on the presence of at least one PAMP. Thus, unwanted T-cell responses to host tissues are avoided and the most appropriate type of T-cell response is developed, all as a result of triggering the correct innate immune receptors in the DC.

A second type of DC, the plasmacytoid DC, plays a quite different role in defence. By expressing many PRRs and signalling adaptors specific for viruses (e.g. TLR3, TLR7, TLR9 and IRF7), they are highly efficient sensors of viral infection and are the cell type responsible for the majority of production of protective type I interferons during viral infections.

Mast cells

Mast cells express abundant cytoplasmic granules containing pro-inflammatory cytokines, lipid mediators and histamine, which can be released by degranulation to promote inflammation and other physiological effects when these cells are activated. Such events can be triggered by the crosslinking of IgE antibodies on the cell surface (discussed further in Chapter 17), but also by innate immune receptors, such as TLRs. PAMP-induced degranulation of mast cells is thought to play a role in defence against diverse microbes.

Innate lymphoid cells

Not all lymphocytes are devoted to the adaptive immune system. A subset of lymphocytes, termed innate lymphoid cells (ILCs) plays a key role in innate immune defence. Although these cells arise from the common lymphoid progenitor, they do not express the type of diversified antigen receptors expressed on T cells and B cells. Instead, their role is to act early in the immune response, not in response to antigens, but rather to more generalised signals of infections or stress given by neighbouring cells in the tissue.

Most of the ILCs have functions that are very similar to those of helper T-cells. As we will see in later chapters, the helper T-cells (Th cells) fall into three major subsets, corresponding to the type of threat they are best suited to counter. Th1 cells react to intracellular pathogens, such as viruses. Th2 cells respond to large extracellular parasites and allergens. Th17 cells combat extracellular microbes, such as bacteria and fungi. There are also three major classes of ILC, and their functional profiles, in terms of their expression of specific transcription factors and their capacity to release different classes of cytokine, map very closely to these 3 classes of helper T-cell.

Group 1 ILCs express the transcription factor T-bet and produce cytokines such as IFN-γ and TNF. Their main functions are therefore very similar to those of Th1 cells. However, natural killer cells, which were once considered to be part of the group 1 ILCs, behave more like cytotoxic CD8 killer T-cells, and so it has been suggested that NK cells should belong to their own, separate ILC grouping. As NK-cell function is critical to early defence against viruses and tumours, a section is given to this specific ILC subset below.

Group 2 ILCs express the transcription factor GATA3, and produce classical type II cytokines, including IL-4, IL-5, IL-9 and IL-13. Their functions are therefore very similar to those of Th2 cells. However, while Th2 cells must be activated by antigen, group 2 ILCs are activated by cytokines (such as TSLP, IL-33 and IL-25), released from stressed epithelial cells. In this way, ILC2 cells play a key role in the early response to allergens and helminths (parasitic worms), by triggering mucus secretion and contraction of smooth muscle, both of which help to expel worms.

Group 3 ILCs express the transcription factor RORγt, and produce cytokines including IL-17A and IL-22. They are therefore very similar in function to Th17 cells. They are found mainly in the intestinal tract and other mucosal tissues. Their production of IL-22 is thought to be a key player in the maintenance of the integrity of the gut barrier, by promoting epithelial cell replenishment and the secretion of anti-bacterial peptides. This group was also considered until recently to contain the ‘Lymphoid tissue inducer’ subset of ILCs. These cells are required for the development of lymphoid tissue throughout the body during embryogenesis and after birth. However, like NK-cells, it has been suggested more recently that they should form their own, specific ILC group.

Natural killer cells

Natural killer cells are a subset of innate lymphoid cells, which play key roles in defence against viruses and tumours. Their role is to patrol the tissues seeking, and killing, host cells which have become either damaged or infected. Unlike lymphocytes of the adaptive immune system, they do not require any receptor rearrangement or clonal expansion to achieve this. Instead, they make contact with and ‘feel’ the surface of each host cell in turn using germ-line encoded receptors. Some cell surface markers are commonly associated with healthy cells, and others are expressed mainly by damaged or infected cells. NK cells have receptors for each of these types of molecule: one type, called activating receptors, recognises the ‘unhealthy’ markers, and another type, called inhibitory receptors, recognises the ‘healthy’ markers. If more activating receptors are stimulated than inhibitory receptors, the NK cell decides that the target cell must be infected or badly damaged, and releases cytoplasmic granules which destroy the target cell (Figure 4.8).

These granules contain a protein called perforin which forms pores in the target cell membrane through which other granule proteins termed granzymes enter into the target cell. The granzymes cleave specific proteins to trigger apoptosis in the target cell, thus removing a potential reservoir of infectious organisms, and the rate of infection is limited. Because NK cells do not require any time for receptor rearrangement, clonal selection or proliferation to perform these functions, they play a key role in the defence against intracellular pathogens in the early stages of infection, before CD8+ T-cells have expanded sufficiently to deal with the infection. Notably, a key inhibitory receptor in this pathway is MHC-I. Viruses often force their host cells to reduce expression of MHC-I in an attempt to evade detection by T-cells. This low MHC-I expression is recognised as a sign of infection by NK-cells, thus countering this otherwise simple method of viral evasion of MHC-I mediated immunity. A second function of NK cells is to release cytokines when activated, particularly IFN-γ, which activates macrophages to enhance their capacity to kill intracellular pathogens.

![](image_rsrc4BT.jpg)

Lymphocytes with antigen receptors of low diversity

While most T- and B-lymphocytes express highly diverse antigen receptors, certain subsets of T- and B-cells express antigen receptors of very low diversity. These receptors tend to recognise highly-conserved molecules associated with pathogens (i.e. PAMPs), and the cells that express these receptors can therefore be considered to be components of the innate immune system. Examples of such cells include αβ intraepithelial T-cells, invariant natural killer T-cells, B-1 B-cells and marginal zone B-cells.

Effector mechanisms of innate immunity

We can consider certain aspects of innate immunity, such as physical barriers and the opsonisation of pathogens, to be passive obstacles to infection. However, the triggering of innate immune signalling by PRRs can also result in three major active responses which aid the clearance of pathogens. These are: (i) initiating the inflammatory response (ii) initiating antiviral responses and (iii) stimulating adaptive immune responses to antigen. Each of these functions may be induced individually or together in response to infection, and will now be discussed in turn.

Innate immune signalling is a key trigger of inflammation

The simplest definition of inflammation is that it is the programmed recruitment of leukocytes and plasma constituents into the tissues. This process is required for effective defence because in most tissues of the body, the numbers of resident phagocytes and effector lymphocytes are typically too low to deal with an aggressive infection. The concentrations of antibodies, and many of the soluble mediators of innate immunity (see above), are also typically low in the tissues. The process of inflammation addresses both of these weaknesses by modifying the walls of blood vessels to allow both leukocytes and plasma from the blood to enter the tissues when and where they are required.

Inflammatory responses are typically triggered by the detection of so-called ‘danger signals’, by cells resident in the affected tissues. These signals may be related to infectious organisms (i.e. PAMPs or antigens) or tissue damage, and the cells that initially sense these molecular danger cues are often mast cells, macrophages or other cells normally present in the tissue which may express a basic PRR repertoire. Once PRRs are activated in these cells, they release a variety of pro-inflammatory mediators into their surroundings. These mediators can include cytokines (such as IL-1β, TNF-α and IL-6), chemokines (such as IL-8 and MCP-1), lipid mediators (such as prostaglandins and leukotrienes) and histamine. Which types of messengers are produced and to what extent can be quite stimulus specific, and this modifies the type of inflammatory response that results. These mediators act on the endothelial cells that line the blood vessels, causing them to express adhesion molecules and chemokines that begin the recruitment of specific subsets of leukocytes into the tissues. The same mediators also promote changes to the blood vessels themselves that increase their permeability and allow plasma to leak into the tissues.

Inflammation can be triggered within minutes of detection of a stimulus. However, because the process of inflammation can itself be damaging to tissues, numerous mechanisms are in place to prevent inflammation from continuing for too long. Ideally, the initial stimulus is cleared rapidly, and the inflammation subsides (resolves) quickly afterwards. This is termed acute inflammation, and is the type the body prefers. However, if the stimulus is not easily cleared and persists, a state of chronic inflammation can result. This reaction is quite different from acute inflammation in terms of the cell-types involved and the mediators produced. These concepts, and the major mechanisms of inflammation will be discussed in more detail in the next chapter.

Antiviral responses induced by innate immune receptors

All cells have the ability to detect when they have become infected with viruses, and this can cause them to secrete type I interferons, which are major mediators of anti-viral defence. Type I interferons instruct nearby cells to switch on their anti-viral machinery, and so limit the potential for these cells to become infected by and propagate more virus particles, thus slowing or halting the spread of the infection (Figure 4.9). Type I interferon expression is mainly regulated at the transcriptional level, and is typically induced by the detection of viral nucleic acids by PRRs such as the RIG-like receptors, or TLRs 3, 7, 8 or 9. All cells express at least some PRRs specific for viral PAMPs, but plasmacytoid DCs express the widest array of anti-viral PRRs and are the major source of type I interferon in most viral infections.

![](image_rsrc4BU.jpg)

There are two major types of type I interferon (IFN), IFN-α (of which there are 13 subtypes) and IFN-β (of which there is only one type). Most cells are able to secrete IFN-β, but IFN-α is mainly produced by pDCs. All type I interferons stimulate the type I IFN receptor, which is a heterodimer composed of the IFNAR1 and IFNAR2 transmembrane proteins. When this receptor is triggered, a number of genes are expressed to help defend the cell against viral infection. These include protein kinase R, which helps to inhibit viral protein synthesis, 2’,5’-oligo A synthetase, which helps degrade viral RNA, and Mx GTPases, which impede virion assembly and viral gene expression.

Type I interferons can also modulate other immune processes to promote broader defence against viruses. They increase the expression of MHC-I on cells, which improves the potential of virus-specific CD8+ T-cells to detect and kill infected cells and thereby clear the infection. They also enhance the capacity of CD8+ T-cells, and NK-cells, to kill virally infected cells. They promote the differentiation of naïve T-helper cells into a class called Th1, which are able to enhance anti-viral responses further. They also cause lymphocytes to remain longer in lymph nodes, thus increasing the chance that they may meet their target antigen and generate a protective adaptive immune response.

Contribution of innate immunity to adaptive immunity

One of the key challenges faced by the adaptive immune system is determining when to mount a robust response to a new antigen and when not, since responses are clearly warranted to antigens expressed by pathogens, but not to innocuous molecules, such as food antigens. A further challenge is the decision of which type of adaptive response to initiate to target a particular antigen, since different types of antibody or T-cell response are effective at clearing different types of invader. For example, a Th1 T-cell response is useful to help deal with intracellular pathogens and a Th17 T-cell response promotes neutrophil recruitment to deal with fungal infections.

The mechanism used to address this difficulty is called the two signal hypothesis. In essence, it is proposed that exposure to antigen (i.e. the first signal) is not normally sufficient to activate a naïve lymphocyte. A second signal is required to inform the lymphocyte that the antigen it has detected is associated with some form of danger and therefore merits development of a response to that antigen. This second signal comes from the innate immune system, since PRRs are very good at differentiating molecules associated with infectious organisms from those of host tissues or other harmless sources.

There are three major ways in which the innate immune system can provide this second signal. First, complement breakdown products can help B-cells to produce antibody. Second, innate immune signalling in antigen presenting cells (APCs) causes them to increase expression of so-called co-stimulators, which are molecules expressed on the APC surface that stimulate respective receptors on the lymphocyte and instruct it to become activated (see Chapter 9). Third, cytokines released from APCs or other nearby cells in response to PRR signalling can instruct B- and T-lymphocytes to differentiate in appropriate ways. By these means, PAMPs from particular classes of organism can promote specific elements of the innate immune response that result in an adaptive response tailored to deal with that type of organism.

Summary

1) Innate immunity does not require any previous exposure to an organism or antigen and does not improve on subsequent challenges with the same pathogen

2) The innate immune system is required to protect the host during the early stages of infection before an adaptive immune response can be generated

3) Receptors of the innate immune system, termed pattern recognition receptors (PRRs), tend to recognise broad features common to many different organisms or infections, but not specific details of antigens that can be highly variable between organisms

4) The molecules associated with microbes that bind to or stimulate PRRs are called pathogen-associated molecular patterns (PAMPs)

5) Damaged host cells can also release damage-associated molecules (DAMPs), which can modify innate immune responses

6) Passive mechanisms of innate immunity include epithelial and chemical barriers, mucous production, low pH in the stomach and anti-microbial enzymes and peptides secreted on external surfaces exposed to microbes

7) Active mechanisms of innate immunity include the inflammatory response, the anti-viral response and the stimulation of adaptive immune responses

8) Soluble PRRs are abundant in plasma, and many serve to bind to the surface of microbes, labelling them for uptake and clearance by phagocytic cells - this process is termed opsonisation

9) Key soluble PRRs include proteins of the complement system, pentraxins, collectins, ficolins and natural antibodies (those with limited diversity), all of which are efficient promoters of opsonisation

10) The three major pathways of complement activation are the classical pathway (induced by antibodies), the alternative pathway (triggered by spontaneous or PAMP-induced activation of C3 itself), and the lectin pathway (triggered by mannose-binding lectin)

11) Each of the three complement pathways results in cleavage of complement protein C3 to yield soluble C3a, which stimulates inflammation and C3b, which covalently attaches to the microbe surface to recruit further proteins that generate the membrane attack complex which promotes lysis of the microbe

12) Cell-associated PRRs can be expressed in the cytosol to detect intracellular PAMPs, on the cell surface to detect extracellular PAMPs, or in the endosomes to detect phagocytosed PAMPs

13) Many cell-associated PRRs induce pro-inflammatory signalling inside the cell, which often involve activation of the NF-kB and MAP-kinase signalling cascades

14) There are 9 functional Toll-like receptors in the human genome, and these transmit signals by dimerising on detection of diverse PAMPs

15) TLRs 1, 2, 4, 5 and 6 are expressed on the cell surface and detect molecules from bacterial cell walls

16) TLRs 3, 7, 8 and 9 are expressed in endosomes and detect viral and bacterial nucleic acid motifs

17) Some transmembrane PRRs bind PAMPs and enhance phagocytosis, but do not promote inflammatory signalling, such as the mannose receptor and scavenger receptors

18) Cytosolic PRRs include the NOD-like receptors (NLRs), which detect a wide variety of PAMPs and indicators of cell damage or infection, and the RIG-like receptors, which detect viral RNA

19) NLRs containing a pyrin domain are referred to as NLRPs, and on detection of PAMPs they can trigger the formation of an intracellular complex termed the inflammasome, which activates the protease caspase-1 to cleave pro-IL-1β and pro-IL-18 and yield the active, pro-inflammatory form of these cytokines

20) Prolonged inflammasome activation causes pores to form in the cell membrane, which causes the cell to die by pyroptosis

21) The uptake and degradation of particulate materials by phagocytes (phagocytosis) is required for the clearance of dead host cells, tissue debris and microbes

22) Some organisms can resist killing in the phagolysosome of unstimulated phagocytes, and so these cells may be activated further by the cytokine interferon-γ to increase production of toxic molecules, such as reactive oxygen species and elastase, enhancing killing of such organisms

23) There are three main classes of innate lymphoid cell (ILC 1-3), and each produces cytokines which closely resemble those secreted by the helper T-cell subtypes Th1, Th2 and Th17, of the adaptive immune system

24) Natural killer (NK) cells are a type of ILC that patrol the tissues, making contact with each cell they pass, to determine if any cells are infected or badly damaged

25) NK cells kill host cells that express low levels of molecules that stimulate their inhibitory receptors, or high levels of molecules that stimulate their activating receptors, by releasing cytotoxic granules onto the target cell, and thus serve as key mediators of early defence against viral infection

26) Some lymphocytes with low receptor diversity are considered to be a part of the innate immune system, since they detect conserved molecular features (i.e. PAMPs) rather than antigens

27) Inflammation is the process of recruitment of leukocytes and plasma components from the blood into the tissues, it may be either acute (resolving quickly), or chronic (not resolving quickly)

28) Type-I interferons (IFN-α and IFN-β) play a key role in the innate immune response to viral infections

29) IFN-b can be produced by most cells, while IFN-α is produced mainly by plasmacytoid dendritic cells

30) Type-I interferons are induced by the sensing of viral nucleic acids by PRRs and cause both the cell that secreted them and nearby cells to upregulate expression of genes that limit the replication of viruses

31) PAMPs promote PRR signalling in antigen-presenting cells which helps them to activate lymphocytes and initiate an adaptive response to antigens presented at the same time by the APC

32) This is referred to as the two signal hypothesis (the first signal is antigen, the second is the PAMP), and prevents unwarranted responses to innocuous molecules

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innate_immunity>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complement_cascade>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pathogen-associated_molecular_patterns>

Franchi L, Muñoz-Planillo R, Núñez G. Sensing and reacting to microbes through the inflammasomes. Nat Immunol 13:325-32 (2012) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pubmed/22430785>
