# Chapter 5: Inflammation

Most tissues of the body generally do not contain a sufficient number of phagocytes, or protective plasma proteins, to effectively deal with infectious organisms or tissue damage. To address this, the process of inflammation enables leukocytes and plasma proteins to be recruited from the blood to enter the affected tissues. Inflammation is by and large a transient process localised to small areas of tissue, serving to repel or inactivate invading micro-organisms, and to initiate repair processes in damaged tissues. Changes to the blood vessels which allow leukocytes and plasma to enter the affected tissues cause the five cardinal signs of inflammation: redness, heat, swelling, pain and loss of tissue function. Healthy tissue can become inflammed within minutes of infection or tissue damage. In most cases, the infectious or injurious stimulus is quickly cleared and the inflammatory response subsides, returning the tissues to normal within days. As will be discussed below, chronic inflammation can ensue if the stimulus is not successfully cleared.

Common triggers of inflammation

The inflammatory response is generally triggered by the detection of so-called ‘danger signals’, by cells resident in the affected tissues. Common triggers of inflammation include:

(i) Infection by pathogens (such as bacteria, viruses or fungi)

(ii) Tissue damage (such as by foreign bodies, chemical irritants, ionizing radiation, burns and toxins)

(iii) Adaptive immune responses to antigens (such as antibody binding to antigens, or effector T-cell responses to antigen)

A very common trigger of inflammation is the induction of inflammatory signalling pathways in response to the detection of PAMPs by PRRs expressed by cells normally present in the tissue. The cells initiating these responses are most often macrophages or mast cells, although other cells in the affected tissue may play a role in the detection of stimulus and induction of the inflammatory response. These activated cells then begin to secrete soluble ‘pro-inflammatory’ mediators which act on the nearby blood vessels to cause the required changes to blood vessel function.

Inflammation causes increased permeability of blood vessels in the affected tissues

Most of the leukocytes that circulate in the blood are typically travelling too quickly to stop and enter the tissues. To aid in their recruitment, the velocity of the passing blood is reduced in the affected tissues by vasodilation (i.e. the relaxation of the blood vessels and subsequent increase in diameter) of the local blood vessels. This process also increases the amount of blood flowing to the tissues, causing the redness and heat of inflammation. Concurrently, the endothelial cells that line the blood vessels begin to contract and weaken the tight junction complexes that bind them together, thereby breaking down the barrier they normally create between the blood and the tissues beneath (Figure 5.1). This causes plasma to enter the tissues, and the swelling that is characteristic of inflammatory responses to occur.

![](image_rsrc4BV.jpg)

Plasma constituents are recruited into the tissues during inflammation

Plasma contains many proteins that have anti-microbial and other protective properties that are present at low levels in normal tissues. One of the key purposes of inflammation is to allow the recruitment of 5 major classes of protein mediator that all confer protective properties to aid the immune response. These are:

1) Antibodies \- these are required to bind to pathogens to enhance clearance by phagocytes (i.e. opsonisation, for which IgG type antibodies are particularly effective) or to enhance the binding and activation of complement on the surface of microbes (for which IgM type antibodies are particularly effective), or simply to bind to the surface molecules of the invader and hinder its progress or proper function.

The following 4 key plasma components are systems comprising multiple proteins, which are activated by proteolytic cleavage cascades.

2) The complement system \- Activated complement promotes pathogen removal via opsonisation and damage to microbial membranes. C3a and C5a stimulate inflammation, and C5a acts as a chemoattractant.

3) The coagulation system \- This system is required for clotting, which is the formation of a protective protein mesh over sites of blood vessel injury. In this cascade, the plasma protein thrombin cleaves plasma fibrinogen to produce insoluble fibrin, which aggregates to form a mesh-like plug that is required to prevent bleeding in damaged tissues.

4) The fibrinolytic system \- Fibrinolysis is the process that acts in opposition to the coagulation system. It is required to ensure that clots do not grow too large and cause blockages in blood vessels.

5) The kinin system \- This system generates proteins capable of sustaining vasodilation and vascular permeability. The active molecules bradykinin and kallidin are tiny (8-9 amino acid) peptides made by proteolytic degradation of the plasma protein α-globulin.

Leukocyte recruitment requires rolling, firm binding and transmigration

For effective defence against infections, many phagocytic cells, particularly neutrophils and monocytes, must be recruited to the site of infection. This is achieved by a highly organised multi-step process, each of which occurs in the order described below (see also Figure 5.2):

![](image_rsrc4BW.jpg)

1) Detection of stimulus \- cells resident in the affected tissue become activated by the inflammatory stimulus and begin to secrete soluble pro-inflammatory mediators, such as cytokines and chemokines.

2) Endothelial activation \- the mediators released by cells which have been activated in the tissues cause the endothelial cells that line local post-capillary venules to express adhesion molecules, which are molecules that enable cells to bind to other cells.

3) Rolling \- leukocytes in the circulation typically pass the endothelial cells too quickly to be efficiently recruited. To slow them down, adhesion molecules called selectins are expressed by the endothelial cells. The leukocytes bind to these, but with relatively low affinity, and they continually attach and detach, slowly rolling along the endothelial surface in the direction of the blood flow.

4) Firm adhesion \- chemokines displayed on the surface of the endothelial cells cause the rolling leukocytes to activate another type of adhesion molecule, termed integrins. The binding of integrins to their receptors is much stronger than that of selectins, and the leukocytes begin to stop in one place, becoming firmly adherent and spreading out on the endothelial surface.

5) Transmigration \- leukocytes which have been firmly bound for some time migrate slowly towards a junction between the endothelial cells, and cause these junctions to temporarily open up to allow them passage through the endothelial cell layer into the tissues beneath. This process is also called extravasation or diapedesis.

6) Chemotaxis \- the recruited leukocytes must migrate towards the site of infection or damage within the affected tissues to perform their antimicrobial and phagocytic functions. This is achieved by the detection of chemokine gradients, since the concentration of chemokine is highest at the source of chemokine production (Figure 5.3).

![](image_rsrc4BX.jpg)

Adhesion molecules

The different stages in leukocyte recruitment described above are mediated largely by the expression of different adhesion molecules expressed both by leukocytes and endothelial cells, at different times. The two major classes of adhesion molecule involved in this process are selectins, which are expressed earliest and mediate tethering and rolling, and integrins, which are upregulated or activated slightly later, and mediate firm adhesion prior to transmigration. The major selectins, integrins and their binding partners will now be discussed.

Selectins mediate the tethering and rolling of leukocytes

Selectins are cell-membrane receptors that bind to carbohydrate groups displayed on the surface of other cells. Their main role in inflammation is to promote the slowing and rolling of passing leukocytes. This is mediated by C-type lectin domains found in the extracellular component of the protein. The three major selectins involved in inflammation are L-selectin, P-selectin and E-selectin.

P-selectin is expressed by activated endothelial cells. It is stored within granules close to the endothelial cell surface, and can be released from this compartment to the endothelial cell surface rapidly, with no requirement for new protein synthesis, in response to many different pro-inflammatory triggers. It binds to sialylated carbohydrate groups related to the Lewis X family which are added as post-translational modifications to some surface proteins of certain leukocytes. For example, the cell-surface protein P-selectin glycoprotein ligand 1 (PSGL-1), which is expressed by some leukocytes, can be modified to display such sugar groups and therefore binds P-selectin.

E-selectin is also expressed by activated endothelial cells and performs a similar role to P-selectin. However, it is not stored in granules but is upregulated by transcriptional mechanisms in response to pro-inflammatory stimuli that activate the NF-kB signalling pathway in endothelial cells. This receptor also binds sialyl Lewis X modified proteins and is particularly efficient at recruiting neutrophils.

L-selectin (CD62L) is expressed by leukocytes rather than endothelial cells. It binds to peripheral node addressin (PNAd), which is composed of different sialomucins expressed on high endothelial venules. PNAd is upregulated on endothelial cells during inflammatory responses to enhance leukocyte recruitment.

Integrins mediate firm adhesion of leukocytes to endothelial cells

Integrins form a complex family of many different members, each being a heterodimer of one α-chain (of which there are 15 different types) and one β-chain (of which there are 7 different types). Their major role is to help cells to bind to each other and to the extracellular matrix. The different integrin members play key roles in multiple processes in the body, and several play key roles in inflammation. The integrins of most relevance to inflammation are all expressed by leukocytes: LFA-1, VLA-4 and Mac-1.

LFA-1 (leukocyte function associated antigen 1, also called CD11aCD18 or β2αL), expressed by neutrophils, monocytes and T-cells, binds firmly to a molecule expressed by activated endothelial cells called ICAM-1 (intercellular adhesion molecule-1) to promote firm adherence of leukocytes to the endothelium.

VLA-4 (very late antigen 4, or β1α4), is expressed by monocytes and T-cells, and binds to VCAM-1 (vascular cell adhesion molecule-1), which is also expressed by activated endothelial cells.

Mac-1 (β2αm, or CD11bCD18), is expressed by monocytes and dendritic cells, and binds to ICAM-1. It also binds to complement breakdown products to aid the phagocytosis of microbes opsonised by complement.

A remarkable property of integrins is that they have the capability to transmit signals both into the cell and outwards from the cell. The latter function plays a key role in leukocyte recruitment. Integrins can be expressed on the cell surface in a low affinity, non-activated form, which does not bind well to its ligand. However, signals from inside the cell can alter the conformation of the extracellular component of the integrin dimer, so that it projects its binding domain outwards, thus gaining a higher affinity for its ligand. This process is stimulated by the detection of chemokines, which are presented on the surface of activated endothelial cells, and cause the rolling leukocyte to become much more adherent to activated endothelial surfaces, thereby enhancing recruitment. The process by which integrins increase their affinity for ligands in response to a signal from inside the cell, is called ‘inside-out signalling’ (depicted schematically in Figure 5.3B).

Table 5.1: Major adhesion molecules and their binding partners involved in inflammation

![](image_rsrc4BY.jpg)

Chemokines and chemotaxis

Chemokines are cytokines that promote the movement of cells towards the source of the chemokine. There are about 50 human chemokines which play diverse roles in development and the normal function of tissues. Some chemokines play a key role in inflammation, particularly with respect to integrin activation on rolling leukocytes and promoting their subsequent migration to the source of infection or tissue damage.

All chemokines are small (8-12 kDa) proteins which contain two internal disulphide bonds between cysteine amino acids. Most chemokines belong to the CXC (or α) family, or the CC (or β) family, based on whether or not there is an amino acid (X) between two cysteines. Some chemokines only have one cysteine (C family) or 3 intervening amino acids (CX3C family). Many of the CC and CXC chemokines play a role in inflammation, as their secretion may be induced by diverse cells exposed to PAMPs or pro-inflammatory cytokines. CC chemokines tend to recruit neutrophils, and CXC chemokines tend to recruit monocytes and lymphocytes.

Cells are only responsive to particular chemokines if they express the specific receptor for that chemokine. This system allows cells to home towards particular locations in the body, sometimes very specifically, by the expression of particular chemokine receptors. For example, cells resident in lymph nodes secrete the chemokines CCL19 and CCL21. When dendritic cells in the tissues are activated, and need to migrate towards the lymph node, they do this by switching on expression of the chemokine receptor CCR7, which detects CCL19 and CCL21 and allows the DCs to home towards the lymph node. Thus, the chemokine receptors expressed on the surface of a cell dictate where in the body, and under which conditions, it may migrate towards, and this can change under different states of cellular activation. There are 10 CC chemokine receptors and 6 CXC chemokine receptors, with some overlap in specificity.

All chemokine receptors are seven-transmembrane, guanosine-triphosphate binding protein coupled receptors (or 7-TM GPCRs, for short). When activated by binding their respective chemokine, they bind GTP to initiate multiple intracellular signalling pathways that culminate in alterations to the cytoskeleton which promote cell migration in the direction of the side of the cell containing the receptor that was triggered. Because the concentration of any chemokine is greatest at the source of its production and diminishes with distance from the source, a concentration gradient is set up (Figure 5.3). Cells can sense this, since the concentration on the side of the cell which is closest to the source is greater than the concentration on the side which is distant from the source. To enable rolling leukocytes to experience a high concentration of chemokine close to sites of inflammation, heparan sulphate proteoglycans expressed on endothelial cells capture chemokines and present them to passing leukocytes.

Table 5.2: Examples of major chemokines and chemokine receptors

![](image_rsrc4BZ.jpg)

Soluble inflammatory mediators

Inflammatory signals are transmitted from affected tissue to the nearby blood vessels via the release of soluble pro-inflammatory mediators. These agents include chemokines, cytokines, histamine, lipid mediators, complement breakdown products and other molecules. Because inflammation can be damaging to tissues, these messengers typically have short half lives, act locally and are quickly degraded. Each of these mediators will now be discussed in turn.

Inflammatory cytokines

Cytokines are small proteins that mediate most of the signalling between cells of the immune system, and occasionally between immune cells and non-immune cells. The three major cytokines involved in the inflammatory response are interleukin (IL)-1, tumour-necrosis factor (TNF)-α and IL-6, and these are mostly produced by macrophages in response to the detection of PAMPs.

Many different cells express the IL-1 receptor, the downstream signalling of which is very similar to that of the TLRs. As a result, via the activation of NF-kB, IL-1 induces pro-inflammatory gene expression in diverse cells, most notably adhesion molecules on endothelial cells. There are two forms of IL-1: IL-1α and IL-1β, both of which promote the same signalling events through binding to the same IL-1 receptor. The mechanism by which IL-1β is produced is unusual in that it requires the cell to receive two distinct signals before it can be secreted in an active form. The first signal is generally mediated by TLR-signalling, which activates the NF-kB pathway, leading to transcriptional upregulation of pro-IL-1β. This 33 kDa protein is not yet biologically active and remains in the cytosol. However, should a second signal be obtained through certain cytosolic receptors, inflammasome activation results in the proteolytic processing of pro-IL-1β to yield mature, active 17 kDa IL-1β that is then released from the cell to promote inflammation.

TNF-α is produced mainly by tissue macrophages in response to the detection of bacterial or fungal PAMPs. Initially, it is expressed as a homo-trimeric membrane protein which can be cleaved to release trimers of TNF-α into the extracellular fluid or the circulation. Most cell types are responsive to TNF-a via expression of the two TNF-α receptors: TNF-RI and TNF-RII. Signalling downstream of these receptors can promote either inflammatory gene expression (such as chemokines, via NF-kB and MAP-kinase signalling), or apoptosis, via caspase activation.

IL-6 can be produced by monocytes, macrophages, endothelial cells, fibroblasts and dendritic cells via detection of PAMPs, IL-1 or TNF-α. The IL-6 receptor shares a subunit (gp130) with several other cytokine receptors to transmit signalling into the cell via activation of the transcription factor STAT3.

There are also cytokines which inhibit inflammatory responses, and these are always produced (to some extent) at the same time, or shortly after, production of pro-inflammatory cytokines. Their role is to limit the damage that excessive inflammatory responses could have on the tissues, and to aid the process of resolution, which is described in more detail below. This induction of two opposing signals at the same time is a common theme in the immune system. In simple terms, by keeping one foot on the brake and one on the accelerator, better control the overall response is enabled. Key anti-inflammatory cytokines include transforming growth factor (TGF)-β, IL-1 receptor antagonist (IL-1RA) and interleukin-10.

Systemic effects of inflammatory cytokines

Although most mediators of inflammation act locally and have little impact systemically, IL-1, TNF-α and IL-6 have the capacity to cause changes throughout the body. Each of these cytokines are normally present at very low concentrations in plasma (<1 pg/ml). However, if a large mass of tissue is inflammed, or if the primary inflammatory stimulus accesses the circulation, the concentrations of these cytokines in the blood can increase to reach levels that cause a range of systemic effects. These include: fever (via detection of the cytokines by the hypothalamus), malaise, loss of appetite, tiredness, changes to circulating leukocyte numbers (via detection of the cytokines in the bone marrow), and the induction of acute phase proteins. It is thought that each of these responses aids host defence against infection.

Acute Phase Proteins

Hepatocytes in the liver are highly sensitive to IL-1, TNF-α and IL-6. When these cytokines are detected, they alter hepatocyte expression of certain genes, resulting in changes to the concentrations of the proteins they encode in the circulation. The blood proteins which change in concentration in response to inflammatory stimuli in this way are termed acute phase proteins. Some plasma proteins increase (positive acute-phase proteins) and some decrease (negative acute-phase proteins) during the acute phase response. Some acute phase proteins, such as C-reactive protein (CRP) and serum amyloid A, change concentration dramatically (up to several hundred-fold) during infections and remain elevated for days afterwards. They can therefore be used clinically as useful markers of recent exposure to inflammatory events, such as infections. Table 5.3 lists the key functions of several major acute phase proteins. Figure 5.4 shows the typical kinetics of inflammatory cytokine production and acute phase proteins in human volunteers exposed to an acute bacterial stimulus.

![](image_rsrc4C0.jpg)

Table 5.3: Examples of acute phase proteins and their actions

![](image_rsrc4C1.jpg)

Lipid mediators of inflammation and resolution

Not all inflammatory mediators are proteins. Major classes of inflammatory lipid mediator include the prostaglandins, thromboxanes and leukotrienes. These are each generated by the modification of arachidonic acid, which is a 20 carbon fatty acid chain found in phospholipids in the cell membrane. During inflammation, free arachidonic acid is released from phospholipids by the actions of the enzyme phospholipase A2 (PLA2). This free arachidonic acid may then be acted on by cyclo-oxygenase enzymes (COX-1 or COX-2), and then subsequently other enzymes, to produce biologically active prostaglandins and thromboxanes (Figure 5.5). Alternatively, it may be acted on by lipoxygenase enzymes to yield leukotrienes. These molecules have diverse structures and mediate many different functions in the body, only one of which is inflammation. Some lipid mediators, generally produced later in the inflammatory programme, are anti-inflammatory.

![](image_rsrc4C2.jpg)

Histamine

Histamine is a small molecule that regulates diverse physiological functions, depending on which of the four different histamine receptors (H1 - H4) is expressed by the target cell (Figure 5.5). For example, H2 controls gastric acid secretion and H3 controls signalling in the nervous system. However, stimulation of the H1 receptor by histamine plays a role in some inflammatory responses. Histamine is abundant in the granules of mast cells which are especially numerous at sites of potential injury or infection - the nose, mouth, and feet, internal body surfaces, and blood vessels. When mast cells or basophils degranulate, for example in response to IgE cross-linked by the appropriate antigen, histamine is released. Inflammatory effects of histamine include:

1) Increased dilation of blood vessels

2) Increased permeability of the capillaries to white blood cells and proteins

3) Stimulation of sensory nerves (itching)

Platelet activating factor

Platelet activating factor (PAF) is released from most inflammatory cells when activated. It is generated by the release of lysophosphatidylcholine from cell membrane phospholipids, the joining of the sn-1 acyl chain by an ether bond and the replacement of the sn-2 acyl chain with an acetyl group (Figure 5.5). The resulting molecule is more hydrophilic than the phospholipid from which it was derived, and so can transmit signals through the aqueous compartment between cells. PAF is active at low concentrations (from 100 pM), and mediates a range of activities. For example, it can:

(i) act a potent chemotaxin for monocytes and neutrophils

(ii) increase vascular permeability and the aggregation of platelets

(iii) activate PLA2 to upregulate generation of eicosanoids

Neuropeptides

Neuropeptides are released from sensory neurons and can contribute to inflammatory reactions. The three main neuropeptides are: substance P, neurokinin A and calcitonin gene related peptide. Substance P and neurokinin A can act on mast cells to release histamine, induce mucus secretion and smooth muscle contraction. Calcitonin gene related peptide is a potent vasodilator.

Resolution of inflammation

As mentioned above, the process of inflammation can be damaging to tissues, not least because of the proteases used by leukocytes to migrate through the matrix between cells, and their release of anti-microbial molecules, such as reactive oxygen species and peroxynitrite, which are also toxic to host cells. To limit this damage, a number of mechanisms are in place to ensure that inflammation does not become overly aggressive, and once the stimulus is cleared (antigen or infection, for example), the inflammatory response subsides and tissues return to normal. Such mechanisms include the fact that inflammatory mediators generally have short half lives, their production by cells occurs during a narrow window following activation (see Figure 5.4), and anti-inflammatory mediators are also produced in inflammed tissues with longer lasting kinetics.

In addition to these mechanisms, there is also a coordinated program by which inflammatory responses subside and recruited leukocytes leave the tissues. This process is called resolution, and usually initiates several hours after an inflammatory response begins. The key steps in resolution are:

1) Switching off pro-inflammatory mediators

Most pro-inflammatory cytokines are produced with fairly acute kinetics, meaning that although they are rapidly induced, their production also ceases within several hours (see Figure 5.4).

2) Switch to production of anti-inflammatory mediators

As time progresses, the balance of modulators of inflammation shift from mostly pro-inflammatory (such as IL-1 and TNF-α) to mostly anti-inflammatory mediators (such as IL-10 and IL-1RA). Neutrophils, which earlier in the inflammatory response produced mainly pro-inflammatory lipid mediators, begin to express alternative lipid modifying enzymes and utilise fatty acids with more unsaturated bonds than arachidonic acid. This leads to a change in the types of lipid produced by these cells from mainly pro-inflammatory lipids derived from arachidonic acid, to mainly anti-inflammatory mediators derived from arachidonic acid, such as lipoxins, and also eicosapentaenoic acid and docosahexaenoic acid, such as resolvins and protectins. These mediators inhibit the recruitment of further leukocytes and the blood vessels begin to return to normal, thus reducing swelling in the affected tissue.

3) Apoptosis of recruited neutrophils

Neutrophils are short lived cells, and following their recruitment to the tissues, they may only survive for several hours or less, before dying by apoptosis. This prevents these cells from becoming reservoirs of infection for organisms specialised to survive in phagolysosomes. This also prevents further damage being caused to the tissues by molecules released from activated neutrophils, such as ROS and proteases.

4) Clearance of apoptotic bodies by macrophages

The apoptotic programme cleaves the neutrophil into small membrane-bound fragments termed apoptotic bodies. Macrophages efficiently engulf and digest these apoptotic bodies, thereby removing much of the cellular debris caused by the massive influx of neutrophils from the site of inflammation over the next day or so. Killed micro-organisms are also cleared from the tissues in this manner.

5) Production of TGF-β and IL-10 by macrophages

The ingestion of apoptotic bodies by macrophages causes them to initiate production of two major anti-inflammatory cytokines: transforming growth factor-(TGF)-β and IL-10. Both of these cytokines act on immune cells to inhibit inflammatory signalling pathways and reduce recruitment of further leukocytes to the site of inflammation. TGF-β furthermore promotes collagen formation and wound healing.

6) Macrophages exit via the lymphatics

In order for tissues to return to normal function after the inflammation has subsided, most of the remaining macrophages need to be cleared. This is achieved largely by the migration of macrophages towards the draining lymphatic vessels and out of the affected tissues, using mechanisms similar to those employed by activated dendritic cells.

7) Tissue is restored to normal

In successful resolution, the blood vessels return to normal, endothelial cell expression of adhesion molecules and chemokines returns to baseline levels, and the leukocytes that were recruited initially die or migrate away from the tissues. Ideally, the resident cells of the affected tissues retain their function, and the extracellular matrix between them is effectively repaired to yield tissue that is much the same as it was before the inflammatory event.

Potential outcomes of inflammation

The above section on resolution of inflammation describes the ideal outcome from inflammation: i.e. a brief period of leukocyte recruitment followed by restoration of the tissues to homeostasis. However, depending on the nature of the injury which has occurred and the injurious agent that caused it, there are several possible outcomes from acute inflammation:

1) Successful resolution

Most often, inflammed tissue is restored to a normal status. Blood vessels lose their permeability, production of inflammatory mediators and leukocyte infiltration ceases, and tissue cells regenerate the extracellular matrix.

2) Fibrosis

If the damage caused by inflammation cannot be effectively regenerated, fibrous scarring can occur in some tissues, forming a scar composed primarily of collagen. Parenchymal cells are typically absent in the scar, potentially leading to some loss of tissue function.

3) Abscess formation

An encapsulated structure called an abscess can occasionally be formed in an attempt to limit the spread of an infectious organism. The cavity of the structure contains pus which is composed of dead leukocytes and microbes and other inflammatory debris.

4) Chronic inflammation

The goal of acute inflammation is to eliminate the injurious stimulus. If this is not achieved and the primary inflammatory stimulus remains in the affected tissue, then chronic inflammation will ensue. Chronic inflammation is characterised by a domination of the lesion by macrophages, but no longer neutrophils, which distinguishes it from acute inflammation, and a failure of the final stages of resolution. Tissues can remain in this state for many months or even years, leading to further tissue damage and a cycle of unresolving inflammation.

Inflammatory disorders

As mentioned above, inflammation is most often acute, localised, successful and self-limiting. However, unwarranted or chronic inflammation can occasionally result, playing a key role in the development of several diseases, as described in the examples below.

Crohn’s disease \- this chronic inflammatory bowel disease (IBD) can affect any part of the gastrointestinal tract, although it is most common in the terminal ileum. It is thought to be triggered by responses directed at penetration of the host commensal microbiota into the gut wall as a result of inadequate innate immune defences at the epithelial layer.

Ulcerative colitis \- unlike Crohn’s disease, this inflammatory bowel disease affects only the colon. The cause of the disease remains unclear, although genetic and environmental factors are thought to play a role in triggering disease in susceptible individuals.

Rheumatoid arthritis \- this chronic inflammatory disease is caused by inappropriate adaptive immune responses to self-proteins, i.e. it is an auto-immune disease. The inflammation caused by these responses leads to tissue destruction in the joints of patients. Anti-inflammatory drugs have some success in reducing symptoms but do not prevent joint destruction.

Atherosclerosis \- this chronic inflammatory disease of the arteries is the major cause of heart attacks and strokes, and is therefore one of the leading causes of premature death in the Western world. It is characterised by the recruitment of circulating monocytes into the subendothelial space of the arteries and their subsequent uptake of lipids to develop an atherosclerotic plaque. Macrophages are abundant in such lesions, and it is thought that their failure to resolve results in plaque growth over decades. Animal models of atherosclerosis indicate that inflammatory signalling is essential for development of the disease. Raised levels of circulating inflammatory markers, such as CRP, are predictive of risk of developing atherosclerosis in human population studies.

Psoriasis \- this lifelong condition causes inflammatory lesions in the skin, and in some patients, also the joints. The cause remains unclear, although genetic studies point towards a role for inherited variants in genes controlling inflammatory and adaptive immunity signalling pathways.

Autoinflammatory syndromes \- this term describes a number of rare conditions, most of which involve genetic deficiencies in the genes controlling pathways of inflammation and/or resolution. They are heritable and tend to cause periodic flares of skin lesions and fever. Examples include Familial Mediterranean fever, which is caused by mutations in the pyrin gene, and Muckle-Wells syndrome, which is caused by mutations in the cryopyrin gene.

Systemic inflammatory response syndrome \- most inflammatory stimuli generally result in a response that is localised to a small area of tissue. However, if a very large tissue mass is affected by inflammation, or if large quantities of the inflammatory stimulus enter the blood, inflammatory signalling can be triggered throughout the body. This is called the systemic inflammatory response syndrome (SIRS), and is characterised by marked increases in pro-inflammatory cytokines in the blood. Physiological symptoms include, fever, raised heart rate and respiration and increased leukocyte counts. The effect of the cytokines on blood vessels can lead to a marked reduction in blood pressure (shock) and potentially organ damage. It is often associated with the presence of infectious organisms in the bloodstream (termed sepsis).

Obesity \- it is believed that the onset of obesity is associated with the development of a very mild, chronic inflammatory response, as evidenced by the strong association between increased body mass index and modestly raised levels of CRP in population studies.

Anti-inflammatory drugs

Treatment of the symptoms of many diseases includes the use of drugs targeting inflammation i.e., anti-inflammatories, because inflammation is often a cause of pain and fever associated with various ailments. The two most widely used classes of anti-inflammatories are steroids and non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs), but there are some others as described below.

Steroidal anti-inflammatories \- steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, such as dexamethasone, are related in both structure and function to naturally occurring glucocorticoids, which are steroid hormones used by the body to modulate a number of different physiological processes. They mediate their effects by binding to the glucocorticoid receptor, resulting in the inhibition of both NF-kB activation and PLA2. Because these two pathways play key roles in the generation of inflammatory cytokines and eicosanoids, respectively, glucocorticoids are potently anti-inflammatory. Examples of inflammatory diseases treated with steroids include asthma, allergies, arthritis and IBDs. However, because of the large number of other cellular processes affected by glucocorticoids, side effects can occur after long term systemic use. These include increased risk of infection caused by immunosuppression, weight gain, osteoporosis and muscle weakness.

Non-steroidal anti-inflammatories \- The generation of pro-inflammatory lipid mediators from arachidonic acid (eicosanoids) is dependent on the cyclo-oxygenase (COX)-1 and COX-2 enzymes. Most NSAIDs are inhibitors of both of these enzymes. Commonly used NSAIDs include aspirin and ibuprofen, which are often prescribed for symptomatic relief of fever (via reduced prostaglandin production in the hypothalamus) and mild to moderate pain associated with inflammation. Side effects are rare for short term use of NSAIDs. However, there is mounting evidence that long term NSAID use, other than low-dose aspirin, can increase risk of gastrointestinal bleeding and cardiovascular disease.

Anti-histamines \- these drugs block either the production of histamine or the binding of histamine to the H1 (inflammatory) histamine receptor. They are most commonly used to treat the symptoms of allergies, since the symptoms of allergy are typically mediated by histamine released from mast cell or basophil degranulation in response to IgE crosslinking by antigen.

Biologics \- recently, a number of protein-based drugs have been developed to mimic various components of the immune system itself. These biologics (or biologicals) are therefore able to target components of the inflammatory cascade with much greater specificity than the traditional anti-inflammatory drugs described above. Examples include monoclonal antibodies that neutralise IL-1β (e.g. Canakinumab, used to treat autoinflammatory syndromes) or TNF-α (e.g. infliximab, used to treat rheumatoid arthritis and Crohn’s disease). By also binding and neutralising TNF-α, the recombinant human TNF-receptor fusion protein etanercept is useful for treatment of the same diseases as infliximab. Recombinant IL-1RA (anakinra) is also used to treat rheumatoid arthritis.

Summary

1) Inflammation is the process by which plasma constituents and leukocytes from the blood are recruited into the affected tissues

2) Inflammation is typically a localised process that aids defence and tissue repair by concentrating leukocytes at sites of infection or tissue damage

3) Inflammation can develop within minutes after being triggered, and common triggers of inflammation are the detection of PAMPs by PRRs expressed on cells resident in the infected tissues, immune complexes, tissue damage and IgE-mediated mast cell degranulation

4) Changes to the blood vessels during inflammation include vasodilation, slowing of the blood, increased permeability and increased adhesion molecule and chemokine expression by endothelial cells

5) Key plasma constituents that are recruited during inflammation include antibodies, and components of the complement, coagulation, fibrinolytic and kinin systems

6) Leukocyte recruitment into tissues is a multistep process beginning with tethering to activated endothelial cells, then rolling, firm adhesion and diapedesis (transmigration) of the leukocyte

7) Leukocyte binding to endothelial cells is mediated by selectins (which promote loose attachment and tethering) and integrins (which promote firm binding)

8) Key selectins expressed during inflammation include E- and P-selectin expressed by endothelial cells and L-selectin expressed by leukocytes

9) Key integrins involved in inflammatory recruitment include VLA4, which binds to VCAM-1, and Mac-1, which binds to ICAM-1

10) Soluble mediators released from tissue macrophages and other cells to promote inflammation include: cytokines, chemokines, histamine and lipid mediators

11) Key inflammatory cytokines are IL-1β, TNF-α and IL-6, which promote inflammation and can have effects systemically if produced in large quantities, such as induction of fever, malaise, tiredness, acute phase protein production by hepatocytes and changes to circulating leukocyte numbers

12) Acute-phase proteins such as C-reactive protein (CRP) and serum amyloid A (SAA) are useful circulating markers of recent inflammatory events in an individual

13) The resolution of inflammation is a carefully co-ordinated process, involving the switching of lipid mediator production to anti-inflammatory variants, apoptosis of recruited neutrophils, clearance of apoptotic bodies, production of TGF-β and IL-10 by macrophages, the exit of macrophages via the lymphatics and restoration of tissue to its normal function

14) If inflammation is not successfully resolved, typically because the stimulus is not successfully cleared, it can become chronic, resulting in changes to the types of immune cell present in the lesion and alterations to the tissue that may damage its function, such as fibrosis (caused by collagen deposition), or formation of chronic wounds or abscesses

15) Inflammatory disorders tend to occur when acute inflammation is not resolved successfully, examples include rheumatoid arthritis, Crohn’s disease, ulcerative colitis and atherosclerosis

16) Anti-inflammatory drugs are typically steroidal (such as dexamethasone, which target NF-kB and PLA2) or non-steroidal (such as ibuprofen and aspirin, which target COX enzymes), newer anti-inflammatories include antibodies and other recombinant proteins targeting inflammatory cytokines such as IL-1β or TNF-α

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflammation>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pattern_recognition_receptor>

Ortega-Gómez A, Perretti M, Soehnlein O. Resolution of inflammation: an integrated view. EMBO Mol Med 5:661-74 (2013) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3662311/>

Chen L, et al. Inflammatory responses and inflammation-associated diseases in organs. Oncotarget 9:7204-18 (2018) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5805548/>
