# Chapter 6: Overview of adaptive immunity

While the innate immune response discussed in earlier chapters is often sufficient to repel or eliminate infectious organisms, the clearance of many other pathogens requires a more sophisticated response. This is provided by the adaptive immune system, which is mediated by B- and T-lymphocytes. This chapter will introduce some of the key concepts of adaptive immunity, before we begin to explore the various mechanisms of this system in more depth in subsequent chapters.

Proteins, and other highly variable antigens, make excellent targets for immune surveillance

As discussed earlier, the innate immune system is able to distinguish micro-organisms (or non-self) from host cells, by the detection of PAMPs using germline encoded receptors. This mechanism depends on the expression of highly conserved molecular motifs by diverse species of microbes. However, the number of different types of molecule that meet those criteria are relatively low, and many pathogens have evolved mechanisms to evade detection by the innate immune system.

Fortunately, in addition to PAMPs, there are other features of micro-organisms that are quite distinct from human cells. The most obvious of these is that the DNA sequence of their genes is different from the DNA sequence of human genes. It follows that the amino acid sequences of most microbial proteins, and therefore the structures of these proteins, are also different from the amino acid sequences and structures of proteins expressed by human cells. Because a microbe may express anywhere between 10 and 10,000 different proteins, the different molecular shapes of these proteins make excellent targets to enable the discrimination between self and non-self. Detecting and remembering these subtle differences is the main purpose of the adaptive immune system. However, achieving this feat requires solving six key distinct and difficult problems.

Problem 1: Traditional pattern recognition receptors cannot detect variable antigens

Pattern recognition receptors, encoded by a small number of genes which evolve very slowly over millions of years, are ideally suited to the detection of microbial molecules that also do not change greatly over similar timeframes. We describe such molecules, which change little over time and tend to be shared by many species of the same type of organism, as being highly ‘conserved’. Examples include peptidoglycan and the lipid-A component of lipopolysaccharide, both of which have remained largely unchanged in bacteria for the last 3 billion years or so. Such molecules remain conserved over millennia because the machinery of the organism becomes dependent on these molecules working in a particular way. The fact that it takes millions of years for vertebrates to evolve a pattern recognition receptor to detect a particular target molecule is not a problem, since PAMPs, by definition, change little over such periods of time.

By contrast, however, microbes are able to evolve new amino acid sequences of almost any of their proteins very quickly and very easily. This is because small numbers of mutations accumulate in the chromosomal DNA of microbes every time they divide, and these mutations may change the amino acid sequence of a protein expressed by that organism. For example, if one bacterium gives rise to ten million bacteria over the course of a week, the chances are that one of those bacteria will express a mutant version of a protein that could have become a target for an emerging PRR. So-called ‘escape mutants’, would then have a selective advantage over other microbes without the mutation, as they would be able to evade the immune system and multiply. It is this rapid rate of mutation and selection, leading to the inherent capacity of microbes to rapidly generate new variants of proteins, that makes it almost impossible for PRRs to evolve to detect microbial proteins.

Solution: Generate millions of receptors with specificity for millions of different possible antigens

To combat this first problem, the adaptive immune system has evolved a strategy to generate not just a dozen or so receptors targeting a small number of PAMPs (as we see in innate immunity), but millions of different receptors which are collectively able to detect and initiate responses to millions of different possible antigens. Individual clones of B- or T-lymphocyte, each derived from a single cell, express a different antigen receptor with specificity for a different antigen. Because our bodies contain millions of lymphocytes, we can express millions of different receptors, and so have cells ready to respond to almost any antigen that we are likely to encounter. In this way, no matter what type of infectious organism may challenge the host, it is likely that one or more of the proteins expressed by that organism will be recognised by a corresponding receptor expressed by at least one B- or T-lymphocyte clone. Thus, the problem of detection of microbial proteins, and other highly variable antigens, is solved (Figure 6.1).

![](image_rsrc4C3.jpg)

Problem 2: The number of lymphocytes responsive to any particular antigen is too low at the start of an infection

The next problem faced by the adaptive immune system is that while there are millions of receptors able to detect millions of different antigens, when infected with a particular microbe for the first time, the number of lymphocytes expressing a receptor responsive to that particular pathogen is very low (perhaps as low as one cell with a useful receptor in one million circulating lymphocytes). This means that at the start of an infection, the numbers of responsive lymphocytes are too low to mount an efficient defensive response against that organism.

Solution: Clonal selection allows the proliferation of cells expressing useful receptors

The solution to problem 2 is a dramatic expansion of the numbers of cells expressing antigen receptors that correctly recognise an invading microbe during the course of infection. This process is referred to as clonal selection. The rare reactive lymphocytes that are specific for a particular antigen become activated by exposure to that antigen. This detection of antigen, together with co-stimulatory signals from other cells, causes the lymphocytes expressing that receptor to begin to proliferate rapidly by cellular division. Eventually, thousands of lymphocytes, all expressing the same (correct) antigen receptor, arise from this proliferative phase and together they are able to mount an effective response to eliminate the source of the antigen. Thus, the problem of low numbers of responsive lymphocytes at the start of an infection is solved (Figure 6.1).

Problem 3: Millions of receptors cannot be coded for by traditional genetic mechanisms

The next problem to be addressed is how all the millions of receptors required by the adaptive immune system should be coded for in the genomic DNA. If they were to be coded for in the manner that most other genes are, our genomes would need to contain millions of genes coding for each different receptor. This simply isn’t possible, as the human genome contains DNA coding for only ~20,000 proteins, and the amount of DNA required to code for millions of receptors could never fit inside a cell if they were coded for in the traditional manner.

Solution: Somatic recombination generates enormous receptor diversity from a relatively small amount of DNA

A remarkable mechanism has evolved to address this particular problem. Rather than having one gene coding for each possible receptor, a strategy which would require millions of genes, lymphocytes edit their genomic DNA during development to prepare new genes by building them up from a selection of a much smaller number of ‘pieces’ of receptor gene. Because the number of possible variations in which these pieces can be put together is vast, an enormous variety of possible gene sequences, and hence receptor specificities, can be generated. This highly efficient system requires only a small section of the human genome to generate the millions of diverse receptors required, and is discussed in more depth in Chapter 10.

Problem 4: Generating receptors at random could lead to the development of lymphocytes that target host proteins

It stands to reason that generating millions of receptors using the randomised process of somatic recombination could lead to receptors that accidentally target host proteins. This is a serious problem, as generating receptors to self proteins could lead to the development of inappropriate immune responses that damage host cells and tissues, so-called autoimmunity.

Solution: Deletion or restraint of self-reactive lymphocytes

The solution to this problem is to delete lymphocytes that display receptors that target host proteins or other molecules during their development. In this way, the pool of mature B-lymphocytes that leave the bone marrow, and mature T-lymphocytes that leave the thymus, should no longer contain any lymphocytes with receptors that target host antigens (Figure 6.2). This capacity to prevent immune responses to host proteins is called tolerance. On the rare occasions where this mechanism of tolerance is not successful, other mechanisms are initiated in the periphery to prevent activation of inappropriate self-targeting lymphocytes. These mechanisms of tolerance and the prevention of autoimmunity are discussed in more depth in Chapter 14.

![](image_rsrc4C4.jpg)

Problem 5: Some antigens are inaccessible to cell-surface antigen receptors

A further problem faced by the adaptive immune system is that many of the proteins expressed by a microbe are buried deep inside the structure of the organism. For example, the majority of bacterial proteins are present inside the bacterial cell, and only a minority are expressed on the surface of the microbe. Furthermore, many pathogens employ a strategy of invading host cells to hide from the cells of the innate immune system. Detecting these buried antigens and intracellular pathogens therefore requires some mechanism of making them visible to the immune system.

Solution: MHC molecules present processed peptides to T-cell receptors

To address each of these issues, a mechanism has evolved by which microbial proteins, either from inside the cell or outside the cell, can be broken down into short stretches of amino acids, termed peptides. These peptides are then attached to a protein called MHC and displayed on the cell surface (Figure 6.3). The cells that perform this degradation of microbial proteins and presentation of peptides on MHC are called antigen-presenting cells. The peptides presented on cell surface MHC molecules are then subject to surveillance by passing T-lymphocytes, which are the only cells adapted for this process. T-lymphocytes expressing a receptor that recognises the peptide being displayed can then be activated to proliferate, as described above. In this way, peptide sequences from the internal proteins of pathogens, and from intracellular pathogens such as viruses, can be detected by the receptors of the adaptive immune system.

![](image_rsrc4C5.jpg)

Problem 6: Innate immune responses do not improve with repeated exposures to specific pathogens

Because the innate immune system has no mechanism for memory of exposure to pathogens, the responses elicited by innate immunity do not improve when exposed the same pathogen on subsequent occasions.

Solution: Generate long-lived cells that retain memory of pathogen-associated antigens

To address this problem, mammals evolved a means of differentiating a proportion of the lymphocytes that respond to specific antigens into very long-lived cells (called memory cells). These cells are able to proliferate rapidly and re-differentiate into effector cells, enabling rapid responses to antigen should it be encountered on a second or subsequent occasion.

To summarise the above, the adaptive immune system is based on the expression of millions of different receptors by T- and B-lymphocytes, which constantly recirculate through lymph nodes, seeking antigen that might bind to their receptors. These receptors are generated by the random editing and piecing together of short chunks of DNA, and any lymphocytes with receptors that accidentally target a host protein are either deleted, or inhibited in other ways. The B-lymphocyte receptor is antibody, and it can be expressed in either a membrane bound or soluble form. The T-lymphocyte receptor, or TCR, is not secreted, but remains in the membrane to detect short segments of peptide presented to them on MHC molecules expressed by antigen presenting cells. We will now briefly discuss the basic effector mechanisms of adaptive immune responses as a prelude to discussing them in more depth in the following chapters.

The basics of B-cell responses

To recap from earlier chapters, the antigen receptor of the B-lymphocyte is called antibody. Initially, it is expressed as a cell-bound, membrane-spanning receptor, which naïve B-lymphocytes use to detect antigen and initiate cellular activation. Activated B-cells can change the way their receptor is expressed such that the antibody is no longer bound to the cell membrane, but secreted in a soluble form that diffuses through the circulation to bind to and neutralise invading toxins or micro-organisms wherever they may occur in the body. The decision as to whether or not a particular B-cell should become activated and produce soluble antibody is made in the lymph node (or spleen or other secondary lymphoid tissue) with or without help from helper T-cells, which sense the peptides presented on MHC-II molecules expressed by those B-cells. If they recognise a peptide that they have already encountered and at that time received co-stimulatory signals to indicate that the particular peptide is associated with an invader, these T-cells will provide the naïve B-cell with signals that enable it to proliferate and ultimately produce soluble antibody. The mechanisms of T-cell-dependent and T-cell independent B-cell activation will be described in more depth in Chapter 11.

Antibodies are also called immunoglobulins, and there are several different major classes of antibody that a B-cell can produce: immunoglobulin (Ig)M, IgD, IgA, IgG, and IgE. Naïve B-cells express only IgM and IgD, and require T-cell help to change the class of antibody they produce, through a process termed class switching. This mechanism ensures that the right class of antibody is produced to deal with a particular threat, since the different classes of antibody are useful at dealing with different types of invader. For example, IgG is very good at opsonising microbes, IgA is very stable and suitable for secretion into the gut lumen, IgM is very good at activating complement and IgE promotes defence against helminths by causing mast cells or basophils to degranulate.

While most activated B-cells eventually die off after the infection is cleared, a small proportion of the plasma cells will remain alive and continue to produce antibody at a moderate level for some time after the infection. Some activated B-cells may also become memory cells, which are very long-lived. Their main purpose is to remain quiescent but ready to proliferate rapidly and mount an efficient response should the same antigen be encountered in the future. They also continue to secrete antibody, although at much lower levels than is produced by plasma cells. This low level of residual antibody production is often sufficient to confer on the host resistance to re-infection by the same organism. There are other types of B-cell that will be discussed in more depth in subsequent chapters.

The basics of T-cell responses

Although the genes coding for T-cell receptors (or TCRs) are generated in a very similar way to antibody, these receptors work in a very different way. First, TCRs cannot be secreted, so do not neutralise microbes or toxins in the same way that antibodies can. Second, while antibodies can only detect antigens on the outer surface of a microbe, TCRs can recognise peptide sequences from the inside of a microbe. This is achieved through the process of antigen-presentation, where microbial proteins are degraded into short peptides by APCs and loaded into a groove present on the MHC molecule (described in Chapter 9). This complex of MHC with peptide is moved to the APC surface for display to passing T-cells.

There are two types of MHC: MHC-I and MHC-II. MHC-I, expressed by most types of cell, displays peptides from proteins in the cytosol, which typically come from intracellular pathogens such as viruses. MHC-II, expressed mainly by so-called professional APCs, such as DCs and macrophages, displays peptide from extracellular proteins, such as from extracellular parasites (Figure 6.3).

The way that T-cells respond to these different MHC molecules is quite different. Peptide displayed on MHC-I is recognised by CD8+ T-cells, which serve to kill host cells expressing non-self peptides. Peptides displayed on MHC-II are detected by CD4+ T-cells, which help to regulate the overall shape of the immune response, and so are called ‘helper T-cells’ (Th). Co-stimulatory signals from the APCs inform CD4+ T-cells what type of pathogen the peptide is likely to be associated with. The CD4+ T-cells are then able to help immune responses proceed in the most appropriate direction to deal with that particular type of pathogen.

There are four major types of CD4+ T-cell, each serving the purpose of promoting a slightly different type of immune response. They include the helper T-cells (termed Th1, Th2 and Th17) and regulatory T-cells (or Treg) (Figure 6.4). Th1 responses promote inflammation and the defence against intracellular pathogens. Th2 responses promote responses to helminths and regulate allergy. Th17 responses promote neutrophil-rich inflammation and the defence against extracellular bacteria and fungi. Regulatory T-cell responses inhibit inflammatory responses and the actions of other T-cells, helping to maintain tolerance to self and innocuous antigens that do not require an immune response to be directed against them. Each of these different classes of CD4+ T-cell mediate their effects via specific cytokines associated with each type of response, as will be discussed in more depth in the following chapters. Like B-cells, a small number of activated T-cells remain alive as quiescent, long-lived memory cells following an infection. Their role is also to enable rapid responses to antigens encountered previously should the same antigen be encountered again. There are other types of T-cell that will be discussed in more depth in subsequent chapters.

![](image_rsrc4C6.jpg)

Summary

1) The adaptive immune system is based on the expression of millions of different receptors by T- and B-lymphocytes

2) These receptors are generated by the random piecing together and editing of short chunks of DNA

3) Lymphocytes with receptors that accidentally target a host protein are either deleted by apoptosis in the generative organ, or inhibited by other means in the periphery

4) Naïve T- and B-lymphocytes constantly recirculate through lymph nodes and the circulation to seek antigen that might bind to their receptors

5) The B-lymphocyte receptor is antibody, and it can be expressed in either a membrane bound or soluble form of the following different classes, each of which has specific defensive properties: IgM, IgD, IgG, IgA, IgE

6) The T-lymphocyte receptor, or TCR, is not secreted, but remains in the membrane to detect short segments of peptide presented on MHC molecules expressed by antigen presenting cells

7) There are two types of MHC, MHC-I and MHC-II, which present peptides to CD8+ and CD4+ T-cells, respectively

8) MHC-I presents peptides generated by the partial degradation of proteins in the host cell cytosol, such as viral proteins

9) MHC-II presents peptides generated by the partial degradation of extracellular proteins

10) CD8+ (cytotoxic) T-cells kill target cells expressing non-self peptide to eliminate reservoirs of infection by intracellular pathogens, such as viruses and intracellular bacteria

11) CD4+ regulatory T-cells inhibit immune responses to prevent or limit inappropriate responses to self proteins or other innocuous antigens

12) There are 3 major types of CD4+ helper T-cell: Th1 cells secrete cytokines that promote defence against intracellular pathogens, Th2 cells promote defence against helminths and Th17 cells promote defence against extracellular bacteria and fungi

13) Most naïve T- and B-cells do not encounter antigen complementary to their receptors, and eventually undergo apoptosis to be replaced by haematopoiesis

14) After activation and proliferation, most members of the activated T- or B-cell clone die after the infection is cleared, but a small number remain alive as very slowly-dividing (quiescent) memory cells

15) Memory cells are very long-lived and can remain in an inactivated state for years. If they encounter the same antigen again, they proliferate quickly to enable a rapid immune response to be initiated to that antigen

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adaptive_immunity>

Luckheeram RL, Zhou R, Verma AD, Xia B. CD4+T Cells: Differentiation and Functions. Clin Dev Immunol 2012:925135 (2012) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3312336/>

---

# Chapter 7: Lymphocyte subsets and development

In this chapter we will introduce the major lymphocyte subsets, their pathways of development, and the major functions they perform in adaptive immunity. Lymphocytes are unique among the cells of the body as they are the only cells expressing a receptor that is created by the random joining together of multiple pieces of DNA - a process that yield millions of clones, each with a different receptor specificity. One of the key features of B- and T-lymphocyte development is that the process is intimately linked to the generation of a functional antigen receptor by genetic recombination. The mechanisms underpinning genetic recombination will be explored in more depth in chapter 10. For now though, it is sufficient to say that this mechanism does not always create a useful antigen receptor, and only those lymphocytes that are able to successfully generate a useful receptor survive to become mature lymphocytes.

Lymphocyte commitment during haematopoiesis

As discussed in earlier chapters, all leukocytes are created by the process of haematopoiesis, which occurs in the bone marrow in adults, and largely in the foetal liver before birth (Figure 7.1). In both locations, the process involves the proliferation and differentiation of a pluripotent stem cell called the haematopoietic stem cell. Recall from chapter 2 that a pluripotent stem cell is one which can divide to yield daughter cells which may either remain pluripotent with the capacity for self renewal, or they can differentiate into many, but not all, different types of cell. HSC proliferation gives rise to the precursor cells in the bone marrow that can then differentiate along a number of different paths to eventually become the different types of blood cell. Which type of cell a progenitor becomes depends largely on the signals received from other cells in the local environment, or occasionally from systemic cytokines.

![](image_rsrc4C7.jpg)

The different stages in lymphocyte generation can be summarised as follows:

Step 1 - Proliferation and self-renewal of HSCs

HSCs continue to proliferate throughout life without becoming senescent (this property of stem cells is termed ‘self renewal’). About half of the progeny of HSCs remain pluripotent and self-renewing so that the reserves of functional HSCs are not depleted over time. The other half of the HSC progeny will, however, be induced to differentiate down any of a number of distinct different pathways. Each of these different pathways of development are termed lineages, and cells of a particular lineage tend to express markers that are specific for that lineage. For example, HSCs express the markers CD34 and c-kit. However, they lack expression of the lineage specific markers that begin to be expressed later in development.

Step 2 - Commitment to myeloid or lymphoid lineage and the formation of multipotent progenitor cells

Initially, the HSC has the potential to become any leukocyte. However, at each stage of differentiation, HSC derived cells lose some of their potential to give rise to various types of leukocyte, and become capable of developing into a smaller range of blood cell types. The first choice they must make is whether to differentiate down the myeloid, or lymphoid lineage. If they choose the first of these options, they will become multipotent progenitors called common myeloid progenitors. These are cells which can become monocytes, neutrophils, DCs, platelets or red blood cells, but they cannot become lymphocytes. Alternatively, they can choose to become common lymphoid progenitors, which can become T-cells, B-cells or NK-cells, but not any of the myeloid cell types.

Once this decision has been made, the cells cannot revert to an earlier form, or cross to the other lineage. For this reason, it is said that HSCs commit to become either of two multipotent progenitor cell types, and each checkpoint in differentiation where one of these decisions must be made is called commitment. The percentage of cells arising from HSCs which follow either of these two pathways (i.e. myeloid or lymphoid) can vary over time, and is dictated by the concentrations of various cytokines and related molecules termed colony stimulating factors. These factors are derived mainly from stromal cells in the bone marrow, but can also arise occasionally from cells in the peripheral tissues, for example when infection demands that further cells of a particular type should be produced. For example, granulocyte-monocyte colony stimulating factor (GM-CSF) promotes the differentiation of HSCs down the myeloid pathway rather than the lymphoid pathway.

Step 3 - Commitment of lymphoid precursors to T- or B-cell lineage

As mentioned above, some of the HSCs differentiate along the lymphoid pathway to become common lymphoid progenitors (CLP). These cells still have the potential to become either B-cells, T-cells or NK cells. However, the next major choice they must make is whether to follow the B-cell lineage (and become pro-B-cells) or down an alternative pathway where they can become either T-cells or NK-cells. Which of these two pathways is followed depends on signals received from cell surface receptors, which mediate the choice by switching on the correct transcription factors to switch on the genes required to commit to a particular pathway. A cytosolic component of one of these receptors, Notch-1, is proteolytically cleaved when the receptor is activated by its ligand to release a small fragment which acts as a transcription factor. This co-operates with another transcription factor, GATA-3, to promote commitment to the T-cell lineage. Different receptors induce activation of different transcription factors to promote commitment to the B-cell lineage, in particular the transcription factors EBF, E2A and Pax5 promote B-cell commitment.

Step 4 - Proliferation of early stage lymphocyte progenitors

Once the CLP has committed to become either a T-cell precursor (pro-T-cell), or a B-cell precursor (pro-B-cell), a phase of proliferation is entered. This generates larger numbers of each type of precursor and is driven largely by the cytokine IL-7 in mice (in humans only T-cell precursors require IL-7 to proliferate). At this stage, neither the pro-T-cells nor the pro-B-cells express any form of antigen receptor.

Step 5 - Rearrangement and expression of the first of the two antigen receptor genes

As will be discussed in more detail later, the antigen receptors of mature T-cells and B-cells are composed of two different polypeptide chains, coded for by two different genes, both of which are required to form a functional heterodimer. The two genes coding for each of these chains do not yet exist in a mature form in the genomes of the pro-lymphocytes. Instead, both genes must be made during lymphocyte development by genetic recombination (which essentially means editing the chromosomal DNA, as will be discussed in more depth in chapter 10). At this stage, the cells stop proliferating and begin the process of editing their DNA to create these two essential genes.

The cells do not attempt to create both receptor genes at the same time. First, the cell attempts to create a gene that successfully codes for one functional receptor chain (in αβ T-cells, the β-gene is attempted first; in B-cells the heavy-chain is attempted first). Should the cell successfully create a functional first chain, intracellular signalling pathways are triggered by this half receptor (called a pre-BCR or pre-TCR) which are required to promote cell survival.

Because one receptor chain alone is not sufficient to provide survival signals, a number of other proteins are expressed by the cells which form a complex with the newly formed pro-receptor to provide the necessary survival signals. Developing B-cells also express the V-pre-B, λ5, Igα and Igβ proteins, which together with the first chain (called the μ heavy chain) make up the functional pre-BCR. Developing T-cells express the first chain of their receptor (TCR-β), together with CD3 and ζ (pronounced ‘zeta’) proteins, to make up the pre-TCR. These pre-receptors provide the signals that allow the cells to survive. At this stage, the cells which have successfully created the first chain of their antigen receptor are no longer called pro-B-cells or pro-T-cells, but rather pre-B-cells and pre-T-cells. Now, they begin to proliferate once again, so increasing the numbers of cells which have completed this part of the differentiation process.

Notably, many of the cells which begin this process do not successfully create a gene that gives rise to a working receptor. When this happens, these cells do not receive the appropriate survival signals (because they lack functional pre-receptor) and they die by apoptosis.

Step 6 - Rearrangement and expression of the second of the two antigen receptor genes

After the first chain has been successfully expressed, and a further round of proliferation completed, it is time for the pre-B-cells and pre-T-cells to stop proliferating and create the next of the two receptor genes by DNA rearrangement. The helper proteins described in the paragraph above which enable the first chain to make up a functional pre-receptor are no longer expressed, such that the cells now become dependent on a functional second chain for survival. In B-cells, the receptor second chain is called the k (kappa) light chain, and in T-cells, it is called TCR-α. Should the k light chain not be created successfully, the B-cell has the opportunity to try again, this time attempting to rearrange a different light-chain, called the λ light chain. Once again, if the rearrangement of the gene for the second chain fails (as quite often happens), the receptor that is generated is not functional, and the cell will not receive survival signals. Thus, cells with two functional receptor chains survive, and those with only one undergo apoptosis. Now, with two functional receptor chains, the developing cells are referred to as immature T-cells and immature B-cells.

Step 7 - Selection of cells that express functional receptors

As mentioned in the preceding chapter, the process of antigen receptor gene formation is essentially random, and it is quite possible for developing lymphocytes to generate receptors that target self proteins. This could cause serious problems (i.e. autoimmunity) for the host should the receptor bind strongly to self-antigens. At the same time, T-cells are required to detect MHC, which is itself a host molecule, and so the receptors of T-cells must have at least some affinity for MHC to be functional. Therefore, the newly generated immature B- and T-cells need to undergo two major final checkpoints, called positive selection, where cells expressing useful receptors are given survival signals, and negative selection, where cells expressing inappropriate receptors are deleted by apoptosis (the major checkpoints of B- and T-lymphocyte development are summarised in Figures 7.2 & 7.3).

![](image_rsrc4C8.jpg)

![](image_rsrc4C9.jpg)

In B-cells, it is thought that simply expressing two functional receptor chains is sufficient to provide the survival signals required for positive selection, and no antigen seems to be required to provide these signals. However, should any immature B-cell express a receptor that binds strongly to self antigens in the bone marrow, they have the opportunity to edit their receptor genes further in an attempt to modify their receptor to prevent recognition of host antigens (described in more depth in chapter 10). If this fails, the cells receive signals from the strong interaction of their receptor with host antigens that causes them to undergo apoptosis (i.e. negative selection). Those that do not bind strongly to self antigens are free to exit the bone marrow and migrate through the blood to the spleen, where they will complete their maturation.

Developing T-cells spend little time in the bone marrow, migrating early in development (as pro-T-cells) to the thymus. Once they have arrived in the thymus, they are called thymocytes, and it is here that they begin the process of receptor generation and ultimately, maturation. Once these cells have successfully created a two chain receptor, they are exposed to a wide range of self peptides presented on both MHC-I and MHC-II by thymic epithelial cells and DCs resident in the cortex. Several different binding options are now possible for these newly formed receptors:

(i) some will not bind to self MHC molecules at all

(ii) some will bind weakly to self MHC molecules

(iii) some will bind strongly to self MHC molecules

(iv) some will bind to a related presentation molecule called CD1

Which of these binding options the receptor fulfils dictates what type of T-cell it will become, as described below. Generally speaking, however, those cells expressing receptors that bind too strongly to MHC die by negative selection (or occasionally become regulatory T-cells), those that bind weakly to MHC receive survival signals and are positively selected, and those that don’t bind at all to any presentation molecule die by apoptosis. Thus, only those T-cells which bind moderately to MHC or CD1 are allowed to survive, and the T-cells which had potential to respond to self peptides die by apoptosis and so do not leave the thymus.

Step 8 - Differentiation into specific lymphocyte subsets

Once a functional receptor has been formed, B-cells can develop into follicular, marginal zone or B-1 B-cells. T-cells can develop into CD8+ CTL, CD4+ helper, CD4+ regulatory or γδ T-cells. The mechanisms underpinning differentiation into these different subsets and their ultimate functions will now be discussed.

B-lymphocyte subsets and development

There are three major types of B-cell: B-1, marginal zone and follicular B-cells. While B-1 B-cells are generated in the foetal liver, marginal zone and follicular B-cells arise from the bone marrow in adults and are collectively called B-2 cells. B-2 cells do not fully complete their maturation in the bone marrow. They leave the bone marrow in an immature form (expressing only the membrane form of IgM) to complete their differentiation in the spleen. Here, they mature fully to become either follicular B-cells (which express both IgD and IgM), or marginal zone B-cells (which express IgM and CD21). The major stages of B-lymphocyte development are summarised in Figure 7.4. Essentially, however, the major pathway of B-2 cell development is as follows: HSC → CLP → pro-B-cell (no receptors) → pre-B-cell (one receptor chain) → immature B-cell (two receptor chains) → positive and negative selection to delete self reactive B-cells → decision to become follicular or marginal zone B-cell. The functional properties of each type of B-cell will now be discussed.

![](image_rsrc4CA.jpg)

B-1 B-cells

B-1 B-cells are best characterised in mice, but similar B-cells are also present in man. Unlike most B-cells, they develop in the foetal liver rather than the bone marrow, and express much lower receptor diversity than follicular B-cells. The antibodies they produce therefore bind to a limited range of antigens, typically bacterial lipids and polysaccharides. These antibodies are called natural antibodies, because they are considered by many immunologists to be part of the innate immune system. Such antibodies are typically of the IgA or IgM variety, and as much as 50% of the IgA secreted into the gut lumen may be derived from B-1 B-cells. They tend to reside in mucosal tissues (such as the lamina propria), and have the further unusual property of being able to live for long periods and self-renew.

Follicular B-cells

The majority of circulating B-cells are follicular B-cells. These are the B-cells that express antibodies with the widest diversity of antigen specificities, and perform the function of patrolling the lymph nodes to initiate antibody responses to highly variable antigens. These cells mature mostly in the bone marrow, and complete maturation in the spleen, where they begin to express both IgD and IgM membrane-bound receptors (i.e. they are IgD+IgM+). The IgD and IgM receptors have the same antigen specificity, but different Fc portions (described in more detail later). These cells recirculate between the B-cell follicles of different lymph nodes throughout the body seeking antigen as they go. If they do not meet antigen for which their receptor is specific within several months they die by apoptosis, to be replaced by new follicular B-cells from the bone marrow. Follicular B-cells may be activated by T-cells to respond to protein antigens.

Marginal zone B-cells

Marginal zone B-cells are similar to follicular B-cells in that they also develop in the bone marrow first and then the spleen. However, they are more akin to B-1 cells in that they also display low receptor diversity and secrete natural antibodies that tend to target polysaccharides. These cells tend to reside in the marginal sinus of the spleen of mice, although in humans they can also be found in lymph nodes. These cells are effective at producing antibody rapidly in response to blood-borne microbes, particularly through the secretion of IgM. The responses of marginal zone B-cells are typically induced without help from CD4+ helper T-cells (i.e. they are mainly T-independent), but they can also perform T-dependent responses.

T-lymphocyte subsets and development

The major T-cell subsets can be distinguished largely by the type of T-cell receptor (TCR) they express (either αβ or γδ), and whether or not they express the surface antigens CD4 or CD8. There are three main types of T-cell that express the αβ (alpha beta) type TCR. These include CD4+ T-helper lymphocytes, CD8+ cytotoxic T-lymphocytes (CTLs) and CD4+ regulatory T-cells. A further type of T-cell, expressing the γδ (gamma delta) TCR, has much lower receptor diversity than αβ T-cells, expresses CD4 and variably CD8, and these are simply called γδ T-cells (Table 7.1).

Table 7.1: Cell surface markers of major T-lymphocyte subsets

![](image_rsrc4CB.jpg)

The major stages of T-lymphocyte development are summarised in Figure 7.5. Essentially, however, the major pathway of αβ T-cell development is as follows: HSC → CLP → pro-T-cell (no receptors) → move to thymus → pre-T-cell (one receptor chain) → double positive thymocyte (two receptor chains and expression of BOTH CD4 and CD8) → single positive thymocyte (two receptor chains and expression of EITHER CD4 or CD8) → positive and negative selection to delete cells that bind too weakly or too strongly to self MHC → mature naïve T-cells leave the thymus to patrol the body.

The specific milestones in the generation of each type of T-cell and their ultimate functions will now be discussed.

![](image_rsrc4CC.jpg)

The decision to become CD4+ or CD8+ T-cells occurs in the thymus and is determined by whether their TCR binds to MHC-II or MHC-I, respectively

Thymocytes begin their journey through the thymus from the subcapsular sinus and outer cortical regions of the thymus, progressing slowly through the cortex and eventually into the medulla (Figure 7.6). Along this journey, they encounter many different resident cells which express soluble factors (such as IL-7) and cell-bound molecules (such as MHC-I and MHC-II) that promote their maturation. Finally, the thymocytes that survive the multiple checkpoints and rounds of selection during this journey arrive at the medulla, and it is from here that they finally exit the thymus to enter the circulation as mature, naïve T-cells.

![](image_rsrc4CD.jpg)

A critical aspect of αβ T-cell function is that T-cells can only properly recognise MHC-II if they express the co-receptor CD4, and they can only recognise MHC-I if they express the co-receptor CD8. This concept will be expanded upon in later chapters. The decision as to which co-receptor, CD4 or CD8, becomes expressed is therefore critical to the successful generation of functional mature T-cells. When thymocytes first populate the thymus, they express neither CD4 nor CD8, and are called double negative thymocytes. However, around the time they develop functional TCRs, they switch on expression of both CD4 and CD8, to become so-called double positive thymocytes.

As described above, the developing thymocytes migrate slowly through the thymus being exposed along the way to self peptides displayed on MHC-I and MHC-II. Because of the inherently random nature of receptor gene formation, some of the cells will have receptors that recognise MHC-I with moderate affinity, and some will have receptors that recognise MHC-II with moderate affinity. This moderate affinity recognition appears to be the sweet spot for useful TCR deployment, as high affinity and no affinity receptors results in cell death. Via mechanisms that are poorly understood, the cells that recognise MHC-I switch off CD4, and begin to express only CD8. Likewise, the cells that recognise MHC-II switch off CD8 and begin to express only CD4. In this way, the double positive thymocytes become single positive immature T-cells, expressing either CD4 or CD8, and are now ready to leave the thymus.

CD4+ helper T-cells

The T-cells that express αβ TCRs and recognise self peptides presented on MHC-II with moderate affinity become CD4+ helper T-cells. The role of these cells is to control and co-ordinate immune responses by expressing cytokines and membrane-bound molecules that ‘help’ B-cells, macrophages, DCs and other types of T-cell to elicit immune responses. Depending on the way in which they are first activated, they can become proficient at promoting one of three different types of immune response, and are referred to as Th1, Th2 or Th17 cells, accordingly (Figure 7.7). For example, Th1 cells promote macrophage activation and defence against intracellular pathogens. Th2 cells promote B-cell class switching and eosinophil activation required for defence against helminths. Th17 cells are so-called because they secrete the cytokine IL-17, and promote defence against extracellular pathogens such as bacteria and fungi. Each of these different phenotypes is characterised by specific patterns of cytokine production (such as IFN-γ for Th1 cells; IL-4, IL-5 and IL-13 for Th2 cells; and IL-17 and IL-22 for Th17 cells), and transcription factor expression (Tbet, GATA3 and RORγT for Th1, Th2 and Th17 cells, respectively). Notably, in practice there is some plasticity between these different subtypes, and it is thought that not only do intermediate phenotypes exist, but also that it is possible for CD4+ T-cells of one subset to re-polarise into a different subset under certain, rarely occurring conditions (e.g. Th2 to Th17).

![](image_rsrc4CE.jpg)

CD4+ regulatory T-cells

As is often the case in biology, there are exceptions to some of the rules of T-cell development. One of these rules is that CD4+ αβ T-cells that bind strongly to self peptides presented on MHC-II should be killed by apoptosis. Curiously, however, through mechanisms that remain unclear, a small number of such cells are not killed, and these cells survive to become a fourth type of CD4+ T-cell termed regulatory T-cells (Figure 7.7). Unlike Th1, Th2 or Th17 cells which serve to promote rigorous immune responses, regulatory T-cells (or T-regs), serve to inhibit inflammation and adaptive immune responses. Their chief role appears to be to maintain tolerance to self antigens, which means they help to prevent inappropriate responses developing to self molecules in the periphery, and are thus part of the system of central tolerance, which will be described in more depth in Chapter 14. The cytokines expressed by these cells when activated are quite different from those secreted by activated helper T-cells, including the anti-inflammatory cytokines IL-10 and TGF-β. A further marker of CD4+ regulatory T-cells is their expression of the FOXP3 transcription factor, which is not expressed by other T-cells and is required for T-reg development.

CD8+ cytotoxic T-cells

Some of the αβ TCR expressing T-cells do not recognise peptides expressed by MHC-II, but do recognise peptides expressed on MHC-I. The double positive (i.e. CD4+CD8+) thymocytes that recognise MHC-I with moderate affinity slowly switch off expression of CD4 to become CD4- CD8+ cytotoxic T-lymphocytes (CTL). These cells perform a very different function compared to CD4+ T-cells. Their role is to kill cells infected with intracellular pathogens. They do this by recognising foreign peptides presented by MHC-I molecules on the surface of almost any type of cell in the body. This critical function is required to enable reservoirs of infection to be destroyed, so that viral or intracellular bacterial infections can be effectively cleared. There are two major mechanisms by which CTLs may kill host cells. First, if they are activated, they may express the molecule FasL (Fas ligand) on their surface. This binds to a molecule called Fas expressed on many types of target cell, causing intracellular signalling that results in rapid apoptosis if the two cells remain in contact. Alternatively, CTLs carry many granules - the contents of which can be released onto target cells on activation. These granules contain the molecule perforin, which punctures the plasma membrane of the target cell, and granzymes, which enter the cytosol of the target cell through the pore to induce apoptosis. CD8+ CTLs play a critical role in the defence against many viruses (Figure 7.8).

![](image_rsrc4CF.jpg)

γδ T-cells

A further exception to the rules of T-cell development is that not all T-cells detect peptide presented to them in the context of MHC molecules. In fact, a small proportion of T-cells recognise lipids and other small non-peptide molecules presented to them not by MHC, but by a specialised ‘non-classical’ presentation molecule called CD1. This molecule is similar in some ways to MHC-I, but is specialised to contain a hydrophobic pocket capable of binding lipids and presenting them to T-cells. A different type of TCR, the γδ TCR is expressed by ~5% of T-cells, and these cells are simply referred to as γδ T-cells. The γδ TCR is encoded for by genes which are distinct from those which give rise to αβ TCRs, but they are also generated by somatic recombination. The receptor diversity of γδ TCRs is low, and it is thought that they recognise well-conserved lipid moieties (i.e. PAMPs). Many immunologists therefore consider γδ T-cells to be components of the innate immune system. They tend to be found most commonly in epithelial tissues, such as the intestine and skin, and many intraepithelial lymphocytes express γδ TCRs. The roles played by γδ T-cells in infection and immune responses remain poorly understood.

Natural killer cells

Natural killer (NK) cells are lymphocytes that do not express any recombined antigen receptor, that is to say, they do not express a TCR or BCR. They can be further distinguished from T-cells and B-cells by their expression of the marker CD16, while lacking expression of CD3 or CD19. Because they lack a recombined antigen receptor, they are mediators of innate, rather than adaptive immunity, serving to detect and eliminate host cells which are stressed or expressing low MHC-I (as described in Chapter 4). NK cells mature entirely in the bone marrow along a relatively simple developmental pathway as follows: HSC → CLP → T/NK precursor → mature NK-cell.

Natural killer T-cells

Natural killer T-cells (or NKT-cells), are a type of T-cell that expresses several cell-surface markers that are also expressed by NK-cells, such as CD161, and occasionally CD56. One subset of these cells, termed invariant NKT (iNKT) cells, express αβ TCRs with limited diversity, while other NKT subsets express αβ TCRs with high receptor diversity. Like γδ T-cells described above, NKT cells recognise lipid antigens presented to them by CD1. It is thought that they may promote the production of antibodies targeting bacterial lipids. Their role in immunity and disease is little understood.

Summary

1) The lymphocytes, which include B-cells, T-cells, NK-cells and NKT-cells, are replenished throughout life by haematopoiesis in the bone marrow

2) B-cell subsets include B-1, marginal zone and follicular B-cells

3) T-cell subsets include CD4+ helper T-cells (Th cells), CD4+ regulatory T-cells (Tregs), CD8+ cytotoxic T-cells (CTLs) and γδ T-cells

4) B-cells and T-cells express an antigen receptor that is generated by the random joining together of different sections of DNA from the genome. This process has the capacity to generate receptors with enormous diversity for different antigens.

5) The B-cell antigen receptor (BCR) is called antibody, and recognises intact proteins and other antigens by binding directly to them

6) The T-cell antigen receptor (TCR) can only detect peptides (or occasionally other molecules) presented to them in complex with a presentation molecule, which is most often MHC-I or MHC-II

7) To detect peptides presented on MHC-I, T-cells must express the CD8 antigen (CD8+)

8) To detect peptides presented on MHC-II, T-cells must express the CD4 antigen (CD4+)

9) The haematopoietic stem cell (HSC) is pluripotent, which means it has the capacity to self-renew and also to give rise to progenitors that can differentiate to become a number of different types of cell

10) As HSC derived progenitors develop in the bone marrow, they are influenced by cytokines, colony stimulating factors and other signals produced locally (and sometimes systemically) that direct them to commit to particular lineages, each of which can develop only into certain types of cell

11) After a developing cell has committed to a certain lineage, it cannot normally revert to an earlier form, or change to a different lineage

12) The first major decision the HSC daughter cells must make is whether to become a common myeloid progenitor (CMP) or a common lymphoid progenitor (CLP); which has the potential to become T-cells, B-cells or NK-cells

13) T-cell and B-cell development is subject to several checkpoints, at which cells without useful receptors are forced to undergo apoptosis

14) Antigen receptors of mature T-cells and B-cells are composed of two different polypeptide chains, coded for by two different genes, generated by genetic recombination

15) T-cells and B-cells attempt to make one of their two required antigen receptor genes first, and only those cells that successfully complete this stage receive survival signals that prevent apoptosis, and permit them to continue to rearrange a second chain

16) T-cells and B-cells that do not successfully form a gene coding for a functional second chain undergo apoptosis

17) Those cells that do express a functional two-chain TCR or BCR then undergo positive and negative selection, in the spleen for B-2 B-cells and in the thymus for T-cells

18) B-cells that bind too strongly to self antigens are deleted by apoptosis in the spleen

19) T-cells that recognise MHC too weakly or too strongly, are also mostly killed, and those that display moderate binding affinity of their TCR to MHC are allowed to survive and become mature T-cells

20) Developing T-cells in the thymus (called thymocytes) initially express both CD4 and CD8 (double positive), but they become single positive (CD4+CD8- or CD4-CD8+) depending on whether their newly formed TCR recognises MHC-II or MHC-I

21) The major pathway of B-2 cell development is as follows: HSC → CLP → pro-B-cell (no receptors) → pre-B-cell (one receptor chain) → immature B-cell (two receptor chains) → positive and negative selection to delete self reactive B-cells → decision to become follicular or marginal zone B-cell

22) The major pathway of αβ T-cell development is as follows: HSC → CLP → pro-T-cell (no receptors) → move to thymus → pre-T-cell (one receptor chain) → double positive thymocyte (two receptor chains and expression of BOTH CD4 and CD8) → single positive thymocyte (two receptor chains and expression of EITHER CD4 or CD8) → positive and negative selection to delete cells that bind too weakly or too strongly to self MHC → mature naïve T-cells

23) Through mechanisms which are currently unclear, some CD4+ αβ TCR thymocytes bind strongly to self antigens but are not killed, developing instead into regulatory T-cells

24) B-1 B-cells express receptors of limited diversity termed natural antibodies that tend to target bacterial lipids and polysaccharides and be of the IgA or IgM class

25) Follicular B-cells express very large receptor diversity and recirculate between the B-cell follicles of lymph nodes as IgM+IgD+ naïve B-cells for several months before being replaced if they are activated by their specific antigen

26) Marginal zone B-cells secrete natural antibodies of limited diversity, and reside mainly in the spleen where they promote IgM responses to blood-borne microbes

27) CD4+ helper T-cells control immune responses by expressing cytokines and membrane-bound molecules that ‘help’ B-cells, macrophages, DCs and other types of T-cell elicit immune responses

28) Depending on the way they are activated, T-helper cells can polarise to become Th1, Th2 or Th17 cells, and these subsets express cytokines and other mediators that promote defence against intracellular pathogens, helminths and extracellular microbes, respectively

29) Regulatory T-cells (Tregs) maintain tolerance to self antigens by inhibiting inflammation and adaptive responses to such antigens through the secretion of cytokines such as IL-10 and TGF-β

30) CD8+ cytotoxic T-lymphocytes (CTLs) recognise peptide associated with MHC-I to kill host cells containing intracellular pathogens, thus eliminating reservoirs of infection, and are particularly useful in the clearance of viral infections

31) γδ T-cells express the γδ TCR, which has low diversity, and recognise small molecules, particularly lipids, presented by the MHC-like molecule CD1

32) Natural killer (NK) cells do not express a recombined antigen receptor, and serve instead as mediators of the innate immune system by killing host cells that may be damaged or infected

33) Natural killer T-cells (NKT) are T-cells expressing αβ TCRs but also some NK-cell surface markers, such as CD161. They recognise lipid antigens presented to them on CD1

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lymphocyte>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T_cell>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B_cell>

Garraud O, et al. Revisiting the B-cell compartment in mouse and humans: more than one B-cell subset exists in the marginal zone and beyond. BMC Immunol 13:63 (2012) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pubmed/23194300>

Jin B, Sun T, Yu XH, Yang YX, Yeo AE. The effects of TLR activation on T-cell development and differentiation. Clin Dev Immunol 2012:836485 (2012) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pubmed/22737174>

# Chapter 8: Antibodies and the humoral response

Antibodies are glycoproteins produced by B-lymphocytes for the purposes of detecting and eliminating antigens. Millions of B-cell clones circulate in the adult human body, each expressing a different antibody with exquisite specificity for a particular antigen. Antibodies may be developed to target almost any type of antigen the host may be exposed to, from proteins and carbohydrates to nucleic acids and lipopolysaccharides. Antibodies are expressed in a membrane form which serves as the antigen receptor for naïve and memory B-lymphocytes. However, plasma B-cells can also express antibody in a soluble form that circulates in the blood and is secreted onto the mucosal surfaces. Such soluble antibodies provide protection against infection by limiting the entry of pathogens, neutralising toxins, activating complement, enhancing the phagocytosis of microbes and triggering defensive responses against multicellular parasites. Inducing the development of memory B-cells which are able to produce antibodies targeting specific antigens remains the goal of most vaccination strategies. In this chapter, we will discuss how the basic structure of antibody molecules enables them to provide the host with the different protective functions that combine to form the humoral response.

The B-cell receptor is a transmembrane protein which can become secreted as soluble antibody after B-cell activation

As mentioned in earlier chapters, the adaptive immune system has evolved to distinguish host molecules from non-host molecules by generating millions of different receptors that each recognise the detailed structure of a different non-host molecule. Such molecules that may be recognised by the B-cell receptor or the T-cell receptor are called antigens. Some antigens are present deep inside the microbe, and these are detected by T-cells after the presentation of certain components from digested organisms on the surfaces of antigen-presenting cells. However, other antigens are present on the surface of the microbe, and these make not only excellent markers for the detection of invaders, but also useful targets for effector mechanisms of immune defence. Such surface antigens are detected by antibodies.

Initially, naïve B-lymphocytes express only the transmembrane form of antibody, and do not secrete soluble antibody (Figure 8.1). The antibody molecule will remain expressed in this form until the naïve B-cell encounters the antigen for which the receptor is specific. The likelihood of this occurring is enhanced by the recirculation of follicular B-cells between the different lymph nodes of the body, seeking antigen to which their receptor may bind. Such cells continue to patrol the lymph nodes for several months before eventually dying by apoptosis if they do not detect antigen in that time. However, those naïve B-cells that do meet antigen for which their receptor is specific become activated. This means that they begin to proliferate rapidly, and initiate the intracellular machinery required to induce the secretion of antibody. This secreted antibody is soluble and can circulate in the blood to bind to antigens almost anywhere in the body. Some forms of antibody may also be actively transported across epithelial cells to coat the mucosal surfaces and provide protection from invasive micro-organisms.

![](image_rsrc4CG.jpg)

The cells that secrete antibody are referred to as plasma cells. These cells are structurally quite different from the naïve lymphocytes that they are derived from. Their cytosol becomes distended as the endoplasmic reticulum and golgi apparatus swells and dedicates protein production almost entirely to the synthesis of soluble antibody. In such cells, expression of membrane BCR slowly diminishes and production shifts over to soluble antibody. The transmembrane domain of the BCR is removed, allowing the antibodies to be secreted into the blood. Notably, this production of a soluble form of antigen receptor is not shared by T-cells, since the T-cell antigen receptor always remains a transmembrane receptor protein and cannot be secreted (Figure 8.1).

Basic structure of antibodies

There are several major types of antibody (also called immunoglobulins, or gamma globulins), but they all share the same basic structure. Antibodies can be considered to have two ‘ends’ which serve different purposes: one end binds to antigen, and the other binds to host cell receptors and other molecules of the immune system.

Each antibody unit is symmetrical, being composed of two identical heavy chains and two identical light chains. These four chains are covalently joined together by disulphide bridges (as shown in Figure 8.2) to constitute the basic antibody unit, which we refer to as an antibody monomer. This symmetrical structure enables any antibody monomer to bind two identical antigens simultaneously, one on each arm. Of the two protein chains that join in this way to make up antibodies, one is short (called the light chain) and the other is longer (called the heavy chain).

![](image_rsrc4CH.jpg)

Both the heavy and light chains are composed of several distinct structural units called immunoglobin (Ig) domains. Ig domains are a commonly used structural unit employed in a number of immune and non-immune proteins, which consist of several β-sheets covalently held together by disulphide bonds. In any particular antibody molecule, some of the Ig domains are constant, which means their sequence is not altered between B-cell clones of the same isotype, and some are variable, meaning their sequence is different between each B-cell clone. The variable domains (denoted ‘V’) are responsible for binding to antigen, and the constant domains (denoted ‘C’) interact with receptors of the host immune system. Light chains have one V region and one C region. Heavy chains have one V region and 3 or 4 C regions (Figure 8.2). These domains are further annotated according to the chain to which they belong and where they are located in the protein chain. For example, CH1 means constant domain 1 of the heavy chain, where the 1 indicates that it is the C domain closest to the start of the protein (which is the amino, or -NH2, terminus). VL means the variable Ig domain of the light chain and is not numbered because there is only ever one variable domain in either chain.

Two different types of light chain may be used to make up any particular antibody, and these are termed lambda (λ) or kappa (k). It is thought that both types of light chain perform much the same function, and they are not involved in determining the isotype or membrane binding of the antibody.

Each antibody isotype is defined by the sequence of its heavy chain Fc component

Early experimentation on antibodies revolved around the limited digestion of the proteins using proteases such as papain and pepsin. It was discovered that papain split the antibody molecule into two different types of fragment. One of these bound to antigen, and was termed Fab. The other could not bind to antigen, and was termed Fc (fraction crystallisable, since this component readily formed crystals). Limited pepsin digestion also yielded a fragment that bound antigen, called F(ab’)2. Fab could bind but not cross-link antigens because it was a monomer, while F(ab’)2 could both bind and cross-link antigens, as it is a covalently linked dimer (Figure 8.2).

Today, it is now clear that the Fab portion of antibodies contains the variable domains and binds antigen. The Fc portion contains only constant domains and is the part of the antibody responsible for binding host cell receptors and complement proteins. This is why host cell receptors that bind antibodies are called Fc receptors. Notably, as the light chain does not form part of the Fc component, it was deduced that the protein sequence of the C-domains of the heavy chain dictates not only whether or not the antibody is anchored to the membrane, but also what class that antibody belongs to, and which effector functions that antibody may eventually elicit.

As described in earlier chapters, there are 5 major isotypes (or class) of antibody that may be produced: IgA, IgD, IgG, IgE and IgM. These are synthesised by expression of the corresponding heavy chain, which is referred to by the Greek letters alpha (α), delta (δ), gamma (γ), epsilon (ε) and mu (μ), respectively. In humans, IgA and IgG can be further subdivided into the subclasses IgA1, IgA2, IgG1, IgG2, IgG3 and IgG4. In mice, the subclasses of IgG are IgG1, IgG2a, IgG2b, IgG2c and IgG3. Most heavy chains exist in two forms, one transmembrane and one secreted, although IgD can exist only in membrane form and is not secreted. The membrane form of Ig has a transmembrane helix that anchors it to the membrane.

Different antibody classes promote different effector functions

Naïve B-cells typically express only the transmembrane forms of IgD and IgM, but do not secrete soluble antibody. When they are activated by antigen (as described below), they may change the type of heavy chain gene they express from transmembrane delta (δ) and mu (μ) type heavy chains, to the alpha (α), gamma (γ), epsilon (ε) or mu (μ) heavy chain variants, which lack the transmembrane domain. However, during this process, the V-regions do not change, and so the specificity for antigen remains exactly the same as before. Thus, the class of the antibody is said to be switched, and the process is termed class-switching.

This enables the B-cell to produce the type of antibody that is likely to be most effective at dealing with the microbe it has encountered. For example, IgA (requiring the alpha heavy chain) is resistant to degradation in the intestinal lumen, and is secreted into the gut to bind to and prevent entry of pathogens. Indeed, most of the antibody made each day (2-3 grammes in an adult human) is of the IgA class. IgG is generally developed in response to bacterial and viral infections and promotes opsonisation and therefore uptake of such organisms by phagocytes such as macrophages and neutrophils. IgE is able to activate mast cells and eosinophils, and is therefore useful in the defence against helminths. IgM is particularly good at promoting the classical complement activation pathway.

These different effector functions are mediated by differences in the sequence of the heavy chain Fc portion. This is in part because the different Fc receptors expressed by immune cells are specific for particular classes of antibody, as will be discussed below. Table 8.1 summarises the major properties of each isotype of antibody and the effector functions to which they contribute. As a further adaptation, it should be noted that not all antibodies are produced solely as Y-shaped monomers. Both IgA and IgM can be secreted as multimers of this basic antibody unit. Specifically, IgA is typically produced as a dimer and IgM as a pentamer. In both cases, an additional peptide called the J-chain us used to help IgA and IgM multimerise (Figure 8.3).

![](image_rsrc4CJ.jpg)

Table 8.1: Major functions of different antibody classes (isotypes)

![](image_rsrc4CK.jpg)

Antibody binding to antigen is mediated by six highly variable peptide loops called CDRs

At the other end of the antibody, the two variable domains of the Fab region are responsible for binding antigen. One heavy chain V domain, and one light chain V domain, are bound tightly together so that the outer tip of the antibody forms a single ligand-binding surface. The amino acid sequences of the V regions are highly variable, and unique to each B-cell clone. This diversity is required to bind many different antigenic structures and is generated by genetic recombination during B-cell clone development (see Chapter 10). Notably, not all of the amino acids in the V regions are equally variable. Some sections of the V domains are not very variable at all, and others show enormous diversity of amino acid sequences. The relatively constant sequences form the basic framework of the molecule, which permits the V-domain to fold correctly and maintain its overall structure. However, protruding from the outer surface of each V-domain, there are three flexible loops, each around 10 amino acids long, which have extremely high sequence diversity (they are said to be hypervariable). These loops are the parts of the antibody that make most of the contact with the antigen surface, and thereby give antigen specificity to any particular antibody (Figure 8.4). Amino acids in the basic framework do still have some variability and can also occasionally make contact with antigen to play a role in binding.

![](image_rsrc4CM.jpg)

Because the structures of the loops are generally complementary to the structure of the antigen they bind to, they are referred to as complementarity determining regions, or CDRs. The CDRs are numbered CDR1, CDR2 and CDR3, in order of their position from the start of the protein (i.e. the amino terminus). The greatest sequence diversity tends to be observed in CDR3, driven by specific mechanisms discussed later, and this is the CDR that generally makes the most physical contacts with antigen. Different surface shapes created by the 6 CDRs are called idiotypes, and these can in turn be bound by antibodies that recognise the CDRs, called anti-idiotypes.

Antigen binding, valency, affinity and avidity

A number of structural features enable antibodies to bind well to antigens. First, antibody molecules possess a flexible hinge region, that allows the antigen binding domains to reach antigens over a range of distances, and also to rotate a little, which may help if the antigens are distributed over the surface of a pathogen. Next, the amino acids in the CDRs have at least 4 different mechanisms by which they can aid the binding of antigen:

(i) Hydrogen bonds - between any two amino acids with hydrogen atoms bound to oxygen or nitrogen (for example, serine to serine)

(ii) Electrostatic forces - for example between positively and negatively charged amino acids, such as lysine and glutamate

(iii) Van der Waals forces - for example, between phenylalanine and isoleucine

(iv) Hydrophobic interactions - for example, between any two hydrophobic amino acids, such as leucine residues

Notably, the binding of antigen to antibody is non-covalent and therefore reversible, so a strong interaction between antigen and antibody is desirable for maximum defensive potential. The strength of this binding is called affinity, and is described by the dissociation constant (Kd), where stronger binding is indicated by a lower Kd value. During the early phases of the antibody response, the antibodies that are produced generally do not have excellent binding, and the Kd is typically in the region of 10-7 to 10-9 M. However, after the immune system has had time to optimise the CDR sequences through somatic hypermutation and affinity maturation (described later), the strength of binding to a particular antigen can be markedly improved, and the Kd can be reduced to 10-11 M or lower.

Antibodies can use their multivalency to get around the problems of relatively low affinity during the early stage of infection. The binding of one antibody unit to one antigen is referred to as the binding affinity. However, if multiple antibody units are joined together, they can all bind to antigen on a surface at the same time, helping each other to remain attached. The binding of these multiple antibody units which are joined together to multiple antigens at the same time is called avidity. For example, the IgM molecule, which is produced early in the antibody response, has 10 separate antigen binding regions, and so the avidity of this molecule for target antigens is high, even if the binding affinity of the individual V-regions is not great.

This type of binding only works well when antigens are polyvalent. This means that the surface of a molecule or particle contains many identical units of antigen, and the number of possible antibody/antigen interactions available for binding is referred to as the valency. Such polyvalent antigens occur frequently on bacterial cell walls and in highly repetitive molecules, such as lipopolysaccharides and carbohydrates. A further benefit of polyvalency is that the close apposition of antigens brings the Fc portions of binding antibodies close together, which, as described later, is a key event in triggering many immune functions.

A notable potential outcome of antigen/antibody binding is that which can occur when the local relative concentrations of antibody and antigen are very similar. Occasionally, such conditions arise in regions called the zone of equivalence. In such regions, if the antigen is polyvalent, such conditions favour the formation of so-called immune complexes, which are highly cross-linked mesh-like webs of Ab and Ag bound together into large inter-linked structures. Because such complexes bring large numbers of Fc components close together, the activation of complement and other pathways occurs, and this can cause inflammation and damage to the tissues.

Epitopes targeted by antibodies may be linear or conformational

Epitopes, also called determinants, are the regions of the antigen that are actually bound by antibody. As most antigens are larger than antibodies, this means that it is possible for several different antibodies with different specificities to bind to different epitopes on the surface of a single antigen (Figure 8.5). As discussed briefly in Chapter 2, proteins are made by folding long chains of amino acids into structures that remain stably folded under most conditions. However, when treated harshly, such as by excessive heat or the use of detergents experimentally, proteins can unfold to yield linear peptide chains. Such proteins are said to be denatured. If an antibody recognises an epitope that is formed mainly of several amino acids all next to each other in this linear form, the epitope is said to be linear. More often however, antibodies recognise epitopes made up of several different amino acid loops that form a particular surface patch of the protein, and the amino acids that contribute to the patch are often far away from each other in the linear sequence of the protein (Figure 8.5). Such epitopes are said to be conformational. Antibodies that bind linear epitopes can detect denatured or unfolded proteins, while antibodies that bind to conformational epitopes cannot normally bind to denatured proteins. This distinction is useful experimentally, as will be discussed in Chapter 20.

![](image_rsrc4CN.jpg)

Basics of B-cell activation

We each have millions of naïve B-cell clones circulating in the body seeking antigen. The vast majority of these will not meet the antigen for which they are specific, and will die after several months to be replaced by new naïve B-cells from the bone marrow. However, during an infection, some naïve B-cells may encounter antigens that bind to their receptor. We will now discuss the basic mechanisms by which B-cells detect the binding of such antigens, and how they become activated to secrete soluble antibody, before exploring the process in more depth in Chapter 11.

As described above, naïve B-cells express the transmembrane form of IgM and IgD. This transmembrane form is required to enable the B-cell to detect when its antibody binds to antigen. B-cells can then be activated in one of two ways, depending on whether the type of antigen they encounter is T-dependent or T-independent, as will now be discussed.

T-independent antigens

T-independent (TI) antigens are antigens which can induce B-cell activation without help from the thymus, or more specifically, from helper T-cells. Such antigens are generally carbohydrate and polysaccharide based antigens containing multiple repetitive epitopes. When such multivalent antigens bind to the transmembrane receptors of the naïve B-cell, this causes them to cluster close together in small patches on the cell surface. This receptor clustering is recognised by proteins inside the cell, which trigger signalling pathways that promote B-cell activation. These signals are enhanced by the presence of PAMPs, which the B-cell can detect by expression of its own PRRs, or indirectly in the form of cytokines secreted from nearby macrophages (Figure 8.6).

TI antigens do not induce affinity maturation, and rarely induce class-switching. Thus, antibodies raised to TI antigens tend to be low affinity IgM, although some IgA can also be produced. As a general rule, TI antigens tend to give rise to short lived IgM secreting plasma cells. The antibody responses of marginal zone and B-1 B-cells are mainly T-independent as these cells are specialised to respond to poyvalent antigens.

![](image_rsrc4CP.jpg)

T-dependent antigens

Proteins, on the other hand, rarely have repetitive epitopes that can be used to activate B-cells. Instead, antibody responses to protein antigens can only be induced with assistance from helper T-cells. Protein antigens are therefore said to be T-dependent antigens. In this mode of B-cell activation, follicular B-cells can take up some of the antigen bound to their receptors and process it internally for presentation of peptides on MHC-II to helper T-cells. If there are helper T-cells nearby that recognise this peptide as foreign, they give the B-cell signals, partly from cytokines and partly from the binding of their membrane molecule CD40L to CD40 expressed on the surface of the B-cell. Together, these act as powerful signals to induce B-cell activation. Because helper T-cells can promote class-switching, T-dependent antigens can induce antibodies of the IgA, IgG or IgE classes, depending on CD40 ligation and the types of cytokine given by the helper T-cell at the time of B-cell activation (Figure 8.6).

Naïve follicular B-cells that meet antigen and receive T-cell help via cytokines and CD40 begin to proliferate rapidly. This means that a single B-cell can give rise to thousands of daughter cells within days of activation - this proliferation stage is essential so that sufficient quantities of antibody may eventually be produced to defend against an infection. Most of these cells become plasma cells, which are specialised for the production and secretion of antibody. However, a small proportion do not switch their antibody to the secreted form, but instead retain the transmembrane form of their antibody to become memory B-cells.

With further help from T-cells, follicular B-cells within the germinal centre can undergo somatic hypermutation, resulting in the production of antibodies with enhanced affinity for antigen. In this process, called affinity maturation, the sequences of the genes coding for the CDRs, particularly CDR3, are revised. The binding strength of antibodies to a particular antigen can thus be markedly improved, with the resulting antibodies perhaps binding antigen 1,000 fold more avidly than the antibodies produced early in the response. This is why antibodies developed against protein antigens are generally of much higher affinity than those developed to other types of antigen, such as polysaccharides.
