For this reason, vaccines generally either target proteins directly, or add a protein tag (called a hapten) to polysaccharide antigens to form so-called conjugate vaccines which then induce both memory B-cells and high affinity antibodies.

After the infection is cleared, most of the plasma cells of the B-cell clone die off. Some remain alive and migrate to the bone marrow, where they can survive for many years, secreting low amounts of antibody into the circulation. Often, this low level of antibody is sufficient to protect the host from re-infection with the same organism, which is why current vaccination strategies often aim to induce a humoral response to antigen associated with an infectious organism. By contrast, the memory B-cells either remain in the lymph node where they were generated, or recirculate to patrol other lymph nodes around the body.

Primary and secondary humoral responses

As described above, the antibody response to TI-antigens generally comprises a brief pulse of low affinity IgM secreted by short-lived plasma cells. However, T-dependent antigens are efficient inducers of affinity maturation, class-switching and memory B-cell formation, and thereby give rise to two distinct phases of the humoral immune response.

The primary antibody response is the response made by the host to an antigen it has encountered for the first time. This is a slow process, taking up to two weeks in man, as it requires the activation, selection, proliferation and differentiation of naïve B-cells, together with helper T-cell activation, in the lymph nodes exposed to the new antigen. Often, the infection is repelled by the low affinity antibodies that are produced by short-lived plasma cells developed early in the process. However, at a later stage in the primary response, affinity maturation and class-switching occurs and memory B-cells are generated that express high affinity antibodies that are appropriately class-switched. Memory T-cells are also formed at the same time which are responsive to the same antigens.

After the primary response has repelled the infection, most of the plasma cells that were generated die off, and the amount of specific antibody in the blood falls back to relatively low levels. This phase of the adaptive immune response is called contraction.

If the host then encounters the same antigen again at some stage in the future, the antigens from that microbe would be transported to one or more lymph nodes. Here, memory B-cells and T-cells can be activated very rapidly, as there is no requirement for further selection, affinity maturation or class-switching. Instead, the memory cells can be triggered by the antigen to rapidly proliferate and produce high affinity antibody. Because there are more memory cells than there were reactive naïve cells, this so-called secondary antibody response is generally much quicker, and leads to higher circulating antibody levels, than the primary antibody response (Figure 8.7). This ability to generate improved secondary responses using memory of antigen generated during the primary response is the basis of adaptive immunity.

Often, during the secondary response, further affinity maturation of existing memory cells can occur leading to even better antibodies. Additionally, naïve cells may develop new antibody responses to other epitopes or antigens that were missed during the first infection. Thus, each time the host is exposed to a particular microbe, the antibody response becomes quicker, more pronounced and of a higher affinity.

![](image_rsrc4CR.jpg)

Some useful terms regarding the humoral response: Carriers, haptens, serum and titres

Much experimental immunology has been performed using blood that has been taken from a volunteer or an animal and allowed to clot. This clot removes from the blood plasma all of the proteins involved in clotting, but leaves behind the other plasma proteins, including antibodies. The clear liquid that is left behind is called serum. Often, immunologists use multiple serial dilutions of serum generated in this way to determine how far it may be diluted before it stops binding to antigen. This is referred to as the antibody titre of the serum, and is often expressed as how many two-fold dilutions were required to be made before the serum had no effect on antigen. For example, a particular antibody present in serum at a low level may have a titre of 1:32, while high levels of the same antibody could have a titre of 1:1,024.

As mentioned above, antibodies can bind and recognise all sorts of molecule, but not all antigens are able to stimulate an antibody response. Molecules that activate immune responses from T-cells or B-cells are called immunogens, and antigens that do not elicit such responses are called non-immunogenic. Sometimes it is necessary, for therapeutic or experimental purposes, to generate antibodies against molecules which are too small for the immune response to normally produce antibodies against them. In these instances, the small molecule can be covalently linked to a larger molecule, particularly one which presents the smaller molecule at repetitive distances, and this can often then enable the host immune system to generate an antibody response to the smaller molecule. In such experiments, the small molecule is said to be a hapten and the larger molecule is a carrier.

Effector functions of antibodies and the humoral response

The main functions of the humoral response are to neutralise and eliminate microbes and their associated molecules. Antibodies enable this by promoting the following effector functions: (i) neutralisation of molecular bioactivity, (ii) opsonisation of pathogens, (iii) antibody-dependent cell mediated cytotoxicity, (iv) promoting anti-parasite defences and (v) complement activation via the classical pathway. These specific functions will now be discussed in turn.

Neutralisation of microbes and toxic molecules by antibodies

Microbes, particularly viruses and invasive bacteria, often use proteins that bind to receptors expressed by host cells to gain access to those cells and enter the body. Alternatively, they may secrete toxins (such as the tetanus toxin) that damage host cells or otherwise enable them to infect the host. In both of these cases, antibodies that bind to the molecules produced by the microbes that exert these effects can block the biological activities of those molecules. This is called neutralisation. This function of antibodies is particularly useful in the defence against viruses. Unlike most effector mechanisms of antibodies, this function is not dependent on the presence of the Fc portion of the antibody (i.e. the antigen-binding Fab component alone is sufficient). Neutralisation is best achieved by high affinity antibodies, generated in response to T-dependent antigens. IgG in the blood and IgA in the lumen of the gut play key roles in neutralisation of viral and bacterial molecules, respectively.

Opsonisation of particles by IgG enhances phagocytosis

One of the major effector functions of antibodies is to label microbes for uptake and degradation by phagocytic cells (Figure 8.8). This is mediated by certain subclasses of IgG antibodies, but not other classes of antibody. Macrophages and neutrophils express receptors on their surface that bind to the Fc portion of IgG antibodies. Such receptors are called Fc receptors (or FcR), and these are specific for individual classes or subclasses of antibody (discussed below). Some other molecules, such as the complement component C3b, also target particles for phagocytosis in this way (they are collectively referred to as opsonins), but IgG antibodies are particularly effective because of the high binding affinity to specific antigens on the microbe surface. The cross-linking of FcRs by IgG also promotes phagocyte activation, resulting in a respiratory burst and increased expression of phagocyte oxidase and inducible nitric oxide synthase (iNOS). Together, these mediators enhance the likelihood that ingested organisms are killed in the phagolysosome.

![](image_rsrc4CS.jpg)

Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity

Occasionally, host cells may become parasitized or infected in some other way and express non-host proteins on the cell membrane. If IgG antibodies of certain classes have been generated that bind to these proteins, the cell becomes coated in IgG antibodies with their Fc portions protruding outwards from the cell (Figure 8.9). This is recognised by FcRs expressed by natural killer cells. Because the IgG is bound to a surface, and not merely soluble, they cause the FcRs on the natural killer cells to cluster together in small patches of the membrane. This causes the natural killer cell to become activated, and it kills the target cell using the mechanisms described in Chapter 4. This process is called antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC). Notably, soluble IgG cannot cross-link receptors, and so ADCC can only be activated by IgG which is bound to a cell surface by the V-regions, leaving the Fc portions free to interact with FcRs.

![](image_rsrc4CT.jpg)

Helminth defence

Some multicellular parasites, such as parasitic worms (helminths), are too large to be phagocytosed by macrophages or similar cells. In these instances, alternative mechanisms are induced to destroy the parasite or otherwise promote clearance of the infection. As mentioned earlier, antibodies of the IgE class are often generated in response to parasite antigens with help from T-helper type 2 (Th2) cells. These IgE antibodies coat the parasite, and thereby cause cross-linking of IgE-specific Fc-receptors on mast cells. The degranulation that results leads to the release of various molecules that are toxic to helminths, from the mast cells. Other mediators released from activated mast cells promote mucous flow and increase the rate of gastrointestinal motility, both with the aim of enhancing expulsion of the worms. Eosinophils may be furthermore recruited, and these may degranulate not only in response to IgE, but also IgG or IgA bound to the parasite. The exocytosed granule contents of eosinophils contain additional substances that are toxic to the parasite, such as major basic protein.

Complement activation

Recall from Chapter 4 that there are three possible pathways of complement activation: the classical, alternative and lectin pathways. The classical complement pathway is triggered when IgG or IgM antibodies bind to a surface, allowing the Fc portions of the antibodies to be bound by a multiprotein protein complex called C1 (Figure 8.10). C1 is composed of C1q, which has 6 arms that bind to the antibody Fc portions, and the helper proteins C1r and C1s, which are proteases that are activated when C1q binds antibody. Such binding causes activation of the protease C1r, which cleaves and thereby activates C1s. Activated C1s then cleaves C4 to initiate the classical complement cascade, leading ultimately to cleavage of C3 and C5 and the generation of the products required to mediate the effector functions of complement.

![](image_rsrc4CU.jpg)

Notably, C1q can only be activated by a minimum of 2 Fc portions remaining in close proximity for some time. This means that unbound, soluble IgG cannot activate the C1 complex. Soluble IgM that has not bound to antigen also cannot trigger C1 activation, even though it possesses 5 Fc portions in close proximity. This is because the IgM molecule normally adopts a shape that cannot bind C1q. When the variable regions of IgM contact antigen, they bend downwards (to adopt the so-called ‘staple’ form), allowing C1q to bind and complement to be activated. Through these mechanisms, soluble antibody that has not bound antigen does not trigger complement activation.

To recap on earlier chapters, the main roles of the products of complement activation are to:

(i) opsonise microbes (particularly via C3b and C4b)

(ii) form a membrane attack complex (MAC) on the microbial surface to lyse the microbe

(iii) promote inflammation

(iv) promote B-cell activation and the generation of antibody responses

(v) aid binding of antigen to follicular dendritic cells

Fc receptors

Various cells express cell-surface receptors that are specific for the Fc portions of particular classes, or subclasses, of antibody. The functions of these various FcRs, and the classes of antibody they recognise, are summarised in Table 8.2. The binding of antibody to FcR alone is not sufficient to cause activation of the receptor and intracellular signalling. This is because transmembrane proteins, such as FcRs, diffuse freely around the surface of the cell, a bit like individual boats drifting around on a pond. So long as this happens, the intracellular domains of the FcRs are rarely in close proximity. However, if several FcRs bind to several antibodies that have bound antigen on a surface, such as a microbial cell wall, the FcRs become clustered together for long periods of time. This receptor clustering is a critical requirement for the activation of FcRs (and many other transmembrane receptors), and prevents the activation of cells by unbound antibody (Figure 8.11).

![](image_rsrc4CV.jpg)

FcRs are named according to the type of heavy chain that they interact with. For example, FcγRI binds to the gamma-1 type Fc chain of IgG1 antibodies. FcRs do not all bind antibodies with equal affinity. FcγRI and FcεRI have high affinity for IgG antibodies, but the others tend to bind their respective antibodies with relatively low affinity. A further general rule is that while cross-linking of most types of FcR causes cellular activation, FcγRIIB (which is only expressed on B-cells) promotes inhibitory signalling, as will be discussed below.

FcγRI is the major IgG receptor expressed by macrophages and neutrophils. It preferentially binds IgG1 and IgG3, which is why IgG1 and IgG3 are efficient opsonins. FcγRI is upregulated by IFN-γ, which thereby promotes the uptake of opsonised microbes.

Table 8.2: Properties of human Fc receptors

![](image_rsrc4CW.jpg)

Antibody feedback

A mechanism exists to prevent the excessive production of soluble IgG. This self-limiting system kicks in when B-cells detect that the specific IgG they are producing is bound to antigen in their vicinity, causing a reduction in IgG secretion by that B-cell. B-cells can detect when this occurs because the presence of both polyvalent antigen and specific IgG causes clustering of BOTH their own transmembrane antibody receptor (which binds the antigen directly) AND the inhibitory receptor FcγRIIB, which binds to the IgG stuck to the antigen. When these two different types of receptor cluster together, signalling pathways are triggered that downregulate production of antibody in the B-cell.

As mentioned above, cross-linking of most types of FcR causes cellular activation. This is mediated by an intracellular domain called the ITAM (immunoreceptor tyrosine based activation motif) which is part of the FcR or a related accessory chain. However, FcγRIIB causes inhibition of signalling in B-cells, because it contains an ITIM (immunoreceptor tyrosine based inhibitory motif) intracellular domain.

This feedback-inhibition pathway is sometimes exploited therapeutically. Occasionally, autoimmune diseases can be triggered by IgG antibodies directed at self proteins and it is advantageous to therapeutically reduce secretion of IgG. This can sometimes be achieved by the administration of pooled human intravenous IgG (IVIG), which triggers the negative feedback pathway and reduces secretion of the damaging auto-antibodies.

Passive immunity

Antibodies purified from the blood of a human donor can also be administered to patients for other purposes. This approach, which is called passive immunisation, is used to provide protection against hepatitis and rabies virus, and occasionally to neutralise toxins, such as tetanus toxin or the venom received from a snake-bite. The protection afforded by such antibodies only lasts for as long as these antibodies remain in the circulation. Moreover, no immunological memory is generated during passive immunisation since the host lymphocytes are not activated.

Placental IgG transfer

When babies are born, they are removed from the sterile environment of the womb and immediately exposed to millions of microbes, many of which can be dangerous pathogens. In order to provide some defence to the offspring during this critical period, protective antibodies are transferred from the mother to the child both before birth, and for many months afterwards, to confer a form of passive immunity. Before birth, IgG antibodies are actively transported across the placenta into the circulation of the developing foetus by the unique FcRN (neonatal Fc) receptor. After birth, if the child is breastfed, it receives both IgG and IgA antibodies which are secreted into the milk. The ingested IgA antibodies help protect the infant’s gut from intestinal pathogens, and the ingested maternal IgG is absorbed into the circulation from the intestinal lumen through the process of transcytosis. In this way, the infant obtains protective antibodies against essentially the same organisms to which the mother has already developed humoral immunity.

Most antibodies have relatively short half-lives (of 2-4 days) in the circulation. However, the half-life of circulating IgG is 3-4 weeks. This is because endothelial cells which line the blood vessel walls express the FcRN receptor, which binds to IgG and internalises it within the endothelial cell. Here, it is not degraded, but retained for a time before being released. This slows the clearance of IgG from the circulation. Some recombinant proteins developed for therapeutic use are fused to the Fc portion of IgG to take advantage of this pathway and enhance their half-life in the circulation.

Summary

1) The B-cell receptor is termed antibody or immunoglobulin, it can be secreted or remain attached to the cell membrane to serve as a signalling receptor

2) Only activated B-cells and plasma cells release soluble antibody, otherwise it remains attached to the cell as a transmembrane protein

3) Humoral immunity is mediated by secreted antibodies, which can bind strongly to and with high specificity for antigens to neutralise or otherwise promote clearance of the target molecule or organism

4) Most antibody molecules are composed of four covalently linked protein chains: two identical heavy chains and two identical light chains

5) The type of heavy chain that is expressed by the B-cell determines the class, or isotype, of the antibody (IgA, IgD, IgE, IgG or IgM)

6) Different classes of antibody perform different functions and contribute to different types of immune response

7) The part of the antibody that binds to antigen is the variable region (V-region), which is at the opposite end of the molecule from the part that interacts with host antibody receptors (Fc region)

8) 3 flexible loops, each ~10 amino acids in length, protrude from the N-terminal end of each heavy and each light chain. The sequences of these loops are highly variable, and the six loops together form the major part of the antibody surface that recognises and binds to specific antigens.

9) There are two types of antibody light chain that may be expressed: kappa (k) or lambda (λ)

10) Large antigens may contain several different epitopes, which are individual sites where antibodies may bind on the surface of the antigen

11) Affinity refers to the strength of an interaction between two molecules, while avidity refers to the strength of interaction between several pairs of the same molecules working together, such as the 10 variable regions of IgM binding to 10 antigens on a microbial surface

12) Cross-reactivity refers to the occurrence when one antibody can bind to two or more different antigens

13) Antibody heavy chain genes contain a C-region and a V-region, the C-region dictates the class of antibody that is expressed and can be changed through the process of B-cell class switching

14) Antibodies produced early during immune responses may have limited binding affinity for antigen, but point mutations may be introduced into the V-regions through a process called somatic hypermutation, and those B-cells that bind antigen more effectively are positively selected through a process called affinity maturation, which occurs in the germinal centres of lymph nodes

15) Epitopes targeted by antibodies can be linear (several amino acids in a row) or conformational (a surface patch of a protein made up of amino acid loops from different parts of the protein)

16) B-cells can mount antibody responses in a T-cell (T)-dependent or T-independent (TI) manner

17) TI antigens do not require T-cell help, but require polyvalent antigens that cross-link B-cell receptors, mostly leading to short-lived plasma cells secreting low-affinity IgM

18) Proteins are T-dependent antigens, requiring T-cell help, but can develop high affinity antibodies of different classes and memory B-cells

19) The primary antibody response is generated by the activation of naïve B-cells, is slow to develop and is dominated by low affinity antibodies

20) The secondary antibody response is generated by the activation of memory B-cells, and rapidly yields high levels of correctly class-switched, high affinity antibodies

21) The major effector functions of antibodies are to promote: opsonisation, the classical pathway of complement activation, neutralisation of toxins, antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity and defence against helminths

22) Receptors for the Fc portion of antibodies (FcR), are specific for classes or subclasses of antibody, through binding of the respective types of heavy chain

23) Passive immunity is the transfer of antibodies from one individual to another to provide temporary protection from toxins or certain infectious agents

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antibody>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humoral_immunity>

Stavnezer J, Guikema JEJ, Schrader CE. Mechanism and regulation of class switch recombination. Annu Rev Immunol 26:261-92 (2008) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC2707252/>

---

# Chapter 9: Presentation to T-cells

T-lymphocytes cannot detect antigen directly. Instead, they detect small fragments of proteins presented to them on specialised cell surface molecules by antigen-presenting cells (APC). There are two main types of presenting molecule: class I major histocompatibility complex (MHC) and class II MHC. Both molecules present peptides to T-cells, but while MHC-I presents fragments of cytosolic proteins to CD8+ T-cells, MHC-II presents peptides from extracellular proteins to CD4+ T-cells. This chapter will cover the major mechanisms involved in antigen processing and presentation to T-cell receptors.

Why do we need T-cells?

As discussed in the preceding chapter, the antibody response is highly efficient at targeting antigens for neutralisation or elimination. However, there are some limitations as to the types of antigen that may be targeted by this mechanism. For instance, antibody responses can only be developed against antigens that are soluble or present on the outer surface of a micro-organism. Moreover, many pathogens, particularly viruses and intracellular bacteria, are able to entirely evade the antibody response by hiding within host cells. When microbes enter host cells, they cannot be detected by antibodies because antibody molecules are too large to cross the plasma membrane and enter the cytosol. For these reasons, a mechanism is required to enable detection of the internal antigens of microbes, and also the antigens of microbes that hide in the cytosol of host cells. Furthermore, the interactions between the different arms of the immune system are complex, and some mechanism is required to enable the fine control of the magnitude and type of immune responses according to the type of antigen encountered.

T-cells address each of these three key requirements by regulating immune responses, enabling class-switching and memory formation in B-cells and killing virally infected cells.

T-cells detect antigen presented on antigen-presenting cells

As described briefly in earlier chapters, T-cells cannot detect free antigen. This is because the T-cell receptor (TCR) is fundamentally different from the B-cell receptor (antibody). While B-cells can, through their antibody based receptor, bind to and recognise an epitope on the surface of any type of antigen, including protein, carbohydrates, lipids and indeed almost any other type of molecule, the majority of T-cells can only respond to protein antigens. Moreover, while B-cells can respond to free antigen in its native form, T-cells cannot do so. Instead, T-cell activation is entirely dependent on physical contact with another cell, which has degraded and presented small pieces of the antigen on the surface of that presenting cell.

This process of antigen uptake, processing and display of small fragments on the cell surface is termed antigen presentation. Recall from Chapter 2 that proteins are simply the folded versions of long linear chains of amino acids all joined nose to tail. Proteins can contain anywhere from tens to thousands of amino acids joined in this way. Enzymes called proteases can cleave proteins into smaller, linear, fragments which are termed peptides. These peptide fragments of proteins are the only type of antigen that can be recognised by CD4+ and CD8+ T-cells. T-cells cannot detect free peptide. Instead, the TCR can only detect peptide loaded into a special peptide-binding groove of a MHC-I or MHC-II molecule. Crucially, CD8+ T-cells can only recognise peptide displayed on MHC-I, and CD4+ T-cells can only recognise peptide displayed on MHC-II (Figure 9.1). This distinction is one of the major mechanisms by which the right type of T-cell response can be directed towards particular types of infection, as will be discussed later.

![](image_rsrc4CX.jpg)

Major histocompatibility complex genes

The term ‘major histocompatibility complex’ actually refers to a locus (a region of the genome) that was originally identified as one that largely dictates the success of tissue grafts between strains of mice. Later experiments revealed that T-cell responses to viral antigens were restricted to occur only in the presence of MHC-I molecules on antigen presenting cells from the same strain of mouse. These experiments gave rise to the term MHC-I-restricted, which indicates that this type of T-cell response is dependent on MHC-I, and cannot be induced by MHC-II.

In humans, the MHC locus codes for a number of genes involved in antigen presentation. On each of the two copies of the chromosome harbouring the genes, there are 3 MHC class I genes and 3 MHC class II genes. In man, these genes are called human leukocyte antigens or HLA genes. The class I MHC molecules in man are called HLA-A, HLA-B and HLA-C. The class II MHC molecules in man are called HLA-DP, HLA-DQ and HLA-DR. Unlike the TCR and BCR, which are generated by genetic recombination, the MHC genes do not undergo any somatic recombination or editing.

However, one of the remarkable properties of the HLA genes is just how different they can be between different individuals in the human population. When two individuals carry different versions of the same gene, the different variants of the gene are called alleles. The differences between the genes at the DNA sequence level are referred to as polymorphisms. There are thought to be >3,000 different HLA alleles in the human population. This diversity plays a major role in our capacity to defend against infection, since each different type of HLA molecule, and each different allele, is proficient at binding certain types of peptide. This gives each of us the capacity to present different antigens to T-cells, and some of us are therefore more proficient at dealing with some organisms than others. This variation also means that within any outbred population, there are likely to be some subjects that can respond well to any particular strain of infection, so ensuring the survival of the tribe.

It should be noted that the MHC complex also contains several non-HLA genes that code for proteins involved in antigen processing (such as proteases and peptide transporters), complement (C4, factor B, C2) and several cytokines (lymphotoxin-β, TNF-α).

Many experiments in immunology laboratories make use of mice, and the MHC molecules of mice are very well studied. They are not referred to as HLA genes. Instead, the 3 mouse MHC-I genes are called H-2D, H-2L and H-2K. Mice have only 2 MHC-II genes, called I-A and I-E.

Antigen presenting cells

The term antigen presenting cell (APC) could in theory be applied to any nucleated cell of the body, because every nucleated cell expresses MHC-I and is capable of presenting peptide to CD8+ T-cells. However, immunologists typically apply the term APC only to those cells which express MHC-II and can therefore present peptides to CD4+ T-cells. By this definition, only dendritic cells, macrophages and some B-cells are usually referred to as APCs (or sometimes, professional APCs).

A further distinction is made between these different cells. Macrophages and B-cells can only stimulate memory T-cells, i.e. those which have already been activated as part of a previous immune response. By contrast, dendritic cells are the only APC which can activate naïve T-cells. This critical distinction means that the responses of naïve T-cells to new antigens are entirely dependent on DCs. For this reason, DCs are often referred to as professional APCs, to differentiate their properties from other APCs which cannot perform this function. The different functions of APCs are summarised in Table 9.1.

Table 9.1: Summary of antigen presenting cell functions

![](image_rsrc4CY.jpg)

The recognition of peptide bound to MHC is not sufficient to activate naïve T-cells. A further set of signals are required to be delivered by cell surface molecules of the APC to the T-cell to confirm that activation is warranted in response to the displayed antigen. These signals are referred to as costimulatory signals, and are enhanced by cytokines that are also released by the APC. The expression of costimulatory signals by APCs is enhanced by the detection of microbial PAMPs via receptors such as TLRs and NLRs. In return, the activated T-cell provides signals to the APC, causing the APC to become activated and more efficient at antigen processing and presentation. The cytokine IFN-γ in particular is a potent inducer of antigen processing and expression of MHC and costimulators. Indeed, IFN-γ is able to promote MHC-II expression in cells that otherwise do not express it, such as some epithelial and mesenchymal cells. The mechanisms of this two-way communication will be discussed in more depth in Chapter 12.

Dendritic cells capture antigen and migrate to the T-cell rich areas of lymph nodes

Although infections, and therefore the entry of antigens, may occur anywhere in the body, T-cell responses are raised in the peripheral lymphoid organs. This means that there must be some mechanism for antigen capture and transport from the periphery to T-cells in the lymph nodes. This function is mediated by dendritic cells. DCs are numerous in the tissues that are most likely to be breached by infectious microbes, such as the skin, the gastrointestinal tract and other mucosal surfaces. There are two major types of DC: plasmacytoid DCs, which are rare, blood-borne cells that serve mainly to produce type I interferons during antiviral responses and conventional DCs, which serve mainly to initiate T-cell responses and will now be discussed in more detail.

Conventional DCs are derived from the myeloid lineage and are found in most tissues. In the skin, they are referred to as Langerhans cells. Here, they serve as sentinels to trap and process antigens associated with infection. The DCs achieve this so-called antigen-capture in a number of ways. First, they may ingest intact microbes, or fragments of microbes by phagocytosis. This process is aided by the expression of a range of molecules that bind to microbes, such as the mannose receptor, Fc receptors and complement receptors, by the DC. Alternatively, they may take up soluble antigen by fluid-phase pinocytosis. This mechanism of antigen uptake is distinct from phagocytosis, as it does not require any receptor to bind to the antigen to promote uptake. Instead, the cell routinely ingests small amounts of the extracellular fluid to sample any soluble molecules that may be present nearby. Antigen may be captured in the tissues by DCs resident in those tissues, or soluble antigens may be captured from lymphatic fluid by DCs in the lymph node, or from blood by DCs in the spleen. Regardless of the method of antigen capture, ingested antigens are transported to the phagolysosome of the DC, where their constituent molecules are degraded by digestive enzymes. In particular, the proteins are converted into short lengths of peptides, which are eventually loaded onto MHC-II for presentation to CD4+ T-cells.

Tissue DCs typically exist in an immature form, which is able to capture and sample antigen, but unable to activate naïve T-cells. In order for these cells to mature into fully functional DCs, they must be activated by certain signals, such as exposure to PAMPs. DCs express a wide range of PRRs, such as TLRs and NLRs, and are therefore sensitive to many different types of PAMP. If a tissue DC encounters a PAMP while performing its normal antigen capture functions, it becomes activated and effectively associates the antigens it has recently captured with a danger signal for the rest of the immune system.

This activation causes a number of key changes to occur in the DC (Figure 9.2). First, they become mobile, and upregulate the chemokine receptor CCR7, which is specific for the chemokines CCL19 and CCL21. Because these two chemokines are produced in the T-cell zones of lymph nodes, activated DCs migrate towards the local draining lymphatic vessels, and then travel down this vessel to enter the lymph node, where they enter the T-cell zones to present antigen to naïve T-cells, which also express CCR7 and traffic to the same part of the lymph node. Second, the cells begin to express higher levels of MHC-II, co-stimulatory molecules and cytokines. These changes enable the mature DCs to activate naïve T-cells, which is required for adaptive responses to be generated to new protein antigens. This requirement for DC activation by PAMPs or similar signals also limits the potential for harmless antigens that arise from non-infective sources (such as food antigens) to induce inappropriate T-cell responses.

![](image_rsrc4CZ.jpg)

MHC-I and MHC-II molecules are comprised of α and β chains

A number of molecular features enable MHC class I and MHC class II molecules to efficiently display peptides to T-lymphocytes. Each MHC molecule is a heterodimer composed of two protein chains, called the α and β subunits. MHC-I is composed of one large α chain that has a transmembrane domain and binds the peptide, and one much smaller β chain (called β2-microglobulin) that does not straddle the membrane. By contrast, the α and β chains of MHC-II are of approximately the same size, both chains are transmembrane and each chain contributes to the formation of the peptide cleft that binds and presents peptide (Figure 9.3).

![](image_rsrc4D0.jpg)

In both classes of MHC molecule, a peptide binding cleft exists at the top of the molecule. The bottom of the peptide-binding groove is formed by a type of protein structure called β-pleated sheet, and the sides, or walls, of the groove are composed of one alpha helix on each side. As mentioned previously, there is great diversity in HLA genes in the human population, which means the protein sequence of HLA genes can be subtly different between different individuals. The changes to the amino acid sequences of the proteins are called polymorphisms, and it should be noted that most of the polymorphisms in MHC-I or MHC-II proteins are in or around the peptide binding cleft, but rarely in other parts of the protein.

This variation between individuals in the amino acids that line the peptide cleft is crucial to maintaining immunological diversity in the population. Different amino acids in the floor and walls of the cleft will encourage different peptides to bind. This means that some HLA molecules will be efficient at presenting peptides from some strains of organism, but less efficient at presenting peptides from some other strains of organism, depending on the protein sequence of the antigens expressed by each strain.

A further molecular feature of both MHC-I and MHC-II that is critical to generating T-cell responses is that other domains of the molecule, which are not polymorphic, specifically bind to either CD8 or CD4 expressed by T-cells. This is why only MHC-I can stimulate CD8+ T-cells, and only MHC-II can stimulate CD4+ T-cells (Figure 9.1).

Peptide binding to MHC is enhanced by anchor residues

One of the key features of both MHC-I and MHC-II is that they can only fold properly, and therefore be expressed on the cell surface, if they have successfully bound a peptide. This means that only useful MHC are displayed on the cell surface, and empty MHC molecules are not displayed.

The peptide binding groove of MHC-I is adapted to harbour peptides 8 to 11 amino acids in length. This is because the cleft is closed at both ends by the α chain structure itself. By contrast, the MHC-II groove is able to accommodate much longer peptides, from 10 to 30 amino acids or even longer, because the ends of the groove are open, and the peptide can extend outwards from these openings.

Each MHC molecule is able to bind only one peptide at a time. However, the range of potential amino acid sequences of peptides that may bind to any one MHC molecule is vast. This is quite distinct from the T-cell receptor, which is very specific for only one type of peptide sequence. However, some peptides bind more readily to the groove of certain MHC molecules than others. For example, only peptides of 8-11 amino acids will bind to MHC-I molecules. The types of amino acids (also called residues) that make up the peptide, and the order in which they occur, also contribute to the potential of the peptide to bind to the groove. For example, positively charged amino acids may enhance binding to MHC if they are close to negatively charged amino acids of the MHC groove. Indeed, class I MHC molecules prefer binding of peptides with a positively charged N-terminus and negatively charged C-terminus. Likewise, other interactions can promote the binding of peptides with specific types of amino acid in particular locations, such as van der Waals interactions, and hydrogen bonding. A further adaptation that enhances the binding of some peptides to certain MHC molecules is the presence of “pockets” in the floor of the binding groove, that accommodate and selectively bind bulky amino acids (Figure 9.4). The amino acids that enhance binding of a particular peptide to a MHC molecule through any of the above interactions are called anchor residues. Typically, MHC molecules accommodate peptides with 2-3 anchor residues.

![](image_rsrc4D1.jpg)

Peptide binding to MHC frequently results in a very stable complex. Although the peptide is not covalently attached to the MHC molecule, the affinity of the interaction is strong enough for the presented peptide to remain associated with the MHC molecule for a long time after loading and transport to the cell surface. An individual DC may express tens or hundreds of thousands of individual MHC-molecules, each containing peptide, for display to passing T-cells.

Both self and non-self peptides are displayed on MHC molecules

As described above, the diversity of peptides that can bind to the groove of MHC molecules is vast. APCs in fact do not make any attempt to discriminate between self and non-self peptides, meaning that the majority of peptides expressed on MHC molecules of APCs are derived from self proteins. During an immune response to an antigen, therefore, activated DCs will present both self and non-self proteins in the T-cell zone of the lymph node. However, only naïve T-cells responsive to foreign antigens, but not self antigens, are normally activated because self-reactive T-cells are deleted or inactivated by other mechanisms, as will be discussed in Chapter 14.

Antigen processing yields peptides from protein antigens

We will first describe some of the general aspects of antigen processing that are applicable to both the MHC-I and MHC-II pathways. Generally speaking, both pathways require the proteolysis of protein antigens to yield peptides. This means that the long, folded amino acid chains of proteins are unfolded and broken down into shorter lengths by specific enzymes in the cell. Through this means, peptides of the right length to fit in the groove of each type of MHC are generally produced. Both types of MHC molecule are loaded with peptide soon after they are synthesised in the endoplasmic reticulum (ER), and peptide loading is required for their successful folding and translocation to the cell surface. However, the two pathways by which antigens are processed to be loaded onto MHC-I or MHC-II are different in a number of critical aspects. The most relevant of these is that peptides from cytosolic proteins are presented on MHC-I, and peptides from extracellular proteins are presented on MHC-II (Figure 9.5). This means that cytosolic antigens are recognised only by CD8+ T-cells and extracellular antigens (when captured and presented) are recognised only by CD4+ T-cells. We will now discuss some of the specific features of each pathway of antigen processing and presentation.

![](image_rsrc4D2.jpg)

Cytosolic proteins are processed and presented by the MHC-I pathway

Foreign antigens can enter the cytosol of host cells by a number of means. Most frequently, proteins produced by the transcription of viral genomes yield intracellular viral proteins which accumulate in the cytosol. Some types of bacteria (such as Listeria monocytogenes or Shigella flexneri), can also enter the cytosol after escaping from the phagolysosome. To monitor for the presence of such organisms within host cells, all cytosolic proteins are routinely degraded and a portion of the resulting peptides are transferred to MHC-I. This degradation of cytosolic proteins is part of the normal function of the cell, since all cytosolic proteins have a certain half-life, and damaged or old proteins are coated with a molecule (called ubiquitin) that marks them as such. This molecular tag causes cytosolic proteins to be directed towards a large cytosolic complex called the proteasome. Within this barrel-like structure, ubiquitin-tagged proteins are unfolded into a linear chain, and then degraded into small peptides, most of which will be eventually recycled further into constituent amino acids for reutilisation by the cell. However, a small proportion of these peptides are transported from the cytosol into the lumen of the ER for loading into MHC-I (Figure 9.6).

![](image_rsrc4D3.jpg)

Like most cellular organelles, the ER is enclosed in a phospholipid bilayer membrane that is impervious to most peptides. In order for peptides generated in the cytosol to enter the ER, a specialised peptide transporter protein is required called the transporter associated with antigen processing (TAP). The TAP protein is a dimer that straddles the membrane of the ER and catalyses the transport of peptides from the cytosol into the ER lumen in an ATP-dependent manner. It has a preference for peptides of 8-16 amino acids in length and with amino acid features that are similar to the requirements for loading into MHC-I molecules. The peptides produced by the proteasome, and sometimes other cytosolic proteases, are not always of the correct length to fit in the MHC-I groove. For this reason, an enzyme termed ER-resident aminopeptidase (ERAP) trims the peptides to the right length for assembly on MHC-I.

MHC-I molecules are synthesised in the ER. Before being loaded with antigen peptide, they are inherently unstable, and so are temporarily held together by proteins called chaperones that exist in the ER and help proteins to fold correctly. The recently formed MHC-I molecules are then bound by a linker protein called tapasin, which also binds to TAP. This causes the recently formed MHC-I to co-localise with TAP as part of a complex that includes several other proteins involved in peptide loading. These proteins co-operate to load MHC-I with a peptide of the right length from the ER lumen. This causes a conformational change in MHC-I, as it adopts its highly stable peptide-containing form, resulting in release from tapasin and TAP. MHC-I molecules correctly loaded with peptide are then allowed to leave the ER, and trafficking through exocytic vesicles to be presented on the cell membrane.

Cross presentation of antigens

In addition to the classical MHC-I processing pathway described above, an alternative pathway for MHC-I antigen processing is present in DCs (but no other cell type). Because many viruses are only able to infect certain types of cell, it is possible that during the course of a viral infection, DCs would not be infected by a particular virus. This poses a serious problem, as naïve CD8+ T-cells could then not be activated by the antigens of those viruses, since those antigens would not occur in the cytosol of uninfected DCs. Although every nucleated cell in the body is at risk of viral infection, and therefore expresses MHC-I, non-dendritic cells cannot activate naïve T-cells. To deal with this problem, some DCs are able to ingest dead host cells or their debris, which have been infected and contain viral antigen. The materials are ingested into a phagolysosome, which then fuses with part of the ER. By mechanisms that remain unclear, proteins delivered from the phagolysosome to the ER are exported out of the ER into the cytoplasm. From here, the antigens are processed in the same way as the classical MHC-I pathway. This alternative class I processing pathway is called cross-presentation, or cross-priming, and is essential for the development of CD8+ T-cell responses to many viruses.

Extracellular proteins are processed and presented by the MHC-II pathway

While antigen processing for the class I pathway generates peptides from cytosolic proteins, the class II presentation pathway mainly generates peptides from extracellular proteins. This occurs in professional APCs, such as DCs, macrophages and B-cells, which express receptors for microbes to enhance uptake of antigen, and the machinery required for phagocytosis or endocytosis of antigens. Cell-surface receptors that can promote uptake of microbes include complement receptors, PRRs (such as the mannose receptor), FcRs (because antibody may have bound to the microbe) and antibody itself, expressed by B-cells.

When microbes or antigens are first taken into the cell, the vesicle that contains them is called the endosome. As the endosome moves further into the cell, it matures to become a so-called ‘late’ endosome. As this happens, the pH of the endosome becomes more acidic, enhancing the activity of acid-dependent proteases, such as cathepsins. A subset of such late endosomes contain large quantities of MHC-II protein in macrophages and human B-cells, and these are referred to as MHC-class-II compartments, or MIIC. The endosome may then fuse with another intracellular vesicle called the lysosome, which contains many further proteases. In either case, the ingested protein antigens become cleaved by these proteases to yield peptides that are suitable for further processing and loading onto MHC-II molecules (Figure 9.7).

![](image_rsrc4D4.jpg)

Because MHC-II is unstable without peptide bound to the groove, when it is first synthesised in the ER it is folded first with the help of chaperones. Then, while it is still in the ER, a protein called the invariant chain (li) binds the groove of the newly formed MHC-II molecule to keep it stable. Notably the invariant chain prevents other peptides in the ER lumen from binding to the MHC-II cleft. By this mechanism, MHC-II molecules are not loaded with the peptides that bind to MHC-I molecules in the ER. This complex of MHC-II α and β chains together with li then exits the ER through the golgi apparatus and eventually traffics into exocytic vesicles.

It is at this stage that the exocytic vesicles containing MHC-II fuse with endosomal vesicles containing peptide. Next, in order for peptide to bind to MHC-II, the invariant chain must be removed. First, the invariant chain is cleaved by the same proteases present in the endosome that cleaved the ingested proteins, to yield a small 24 amino acid peptide called class II-associated invariant chain peptide (CLIP), which remains in the groove of MHC-II. A protein that is structurally similar to MHC-II called HLA-DM (H-2M in the mouse) then catalyses the replacement of CLIP with one of the antigenic peptides present in the vesicle. Often, the peptides that bind to MHC-II in this manner are quite long. Such peptides are trimmed further by proteases to yield a final MHC-II peptide complex containing a peptide of ~10-30 amino acids. The assembled MHC-II / peptide complexes are then transported to the cell membrane, where they present peptide to CD4+ T-cells.

Although the majority of proteins processed by the class II pathway originate from extracellular fluid, a minority may enter the pathway from non-classical origins. For example, cell membrane proteins that happen to be present at the time of endocytosis can be ingested with the endocytic vesicle and directed through the class II pathway. Notably, viruses often produce transmembrane proteins that may enter the class II pathway in this manner. The process of autophagy, which is the sequestration and digestion of large parts of the cytosol during times of cell stress, can cause cytosolic proteins to be degraded in the lysosome. By this means, cytosolic proteins of host, virus or intracellular bacterial origin can in principle be directed through the class II pathway.

Presentation on class-I or class-II molecules promotes a different type of T-cell response

The different pathways of antigen processing for class I and class II MHC are required to generate the right type of T-cell response to deal with particular infectious organisms. For example, extracellular proteins are typically processed through the class II pathway and activate CD4+ T-cells. The activated CD4+ T-cells then promote antibody responses against these proteins, which is the correct type of response since antibodies are able to target extracellular microbes. By contrast, cytosolic proteins processed via the class I pathway initiate CD8+ T-cell responses, which are specialised at eradicating intracellular infectious organisms. Thus, the MHC-I pathway promotes defence against intracellular pathogens, and the MHC-II pathway promotes defence against extracellular pathogens.

Not all peptide sequences within an antigenic protein are equally immunogenic

A typical antigenic protein may contain anywhere from 100 to 1,000 amino acids, and can therefore give rise to hundreds of different peptides when processed. However, during an immune response to any particular protein, most of the responding T-cells target only a small number of these possible peptides. Such peptides are said to be immunodominant epitopes. This arises partly because the proteases involved in antigen processing generally cleave proteins at particular, preferred amino acids within the protein sequence. Often, the anchor residues in many of these peptides may not match well with the polymorphic amino acids of the MHC molecules expressed by a particular individual. Thus, different individuals given the same protein antigen may respond to different peptide sequences, depending on which are most likely to fit in the peptide binding grooves of the MHC molecules they happen to have inherited. This means that the immunodominant epitope in any particular antigen is not always the same for different individuals, and can be difficult to predict.

Non-protein antigens can also be presented by non-classical presentation mechanisms

The majority of this chapter has discussed the processing and presentation of protein antigens to T-cells using MHC-I and MHC-II molecules. However, some T-cells are able to detect and respond to non-protein antigens presented by non-classical presentation molecules. For example, certain APCs can express the molecule CD1 (of which there are 4 subtypes in man), which is structurally similar to MHC-I, but which presents lipid antigens to certain subsets of T-cells. For example, NKT-cells recognise lipids and glycolipids presented to them in the context of CD1. Such lipids are captured within the hydrophobic pocket of CD1 as it is recycled through the endosomal or lysosomal compartments, and in this way can be loaded with microbial lipids.

γδ T-cells are also not MHC-restricted, which means they respond to antigens independently of MHC molecules. These T-cells express antigen receptors of limited diversity, but recognise a number of relatively conserved molecules, including some lipids, proteins and small molecules such as alkyl-amines. It is not clear if any presentation or processing pathway is required for γδ T-cell function.

Summary

1) T-cells cannot detect antigen directly, but rather sense peptides derived from protein antigens presented to them by antigen presenting cells (APC)

2) CD8+ T-cells can only recognise peptide displayed on MHC-I, and CD4+ T-cells can only recognise peptide displayed on MHC-II

3) MHC-I molecules display peptides derived from cytosolic proteins, and MHC-II molecules display peptides derived from extracellular proteins

4) All nucleated cells express MHC-I and are able to present peptide derived from cytosolic proteins to CD8+ cytotoxic T-cells

5) Only a small number of cell types, including dendritic cells, macrophages and B-cells, express MHC-II and are able to present peptide to CD4+ helper T-cells

6) Dendritic cells are considered professional APC, since only these cells are capable of stimulating naïve CD4+ T-cells; they achieve this by expressing cytokines and cell surface co-stimulator molecules during presentation

7) Human MHC-I molecules include HLA-A, HLA-B and HLA-C, human MHC-II molecules include HLA-DP, HLA-DQ, HLA-DR

8) MHC-I molecules consist of a large α chain which contains a peptide binding groove and a smaller, invariant, protein called β2-microglobulin

9) MHC-II molecules consist of two similarly sized α and β chains, both of which contribute to the formation of the peptide binding groove

10) Only short peptides of 8-11 amino acids fit in the MHC-I groove, but the groove of MHC-II has open ends and can accommodate larger peptides of 10-30 amino acids

11) The range of peptide sequences that may be accommodated by MHC molecules is enormous, but presented peptides often contain 2 or 3 anchor residues, which are amino acids of particular types that enhance their binding to MHC grooves, often by interaction with hydrophobic pockets in the floor of the groove

12) The amino acids surrounding the MHC-I and MHC-II grooves are highly polymorphic, and these impact on the types of peptides that may bind to the MHC molecules expressed by any individual in a population, potentially impacting on their ability to respond to certain epitopes

13) MHC molecules present peptides from both self and non-self proteins

14) Dendritic cells strategically situated in the tissues capture antigen, become activated by PAMPs and migrate to the T-cell zone of lymph nodes by upregulation of CCR7

15) Only activated (or mature) DCs express sufficient co-stimulatory molecules in order to be able to stimulate naïve T-cells

16) Cytosolic proteins are recycled by the proteasome, which produces peptides that are transported into the lumen of the ER by the TAP protein

17) Only MHC-I molecules which receive peptide in the groove fold correctly and reach the cell surface

18) Cross-presentation is the process by which dendritic cells may ingest virally infected dead host cells and transport their antigens into the cytosol for processing via the MHC-I pathway

19) Extracellular proteins are digested in the endosome or lysosome, and these vesicles fuse with exocytic vesicles containing MHC-II molecules complexed with the invariant chain (li)

20) The invariant chain is then cleaved to yield the peptide CLIP, which is replaced by peptide from degraded endocytosed proteins and the stable MHC-II complex traffics to the cell surface

21) Presentation via the class-II pathway promotes antibody responses, which are effective at dealing with extracellular proteins, and presentation via the class-I pathway is effective at promoting CD8+ T-cells, which is effective at dealing with intracellular pathogens

22) Immune responses to a protein typically target only a small number of the possible peptides that may be produced by cleavage of that protein, these are referred to as the immunodominant epitopes

23) CD1 is a MHC-I-like molecule that binds and presents lipids to NKT-cells

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antigen_presentation>

Rock KL, Reits E, Neefjes J. Present Yourself! By MHC Class I and MHC Class II Molecules. Trends Immunol 37:724-37 (2016) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5159193/>

---

# Chapter 10: Genetic rearrangement: the key to adaptive immunity

While the innate immune system uses conserved germ-line encoded receptors that recognise a small number of different PAMPs, the adaptive immune system uses millions of different receptors to detect millions of different possible antigens. The amount of DNA required to code for all of these genes, if each was coded individually, would make the human genome too large to fit inside a cell. For this reason, an elegant solution to the problem of generating this enormous receptor diversity has evolved. Both the B-cell receptor (antibody) and the T-cell receptor (TCR) are generated by splicing together and editing short sections of DNA in a process called genetic rearrangement. This chapter will discuss the key mechanisms that underpin the processes of genetic rearrangement in developing T-cells and B-cells.

A brief recap of the rules of transcription and translation

The mechanisms by which genetic rearrangement yields receptor diversity are relatively complex, so it may be helpful to begin with a brief recap of the key steps involved in the transcription and translation of genes to produce proteins. First, it should be borne in mind that most of the chromosomal DNA in a cell is tightly wound together in a ‘closed’ form called heterochromatin. Genes that are present in such DNA tend not to be expressed, since the proteins required to read these genes and produce mRNA cannot gain access to the DNA. Thus, whole regions of DNA containing many genes can be switched on by opening up these regions of chromosomes into a more open, less tightly wound format called euchromatin, or switched off by causing the DNA to pack densely into heterochromatin.

Next, the rate at which mRNA molecules are produced by any gene is dictated mainly by the promoter, which lies close to the start of the gene (i.e. at the 5’ end, since genes are transcribed in the 5’ to 3’ direction), and by a variable number of enhancers. These enhancers can be located anywhere upstream, downstream or even within the gene, and serve to switch on the promoter, causing more mRNA to be produced which leads to more protein being produced. Generally speaking, the closer the enhancer is to the promoter, the stronger the activation of gene expression.

When mRNA is synthesised by copying the chromosomal DNA (i.e. transcription), the mRNA molecules that are produced usually contains distinct subsections called exons, which are eventually translated into protein, and introns, which are not. Before the mRNA is translated into protein, it undergoes a process of RNA processing, or splicing, which removes and discards the introns from the mRNA molecule. Notably, this splicing process can often produce several different mature mRNA molecules from the same primary mRNA transcripts, depending on which exons are included and which are spliced out. These different products are called splice variants (See Chapter 2, Figure 2.2).

The mature mRNA molecule is then threaded through a multiprotein complex called a ribosome, which reads the mRNA and produces a peptide chain by converting every 3 base pair sequence (called a codon) into one amino acid. Because there are 4 different base pairs, it means there are 4 x 4 x 4 = 64 different possible 3 base codons. However, there are only 20 different amino acids which means that some amino acids are coded for by several different codons. Key codons worth remembering are AUG, which codes for the START of transcription, and UGA, UAA and UAG, all of which code for the STOP instruction. U (uracil) is the RNA version of the DNA letter T (thymine). The START codon is near the 5’ end of the mRNA, and codes for methionine at the start (N-terminus) of the protein. The STOP codon is near the 3’ end of the mRNA and causes the polypeptide chain to be released from the ribosome.

All of the above basic rules contribute to the generation of B-cell and T-cell receptor diversity.

An analogy for combinatorial and junctional diversity

As mentioned above, the genes coding for B-cell and T-cell receptor chains are generated by joining small sections of chromosomal DNA, called gene segments, together. To understand how this results in receptor diversity, it may be helpful to consider an analogy. Imagine being set the task of generating as many different 3 word sentences as possible. You are given three lists of words to make these sentences. One is a list of words that must come first, there is a selection of middle words and a list of possible end words. The number of possible sentences you could theoretically make from these words is calculated very simply by multiplying the number of available first, second and end words together (Table 10.1). For example, if there are 100 possible first words, 20 possible second words and 5 possible end words, the theoretical number of possible sentences that could be produced would be 100 x 20 x 5 = 10,000. This simple calculation is referred to as the combinatorial diversity of the available segments.

However, let’s assume that you were asked to make the sentences even more diverse. Imagine now that you are allowed to add or remove 1 or 2 random letters from the beginning or end of each word where two words meet. There are 26 letters in the roman alphabet, which means at each junction between words you could have up to 26 x 26 (=676) new combinations on the left-hand word and the same on the right hand word. There are two junctions: one between the first and second words, and the other between the second and third words - which gives four opportunities for letter replacement. This means the number of possible different combinations of inserted letters is vast, equivalent to 676 x 676 x 676 x 676, which is equal to ~200,000,000,000 (two hundred billion, or 2 x 1011). This diversity caused by inserting or deleting letters is called junctional diversity. The maximum theoretical number of possible sentences is given by multiplying the combinatorial diversity (10,000) by the junctional diversity (200 billion), to yield 2x1015 (two thousand trillion) different possible sentences, just from applying these two simple rules.

However, have you spotted the problem in all of this? When you randomly insert or remove letters from words, often the resulting word will not make sense, and cannot be used to make a sentence that reads properly. Exactly the same problem occurs in the developing lymphocyte. Although combinatorial diversity generally yields functional genes, adding the junctional diversity step is risky and can lead to the formation of faulty genes that do not read correctly. This means that the practical diversity of the analogy we described above, and the lymphocyte receptor, is much smaller than the theoretical diversity given the DNA letters available. Nevertheless, this analogy shows that applying some very simple rules can quickly lead to enormous potential diversity.

Table 10.1: Illustration of combinatorial diversity

![](image_rsrc4D5.jpg)

Legend to Table 10.1: Any sentence composed by choosing one word in turn from each of the three columns in this table makes sense. For example, the cat looked, our dog ran etc. The combinatorial diversity in this example is effectively the number of sentences it is possible to make from these words. It can be calculated by multiplying 8 (the number of choices in column 1) by 9 (the number of choices in column 2) by 6 (the number of choices in column 3) = 432. Much the same process occurs when B-cells or T-cells choose V, D and J gene segments to join to make functional receptor genes.

V(D)J recombination

The receptor chain genes that must be prepared by genetic rearrangement include the TCR-α, -β, -γ and -δ chains, the Ig heavy chain and the Ig-k and -λ light chains. They are all created by a largely similar process, with some subtle differences as will be discussed. In each case, short gene segments are selected from a large number of available options and brought together in an essentially random fashion so that each different lymphocyte can, as it develops, generate a unique receptor specific for a different antigen (Figure 10.1). These different gene segments are referred to as the variable (V), diversity (D) and joining (J) gene segments.

![](image_rsrc4D6.jpg)

A key point to remember is that only the TCR-β, TCR-δ and BCR heavy chains include a diversity (D) gene segment. All of the other receptor chains are made up by the joining of V and J segments, and do not include D segments. The process by which these different gene segments are brought together and physically joined to create a functional gene is therefore called V(D)J recombination (the D is in brackets because not all lymphocyte receptor genes include a diversity segment). Recall from Chapters 8 and 9 that both the BCR and TCR are composed of a variable region that binds antigen, and a conserved region, that mediates the other functions of the receptor. The rearranged and joined V, D and J segments eventually constitute the part of the gene that codes for the variable region. This is then attached to another gene segment that codes for a constant domain of the protein.

Each of the different sets of gene segments required to make up the receptor chain genes are grouped together in long sections of chromosomal DNA called loci (the plural of locus). For example, there is an H chain locus, a TCR-β chain locus, a k chain locus and so on. In all other cells of the body, and before lymphocytes begin their process of development, these genes are not functional. This is because before recombination, each receptor chain locus consists of many different gene segments, all separated from each other by hundreds or thousands of base pairs, and the enhancer required to switch on transcription of the gene is too far away from the promoter. All of this changes during the process of recombination.

We will now discuss how the BCR and TCR genes are generated in turn.

The basics of B-cell receptor generation

There are 3 different loci that may be recombined to yield BCR receptor chains: the H chain locus, which codes for the heavy chain, and the k and λ light chain loci that code for light chain genes. The recombination of these loci to produce each receptor chain gene occurs in a largely similar fashion, with the exception that there is no D-segment in the k and λ light chains. We will therefore discuss the mechanisms by which human H-chain rearrangement is achieved (rearrangement of mouse Ig genes is similar, although the numbers of available gene segments are somewhat different).

Figure 10.2 shows the major features of the human Ig heavy chain locus before and after recombination. Closest to the start (5’) end of the locus are ~100 V-segments, each of which is about 300 base pairs (bp) long. Next, a region of DNA containing 23 D-segments is present. Following this, there is a region containing 6 J segments, each ~30-50 bp in length. The J-segments are located close to 9 different constant region segments, each of which codes for the Fc region of one of the 9 different isotypes of antibody that can be produced in man. Throughout the locus, each of the gene segments are separated by non-coding DNA.

![](image_rsrc4D7.jpg)

Ultimately, one gene segment from each of these groupings will be selected to form the variable part of the mature, recombined gene (i.e. 1 V segment, 1 D segment and 1 J segment). As mentioned above, combinatorial diversity suggests that there may be as many as 138,000 different possible combinations of these gene segments. However, the actual diversity of Ig heavy chains is much greater than this, due to nucleotide insertion and deletion at the V-D and D-J junctions, as will be described subsequently.

Before the process of recombination can begin, the chromatin containing the locus is unravelled to expose the DNA. Now, a series of specific enzymes are able to bind to the DNA and recognise sequences within the locus that tell them where the beginning and ends of each gene segment lie. These enzymes form loops of DNA, which can be very long, that are eventually permanently excised from the genome. In the Ig heavy chain example, a D segment is brought close to a J segment, and the two are physically joined. Next, this DJ pairing is brought close to a V-region, and the two units are joined, once again causing the intervening loop of DNA to be excised (removed). This now leaves a potentially functional Ig heavy chain gene.

Note that the primary RNA transcript from this gene contains multiple J-segments and two heavy chain segments (Cμ and Cδ). The extraneous J-segments in this RNA molecule are removed during the process of RNA processing (splicing). At this stage, one of the C domains (Cμ or Cδ) will also be spliced out, leaving just one C-domain remaining. This is the mature mRNA that is used by the ribosome to make the antibody heavy chain protein.

A key point to remember is that once the intervening DNA has been excised from the chromosome and the choices of gene segments finally made, those gene segments cannot be used again by that particular B-cell. In fact, there are only three ways in which the receptor gene can change after the process of receptor recombination is complete. The first is class-switching, where different heavy chains may be spliced onto the gene but the variable region remains the same. The next is somatic hypermutation, where the variable region can be subtly altered, as will be discussed later in Chapter 11. Finally, B-cell receptor editing can occur in cells which are strongly reactive to self-antigens, as will be described later.

The order in which BCR chain genes are recombined is as follows. First, the developing B-cell attempts to generate a functional heavy chain gene. Remember that we carry two copies of each non-sex chromosome (one from each parent). That means for each Ig chain we carry two Ig loci, either of which could be used to generate a receptor chain. In fact, the B-cell will attempt to recombine one of these two loci first. If it fails to generate a functional heavy chain at the first attempt, it will simply open up the second chromosome and attempt to produce a functional heavy chain gene from that locus. If that also fails the cell will not receive survival signals and will die by apoptosis.

Those cells that survive this selection process must then face the hurdle of generating a functional light chain gene. It does this first by attempting to rearrange the k light chain locus. However, if this does not yield a functional protein, it will then attempt to rearrange the λ light chain locus. (This is why some antibodies have k light chains and others have λ light chains). Should the λ locus also fail to recombine successfully, the developing B-cell will have failed to produce a functional BCR, will not receive survival signals and will die by apoptosis.

The basics of T-cell receptor generation

The mechanisms by which T-cell receptor genes are recombined from the TCR locus are essentially the same as just described for Ig receptor chain genes. However, T-cells have the choice of becoming either αβ TCR expressing cells or γδ TCR expressing cells. Which type of TCR they eventually express is determined early during thymocyte development. Early thymocytes are capable of rearranging β, γ or δ loci, and will attempt to rearrange all three at much the same time. If the cell is able to generate both a functional γ and a functional δ gene before a functional β gene is created, it will become a γδ T-cell and proceed down that particular developmental pathway. However, ~90% of developing T-cells form a functional β gene before this can happen, and they are then committed to becoming αβ TCR expressing cells.

While the TCR β and δ genes contain V, D and J-segments, the TCR α and γ genes contain only V and J genes. In αβ T-cells, the β gene is rearranged first by joining a D to a J segment (both of which are chosen at random). This D-J unit is then joined to a randomly chosen V segment. If the developing T-cell successfully creates a functional β chain, it will receive survival signals that also switch on the processes required to initiate α chain rearrangement. The α chain is formed by joining a V segment directly to a J segment, since there are no D-segments available in the α locus. Similar to B-cell receptors, the constant region of either protein is coded for by a C-segment that is close to the J-segment but separated by an intron and is brought together with the J-segment not at the DNA level, but during RNA processing (splicing).

Sequence of molecular mechanisms involved in V(D)J recombination

The mechanisms by which the different gene segments are recognised and brought together are essentially the same for all lymphocyte receptor genes. The key processes of recombination occur in the following order (see Figure 10.3):

1) Conserved recombination signal sequences, located just outside certain gene segments, are recognised by an enzyme complex called the V(D)J recombinase, which binds the DNA in these locations.

2) The enzyme complex causes both strands of the DNA, on both ends of the segments that have been brought together, to break.

3) The intervening genomic DNA that was once between the chosen DNA segments forms a loop of DNA that is excised and discarded.

4) The broken single strand ends of the DNA at the end of each chosen gene segment are joined to themselves, forming DNA hairpin structures at the end of each gene segment.

5) Artemis enzymes cleave these hairpins to yield overhangs of single-stranded DNA.

6) DNA repair enzymes recognise this single stranded DNA and either delete the overhanging nucleotides, or extend the opposite strand by filling it with complementary (P) nucleotides.

7) Further nucleotides (N nucleotides) may be randomly added in the gap between the two strands.

8) The DNA strands are joined together again.

9) 3’ enhancers that were once far away from the gene promoter are now much closer because a lot of the intervening DNA has been removed, and this causes activation of transcription to produce mRNA.

![](image_rsrc4D8.jpg)

The Rag1/Rag2 complex recognises recombination signal sequences that flank segments

In order to successfully recombine gene segments related to TCR and Ig genes, but not other genes in the genome, the gene segments must be marked with specific sequence tags. These are called recombination signal sequences, and they are composed of three elements: (i) a conserved 7 base sequence called heptamer, (ii) a 12 or 23 base pair spacer composed of any sequence (equivalent to either one or two twists of the DNA helix), and (iii) a conserved 9 base sequence called nonamer. Because these DNA sequence motifs occur only within Ig and TCR gene loci, but not elsewhere in the genome, recombination does not occur in other locations in the genome.

The enzyme that recognises these DNA sequences is called V(D)J recombinase, which is a tetramer composed of the proteins Rag1 and Rag2. Rag proteins are only expressed in developing lymphocytes, but not in other cells. Together, these proteins recognise the recombination signal sequences, bind the DNA in these locations (presumably at randomly selected gene segments) and cause the cleavage of the DNA, while at the same time causing the formation of hairpin structures at the ends of each gene segment. Notably, a 12 bp spacer can only be brought together with a 23 bp spacer on the partner gene segment, and vice versa. This rule prevents inappropriate joining of segments that should not be joined together (such as in Ig heavy chains, V to J while missing out D).

Hairpin cleavage is followed by junctional nucleotide insertion and deletion

The next step in the process is to break the single stranded DNA hairpin structure that forms at the ends of the chosen gene segments. The enzyme that performs this task is an endonuclease called artemis. However, the point in the hairpin where artemis cleaves the DNA is somewhat random, and often one of the resulting two free ends is longer than the other (Figure 10.4). This leads to the formation of DNA ‘overhangs’, which are short sections of single-stranded DNA that do not have a complementary DNA strand opposite. These overhangs are recognised as damaged DNA by the repair enzymes that are present in any cell. Some of these enzymes will remove excess nucleotides from the ends of the DNA. Others may fill in the overhang by attaching complementary nucleotides to extend the shorter strand. These complementary nucleotides are called P nucleotides. Additionally, the enzyme terminal deoxynucleotidyl transferase (TdT) may insert up to 20 completely random nucleotides into the gap between the two ends. These randomly inserted nucleotides are referred to as N nucleotides. Once nucleotides have been added or removed from the junction between the gene segments, the DNA is covalently joined together again by DNA repair enzymes common to all cells. Thus, genetic recombination makes use of enzymes that are unique to developing lymphocytes (such as Rag1 and Rag2), and also DNA repair enzymes, which are common to all cells.

![](image_rsrc4D9.jpg)

This process of junctional insertion and deletion of nucleotides results in far greater sequence diversity than can be generated by combining intact gene segments alone. Notably, the part of the Ig or TCR gene that codes for CDR3 covers the regions of junctional diversity. This is why CDR3 has much greater diversity than the other CDRs.

Recall from Chapter 8 that the variable part of the antibody molecule contains 3 hypervariable regions, which correspond to the 3 amino acid loops that extend from the antibody to contact antigen. These are called the complementarity determining regions (CDRs). CDR3 is the most variable and also the loop that does most of the work of binding to antigen.
