Notably, CDR1 and CDR2 are coded for solely by the V-regions while CDR3 is coded for by the region of DNA covering the junctions between V, D and J segments. This is why the number of possible CDR3 amino acid sequences is so vast (i.e., because this region of the DNA is where most of the diversity occurs during the generation of the receptor gene).

Random nucleotide insertion is prone to frame-shift errors

Because the process of N and P nucleotide insertion is effectively random, two major difficulties frequently occur when attempting to express the newly created gene.

The first arises because the genetic code is based on the reading of triplets of nucleotides (a codon) and converting these into one amino acid in the developing protein chain. This means that for every amino acid in the protein to be correct, they must always begin at multiples of 3 from the start of the gene. If there is a one or 2 base insertion, this causes the remainder of the gene sequence to be out of frame. For example, consider the DNA sequence ATG|CAT|TTA|TCT. This codes for four amino acids in this order: Methionine (also START), then histidine, then leucine, then serine. However, imagine that a single nucleotide, let’s say a T, is inserted just after the CAT. Now the DNA sequence reads: ATG|CAT|TTT|ATC|T. The first two amino acids are correctly read (methionine and histidine), but the next two are now converted to phenylalanine, and isoleucine. If this were part of a longer sequence, all subsequent amino acids could also be changed, possibly even to stop codons, because of this so-called change of frame.

This means that if the number of nucleotides inserted (or deleted) from the junction between two gene segments is not a multiple of 3, then the remainder of the gene will code for nonsense amino acids that will not bear any resemblance to an Ig or TCR protein, and likely will not fold properly. As discussed in earlier chapters, B-cells or T-cells that fail to generate a receptor will die by apoptosis. This potential for frameshift errors is the main reason why so many developing B- and T-cells fail to generate a functional receptor.

The second problem that occurs is that of the 64 possible 3 nucleotide sequences (codons), 3 code for a STOP codon. This means that many of the newly generated genes will contain STOP codons in the middle of the junctional sequences. When read by the ribosome, this causes premature release of the protein, which is therefore not complete and non-functional. As above, such events also lead to the failure to generate useful receptors and ultimately apoptosis of developing B-cells or T-cells.

Allelic exclusion switches off expression of TCR or Ig genes from one of the two inherited chromosomes

As described above, it seems likely that the majority of attempts to form antigen receptor genes will fail. Fortunately, the odds are somewhat improved by the fact that we each carry two copies of the non-sex chromosomes. This means that every lymphocyte has two loci, one from each parent, on which to attempt to generate a usable receptor chain. However, the fact that there are two available loci (called alleles) to choose from also presents a problem. If both are expressed at the same time, this could lead to two different antigen receptors being expressed on the cell surface, and the risk of autoimmunity may increase. To address this, a mechanism is in place to switch off expression of the allele on the second chromosome if the chromosome which first began the rearrangement process successfully yields a functional receptor chain. This is called allelic exclusion.

For example, if a developing B-cell successfully generates a heavy chain that leads to a functional pre-BCR, the signals received from the pre-BCR cause the cell to permanently shut down further recombination of heavy chain genes at the other available chromosome. Thus, only one heavy chain gene is expressed, and the other heavy chain remains incompletely rearranged and is not expressed. By similar mechanisms, only one of the parental k or λ light chain genes is expressed. In other words, the light chains are also allelically excluded.

The TCR β chain is also allelically excluded by signals the cell receives from the successful generation of a functional pre-TCR. However, for reasons that remain unclear, the TCR α chain gene is rarely allelically excluded. For this reason, a considerable proportion of circulating T-cells express two different α chains (although it is likely that only one of these is able to pair with the single β chain to form a functional receptor).

Light chain isotype exclusion

In a manner similar to allelic exclusion, B-cells are also limited to expression of either k or λ light chains, but not both simultaneously. The developing B-cell attempts to rearrange the k light chain locus first. If this is successful and a functional BCR is formed, the signalling that results from this receptor causes the shutdown of λ rearrangement. This is called light chain isotype exclusion. Only if the k light chain recombination fails to yield a useful receptor is λ rearrangement allowed to proceed. If both alleles of k, and then both alleles of λ also fail to rearrange successfully, the developing B-cell will not be able to make a functional BCR and will die by apoptosis.

B-cell receptor editing

As discussed in Chapter 7, developing B-cells that bind too strongly to self antigens receive signals that cause them to be deleted by apoptosis. However, before that happens, self-reactive B-cells have one last opportunity to rearrange their light chain gene to generate a BCR that is not self-reactive. This process is called B-cell receptor editing. Briefly, the recognition of self antigens by a BCR with high affinity can cause the developing B-cell to switch on Rag1 and Rag2 once again. Even though the light chain (typically the k light chain) has already been successfully recombined, it is acted upon again by the Rag enzymes to choose different V and J regions. This process often changes the specificity of the BCR sufficiently that it no longer recognises self-antigen, and the B-cell survives to continue its development.

Summary

1) The adaptive immune system uses millions of different receptors that can specifically bind to and initiate responses to millions of different antigens

2) The B-cell receptor (BCR) and T-cell receptor (TCR) genes are not functional in the genome of any cell other than mature lymphocytes

3) BCR and TCR genes must be generated by somatic recombination, which is the editing and splicing together of small sections of the germline chromosomal DNA selected at random

4) This process is referred to as V(D)J recombination

5) Somatic recombination occurs only in developing T- and B-lymphocytes

6) The variable domains of TCR β and δ chains, and the Ig heavy chain, are generated by the random joining together of short gene segments termed V, D and J, of which there are many copies in each TCR and Ig locus

7) The variable domains of TCR α and γ chains, and the Ig light chains (k and λ), are generated by the random joining together of V and J segments (but not D)

8) The number of different receptors that can be generated solely on the basis of choices of V, D and J gene segments is called the combinatorial diversity

9) In the junctions between gene segments, additional diversity (called junctional diversity) is introduced by adding or removing nucleotides from the ends of the DNA segments

10) The nucleotides that are added which are complementary to existing DNA are called P nucleotides, and the nucleotides which are randomly inserted between the gene segments are called N nucleotides

11) Many junctional alterations lead to non-functional proteins because of frame-shift errors or the generation of premature STOP codons

12) Developing lymphocytes that fail to generate functional receptor chain genes do not receive survival signals and die by apoptosis

13) We each carry two copies of each chromosome, so if developing T- or B-cells fail to make a receptor chain from one chromosome, they can attempt to make a receptor from the second chromosome

14) Allelic exclusion is the process by which successful generation of a receptor chain gene on one chromosome causes recombination and expression from the other chromosome to cease

15) Allelic exclusion thus ensures receptor chain genes from only one of the two available chromosomes is expressed, and therefore the cell expresses receptor specific for only one antigen (TCR-α is an exception for this rule)

16) B-cells attempt to rearrange the k light chain locus first, but should this fail to generate a functional receptor, it will then attempt to rearrange the λ light chain locus

17) The V, D and J segments are marked out in each of the receptor loci by recombination signal sequences, which are composed of a conserved 7 base pair sequence (called heptamer), a gap of 12 or 23 nucleotides of any type, and a conserved 9 base sequence (called nonamer)

18) The recombination signal sequences are recognised by the V(D)J recombinase complex which is a tetramer made up of the proteins Rag1 and Rag2

19) The Rag1/Rag2 complex causes the formation of hairpins at the ends of the cleaved gene segments, these are then opened up by an enzyme called artemis and the actions of terminal deoxynucleotidyl transferase TdT, together with DNA repair enzymes common to most cells, add or remove nucleotides between the gene segments

20) Because of this nucleotide insertion/deletion step, the junctions between gene segments have immense sequence variability, which yields great amino acid sequence diversity in the CDR3 region of the Ig or TCR protein

21) CDR3 is the CDR that plays the most central role in antigen recognition and binding

22) B-cells that generate receptors that bind too strongly to self antigens may undergo B-cell receptor editing, wherein Rag1 and Rag2 are expressed once again and the light chain is recombined again in an attempt to generate a non-self binding receptor

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V(D)J_recombination>

Roth DB. V(D)J recombination: mechanism, errors, and fidelity. Microbiol Spectr 2:10 (2014) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5089068/>

---

# Chapter 11: B-cell activation

When the host is exposed to a new antigen for the first time, a humoral, or antibody response, develops by the activation of naïve B-cells. These activated cells can ultimately become short-lived plasma cells, which secrete antibody early in the first response to antigen, long-lived plasma cells which provide lasting protection against re-infection, or memory B-cells, which enable the host to mount rapid humoral responses to the same antigen if it is encountered again in the future. Antibody responses can be categorised as those that are dependent on T-cell help (T-dependent) and those that are not (T-independent, or TI). Only proteins, but not other types of antigen, initiate T-dependent antibody responses. T-dependent responses generate high affinity antibodies that bind more tightly to antigen as time passes, and promote both heavy chain isotype class-switching and the generation of long-lived memory cells. By contrast, responses to other types of antigen such as polysaccharides show limited memory and class-switching, and no affinity maturation. Because the T-dependent response to protein antigens is so critical to host defence and vaccine design, this chapter will focus mainly on the mechanisms by which B-lymphocytes become activated to secrete antibodies against proteins.

T-dependent and T-independent antigens evoke qualitatively different antibody responses

Broadly speaking, there are two different types of antigen that may evoke an antibody response: T-dependent antigens and T-independent (TI) antigens. Only proteins can serve as T-dependent antigens, and B-cell activation in response to this type of antigen can only occur with help from CD4+ helper T-cells. By contrast, T-independent antigens, such as lipids and polysaccharides, trigger B-cell activation without any requirement for T-cell help.

Notably, the antibody responses generated in response to each type of antigen are quite different. For example, repeated exposure to T-dependent antigens results in the generation of antibodies that have improved binding to antigen, as a result of a process called affinity maturation (described later). Heavy chain isotype (class)-switching also usually occurs in response to T-dependent antigens. T-dependent antigens also generally yield a strong memory B-cell response.

By contrast, antibody responses to TI antigens are mainly of the IgM isotype. The plasma cells that are generated by TI antigens are short-lived, and memory or class-switching is limited in these responses. Table 11.1 summarises the major differences between T-dependent and TI antibody responses.

Table 11.1: Key differences between T-dependent and T-independent antibody responses

![](image_rsrc4DA.jpg)

Common features of T-dependent and T-independent B-cell activation include proliferation, clonal expansion and plasma cell formation

Naïve follicular B-cells recirculate between blood and lymph nodes for several months seeking antigen. The majority of these do not meet antigen and die by apoptosis. However, those naïve B-cells that do encounter antigen for which they are specific may be activated, resulting in the following possible routes of differentiation.

Whether a B-cell is activated with T-cell help, or with other stimuli (discussed later), the following common features of cellular activation are observed. First, the responding cell begins to proliferate rapidly, doubling in number every 6 to 12 hours. This means that within days of antigenic stimulation, a single responding B-cell can give rise to an expanded clone of thousands of cells, all expressing the same antigen receptor.

Another common feature of both T-dependent and T-independent responses is that some of the daughter cells produced in both responses give rise to short-lived IgM secreting plasma cells. Plasma cells are distinct from resting B-lymphocytes in a number of ways. Morphologically, naïve B-lymphocytes are small, spherical cells that are composed largely of nucleus, contain little cytoplasm and have limited capacity to produce and secrete protein. By contrast, their differentiation into plasma cells results in dramatic changes to their cellular structure. The cytoplasm becomes enlarged and distended on one side of the cell to accommodate an expansive endoplasmic reticulum and golgi apparatus, which develop as plasma cells specialise in the secretion of antibody. This gives the plasma cell a characteristic pear shape that is visible down the microscope. Notably, naïve B-cells express only the membrane form of antibody, since their antigen receptor contains a transmembrane section that tethers it to the cell membrane. Altered RNA processing enables the plasma cell to express the secreted form of antibody.

Although these relatively early IgM antibody responses do contribute to the elimination of infections (particularly those caused by capsulated bacteria), they are of relatively limited use in the context of some other infections. First, the antibodies which are produced at this stage are generally of low affinity. This is because their CDR amino acid sequence is not yet optimal for binding to antigen. Next, although the IgM antibody isotype is very good at initiating complement activation, there are a number of different types of infection where a different antibody class (e.g. IgG1 or IgE) may be more appropriate. Finally, for an efficient memory to be developed, long-lived plasma cells need to be generated. All of these limitations are addressed by the actions of CD4+ T-cells in responses to protein antigens, as will be discussed later.

Initial stimulation of BCR-signalling by antigen requires Igα and Igβ

When naïve B-cells encounter antigen which binds to their receptor, the first signals that the cell receives are derived from the cross-linking of their antigen receptor. The cytoplasmic domains of the membrane forms of IgM and IgD molecules they express are too small to initiate intracellular signalling (consisting of only three amino acids). Instead, two co-receptor molecules called Igα and Igβ serve this function, as they are both transmembrane molecules with a single extracellular Ig domain and an immunoreceptor tyrosine-based activation motif (ITAM) intracellular domain. These proteins form a disulphide-linked heterodimer which is usually dispersed at low density over the B-cell membrane.

Cross-linking of IgM or IgD by antigen, however, leads to the clustering and close physical proximity of Igα and Igβ heterodimers in the cell membrane. This causes recruitment of Src family kinases which phosphorylate, and thereby activate, the ITAM domains of Igα and Igβ. This in turn causes the activation of a number of key signalling intermediates in the B-cell, including phospholipase C (PLC)-γ, protein kinase C (PKC), Ras and Rac GTPases. These signalling pathways co-operate to activate key transcription factors required for B-cell activation, including NFAT, NF-kB and AP-1 (Figure 11.1).

![](image_rsrc4DB.jpg)

CR2 recognises complement products associated with antigen and promotes B-cell activation

A common feature of many of the microbial antigens that reach the lymph node is that they are frequently attached to large fragments of the microbe, such as cell wall fragments, vesicles or even intact organisms. This feature promotes the cross-linking of BCRs on responsive B-cells and helps the immune system to differentiate between harmless antigens, such as soluble proteins derived from food which are not attached to larger structures and therefore cannot promote cross-linking, and microbe-associated molecules which require an antibody response.

A second feature of this common association with larger microbial fragments is that the latter are often coated by products of complement activation. Recall from Chapter 4 that the alternative and lectin complement pathways are specifically activated by microbial cell surfaces. Complement activation results in the covalent attachment of complement protein breakdown products, such as C3b and its breakdown product C3d, to the microbial surface. B-cells express the receptor for C3d, complement receptor 2 (CR2, also called CD21). When B-cells receive a signal from the BCR in conjunction with co-stimulation via CR2, the activation response is greatly amplified (Figure 11.2). The presence of complement products on structures containing antigen therefore marks those antigens as suitable targets for the generation of an antibody response.

![](image_rsrc4DC.jpg)

T-dependent antibody responses require antigen-specific CD4+ effector T-cells

Antibody responses against protein antigens are dependent on the presence of CD4+ helper T-cells that also recognise those antigens. B-cells internalise antigen that has been captured by their antigen receptor and process the ingested proteins to yield peptides (as discussed in more depth in Chapter 9). These peptides are then displayed on MHC-II molecules on the surface of the B-cell. The naïve follicular B-cell can only be fully activated if a CD4+ helper T-cell recognises that peptide/MHC-II complex and provides co-stimulatory signals to the B-cell.

The activating signals given to the B-cell from the T-cell come from two main sources: membrane protein interactions and cytokines. Activated helper T-cells express the molecule CD40L on their surface. This interacts with the CD40 receptor expressed on the B-cell surface, triggering signalling pathways that results in activation of transcription factors such as NF-kB and AP-1, which promote B-cell activation. At the same time, the activated T-cell secretes cytokines that reach high concentrations in the synapse between the two cells, but which remain at relatively low concentrations at the distance of other neighbouring cells. These cytokines are recognised by cytokine receptors on the B-cell and also promote cellular activation (Figure 11.3).

![](image_rsrc4DD.jpg)

Activated T-cells and activated B-cells migrate to meet at the follicle boundary

As described above, antibody responses to a protein antigen require direct physical contact between a B-cell that is responsive to that antigen, and a CD4+ T-cell that is also responsive to the same antigen. If you think about it, achieving this is an incredible feat. Remember that during the first stages of an immune response to an antigen, perhaps only cell per million of the circulating pool of naïve B-cells or naïve T-cells will respond to that antigen. These cells could be anywhere in the body, and therefore need to somehow meet with the antigen to be activated. Moreover, these two different cells need to find each other and make direct physical contact.

The first challenge, i.e. that of allowing naïve cells to meet antigen, has been discussed in earlier chapters, and is simply mediated by the drainage of tissue fluids through small structures called lymph nodes that tend to trap and retain antigens. Because naïve B-cells and T-cells constantly circulate between the blood and the lymph nodes patrolling for antigen, this maximises the opportunity for a passing lymphocyte to encounter antigen.

The next challenge is to enable the activated B-cell to meet with a T-cell that has already been activated by the same antigen. This is achieved by the migration of activated B-cells and T-cells towards the boundary between the T-cell zone and follicle of the lymph node (Figure 11.4). The following summarises the major steps involved in this response:

1) Antigen drains to the lymph node, where it is taken up by dendritic cells (DCs)

2) The DCs are activated by microbial products, increasing their capacity to present peptides through increased expression of MHC-II and co-stimulatory membrane proteins

3) The DC maintains presentation of the antigenic peptide on its surface until a naïve CD4+ T-cell specific for this peptide eventually encounters the DC

4) The CD4+ T-cell becomes activated by the peptide and co-stimulatory molecules

5) T-cell activation causes increased expression of chemokine receptors on the T-cell surface (such as CXCR5) which cause it to migrate towards the follicle, where the B-cells reside

6) At the same time, a naïve B-cell may have encountered the same antigen in the follicle and become activated

7) The activated B-cell internalises the antigen that is bound to its receptor, then processes the protein and presents the resulting peptides on MHC-II molecules

8) Activation causes the B-cell to change the expression of chemokine receptors on its cell surface, such as increasing CCR7, causing it to migrate towards the T-cell zone

9) At the boundary between the T-cell zone and the follicle, it is likely that activated B-cells may encounter activated T-cells migrating in the opposite direction

10) If these two cells are responsive to the same antigen, they will remain in contact and activate each other further by the signals described above

![](image_rsrc4DE.jpg)

B-cell responses develop initially outside the follicle, and then later in germinal centres within the follicle

The activated B-cell, now receiving further activation signals from a partnering CD4+ T-cell, begins to proliferate. The daughter cells arising from this proliferation can now develop the primary antibody responses in either or both of two locations: (i) outside the follicle (called extrafollicular B-cell activation) and (ii) within the follicle, in organised collections of specialised cells called germinal centres (Figure 11.5).

![](image_rsrc4DF.jpg)

The extrafollicular focus develops in the medulla of the lymph node, or between the T-cell zone and red pulp of the spleen, and consists of B-cells and T-cells which have been activated by the same antigen. Here, some of the B-cells develop into short-lived plasma cells that undergo a limited degree of class switching, and secrete antibody to begin the humoral immune response against the antigen they have encountered. This antibody is among the earliest to be generated during the humoral response (within 3-5 days after encountering antigen). However, the antibodies generated in this manner are of relatively low affinity since somatic hypermutation in this location is quite limited. Moreover, B-cell responses in this location do not lead to the generation of memory cells, or long-lived plasma cells. This type of B-cell activation occurs in germinal centres.

Germinal centres are the main site of B-cell affinity maturation, class-switching and memory cell formation

Germinal centres develop within the B-cell follicle itself several days after the extracellular foci have developed. They are formed by the return of some of the progeny of the original B-cell that responded to antigen and met with a T-cell at the follicle boundary. These cells proliferate rapidly, producing a central zone which stains lightly with histological dyes - this is called the germinal centre. At the same time, some of the CD4+ T-cells that were activated at the follicle boundary differentiate into a specialised form of CD4+ T-helper cell, called a follicular helper T-cell (TFH). These TFH cells also migrate with the B-cells into the follicle, where they direct the activation and differentiation of the B-cells as they develop in the germinal centres, particularly via release of the cytokine IL-21, which is required for germinal centre formation.

Lymph node follicles, as well as being rich in naïve B-cells, are also rich in follicular dendritic cells. These unusual cells are unrelated to classical or plasmacytoid dendritic cells, as they do not express MHC-II, cannot present peptide to CD4+ T-cells and are not derived from the bone marrow (Figure 11.6). Instead, they express receptors, such as complement and Fc receptors that tend to capture intact antigen. This intact antigen is then displayed on the surface of the FDC to assist in the activation of naïve B-cells, and also to facilitate their development in germinal centres.

![](image_rsrc4DG.jpg)

Germinal centres can be further subdivided into two zones: a dark zone and a light zone, depending on how they stain with commonly used histochemical dyes. The dark zone is where B-cells proliferate rapidly and conduct preliminary class-switching, and the light zone is where they migrate to after proliferation, to encounter FDCs, antigen and TFH cells, which together initiate the processes of affinity maturation and differentiation into memory and plasma cells, as will now be discussed (Figure 11.7).

![](image_rsrc4DH.jpg)

T-helper cells play a key role in heavy chain isotype (antibody class)-switching

As mentioned in earlier chapters, different types of antibody with different types of heavy chain, are required to combat different types of infection. As naïve B-cells express only IgM and IgD, they must receive signals that cause them to switch production of their heavy chain from μ and δ to other isotypes, such as γ, α and ε. This process is directed largely by the types of cytokines released by TH and TFH cells, which in turn is driven by the type of pathogen the T-cell has encountered. It is not entirely clear which cytokines lead to which class-switching events in man, but as a general rule in mice IFN-γ (a Th1 cytokine associated with viruses and intracellular bacteria) promotes IgG production, and IL-4 (a Th2 cytokine associated with helminths) promotes IgE production. In the mucosal tissues, high levels of cytokines present in this location such as BAFF, APRIL and TGF-β favour the production of IgA, even in the absence of T-cell help. Generally speaking, however, B-cells that do not receive T-cell help do not class-switch and secrete only IgM.

AID is required for heavy chain isotype (antibody class)-switching

Recall from earlier chapters that one of the key activating signals given to the B-cell by the activated T-cell is the physical interaction between CD40L on the surface of the T-cell and CD40 on the surface of the B-cell. This promotes expression of the enzyme activation-induced deaminase (AID), which is required for B-cell class switching. Figure 11.8 shows the germline organisation of the different heavy chain genes. In B-cells that have not undergone class-switching, the Cμ (constant section mu) heavy chain gene is closest to the V-region of the antibody gene. However, a DNA sequence called the switch region is present just to the 5’ side of all heavy chain genes. Unlike the V-D-J recombination recognition sites which are short, highly conserved sequences, the heavy chain switch regions are longer (1-10 kb) and less conserved (consisting mainly of CG rich repeating DNA elements).

![](image_rsrc4DJ.jpg)

Depending on the cytokine mixture the activated B-cell is exposed to, different switch regions are brought together. The mechanisms of this are complex, but briefly involve the expression of an RNA molecule that binds to the genomic DNA within the switch region, causing a loop of single stranded DNA to be exposed. Cytosine (C) nucleotides in this region are then converted to uracil (U) nucleotides by the enzyme AID. These U bases are then removed from the DNA by repair enzymes, resulting eventually in a double stranded break in the DNA at the switch region site. The broken DNA at each of two switch regions is then brought together and joined by DNA repair enzymes. The intervening DNA is looped out and excised from the genome.

This process results in the relocation of a new heavy chain gene segment close to the V-region gene segments, such that the RNA transcripts that are formed now consist of the original V-region and the new C-region (Figure 11.8). In this way, the antibodies produced by a B-cell before and after class-switching have exactly the same V-region, and respond to exactly the same antigens, but different C-regions.

One of the key principles of class-switching is that it cannot be reversed. Once the intervening DNA containing some heavy chain genes has been excised, it cannot be re-inserted into the chromosomal DNA. This means that an individual B-cell can only class-switch from a heavy chain gene that is closer to the V-region to one that is further away, (i.e. in humans for example, in the direction of IgM → IgG3 → IgG1 → IgA1 → IgG2 → IgG4 → IgE1 → IgA2). Notably, B-cells can (and often do) skip several steps in this list (e.g. IgM → IgE1), in one step. It is also useful to bear in mind that a B-cell clone is a collection of hundreds of cells, and not all of these necessarily class-switch in the same way. For example, some of the progeny of the original responding B-cell may encounter T-cell help that promotes class-switching to IgG, while others encounter different T-cells, perhaps in different locations in the body, that promote class-switching to IgA. This means that an antibody response to a particular antigen can be composed of multiple isotypes (e.g. IgG and IgA concurrently, in the example just given).

Somatic hypermutation of Ig V-regions

The antibodies which are produced early in the primary response to antigen are generally of relatively low affinity. This means that while their V-regions do bind to antigen, the binding is not as good as it could be. Often, all that’s needed to improve the binding of the antibody to antigen is to change two or three key amino acids in the CDRs that contact the antigen. The process that has evolved to carry out this editing of the BCR is called somatic hypermutation (Figure 11.9).

![](image_rsrc4DK.jpg)

In the dark zone of the germinal centre, B-cells receive signals from CD40L expressed by nearby T-cells which induces expression of the enzyme AID. This enzyme, together with a range of DNA repair enzymes and other mechanisms that remain poorly understood, promotes a very high rate of mutation in the V-region of Ig genes. This in turn leads to the production of many hundreds of B-cells, each expressing a BCR with a slightly different amino acid sequence to that of the precursor cell. Somatic hypermutation is entirely dependent on T-cell help and CD40:CD40L signalling, and therefore does not occur in T-independent responses. Somatic mutation also occurs to a limited extent in extrafollicular foci, but is mostly initiated in the germinal centres.

Affinity maturation selects and expands those B-cells with antibody that binds more tightly to antigen

Some of these newly edited genes give rise to antibodies that bind antigen better than the BCRs of precursor cells, and some will bind antigen less well. To select for the B-cells expressing better antibodies, the B-cells move out of the dark zone into the light zone to undergo affinity maturation. The light zone contains many follicular DCs, which capture and present intact antigen, and TFH cells, which secrete IL-21. The increased concentration of IL-21 in this location causes the B-cells to become prone to apoptosis. In order to avoid this apoptotic fate, they must receive strong survival signals from their BCRs. Only B-cells expressing BCRs that bind well to antigen receive these signals and survive, while those B-cells that do not bind well to antigen are likely to die. Early in the immune response, there is quite a lot of antigen in the lymph node, and it is easy for B-cells to survive this step. However, as time passes, less and less antigen is available in the germinal centre (due to normal clearance mechanisms and ingestion for presentation by various cells). This means that as time passes, the competition for binding to antigen becomes more intense. Eventually, only the B-cells that bind very tightly to antigen hold onto the antigen and survive, and the other B-cells, even if they bind to antigen a little less tightly, cannot compete and are eventually selected out. By this means, the antibodies produced by a B-cell clone become better at binding to a specific antigen over time, and the affinity of these antibodies increases (Figure 11.10).

This process is not always a one-time event. B-cells may re-enter the dark zone to proliferate, and then return to the light zone for further affinity maturation, several times. Eventually, some of these B-cells receive signals that cause them to differentiate into antibody secreting plasma cells, or memory cells. Affinity maturation is also not limited to the primary response to antigen, it may also occur when memory B-cells encounter the same antigen again in secondary responses months or years later.

![](image_rsrc4DM.jpg)

Differentiation of activated B-cells into memory and plasma cells

As mentioned above, activated B-cells can differentiate into plasma cells, which secrete soluble antibody, in the extrafollicular focus relatively early in the antibody response. These early antibodies aid in the early phases of defence against infection and are dominant in primary antibody responses. However, these plasma cells are short-lived, and do not offer the host long-lasting protection against re-infection.

Longer lived plasma cells develop mainly from T-dependent responses in germinal centres. Here, B-cells undergoing affinity maturation and class-switching also receive signals from their BCRs, their PRRs, cytokines and from nearby TFH and TH cells that together promote their differentiation into either memory B-cells or plasma cells. The plasma cells formed by this process are unlike those that develop in extrafollicular foci, as they gain the ability to migrate through the blood to the bone marrow, where they receive survival signals, such as BAFF-family cytokines, that enable them to live for decades. After arriving in the bone marrow, these long-lived plasma cells do not patrol the body any further but remain in that location, constitutively secreting soluble antibody that permeates throughout the body and affords the host protection against re-infection. The antibodies produced by the plasma cells are of the same class expressed by the B-cell that left the germinal centre, with the only difference being the lack of a transmembrane domain in the heavy chain, allowing the antibody to be secreted.

By contrast, memory B-cells do not migrate back to the bone marrow. Instead, some remain in the lymph node in which they were generated and others recirculate between the different lymph nodes of the body, seeking antigen, for years after the first exposure to antigen. Memory B-cells express the same antibody V-region and heavy chain isotype that was expressed by the B-cell in the germinal centre just before their release. Like long-lived plasma cells, memory B-cell formation is largely dependent on T-cell help, and therefore occurs in response to protein antigens, but not other types of antigen.

Secondary antibody responses are stronger, faster and more effective than primary antibody responses to T-dependent antigens

When the host is exposed to an antigen it has not encountered previously, all of the above processes take place to generate an antibody response by the activation of naïve B-cells and T-cells. This is called the primary response. The antibodies produced during the primary response are normally sufficient to clear the infection, but this process is rather slow and the antibodies that are produced are not particularly effective, which means the host can be quite ill for some time.

By contrast, if the host encounters the same antigen on a second, or subsequent, occasion, the antibody response that is generated is rapid, leads to higher concentrations of specific antibody and these antibodies are of higher affinity and more frequently class-switched. This is called the secondary response. The secondary response to antigen is quicker and more effective than the primary response because the antibodies generated in the secondary response come mainly from memory B-cells. Because many thousands of memory B-cells may be generated during the primary response, there is no need to wait for the proliferation of a very small number of naïve B-cells. Moreover, time-consuming steps in naïve B-cell activation, including class-switching and affinity maturation, are also neatly avoided because memory-B-cells have already class-switched and undergone affinity maturation. Thus, the secondary humoral response generally involves antibodies which are more class-switched and of higher affinity than the antibodies generated during the primary antibody response (See Chapter 8, Figure 8.7).

Notably, during any secondary response to antigen, another primary response, including the activation of naïve B-cells and T-cells to that antigen, also occurs. Additionally, when memory B-cells are activated by antigen during a secondary response, they can undergo further affinity maturation and class-switching. This means that the antibodies which are produced by the host tend to be of higher affinity and are more effective after each encounter with the same antigen. These high affinity antibodies are often sufficient to prevent infection of cells by specific bacteria or viruses.

Responses to T-independent antigens

The above processes refer to antibody responses developed against protein antigens, which is to say, they are T-helper cell-dependent responses. However, antibody responses can also be generated in response to other types of molecule, and this occurs without T-cell help.

Generally speaking, the types of antigen that promote T-independent humoral responses are polysaccharides, glycolipids and nucleic acids, particularly those with repeating epitopes. While follicular B-cells are the main players in T-dependent antibody responses, B-1 B-cells and marginal zone B-cells are the key players in TI antibody responses to non-protein antigens. These B-cells may be activated by a number of stimuli including (i) the repeating epitopes of TI-antigens (ii) TLR-signalling induced by microbial PAMPs that are often encountered at the same time as the antigen, (iii) complement C3d which is often deposited on the antigen surface and (iv) cytokines from nearby activated macrophages and DCs.

T-cells are not involved in responses to non-protein antigens because they cannot be processed and presented on MHC molecules. For this reason, B-cell activation in response to TI antigens is characterised by very little class-switching and memory cell formation and no affinity maturation. Instead, short-lived plasma cells are generated, which tend to secrete only low-affinity IgM. Occasionally, some limited class-switching can occur during TI-responses, driven by cytokines released from nearby cells, such as macrophages or DCs. Polysaccharide antigens can lead to the production of IgG2 in man (or IgG3 in mice), for example. In the gut, IgA may be produced in mucosal tissues due to the high concentration of TGF-β in this location. Major sites for TI-responses include the mucosal tissues, peritoneum and spleen.

Summary

1) The humoral response is the production of antibodies in response to antigen

2) The response generated when antigen is encountered for the first time is called the primary response, and the response to second or subsequent exposures to the same antigen is called the secondary response

3) Antibody responses to protein require help from CD4+ T-cells, so are referred to as T-dependent responses

4) Antibody responses to polysaccharides, glycolipids and nucleic acids are T-independent (TI), because these molecules cannot be presented on MHC-II

5) Follicular B-cells mainly respond to T-dependent antigens, and B-1 and marginal zone B-cells mainly respond to T-independent antigens

6) B-cell activation by T-dependent or TI-antigens leads to proliferation, clonal expansion and the generation of short-lived plasma cells that secrete soluble IgM

7) The BCR of naïve B-cells consists of the membrane form of IgM and IgD, and the co-receptors Igα and Igβ, which possess cytoplasmic signalling domains that are required for cellular activation by antigen

8) BCR signalling by antigen is generally mediated by cross-linking, caused by multivalency of the antigen

9) B-cells receive additional activation signals from the complement product C3d binding to CR2, from PRR recognition of PAMPs and from cytokines released from nearby cells

10) Activated follicular B-cells migrate to the outer edge of the lymph node follicle, where they meet recently activated CD4+ T-cells migrating in the opposite direction, and the two cells meet at the follicle boundary

11) Activated CD4+ T-cells respond to peptide presented by B-cell MHC-II, giving the presenting B-cells further activation signals, including cytokines and the binding of CD40L on the T-cell surface with CD40 on the B-cell surface, which are critical for further B-cell development

12) Some of the B-cells responding outside the follicle remain there, to become short-lived plasma cells in organised collections of B-cells and TH-cells called extrafollicular foci

13) Some days later, other activated B-cells return to the follicle with some of the activated T-helper cells which become follicular T-helper (TFH) cells, and together these cells form a germinal centre within the B-cell follicle

14) The germinal centre contains a dark zone, where B-cells proliferate, and a light zone, where they encounter antigen, TFH cells and follicular dendritic cells, which are specialised non-myeloid DCs that present intact, unprocessed antigen to B-cells

15) TFH cells promote class-switching, which replaces one heavy chain gene segment (e.g. Cμ) with another (e.g. Cγ1) by looping out and reconnecting segments of genomic DNA, to change the isotype of antibody expressed by that B-cell

16) Class-switching requires the enzyme activation-induced deaminase (AID), the expression of which is induced by CD40:CD40L ligation

17) Class-switching therefore generally requires T-cell help, however some limited class switching can occur without T-cell help in the presence of cytokines from activated macrophages and DCs, or TGF-β in the gut mucosa

18) Somatic hypermutation is the name given to the process that causes many mutations to occur in the V-region of the B-cell BCR gene, particularly in the CDR regions of the gene, resulting in some B-cells expressing antibodies with higher affinity for antigen, and some with reduced affinity for antigen

19) Somatic mutation is T-cell dependent, requires CD40:CD40L signalling, and occurs mainly in the germinal centres, but also to a limited extent in extrafollicular foci

20) Affinity maturation is the term applied to the negative selection (by apoptosis) of B-cells with antibodies that do not bind tightly to antigen, and expansion of B-cell clones with improved affinity for antigen, after somatic hypermutation

21) Affinity maturation happens largely in the light zone of the germinal centre, and B-cells can shuttle several times between the dark zone and light zone generating higher affinity antibodies as the response continues

22) B-cells responding to T-dependent antigens give rise initially to short-lived plasma cells, but later in the response can yield memory B-cells and long-lived plasma cells

23) Memory B-cells recirculate, or remain in the lymph node they were generated in for many years, and are able to respond quickly should the same antigen be encountered again

24) Long-lived plasma B-cells migrate to the bone marrow, where they receive survival signals from the local cytokines that allow them to remain alive and secrete class-switched, high affinity protective antibody into the circulation for many years

25) The primary response is developed at the first encounter to antigen, results in mainly low-affinity IgM isotype antibodies and is quite slow, taking perhaps 7-8 days to peak

26) The secondary response is developed in response to antigens that have already been encountered by the host, and because memory B-cells exist which are responsive to the antigen, the secondary response is generally characterised by higher affinity antibodies of more diverse isotypes, and as little as 2-3 days to reach a peak production of antibody which is higher than during a primary response

27) Non-protein antigens, such as polysaccharides, glycolipids and nucleic acids, promote T-independent (TI) humoral responses

28) TI responses are generally mediated by B-1 B-cells or marginal zone B-cells, and most frequently result in short-lived plasma cells releasing IgM antibodies of relatively low affinity

29) Class-switching and memory cell production is rare during TI responses, and somatic hypermutation and affinity maturation does not occur during TI responses

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humoral_immunity>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plasma_cell>

Roles of Akt and mTOR in B-cell activation: <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pubmed/22888331>

---

# Chapter 12: T-cell activation and cell-mediated immunity

To recap briefly on the roles played by T-cells in adaptive immunity, these cells are generated by haematopoiesis in the bone marrow and mature in the thymus (Chapter 7). They exist as millions of clones, each expressing a unique receptor (the T-cell receptor, or TCR) generated by genetic recombination (Chapter 10). T-cells that have not yet encountered antigen are termed naïve. Only when they meet antigen can they be activated to proliferate and differentiate into either short-lived effector cells, which enable clearance of the infection, or memory cells, which live for many years and initiate rapid responses on detection of the same antigen. Most T-cells can only be activated by protein antigens, and only when that protein has been broken down into peptides which are presented on MHC-I or MHC-II molecules by antigen-presenting cells (APC), particularly dendritic cells (Chapter 9). Because MHC-restricted responses to protein antigens are crucial to defence against many pathogens, this mode of T-cell activation and the resulting differentiation into memory cells or effector cells will be examined in detail in this chapter.

Mature DCs are required to trigger naïve T-cell activation

Naïve CD4+ and CD8+ T-cells are continually produced by the thymus from precursor cells arriving from the bone marrow. These naïve cells express receptors (TCRs) specific for an enormous range of different peptides presented on MHC-I (recognised by CD8+ cytotoxic T-cells) or on MHC-II (recognised by CD4+ helper T-cells). Naïve T-cells express chemokine receptors that cause them to circulate between the different secondary lymphoid organs and the blood, seeking antigen. Of course, the vast majority of these naïve T-cells do not encounter antigen for which they are specific, and should they not meet antigen within several weeks or months, they die by apoptosis to be replaced by new naïve T-cells from the thymus.

Although almost all nucleated cells express MHC-I, and several types of cell express MHC-II (such as B-cells or activated endothelial cells), naïve T-cells can only be activated by one type of cell - the mature dendritic cell (DC). DCs reside in essentially all tissues of the body, but are typically present at the highest densities in tissues at risk of microbial invasion, such as the skin and mucosal tissues. Here, they remain in a non-activated ‘resting’ state. Only exposure to microbial PAMPs, complement activation products or pro-inflammatory cytokines causes these DCs to become activated. This activation process causes the DC to express more MHC molecules and become much more efficient at capturing, processing and presenting antigen to T-cells. Crucially, DC activation also causes increased expression of co-stimulatory molecules, which are essential for naïve T-cell activation as described below.

To summarise the steps involved in naïve T-cell activation, during infection a pathogen may express a protein that is captured by a dendritic cell which becomes activated and presents the protein’s peptides on MHC molecules at the cell surface. By migrating to the T-cell zone of the lymph node, the activated (also termed mature) DC maximises its chances of meeting a T-cell with a receptor that recognises one of the peptides displayed from that pathogen. The T-cell that recognises the peptide physically contacts the DC and becomes activated. The T-cell then proliferates, leading to an expanded clone of T-cells all expressing the same TCR (note, there is no receptor editing in T-cells as is found in activated B-cells). The vast majority of these cells become effector T-cells of different types, which seek out sites of infection and aid the clearance of pathogens. A much smaller number of the activated T-cells become long-lived memory T-cells, which patrol the body for many years expressing the same TCR and seeking the same antigen. If memory cells encounter the antigen again, they respond quickly by proliferating and generating a new pool of short-lived effector T-cells. This is the basis of T-cell memory, as will be discussed in the next chapter. It is also the mechanism of cell mediated immunity, which is defined experimentally as the cellular (i.e. non-humoral) component that may be transplanted from one animal to another animal to confer immunity against a particular antigen or pathogen. Eventually, the effector cells die off by apoptosis, leaving a residual pool of memory T-cells, all expressing the same TCR as the original T-cell that was activated at the first exposure to antigen.

Co-stimulators are required for the activation of naïve T-cells

The majority of the tissues of the body are not patrolled by naïve T-cells. Instead, these cells recirculate continually between the blood and secondary lymphoid organs. Because activated DCs are also generally found in the secondary lymphoid organs, this means that naïve T-cells are almost always activated in secondary lymphoid organs, such as the lymph nodes or spleen. As mentioned above, naïve T-cells can only be activated by one type of cell - the mature dendritic cell. This is because although other cells may express MHC-I or MHC-II, they do not generally express surface molecules called co-stimulators, which are required for naïve T-cell activation. Naïve T-cells that encounter a peptide/MHC complex that is recognised by their receptor, but do not at the same time receive signals from co-stimulators, will not be activated and will not respond to antigen. Notably, the expression of co-stimulators is triggered during DC maturation, and resting DCs express little in the way of co-stimulatory molecules. This means that unstimulated DCs are unable to effectively stimulate activation of naïve T-cells.

The most critical co-stimulatory molecule expressed by activated DCs is B7. There are two B7 molecules, B7-1 (also called CD80) and B7-2 (also called CD86), both of which are transmembrane glycoproteins with extracellular Ig-like domains. These two proteins are recognised by CD28, which is expressed by T-cells. The recognition of B7 molecules by CD28 is not sufficient to trigger T-cell activation alone. Instead, T-cell activation requires both antigen-TCR and B7-CD28 signals to be present simultaneously (Figure 12.1).

![](image_rsrc4DN.jpg)

A further checkpoint in T-cell activation by this system is that B7 expression is typically triggered by the recognition of ‘danger’ signals by the receptors of the innate immune system. For example, the exposure of DCs to PAMPs may stimulate TLR-signalling, resulting in B7 expression. Some cytokines, such as IFN-γ, may also enhance B7 expression on APCs. This ensures that T-cell responses to antigen are only raised when it is likely that the antigen in question is associated with an infectious organism, and does not reflect a harmless protein (such as food or host-derived).

Although B7 and CD28 represent the dominant receptor pairing involved in costimulatory responses, there are other co-stimulatory molecules which also regulate T-cell activation. Interestingly, while some of these molecules enhance T-cell activation, others inhibit the response. For example, ICOS (inducible co-stimulator) expressed by T-cells recognises ICOS-L expressed by APCs to enhance T-cell activation. On the other hand, CTLA-4, expressed by T-cells, also recognises B7-1 and B7-2, but rather than promote T-cell activation, this pairing inhibits T-cell responses. Likewise, the binding of T-cell PD-1 to APC PD-L1 or PD-L2 also inhibits T-cell activation. It is thought that the balance between these pro- and anti-activation signals dictates the extent of response to antigen. Table 12.1 summarises some key examples of such molecules. We will return to these so-called checkpoint proteins in Chapter 19, as they are key targets for cancer immunotherapies.

Table 12.1: Co-stimulators and related molecules

![](image_rsrc4DP.jpg)

Formation of the immunological synapse

When the T-cell binds to an APC, a considerable portion of its membrane comes into very close proximity with the membrane of the APC. These two surface patches are held closely together by an extensive array of surface receptor pairings, including not only the TCR/MHC pairing, but also co-stimulators and their ligands, and cell adhesion molecules. This close apposition of the two membranes is often called the immunological synapse (Figure 12.1). Within the space created between these two membrane patches, a very small volume of extracellular fluid is retained. Cytokines that may be secreted by either cell into this gap reach much higher concentrations than could be achieved if the cytokines were able to diffuse freely into the wider extracellular space. This enables the cells to deliver cytokine-mediated signals to each other while having little impact on other cells nearby that are not part of the synapse. The synapse also serves as a stable platform for the formation of receptor-ligand pairings between the APC and T-cell, which enhance T-cell activation.

T-cell receptor signalling

A number of different receptor-ligand pairings between the APC and the T-cell all contribute to the intracellular signals leading to T-cell activation. We will begin by discussing the TCR itself.

The TCR is a heterodimer of two disulphide-linked, single-transmembrane proteins (α with β or γ with δ), each of which has two extracellular Ig-like domains. The intracellular domain of each TCR chain is quite limited, and therefore the TCR requires co-operation with other co-receptor molecules to enable cell signalling on binding of antigen. This is mediated mainly by the molecules CD3 and the zeta (ζ) chain, which are present in all T-cells (Figure 12.2). The zeta chain exists as a disulphide-linked homodimer of two identical chains containing multiple intracellular ITAM (immunoreceptor tyrosine activation motif) domains. The CD3 receptor is a heterodimer of ε and γ, or ε and δ chains, each of which possesses one extracellular Ig-like domain, and one intracellular ITAM domain. The functional TCR complex consists of 4 dimers: one TCR heterodimer (αβ or γδ), one ζ (zeta) chain homodimer, one εγ CD3 heterodimer, and one εδ CD3 heterodimer (Figure 12.2).

![](image_rsrc4DR.jpg)

In a manner similar to the way in which B-cell receptor (BCR) binding to multivalent antigen causes clustering of the BCR, the binding of TCR to MHC also promotes receptor clustering of the TCR complex. The CD4 or CD8 coreceptor, either of which may be expressed by individual T-cells, then helps to stabilise the interaction between TCR and MHC-peptide complex by making contact with conserved residues on the MHC-II or MHC-I molecule, respectively. Together, these extracellular binding events cause tyrosine phosphorylation of the intracellular ITAM domains of the associated CD3 and zeta chains to occur. This is mediated by a kinase called Lck, which associates with the intracellular domain of CD4 or CD8. The phosphorylation of the ITAMs allows them to bind the protein ZAP-70 (zeta chain associated protein of 70 kDa). Here, ZAP-70 becomes activated and begins to phosphorylate other targets inside the T-cell, resulting in the activation of key signalling pathways required for T-cell activation, including phospholipase-C-γ1 (PLC-γ1), Ras G-protein and MAP-kinases. At the same time, PI3-kinase is activated, leading to the phosphorylation and activation of protein kinase B (also called Akt). TCR signalling causes Ras G-protein activation which results in Erk MAPK activation, and Rac G-protein activation causes Jnk MAPK activation. Erk MAPK promotes activation of the c-Fos transcription factor, and Jnk MAPK pathway activation leads to activation of the c-Jun transcription factor. Together, c-Fos and c-Jun form the AP-1 heterodimer, which is a key transcription factor involved in switching on the genes required for T-cell activation. PLC-γ1 causes inositol triphosphate (IP3) and diacylglycerol (DAG) to be released from membrane phospholipids. The IP3 causes calcium ions to be released from the endoplasmic reticulum, into the cytosol. This is recognised by many calcium-dependent signalling proteins in the cell and contributes to the T-cell activation programme, most notably by activation of the transcription factor NF-AT (nuclear factor of activated T-cells). DAG, on the other hand, activates protein kinase-C (PKC), which promotes activation of the transcription factor NF-kB (nuclear factor kB). Together, the activated forms of the transcription factors AP-1, NF-AT and NF-kB all play key roles in switching on the genes required for T-cell activation. The major components of these signalling pathways are summarised in Figure 12.2.

Co-stimulator signalling promotes T-cell activation

In addition to the signalling events described above, the B7:CD28 interaction is also required for optimal T-cell activation. When T-cell CD28 is ligated by B7 expressed by the APC, this causes activation of the PI3-kinase, Akt and Ras/Erk MAPK pathways within the T-cell. These amplify the existing activation of these pathways caused by the TCR complex itself, resulting in reduced expression of pro-apoptotic proteins, increased IL-2 expression and increased proliferation.

T-cell activation in turn enhances the activation of APCs by CD40:CD40L signalling

One of the surface proteins upregulated on activated T-cells is CD40L. This is recognised by CD40 expressed by the APC, causing activation of intracellular signalling pathways within the APC that result in increased expression of B7 and cytokines. In turn, this increased expression of B7 and cytokines promotes further activation, and ultimately proliferation and differentiation, of the T-cell. In this way, the longer the DC and T-cell remain activated and in contact with each other, the further activated they both become (Figure 12.3).

![](image_rsrc4DS.jpg)

IL-2 and IL-2 receptor is upregulated on activation of naïve T-cells

The activation of naïve T-cells also causes rapid and transient upregulation of the cytokine IL-2, and the IL-2 receptor. IL-2 acts either on the same cell that produced it (this is called autocrine signalling), or on nearby cells (paracrine signalling). In responding cells, IL-2 stimulates increased proliferation and differentiation, and therefore plays a key role in the early clonal expansion of activated T-cells. IL-2 expression is also required for the survival of regulatory T-cells (discussed in Chapter 14).

Kinetics of clonal expansion during infection

When the host is infected with a pathogen that it has not encountered before, it is exposed to protein antigens to which it has no existing memory T-cells. This means that the T-cell response must be generated from the activation of naïve T-cells. At the very start of the infection, it is likely that only ~1 in 1 million of the circulating naïve T-cells are responsive to the antigen. Their constant recirculation through and concentration within secondary lymphoid organs enables them to encounter the antigen quickly (as described above), but the problem remains that they are very low in numbers at this stage, and certainly not enough to counter the infection. To address this, T-cells are required to undergo a phase of massive proliferation, often multiplying the numbers of original responding naïve T-cells by tens of thousands of fold.

This clonal expansion is particularly dramatic among CD8+ T-cells during anti-viral responses, which can reach very high proportions of effector cells in the total T-cell pool after about a week. However, after the infection is cleared, the antigen also begins to diminish, depriving the effector T-cells of an essential survival signal. The vast majority of the responding T-cells (i.e. the effector T-cells) die off over the next week or so, in a process called contraction. Eventually, only a modest number of long-lived memory T-cells remain which circulate and patrol for antigen. These memory T-cells are far more numerous in the blood than the number of naïve T-cells that may be responsive for the same antigen, which enables rapid responses to the same pathogen in future (Figure 12.4).

![](image_rsrc4DT.jpg)

Effector cell subtypes are generated that are suitable to target different types of pathogen

Naïve T-cells are not capable of performing the effector functions required to aid elimination of pathogens. They acquire these abilities, and thereby become effector cells, only after activation by antigen with co-stimulatory signals. As the host may be exposed to a variety of different types of pathogen, so the immune response needs to be tailored to yield the types of effector mechanisms that are most suitable to deal with each type of infection. For example, efficient defence against intracellular pathogens requires production of IFN-γ to promote macrophage killing of ingested organisms, responses to viruses and bacteria require a high affinity IgG response, responses to extracellular bacteria or fungi require strong neutrophilic inflammation and responses to helminths (worms) require the production of IgE and alterations to mucosal surface physiology.

Remarkably, following their activation, naïve T-cells may differentiate into polarised subsets which are highly adapted to support and enable each of these different types of response. These T-helper cell subsets are termed Th1, Th2 and Th17, and each supports a different type of immune response, as summarised in Table 12.2. Naïve T-cells acquire these phenotypes during or soon after activation, depending on signals they receive from the APC and the concentrations of various cytokines present in their local environment. It is thought that early in the process of becoming one of these subsets, T-cells may display intermediate phenotypes, and there is some plasticity with potential to switch between different polarisations. However, once the naïve T-cell has fully differentiated into one of these three subsets, it becomes very difficult for it to change polarisation to another subtype, for reasons that will be discussed later.

Table 12.2: Markers and key functions of Th1, Th2 and Th17 cells

![](image_rsrc4DU.jpg)

Th1 cells express the transcription factor T-bet and secrete IFN-γ

Th1 cells are so-called because they were the first T-helper subtype to be discovered. The key cytokines that promote naïve T-cell polarisation into the Th1 subset include particularly IL-12 and IFN-γ, but also IL-18 and type I interferons. These cytokines are typically secreted by activated DCs, macrophages and NK-cells during either bacterial or viral infections. Thus, the type of T-cell induced by such infections (Th1) promotes responses that are most appropriate to target that type of organism (i.e. macrophage activation and IgG production, see Table 12.2). One of the key transcription factors that drives Th1 polarisation is T-bet. This transcription factor is induced by recognition of IFN-γ by the IFN-γ receptor, which induces STAT1 activation leading to Tbet activation. Concurrently, IL-12 stimulates STAT4 activation, which enhances the capacity of Tbet to promote transcription of IFN-γ. This leads to the generation of a positive feedback loop, wherein production of IFN-γ causes more Tbet activation which in turn sustains production of IFN-γ. This is one of the major mechanisms by which Th1 cells remain polarised to the Th1 subset. Notably, the secretion of IFN-γ also inhibits the polarisation of nearby cells to the Th2 or Th17 subsets.

Th1 cells enhance responses to intracellular pathogens

The two main functions of Th1 cells are to activate macrophages to enhance their ability to kill ingested microbes, and to promote production of high affinity IgG antibodies. They achieve this by the expression of specific cytokines and cell surface receptors. A number of different pathogens have evolved the means to survive within the phagolysosome of macrophages. Dealing with such invaders requires the induction of more powerful anti-microbial responses within the macrophage. For example, the production of reactive oxygen species by phagocyte oxidase, and nitric oxide by nitric oxide synthase, must be induced. IFN-γ released from activated Th1 cells is a key promoter of this type of macrophage activation, which is called ‘classical’ macrophage activation. CD40L expression by the activated Th1 cell further enhances macrophage activation by stimulating the macrophage CD40 receptor. IFN-γ also promotes the antigen processing and presentation potential of APCs, by upregulating expression of B7, MHC molecules and enzymes involved in antigen processing. IFN-γ furthermore promotes the class-switching of B-cells towards IgG isotypes that are efficient at complement activation, and inhibits formation of IgE. Activated Th1 cells also produce other cytokines beyond IFN-γ, such as chemokines and TNF-α, which promote inflammatory responses. The characteristic array of cytokines produced by Th1 cells are commonly referred to as ‘Th1-cytokines’. The mechanisms by which macrophages are activated by Th1 cells are summarised in Figure 12.5.

![](image_rsrc4DV.jpg)

Th2 cells express the transcription factor GATA3 and secrete IL-4, IL-5 and IL-13

Exposure of the host to parasitic worms or allergens can promote the polarisation of naïve T-helper cells towards the Th2 subset. This is initiated at least in part by the cytokine IL-4, which also serves as the main cytokine secreted by Th2 cells that maintains Th2 polarisation. This is achieved in both cases by signalling downstream of the IL-4 receptor which activates the transcription factor STAT6, which in turn drives further production of IL-4. The combination of TCR signalling (through recognition of peptide/MHC complex), and IL-4 signalling promotes activation of the transcription factor GATA-3, which is the key transcription factor that drives and maintains Th2 differentiation. Major cytokines released by Th2 cells include IL-4, IL-5 and IL-13.

Th2 cells enhance responses to helminths and promote alternative macrophage activation

Because helminths (parasitic worms) are too large to be ingested by any cell of the immune system, alternative effector mechanisms are required to deal with these invaders. The principle defence against such pathogens is the antibody isotype IgE, which binds to proteins on the worm surface and promotes the local activation of eosinophils and mast cells by cross-linking of the FcεRs expressed on these cells. This induces the release of molecules that are toxic to helminths, such as major basic protein and major cationic protein, from the granules contained in eosinophils and mast cells. IL-4 secreted by activated Th2 cells promotes the class-switching of B-cells to the IgE isotype, and thereby enhances this type of response. IL-13 promotes mucous secretion from mucosal epithelial cells, and both IL-4 and IL-13 can promote peristalsis in the gut and the recruitment of eosinophils. IL-5 stimulates eosinophil production and activation. Together, these responses limit the capacity of worms to colonise the host.

IL-4 and IL-13 also co-operate to promote an alternative programme of activation in macrophages, termed ‘alternative activation’. This is distinct from classical macrophage activation, in that while classical macrophage activation (termed M1) promotes inflammation, alternatively activated macrophages (termed M2) secrete anti-inflammatory cytokines, such as IL-10 and TGF-β, and other agents which promote tissue repair and the production of the extracellular matrix component collagen to promote tissue fibrosis (Figure 12.6). As before, Th2 cytokines inhibit the polarisation of naïve T-cells towards the Th1 or Th17 subtype.

![](image_rsrc4DW.jpg)

Th17 cells express the transcription factor RORγT and secrete IL-17 and IL-22

Naïve T-cells are induced to become Th17 cells when activated in the presence of the inflammatory cytokines IL-1, IL-6 and IL-23, which are released from macrophages and DCs when exposed to bacteria and fungi. Unusually, the anti-inflammatory cytokine TGF-β also contributes to Th17 polarisation. It is thought that the high levels of TGF-β present in the intestinal mucosa result in preferential polarisation towards the Th17 subset, since Th17 cells play key roles in defence at the intestinal mucosa. Signalling events downstream of the IL-6 receptor promote activation of the transcription factor STAT3, and signalling downstream of the TGF-β receptor promotes activation of the RORγT transcription factor. Together, these act on the promoter of the IL-21 gene to induce secretion of IL-21, which promotes maintenance of Th17 polarisation.

Th17 cells enhance responses to extracellular fungi and bacteria

The main role of Th17 cells is to secrete cytokines that promote inflammation, favouring particularly the recruitment of neutrophils. This is because neutrophils are the key cell type required to ingest and destroy extracellular microbes, such as bacteria and fungi. Th17 cells potentiate inflammation by secreting the cytokines IL-17A and IL-17F (of the six related IL-17 subtypes), and IL-22. IL-17A, commonly referred to simply as IL-17, promotes inflammatory cytokine and chemokine production, which in turn promotes neutrophil recruitment and the inflammatory response. IL-17 also stimulates the production of anti-microbial peptides (as discussed in Chapter 4) from the epithelial cells that line the mucosal surfaces. IL-22 appears also to act on epithelial cells, promoting their survival and barrier properties, thus increasing the defensive capacity of this layer of the innate immune defence (Chapter 15). Th17 cells therefore enhance defences against extracellular microbes and help to maintain the integrity of epithelial barriers.

Mechanisms and stimuli responsible for T-helper cell polarisation

As described above, the polarisation of naïve T-cells towards one of the major subsets (Th1, Th2 or Th17) is associated with the expression of key, characteristic transcription factors and cytokines, which are specific for each type of T-cell. For T-helper cell polarisation to be efficient, it is necessary to ensure that once polarised into one subset, the cells remain largely within this subset. A number of molecular mechanisms promote this commitment to a particular T-cell subset.

The first of these is the concept of the feed-forward, or positive feedback, amplification loop. Simply put, the expression of a particular Th-subset-associated cytokine causes activation of a particular transcription factor that causes release of more of the same cytokine. At the same time, the activation of T-cells by these cytokines inhibits the expression of cytokines specific for the other subsets.

For example, in Th1 cells, the detection of IFN-γ causes activation of the Th1 transcription factor T-bet, which in turn causes transcription of the IFN-γ gene and secretion of IFN-γ protein, resulting in a stable signalling loop that ensures the T-cell remains committed to the Th1 subset (Figure 12.7). In Th2 cells, IL-4 and TCR signalling co-operate to induce STAT6 and GATA3 activation, which leads to further transcription, translation and secretion of IL-4. In Th17 cells, IL-21 is secreted to maintain the Th17 polarisation (by means that remain unclear), which in turn promotes IL-21 secretion.

![](image_rsrc4DX.jpg)

A second mechanism for the stable polarisation of differentiated T-helper cells is modification of the chromatin structure surrounding various key cytokines. As discussed in Chapter 2, genes are only efficiently transcribed (i.e. switched on), when the genomic DNA is in an open state, called euchromatin. Genes can be switched off for long periods of time by condensing the genomic DNA in particular regions to a highly packed form of DNA called heterochromatin. In polarised T-cells, the parts of the chromosome encoding the correct cytokines remain open (as euchromatin), but the genes coding for cytokines of other T-helper subsets become part of the tightly packed, inaccessible DNA called heterochromatin. This is thought to be one of the mechanisms by which memory T-cells retain their polarisation in the absence of high concentrations of signature cytokines.

Effector T-cells acquire the ability to migrate into the tissues

While naïve T-cells migrate principally between the blood and secondary lymphoid organs, effector T-cells need to be able to enter diverse tissues in order to reach the site of infection to aid clearance of the infectious organism. This is achieved by the expression of a different set of chemokine receptors and adhesion molecules that favour their recruitment to sites of inflammation. In particular, effector T-cells modify the carbohydrates expressed on their surface proteins, so that they become ligands for E-selectin and P-selectin, which are expressed by endothelial cells in inflammed tissues. This allows effector T-cells to recognise sites of infection and enter these tissues from the blood. Notably, the different T-helper subtype effector cells each express a slightly different array of chemokine receptors, which enables them to migrate preferentially towards the type of infection they are best able to deal with.

CD8+ cytotoxic T-cell differentiation

The above sections describe the activation of CD4+ helper T-cells. We will now discuss the activation of CD8+ cytotoxic T-cells (CTL). This process is dependent on the presentation of antigen on MHC-I by mature dendritic cells. The activation of CD8+ T-cells is also largely (although not always) dependent on help from CD4+ T-cells recognising antigen from the same source expressed on MHC-II. This CD4+ T-cell dependent help can be delivered either directly, by cytokines released from the CD4+ T-cell acting on the CD8+ cell, or indirectly, by enhancing the presentation and co-stimulatory capacity of the APC through CD40:CD40L signalling. Notably, although naïve CD8+ T-cells absolutely require co-stimulators presented by professional APCs (i.e. DCs), effector CD8+ T-cells do not require co-stimulation for activation, and can be activated by any host nucleated cell expressing antigen on MHC-I.

CTL kill target cells with granzymes and perforin, or with FasL

As described in earlier chapters, the main role of the CD8+ CTL is to kill target host cells that express foreign antigen on MHC-I. Interestingly, this does not happen to the DC that originally activates the CD8+ T-cell. Instead, activated CTLs detach from the DC without degranulating, multiply and become effector cells that seek out antigen in the peripheral tissues. On detection of their specific antigen presented on MHC-I, they bind closely to the target cell and kill it without causing harm to themselves, or to other neighbouring cells. This very specific targeted killing of only infected cells limits the potential for tissue injury during such responses.

CTLs employ two main methods to kill target cells, both of which aim to induce programmed cell death, or apoptosis, in the target cell (see Chapter 7, Figure 7.8). The first of these is the exocytosis of pre-formed granules contained within the CTL, which are released directly onto the surface of the target cell. Products contained in these granules include molecules such as perforin, which punctures membranes, and granzymes, which are proteases that cleave many proteins in the cytosol of the target cell. One of the targets cleaved by granzymes is caspase 3, and this leads to apoptosis. A second mechanism of cell killing is the use of Fas:FasL signalling. Activated CTLs express the surface protein Fas ligand (FasL), which binds to the surface molecule Fas, which is expressed by most cells. This interaction causes activation of caspases and apoptosis within the target cell.

In either case, neither the CTL itself nor neighbouring cells are damaged by this process. The CTL detaches from the target cell to seek further target cells presenting foreign antigen on MHC-I.

Differentiation into long-lived memory T-cells

As infections are cleared, so too is the source of antigen, and antigenic peptides specific for a particular microbe eventually become scarce. As this happens, effector CD4+ and CD8+ T-cells lose one of their key survival signals, which is the presence of the peptide for which they are specific. At this stage, the effector cells begin to die by apoptosis, as part of contraction and the return to homeostasis. However, not all of the progeny of the original naïve T-cells that were activated by antigen during the response are killed. Some become long-lived memory T-cells, which express different cytokines to effector T-cells. These cells may persist for years, often as long as the lifetime of the host, patrolling the body seeking the same antigen.
