If such antigen is detected, they are triggered to proliferate and differentiate once again to rapidly yield large numbers effector T-cells. The major properties of memory T-cells will be discussed in more depth in the following chapter.

Some CD4+ T-cells can also be activated by a bacterial toxin called superantigen

It is thought that approximately one in one million CD4+ T-cells are likely to be activated by a particular peptide expressed on MHC-II. However, some bacteria express toxins called superantigens (for example Staphylococcal enterotoxin B), which stimulates some CD4+ T-cells in an unusual way. Rather than binding to the antigen cleft as peptides do, superantigens bind to conserved residues on the outside of the MHC-II molecule. Superantigen also binds strongly to the TCRs expressing particular types of Vβ region, and thereby causes a similar binding of TCR to MHC, even if the correct peptide is not present in the peptide binding groove. This results in the activation of all T-cells bearing that particular Vβ region, which may be as many as 1 in 50 of all circulating T-cells. Superantigens therefore cause massive cytokine release by diverse CD4+ T-helper subsets, confusing the host immune system and conferring to the microbes an advantage during infection.

Summary

1) Naïve T-cells exit the thymus and circulate between the secondary lymphoid organs, including lymph nodes and spleen, seeking antigen expressed on MHC molecules for which they are specific

2) Naïve T-cells that do not meet antigen within several months die by apoptosis and are replaced with new naïve T-cells from the thymus

3) Naïve T-cells can only be activated by mature DCs, which have been activated by ‘danger’ signals such as microbial PAMPs, to express co-stimulatory molecules such as B7

4) The costimulators B7-1 and B7-2 are recognised by CD28 expressed on the T-cell surface

5) The immunological synapse is the region of close proximity between the membranes of the T-cell and the APC, formed by adhesion molecules, MHC-peptide-TCR and co-stimulator-ligand interactions

6) The TCR signals through the accessory proteins CD3 and zeta (ζ) chain

7) TCR signalling triggers a variety of intracellular signalling cascades, including Erk and Jnk MAP-kinases, Ras G-protein signalling, calcium release, phospholipase-C- γ1, PI3-kinase and protein kinase B, which together activate the transcription factors required T-cell activation, including AP-1, NF-AT and NF-kB

8) TCR signalling is enhanced by CD4 or CD8, and by co-stimulators binding to their ligands

9) APCs may be further activated by the stimulation of their CD40 receptors by CD40L expressed by activated T-cells

10) One of the earliest events of T-cell activation is the upregulation of IL-2 and the IL-2 receptor, which together promote the autocrine and paracrine stimulation of T-cell survival and proliferation

11) Activated T-cells proliferate rapidly during immune responses, yielding many thousands of effector cells that reach peak numbers in the circulation several days after encounter with antigen, and then die off by apoptosis in a process called contraction over the next week or so

12) Different types of pathogen promote different types of cytokine production and other signals by DCs during CD4+ T-cell activation, which causes the T-cells in that location to differentiate into one of three particular T-helper cell subsets (Th1, Th2 or Th17) that are efficient at dealing with that type of infection

13) Once a T-cell becomes polarised to a particular subset, it is committed to remaining in that subset, and the expression of key cytokines and transcription factors ensure the T-cell remains of that subset

14) Commitment to a particular T-cell subset is maintained by cytokine to transcription factor regulatory loops, and by tightly packing the chromatin of cytokine genes associated with other T-cell subsets, thereby switching these genes off

15) The cytokines released by activated T-cells of a particular subset inhibit their potential to convert to another polarisation or subset

16) Th1 cells express IFN-γ and the transcription factor T-bet

17) Th2 cells express IL-4, IL-5, IL-13 and the transcription factor GATA-3

18) Th17 cells express IL-17A, IL-17F, IL-22 and the transcription factor RORγT

19) Th1 cells promote defence against intracellular pathogens by secreting IFN-γ which promotes macrophage activation and the killing of ingested microbes, and also class-switching to IgG

20) Th2 cells promote defence against parasitic worms (helminths) by secreting IL-4, IL-5 and IL-13, which promote IgE production, mucous production and peristalsis

21) Th17 cells promote defence against extracellular bacteria and fungi by secreting IL-17, which promotes neutrophil-rich inflammation, and IL-22, which enhances epithelial barrier function

22) While naïve T-cells do not normally enter the tissues, effector T-cells gain the ability to enter the tissues, particularly at sites of inflammation, to reach the site of infection

23) Activated CD8+ T-cells become cytotoxic T-cells that serve the function of killing host cells that express foreign antigen on MHC-I

24) CD8+ T-cells can only be activated by mature DCs with co-stimulators, and often also require additional help from CD4+ helper T-cells to become fully activated

25) CD8+ T-cells kill target cells by inducing apoptosis in the target cell with granzymes and perforin released from their granules, and also FasL expressed on their surface binding to Fas on the surface of target cells

26) CD8+ T-cell mediated cell killing does not damage the CTL itself, or nearby cells

27) Effector cells die off by apoptosis soon after the infection is cleared and the antigen survival signal disappears

28) A small proportion of long-lived memory T-cells remain, which patrol the body seeking the same antigen and are able to initiate rapid responses to the same pathogen should it be encountered in future

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cell_mediated_immunity>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-cell_receptor>

Roles of CD4 and CD8 in T-cell activation: <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pubmed/23885256>

---

# Chapter 13: Immunological memory and vaccination

In our previous discussions of the innate and adaptive immune systems, it was explained that the innate immune system possesses no immunological memory. This means that each time the host is exposed to the same microbe, the innate immune response develops in much the same way and to much the same magnitude as when it first encountered the organism. However, the adaptive immune system is able to develop immunological memory of prior exposure to specific antigens. This enables defensive responses to be raised more quickly and more effectively each time the host is challenged by the same organism. In particular, when infected by an organism the host has previously encountered, the activation of T-cells and B-cells occurs much more rapidly, and larger numbers of effector cells are generated, than during the primary response (see Chapters 11&12). Moreover, these secondary responses are typically more effective at clearing pathogens than primary responses because the antibodies that are produced are of high affinity and of the most suitable isotype, and helper T-cells are already polarised to the correct subset (Th1, Th2 or Th17, see Chapter 12), both of which provide optimal defence against that particular class of organism. In this chapter we will discuss the development of memory cells during adaptive immune responses, and how these processes are exploited in the design of vaccines.

Common elements of memory B- and T-cell formation

The majority of the daughter cells that arise from the activation of naïve B- or T-cells become short-lived effector cells, which are the cells that produce the mediators (e.g. antibodies or cytokines) required to eliminate microbes. The survival of these cells is partly dependent on their continued exposure to antigen, which provides survival signals through the antigen receptor to inhibit the default pathways of apoptosis. As infections are cleared by effective immune responses, the amount of antigen remaining begins to diminish, and so too do numbers of effector B- and T-cells. Soon after the antigen has been cleared, effector B- and T-cells die by apoptosis.

However, during the activation of follicular B-cells and most types of T-cell, a small proportion of the cells differentiate into a different type of cell, called a memory cell. These cells possess exactly the same BCR or TCR sequence, and therefore the same antigen specificity, as the effector lymphocytes that arose within the same clone. However, memory cells can be distinguished from effector cells by a number of key properties. First, they do not depend on the continued presence of antigen for survival. Instead their survival is mediated by responsiveness to certain cytokines and the expression of anti-apoptotic signalling proteins. Memory cells also live much longer than effector cells, often as long as the host. They tend to circulate in a quiescent form that rarely enters the cell-division cycle. The surface proteins and chemokine receptors they produce are also quite different from effector cells, which enables them to circulate between specific tissues of the body (Figure 13.1).

![](image_rsrc4DY.jpg)

The purpose of memory B- and T-cells is to patrol the different tissues of the body seeking antigen for which they are specific. Once such antigen is encountered, the cells are rapidly induced to proliferate, and they can quickly differentiate into effector cells which rapidly clear the immune response. After this secondary response, the effector cells die off and memory cells remain. By this means, the mammalian host is often able to become immune to re-infection by an organism it has seen before.

Key features of memory B-cells

Although some degree of B-cell memory may be formed in response to polysaccharide antigens, the vast majority of memory B-cell responses develop from the activation of follicular B-cells with T-cell help. Memory B-cells may remain in the lymphoid tissue in which they first encountered antigen, or recirculate between the blood and the secondary lymphoid organs seeking antigen. A notable feature of memory B-cells during secondary responses is that they may re-enter germinal centres, and, with further T-cell help, undergo further somatic hypermutation, affinity maturation and class-switching. This means that each time the host is exposed to a particular antigen, the antibody response to that antigen becomes more effective. Not only are the antibodies for the antigen more appropriately class-switched to defend against the pathogen in question, but the affinity for antigen is much higher than during the primary response. Notably, high affinity antibodies are often required to prevent re-infection with various pathogens, and repeated exposure to antigen to further refine the memory B-cell subsets is therefore a common goal of many vaccination strategies.

Memory B-cells differ from effector B-cells in a number of ways. They do not secrete antibody, and the cells are small with little cytoplasm. Human memory B-cells also express the surface marker CD27. Unlike effector B-cells, memory B-cells express high levels of the protein Bcl2 which inhibits the pro-apoptosis pathways that are otherwise stimulated by default when the cells lose survival signals derived from exposure to antigen. Bcl2, and other related anti-apoptotic proteins, together enable memory cells to survive for long periods in the absence of antigen. Although it has been established that memory cells derive from the same naïve B-cells that gave rise to the effector cells within the clone, the mechanisms that determine whether individual B-cells become effector or memory B-cells remain poorly understood.

Key features of memory T-cells

Memory T-cell formation occurs in the same place that naïve T-cells are first activated, i.e. within the secondary lymphoid organs. It is not clear if memory T-cells are derived directly from the activated naïve cell, or from differentiation of an effector cell intermediate. The specific mechanisms that determine whether individual T-cells become effector or memory T-cells also remain poorly understood. However, memory T-cells can be distinguished from effector cells by a number of functional and phenotypic markers. For example, they do not exert effector functions, such as the killing of target cells or secretion of Th-subset-related cytokines, until re-activated by exposure to antigen. They are also much longer lived than effector T-cells, often surviving for the life of the host. Unlike naïve T-cells, but similarly to effector T-cells, memory T-cells preferentially migrate to sites of inflammation and the mucosal tissues.

The long life span of memory T-cells can be partly explained by their high level of expression of the IL-7 receptor. In fact, their continued survival is largely dependent on IL-7 receptor signalling, which promotes low-level cycling and the expression of anti-apoptotic proteins, such as Bcl2, allowing them to survive for long periods without antigen. High levels of IL-7 receptor expression is therefore a useful marker of memory T-cells, as it is low in naïve T-cells and not expressed by effector T-cells. Similar to naïve T-cells, but unlike effector T-cells, memory T-cells express low levels of the IL-2 receptor.

A further useful marker of memory T-cell status is CD45, which is a protein tyrosine phosphatase that regulates TCR and BCR signalling. CD45 is expressed by all lymphocytes other than plasma cells, and is encoded by a gene containing 34 exons, three of which may be alternatively spliced to yield proteins of slightly different lengths. One of these exons, referred to as the ‘A’ exon, is expressed by some lymphocytes but not others. For example, naïve T-cells express the long form of CD45, containing the A exon, and are referred to as CD45RA+. By contrast, most memory T-cells do not express the A exon, and are termed CD45R0 (the zero indicates a lack of the A exon and expression of a shorter form of CD45). Notably, not all memory T-cells lack the CD45 A exon, and their identification is more accurately established by also considering levels of expression of the IL-7 and IL-2 receptors.

Most CD4+ memory T-cells retain the same polarisation they gained during the primary immune response (i.e. Th1, Th2 or Th17). However, a small proportion of memory T-cells commits to the memory phenotype before they fully commit to a particular effector subset, and may therefore delay their full polarisation to one of the Th subsets until after re-activation during the secondary response. However, once they are fully polarised towards one of the effector subsets, they generally remain within that subset.

Memory T-cells of both the CD4+ and CD8+ subsets can also be subdivided into two different subsets, depending on the types of adhesion molecules and chemokine receptors they express. Some, referred to as central memory T-cells, express CCR7 and the adhesion molecule L-selectin. Others, referred to as effector memory T-cells do not express CCR7 or L-selectin. When encountering antigen, these two different memory T-cell subsets respond quite differently. Central memory T-cells initiate a strong proliferative response to yield large numbers of effector cells, but cannot themselves rapidly yield effector functions after activation. Effector memory T-cells, however, do not proliferate much when stimulated by antigen, but are able to deliver effector functions (such as secreting cytokines or killing target cells) much more quickly. The tissues patrolled by these two major types of memory T-cells are also slightly different. Central memory T-cells mainly recirculate between lymph nodes, and effector memory T-cells patrol the peripheral tissues, especially the mucosal tissues.

The aim of vaccination is to induce antibodies and lymphocyte memory

The exposure of vertebrate animals to a particular pathogen often leads to the these animals becoming resistant to re-infection by the same pathogen. Such animals are said to be immune to infection by that organism. In the late 1700s, Edward Jenner showed that deliberate exposure of a child to the cow-pox virus, which did not cause serious disease in man, resulted in the same child becoming resistant to subsequent infection by the small-pox virus, which normally caused life-threatening infections in man. It was then realised that immunity to dangerous pathogens could be induced by administering substances containing antigens similar to those found in the pathogen itself, without necessarily causing symptoms of the disease. Such preparations containing these substances are referred to as vaccines, and the deliberate administration of such substances to animals or man is termed vaccination, or immunisation.

The aim of vaccination is to induce in the host a state of immunity to infection by a particular pathogen by administering a substance that does not cause severe symptoms of disease. Because this state of immunity is mediated largely by the generation of antibodies and memory lymphocytes specific for antigens expressed by the pathogen, stimulating these responses is the key goal of vaccination strategies. Because high-affinity antibodies are generally required for optimal defence against various toxins and pathogens, and since the generation of high affinity antibodies requires T-cell help, vaccines often aim to generate antigen-specific CD4+ T-helper cells.

Commonly employed strategies for vaccination

There are a number of ways in which the host can be exposed to antigens from a particular pathogen without risk of developing disease. Sometimes, a weakened form of the pathogen can be generated by laboratory treatment, which still expresses antigen and induces immune responses, but is not able to cause disease in the vaccine recipient. Such organisms are said to be ‘live attenuated’, since they are administered as live organisms, but their capacity to induce disease is highly diminished. Alternatively, the virulent form of the organism may be administered to subjects if it has first been killed by means such as by heat-treatment. Such vaccines are referred to as ‘inactivated’. Purified individual antigens may also be administered to induce protective responses. These so-called ‘subunit’ vaccines require the co-administration of other substances to elicit immunity for reasons described below. Vaccines based on bacterial polysaccharides tend to elicit poor memory, and so these are often covalently attached (or conjugated) to foreign proteins to stimulate a CD4+ T-helper cell response. Such vaccines are termed ‘conjugate’ vaccines, and are described in more depth below. Table 13.1 lists some of the vaccines commonly used to immunise man today.

Table 13.1: Vaccines commonly used in man

![](image_rsrc4DZ.jpg)

Adjuvants stimulate ‘danger signals’ that enhance adaptive responses to antigen

The injection of highly purified monomeric protein into the host is unlikely to generate a strong T-cell or B-cell response. This is because a simple system is in place to ensure that only proteins associated with potentially dangerous organisms stimulate adaptive responses. APCs are surrounded by self-proteins and constantly ingest and present these to T-cells, yet very rarely are T-cell responses developed to these proteins. This is because resting APCs do not express co-stimulatory molecules, such as B7. APCs express sufficient co-stimulatory molecules for T-cell activation only when activated themselves, and this only happens when they encounter some form of ‘danger signal’, which is most often the detection of microbial PAMPs by PRRs expressed by the DC. By this means, DCs effectively decide which proteins are associated with dangerous organisms (PAMPs detected at the same time) and which are likely to be harmless (no PAMPs detected at the same time).

For this reason, vaccines that are not derived from attenuated or inactivated organisms (which contain PAMPs) almost always include some form of compound that stimulates a ‘danger signal’ in APCs. These additives are called adjuvants. Such substances are typically related to bacterial molecules (such as Freund’s complete adjuvant, which stimulates TLR2), or to other forms of danger signalling (such as alum, which stimulates inflammasome signalling, as shown in Figure 13.2). The addition of adjuvant to a vaccine typically amplifies enormously the host response to the target antigen. Careful choice of adjuvant can also promote DC production of cytokines that help polarise naïve T-cells towards the type of subset desired.

![](image_rsrc4E0.jpg)

Conjugate vaccines

Because T-cell help is required for the development of high affinity antibodies and efficient B-cell memory, vaccination strategies usually aim to induce T-cell help. If the antigen is a protein, then this presents no difficulty. However, the target molecules for some vaccines are not proteins, but polysaccharides or other non-protein molecules. Because these molecules cannot be presented on MHC molecules, and therefore cannot stimulate a helper T-cell response, they must be chemically modified to do so. This is typically achieved by covalently linking the antigen, such as a polysaccharide, to a protein or short peptide that is foreign to the host, during the manufacture of the vaccine. The intact form of this so-called conjugate vaccine is recognised by the B-cell receptor, and when it is internalised it can yield peptides that promote the T-helper cell activation required for effective responses (Figure 13.3). A similar approach can be employed to stimulate the development of antibodies targeting molecules that are too small to ordinarily stimulate a B-cell response. Here, the small molecule (termed ‘hapten’) is covalently attached to a larger molecule containing peptide (termed ‘carrier’). Antibody responses may thus be made to the hapten-carrier conjugate, since T-cell help is generated by the presence of the peptide component.

![](image_rsrc4E1.jpg)

Passive immunity is short-lived, and involves the transfer of antibody from donor to recipient

The above examples of immunisation all aim to confer long-lived protection against infection by inducing the generation of memory B- and T-cells. However, short-lived protection can also be conferred by the induction of so-called passive immunity. In this scenario, the recipient is not challenged with any form of pathogen or even antigen, but instead receives antibodies derived from an animal of the same species that has already developed humoral immunity, in other words specific antibodies, to that organism. These antibodies can be collected by drawing a small quantity of blood from the donor animal and allowing the blood to clot. The clot, which forms at the bottom of the collection vessel, contains all of the red cells and white cells from the donor’s blood. The clear liquid above the clot that results is called serum, and this can be collected and administered by injection into the blood-stream of the recipient animal.

As mentioned in earlier chapters, circulating antibodies have a relatively short half-life and are eventually cleared from the circulation if they are not continually replaced by secretion from plasma cells. This means that after several weeks, the recipient no longer benefits from the immunity conferred by the transfusion of antibodies. Nevertheless, this transient protection is very useful in the treatment of rabies, and the neutralisation of toxins from snakebites and tetanus infections.

Two quite specialised forms of passive immunity occur in mammals. The first is the transfer of maternal IgG from the mother’s serum across the placenta and into the developing foetus. Only IgG, but not other classes of antibody, are transferred in this process because the receptor that performs the translocation of antibody across placental cells is FcRn (the neonatal Fc receptor), which is specific for IgG. Babies are therefore born with an array of IgG antibodies derived from their mother and therefore temporarily have immunity to the same organisms to which the mother has raised immunity.

The second form of passive immunity employed by mammals is the transfer of IgG and IgA from the mother’s breast milk to the new-born. Mothers transport IgG and IgA from their serum into the milk by means of the poly-Ig receptor, and this serves to coat the surfaces of the human infant gut and thereby confer protection from invasion by diverse intestinal pathogens. Both forms of passive immunity are transient, and once the infant is weaned, the antibodies derived from the mother are cleared and the infant becomes dependent on generation of their own humoral responses.

Summary

1) When naïve B-cells or T-cells are activated by antigen, they may become either effector or memory cells

2) Effector cells perform the functions required to eliminate infections, such as secreting cytokines or killing target cells, and die soon after the infection is cleared

3) Memory cells may live for many years, often for the lifetime of the host

4) Memory cells express the same antigen receptor as the initial responding B- or T-cell clone, and when activated by a second exposure to antigen, proliferate and then differentiate to quickly yield effector cells that are well adapted to clearance of the invading microbe

5) When an individual has gained sufficient lymphocyte memory to prevent serious re-infection by a particular organism, the individual is said to be ‘immune’ to that organism, and such immunity may last for many years

6) When memory B-cells are activated in secondary responses, they may re-enter germinal centres to undergo further somatic hypermutation and class-switching, leading to the generation of higher affinity antibodies of more appropriate isotype each time the host is exposed to the same antigen

7) Human memory B-cells express the marker CD27 and high levels of anti-apoptotic proteins, such as Bcl2

8) Memory CD4+ T-cells may be generated before or after commitment to a particular polarisation, but once fully committed, remain within that subset (i.e. Th1, Th2 or Th17)

9) Memory T-cell survival is dependent on IL-7, and these cells tend to express high levels of the IL-7 receptor, anti-apoptotic proteins such as Bcl2, and a version of the CD45 surface protein that lacks the A exon, termed CD45R0

10) Memory T-cells can be subdivided into central or effector memory T-cells, based on their functional properties and chemokine receptor expression

11) Central memory T-cells express CCR7 and spend some time proliferating to yield effector cells before responses to antigen, such as the secretion of appropriate cytokines, are generated

12) Effector memory T-cells do not express CCR7 and are able to generate effector responses more quickly than central memory T-cells, since they do not spend much time proliferating before a response is generated

13) The aim of vaccination is to induce a high affinity antibody response and the generation of long-lived lymphocyte memory cells

14) Vaccines are forms of antigen that can be administered to people or animals to generate immunity without the symptoms of disease normally caused by the pathogen that expresses the antigen

15) Vaccines may consist of live attenuated organisms (which are alive but made harmless), inactivated (killed) organisms, subunits (individual antigens), or conjugates (such as peptide covalently bound to polysaccharide)

16) Because T-cell help is required for effective B-cell memory, non-protein antigens, such as polysaccharides, are often conjugated by covalently attaching foreign peptides to yield conjugate vaccines

17) The administration of purified antigens alone tends to result in very poor T- or B-cell memory because DCs must be activated by some form of danger signal to present antigen efficiently and express co-stimulatory molecules, resulting in effective T- and B-cell responses

18) For this reason, many vaccines include an adjuvant, which is a substance that causes activation of receptors of the innate immune system, such as Toll-like receptors or the inflammasome

19) Commonly used adjuvants include bacterial PAMPs, or the inflammasome-stimulant alum

20) Passive immunity is the transfer of antibodies from a donor to a recipient animal of the same species

21) Because antibodies have a half-life in the circulation of days to weeks, the protection against infection or toxins afforded by passive immunity is lost after several weeks

22) Mammals provide antibody to their offspring by transcytosis of IgG via FcRn across the placenta and into the foetal circulation, and also by expressing IgG and IgA in breast milk, both of which strategies protect the infant from infection by the same pathogens to which the mother has developed immunity

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccination>
