# Chapter 14: Immunological tolerance and transplantation

One of the key roles played by the immune system is the distinction between which antigens are associated with infectious organisms, and therefore require an immune response, and those which are harmless molecules associated with host cells (i.e. self antigens). It is a remarkable feat of biology that the human body generally does not mount an immune response to any of the ~20,000 proteins expressed by its own cells, yet it can quickly develop powerful responses to any of the millions of different microbial antigens it may be exposed to. This lack of response to self-antigens is termed tolerance, and is underpinned by several distinct mechanisms. The failure of these mechanisms can lead to inappropriate immune responses to self-antigens, termed auto-immunity, that can lead to damage of the host tissues and result in the development of auto-immune diseases. As the mechanisms involved in the control of responses to self antigens are also involved in the responses to transplanted tissues or grafts, the roles played by immunity in transplantation will also be discussed in this chapter.

Central and peripheral tolerance

Both arms of the immune system are continually exposed to self antigens, and must therefore have mechanisms to distinguish between self- and non-self antigens. The innate immune system achieves this by expressing a small number of invariant receptors that detect highly conserved molecular patterns (PAMPs) which are expressed by microbes but not mammalian cells. However, the adaptive immune response relies on the generation of millions of different antigen receptors, each with the ability to bind different antigens, by genetic recombinations that are unique to each B-cell or T-cell clone. Because of the random nature of TCR and BCR generation, receptors specific for host antigens are frequently generated. The adaptive immune system therefore has a number of mechanisms to deal with such self-reactive lymphocytes, which together form part of the two major means of avoiding responses to self-antigens, termed central tolerance and peripheral tolerance.

Central tolerance is so-called because it occurs in the central, or generative, lymphoid organs where T-cells and B-cells are generated, and acts on immature lymphocytes. At this stage in their development, the problem of self-reactive lymphocytes may be dealt with by one of three major mechanisms. Most frequently, self-reactive cells receive excessive signalling through their antigen receptors, triggered by self antigens expressed locally, and are therefore deleted by apoptosis. Next, specifically for B-cells only, self-reactivity may trigger further rounds of receptor editing (see Chapter 10) to yield receptors that do not bind to self-antigens. Third, specifically only for CD4+ T-cells, a small proportion of the cells which are self-reactive may develop into so-called regulatory T-cells, which inhibit, rather than promote, inflammatory and immune responses to antigens.

By contrast, peripheral tolerance is so-called because it takes place in the peripheral tissues (i.e. outside the bone marrow and thymus), and acts on mature, peripheral lymphocytes. Peripheral tolerance is necessary for three main reasons: (i) some T-cells or B-cells reactive for self-antigen may occasionally escape the selection processes of central tolerance, (ii) somatic hypermutation of B-cells may lead to self-reactive clones in the peripheral lymphoid organs and (iii) not all of the ~20,000 proteins that can be expressed from the human genome are expressed in the thymus or bone marrow.

Having outlined the key differences between central and peripheral tolerance (see also Table 14.1), we will now discuss the specific mechanisms of B- and T-lymphocyte tolerance.

Table 14.1: Comparison of the major features of central and peripheral tolerance

![](image_rsrc4E2.jpg)

Central T-cell tolerance

Foreign antigens rarely occur in the thymus, because the lymphatics tend to drain material away from this organ towards secondary lymph nodes. This means that as T-cells develop, they are surrounded by APCs expressing MHC-I and MHC-II molecules complexed only to self-peptides, and not to foreign peptides. Those CD4+ and CD8+ T-cells that bind too strongly to self peptides displayed on MHC-II or MHC-I undergo apoptosis and are therefore deleted. Two main mechanisms ensure that a broad variety of self-peptides is displayed in the thymus. The first is that blood contains fragments of many different types of body cell, and proteins from these fragments are captured, processed and presented on MHC-II in the thymus. Secondly, specialised thymic medullary epithelial cells express a protein called ‘auto-immune regulator’ (AIRE) that enables these cells to express a wide range of proteins that are normally expressed by other cell types in the body. In this way, developing T-cells are exposed to a large proportion of the host self-peptide repertoire.

Occasionally, via mechanisms which are not yet clear, some of the self-reactive CD4+ T-cells do not die by apoptosis, but instead differentiate into a distinct class of T-cell termed the regulatory T-cell (often referred to as T-regs). These T-cells serve to inhibit immune responses to self antigens, and have the capacity to counteract the effects of self-reactive effector cells in the periphery by the secretion of anti-inflammatory and immunosuppressive cytokines, as will be discussed shortly. Notably, this does not occur for CD8+ T-cells.

Central T-cell tolerance therefore ensures that self-reactive T-cells are either deleted, or programmed to become immunosuppressive regulatory T-cells (Figure 14.1).

![](image_rsrc4E3.jpg)

Peripheral T-cell tolerance

Because of the large number of different proteins that may be expressed in the body, and the large number of splice variants of these proteins, it is inevitable that not all of these proteins can be expressed in the thymus. Therefore, mature T-cells occasionally emerge from the thymus bearing TCRs that are responsive to a self-antigen that was not expressed in the thymus. The system that prevents these T-cells, which have escaped deletion during central tolerance, from causing harmful responses to tissues is termed peripheral tolerance.

There are three main mechanisms that serve to limit the responses of mature self-reactive T-cells in the periphery. These are:

(i) Anergy, which is the process by which self-reactive T-cells remain alive but lose their capacity to become an activated effector cell

(ii) Suppression, wherein CD4+ regulatory T-cells generate cytokines in response to the detection of self peptide that inactivate other nearby self-reactive T-cells

(iii) Deletion, wherein activation by self-antigens causes the T-cell to die by apoptosis

Anergy of self-reactive T-cells

We discussed in earlier chapters the requirement for ‘second signals’ during the activation of naïve T-cells, which are derived from interactions with co-stimulators, such as B-7, and pro-inflammatory cytokines expressed by activated DCs (Chapter 12). In the absence of infection, tissue DCs are not activated and do not express relevant levels of co-stimulators or PAMP-triggered cytokines. However, immature DCs do still present low levels of self-antigens on MHC-II. They may also express relatively high levels of inhibitory ligands of CD28, such as CTLA-4 and PD-1, which blunt T-cell responses. It is thought that when naïve CD4+ T-cells encounter self peptide in the absence of co-stimulatory signals in this way, they are rendered incapable of responding to that antigen in future - in other words, they are said to be anergic. The mechanisms by which such signals induce anergy appear to involve long-lasting inhibition of key components of the TCR signalling pathway.

Suppression of self-reactive T-cells by regulatory T-cells

As described above, some of the self-reactive CD4+ T-cells that originate in the thymus are directed to become regulatory T-cells. The function of these cells is to suppress inappropriate responses to self antigens, or other harmless antigens. They achieve this mainly by secreting their signature cytokines, IL-10 and TGF-β, and by inhibiting the activation of APCs, when encountering self-antigen. T-regs express the transcription factor FOXP3, which is required for their function and development, and mainly express high levels of CD25 (the IL-2 receptor α chain), and the inhibitory molecule CTLA-4 (Figure 14.2).

![](image_rsrc4E4.jpg)

Regulatory T-cells may be generated either in the thymus, or in the peripheral tissues. Those that are generated in the thymus during T-cell development are termed natural T-regs. Those that are formed by exposure to self-antigen in the periphery are termed inducible T-regs. Notably, inducible T-regs may be formed in response either to self-antigen, or to foreign antigen if it is encountered in the absence of PAMPs or any other danger signal. It is thought this mechanism may contribute to a mechanism termed ‘oral tolerance’, which is the subsequent limited immune response of the host to proteins that have been ingested orally in the past. It is not clear which signals in particular promote the development of inducible T-regs.

TGF-β produced by either type of T-reg serves to inhibit the activation of macrophages and effector T-cells. It is potently anti-inflammatory and serves to limit the damage caused in tissues by other mediators during the inflammatory response. TGF-β also enhances the tissue repair processes after inflammatory responses have ceased by promoting the production of the extracellular matrix component collagen, and the alternative activation of macrophages. TGF-β is also present at high levels in the gut mucosa, where it serves to promote B-cell class-switching to IgA. Counterintuitively, TGF-β is also required, together with IL-6, for effective differentiation of naïve CD4+ T-cells into the Th17 subset, which are required for efficient gut immunity.

IL-10 is also a potently anti-inflammatory cytokine that inhibits the expression of MHC and co-stimulators by APCs, and their secretion of the Th1 promoting cytokine IL-12. IL-10 is also produced by activated macrophages shortly after the production of pro-inflammatory cytokines. This serves to limit the extent of the immune or inflammatory response. Immune responses commonly feature such concurrent induction of both pro- and anti-activation signals, and it is the balance between the relative strengths of these two signals that dictates the outcome of the response.

Deletion of self-reactive T-cells in the periphery

For reasons that remain unclear, while some self-reactive T-cells that meet self-antigen in the periphery become anergic, others are triggered to undergo apoptosis, and are therefore deleted. It is thought that this pathway is triggered by repeated or continuous exposure to antigen in the absence of danger, or co-stimulatory signals. Such exposure triggers the activation of both the Bim-dependent pathway of mitochondrial (intrinsic) apoptosis, and upregulation of the Fas molecule, which sensitises these cells to death triggered by FasL expression on other cells (the extrinsic apoptosis pathway).

Central B-cell tolerance

After the first round of receptor generation during B-cell development in the bone marrow, many of the resulting antigen receptors may bind strongly to self antigens. This causes strong cross-linking and signalling through the BCR or these B-cells, resulting in the reactivation of the Rag genes and other enzymes required for genetic recombination. Thus, self-reactive B-cells can, at this stage of development, attempt to edit their receptor gene to yield one which does not bind to self antigens. This process is termed receptor editing, as described in more depth in Chapter 10. Those B-cells which successfully generate receptors that do not bind self antigens survive, and proceed to further maturation. Those B-cells that fail to generate a functional receptor after receptor editing, or generate one which still binds to self antigens, are deleted by apoptosis. Alternatively, some B-cells which bind to self antigens do so with an affinity that is a little too low to induce receptor editing. These cells may survive, but exit the bone marrow in a functionally non-competent, anergic state. Central B-cell tolerance therefore consists of receptor editing, deletion and anergy (Figure 14.3).

![](image_rsrc4E5.jpg)

Peripheral B-cell tolerance

When follicular B-cells encounter protein antigen in the periphery, they require help from CD4+ helper T-cells to become activated. If that CD4+ T-cell response is missing, perhaps because of T-cell tolerance to the antigen, the B-cells are not activated, but are either deleted or become anergic. Thus, like T-cells, the lack of a ‘second signal’ during activation can cause peripheral tolerance in B-cells. Receptors expressed by the B-cell, such as CD22, that inhibit BCR signalling, may also contribute to anergy when encountering self-antigens.

Autoimmunity is triggered by the failure of tolerance and adaptive responses to self-antigens

Autoimmune disease occurs when the mechanisms of tolerance fail, and effector lymphocyte activation occurs in response to self antigens. These may be T-cell or antibody responses directed against host molecules, either of which may result in inflammation and tissue damage. Depending on the self antigen targeted by the host immune system, autoimmune disease may affect specific cell types, specific organs or even the whole body. As tissues are damaged by inappropriate immune responses, further danger signals are released which may cause immune responses to develop to further self-antigens derived from the damaged tissue, in a process termed ‘epitope spreading’. It is thought that this may contribute to the chronic nature of most autoimmune diseases.

Genetic predisposition to autoimmunity is dictated by carriage of gene variants

Autoimmunity is most commonly triggered by specific environmental stimuli in genetically susceptible hosts. This means that some people are at higher risk of developing autoimmune conditions because of the genes they have inherited. The majority of the genes in the human genome exist within the population as a number of different variants, where each variant differs by slight changes to the DNA sequence, termed polymorphisms. Sometimes, these variants, called alleles, affect the regulation, level of expression or activity of the protein expressed from that gene. This means that for any particular biochemical pathway, it is possible to inherit variants of genes that tend to promote the activity of a pathway, or variants that inhibit the activity of that pathway. Because most pathways are regulated by dozens of genes, the inheritance of one or two strongly activating variants often has little impact on the function of the pathway. However, some individuals may inherit variants of several different genes that all happen to push the pathway towards either activation or inhibition. When this happens to the mechanisms of effector cell activation or tolerance, autoimmunity is more likely to arise. Notably, it is often observed that while one identical twin may develop autoimmune disease, the other twin, sharing exactly the same genomic DNA sequence and therefore the same gene variants, does not always develop disease. This means that the inheritance of gene variants merely increases the risk of disease development, and exposure to a particular environmental factor, such as infection, smoking or injury, is normally required to trigger the onset of autoimmune disease.

Genetic predisposition to autoimmunity is frequently driven by MHC genes

Genetic studies have identified the genes that are most commonly associated with different autoimmune diseases. By far the most commonly involved genes are those of the MHC complexes, and carriage of particular HLA alleles can markedly increase risk of autoimmunity. For example, inheritance of the B27 version of the HLA-B molecule (MHC-I) increases the risk of developing ankylosing spondylitis by more than 100-fold. Less pronounced associations are also observed in other autoimmune diseases. For example, variants of HLA-DRB1 are associated with rheumatoid arthritis, type 1 diabetes, multiple sclerosis and systemic lupus erythematosus. Many other non-MHC genes also contribute to risk of autoimmunity. For example, polymorphisms in genes encoding IL-2, the IL-2 receptor, CTLA4, NOD2, insulin and PTPN22 are also associated with risk of developing particular autoimmune diseases.

Infection by certain organisms may increase risk of autoimmunity in susceptible individuals

The onset of autoimmune diseases frequently occurs during or shortly after some form of infection. Notably, the tissues affected by the autoimmune responses are not always those that were involved with the infection. This suggests that the microbe has somehow triggered a host immune response to a self antigen. There are thought to be two major mechanisms by which exposure to a microbe may break tolerance to self antigens. The first, termed molecular mimicry, may be induced when a microbial antigen has a very similar amino acid sequence to a host antigen. Responses that are developed to the microbial antigen may therefore ‘spillover’ and cross-react with host antigens that are structurally very similar. The second potential mechanism is called bystander activation. Here, the exposure of APCs to strong activation signals, particularly from PAMP-signalling, results in robust responses to the microbial antigen, but also to self-peptides that were displayed by the same DC. The cells nearby the activated DC may also be acted upon by the cytokines and co-stimulators expressed by the activated DC, resulting in inappropriate priming of a T-cell response to self antigens.

We will now describe several examples of human autoimmune diseases.

Rheumatoid arthritis

Rheumatoid arthritis is a chronic inflammatory disease of the joints, affecting most frequently the fingers, shoulders, elbows and knees. The disease is characterised by inflammation in the synovium and the destruction of underlying cartilage and bone. The damage to these tissues is believed to be caused mainly by proteases and other enzymes released during the inflammatory response that results in degradation of the cartilage and ligaments. The processes that lead to the inflammatory response in the joint remain unclear, but involve the recruitment of T-cells, B-cells and macrophages into the synovium, and their subsequent activation by antigen. The inflammatory response appears to be driven mainly by Th17 responses, although there may also be a role for Th1 responses and the deposition of immune complexes within the joint. The self-antigen responsible for triggering these responses remains to be clarified. However, it has been proposed that collagen, or citrullinated peptides which are formed when arginine residues are chemically altered by inflammation or smoking, may be targets for the autoimmune response. Many patients have high titres of circulating antibodies responsive to citrullinated peptides, and also to the Fc portions of host IgG antibodies (these are called rheumatoid factors). Most strategies for the treatment of rheumatoid arthritis aim to limit inflammation in the joints. This is currently achieved most effectively by the periodic intravenous administration of antibodies against TNF-α. Newer treatments include IL-1 antagonist, antibody against the IL-6-receptor, and CLTA-4-Fc fusion proteins, which inhibit the activation of T-cells by B7 co-stimulators.

Systemic lupus erythematosus

Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) is a chronic autoimmune disease characterised most frequently by skin rashes, and also occasionally glomerulonephritis, vasculitis or arthritis. The disease progresses in a relapsing, remitting manner, and is approximately ten-fold more common in women than in men. SLE is one of the best examples of an autoimmune disease driven mainly by autoantibodies and the deposition of circulating immune complexes. Tissue damage arises anywhere in the body by the deposition of immune complexes in the blood vessels. The antibodies responsible for the formation of these immune complexes bind to self antigens from the nucleus of host cells; particularly nuclear DNA and associated chromatin proteins. Autoantibodies targeting these molecules reach high titres in SLE patients. The formation of antibody-antigen complexes is a normal part of the immune response to infection, and typically these complexes are cleared quickly from the circulation. It is thought that deficiencies in the clearance of apoptotic cells (leading to the accumulation of DNA and other nuclear antigens in the extracellular space), or of the complement system, which is involved in the clearance of immune complexes, increases risk of developing the disease. Production of the type I interferon IFN-α by plasmacytoid dendritic cells also appears to play a key role in SLE, as this cytokine is elevated in SLE patients and its administration may trigger symptoms similar to the disease.

Multiple sclerosis

The autoimmune responses associated with multiple sclerosis affect nerve conduction in the central nervous system, resulting in episodes of weakness and varying degrees of paralysis. The disease reflects damage to the myelin sheath of nerve fibres caused by products released from activated macrophages adjacent to the nerves. This macrophage activation appears to be driven by Th1 and Th17 responses to myelin-associated proteins, such as myelin basic protein and myelin oligodendrocyte glycoprotein. T-cells of both the Th1 and Th17 subset that respond to myelin proteins are observed in multiple sclerosis patients. Some evidence suggests that defects in regulatory T-cells may also contribute to the development of multiple sclerosis. It is not clear how tolerance to myelin proteins is broken in this disease, but molecular mimicry by exposure to viral antigens similar to myelin proteins has been proposed as one potential explanation. Currently, the disease is treated by periodic injections of the cytokine interferon β, although the mechanisms by which this reduces symptoms remain unclear.

Type 1 diabetes mellitus

Type 1 diabetes mellitus (T1DM) is a metabolic disease that results from the inability of the host to secrete sufficient quantities of insulin - the hormone required for cells to take up glucose from the blood. The resulting raised level of blood sugar (hyperglycaemia) promotes damage to the large arteries in the form of atherosclerosis, and the microvasculature may also be damaged, leading to impaired perfusion of tissues. The disease is caused by the destruction of the insulin secreting β-cells, found in the islets of Langerhans in the pancreas. These cells are damaged by autoimmune responses arising from CD4+ T-cells, CD8+ T-cells or autoantibody formation. The self antigen that triggers these responses is thought to be a protein expressed by the islet β-cells, possibly insulin itself. Tolerance to these proteins may be lost due to molecular mimicry, and risk of developing T1DM is strongly associated with specific alleles of HLA-DR3 and DR4. Currently, injections with insulin are used to control the disease, although future therapies may aim to restore β-cell populations and tolerance to these cells.

The success of tissue transplantation is dependent on immune responses

The process by which damaged tissue from one organism (the recipient) is surgically replaced by tissues from another organism (the donor), is termed transplantation. When blood cells or plasma are transferred from one organism to another, the process is termed transfusion. Most frequently, transplantations occur between two animals of the same species that do not share identical genes (i.e. they are not identical twins). The tissue transplanted in this type of procedure is termed an allograft, and the antigens present in the grafted tissue are termed alloantigens.

On the rare occasions when the donor and recipient share exactly the same DNA sequence (say, for example, both are identical twins), the transplantation is generally successful. However, even small differences in the genomic DNA sequence between the donor and the recipient result in slight differences in some of the protein antigens expressed by the donor and recipient tissues. These different antigens are quickly recognised as foreign, and the immune system of the donor will mount an immune response against the grafted tissue, causing the tissue to die and the transplanted organ to fail. This process is called rejection.

MHC genes are major targets of allograft rejection

Today, there is much interest in means of modifying the host immune response to allografts, since successfully preventing the immune rejection of the organ results in a successful transplant and good function of the transplanted organ. Responses to alloantigens may be mediated by T-cells, antibodies, or both. These responses are targeted against proteins that are coded for by genes that, within the population, exist as several different alleles containing amino-acid changing polymorphisms. Thus, the genes with the largest diversity of distinct alleles in the population are the most significant alloantigens in the context of rejection. These genes are termed histocompatibility genes. Because the genes of the major histocompatibility complex (MHC) are very highly polymorphic in human populations, they play a critical role in the outcome of allograft transplants, and are most frequently the target of rejection responses.

Donor MHC proteins may trigger recipient T-cell responses by direct and indirect presentation

Most of the donor cells that are delivered into the recipient animal express MHC-I molecules on their surface, and some will express MHC-II. Because the protein coding sequence of the MHC molecules is different between donor and recipient, when T-cells of the recipient bind directly to these MHC molecules expressed by donor cells, they are detected as being foreign. This is termed direct presentation (Figure 14.4).

![](image_rsrc4E6.jpg)

Both CD4+ and CD8+ T-cells can be activated by the direct presentation pathway, and the rejection response that results is generally rapid and severe. There are several reasons why the host response to direct MHC presentation is so strong. First, T-cells are adapted to recognise MHC molecules, and a large proportion of circulating naïve T-cells will express a TCR that binds to the donor MHC molecules, but which have not been selected out by mechanisms of tolerance. Second, when an APC expresses microbial antigens, most of its surface MHC molecules actually contain self-protein derived peptides, which limit the response, yet all of the MHC on an allograft cell express stimulatory MHC. Third, the donor MHC molecules will contain different anchor residues (see Chapter 9) and will therefore be able to express self peptides that the host T-cells have not seen before.

An alternative means by which host immune responses may be generated to donor MHC molecules is termed indirect presentation. In this pathway, host antigen presenting cells may ingest cellular material from the donated tissue which contains donor MHC molecules. The donor MHC molecules are degraded into peptides and the peptides and then expressed on intact host MHC. Because these peptides have slightly different amino acid sequences to the host versions, they are also detected as foreign, and a T-cell response is triggered. Through the mechanisms of cross-presentation (see Chapter 9), the indirect presentation pathway can induce both CD4+ and CD8+ alloreactive T-cells.

Graft rejection is partly dependent on induction of co-stimulators

The induction of responses to alloantigens generally occurs in the lymph nodes draining the graft tissue. Here, T-cell activation requires the expression of B-7 molecules, and other co-stimulators, by dendritic cells. Danger signals associated with damaged cells from the grafted tissue may also contribute to the activation of DCs and upregulation of co-stimulatory molecules. Therapeutic interventions have been developed to inhibit the co-stimulatory signal that recipient T-cells receive.

Graft rejection can be hyperacute, acute or chronic

During the rejection response, grafted tissues may be damaged by CD8+ T-cell mediated lysis of cells, by antibodies targeting donor antigens (termed alloantibodies), or by cytokines released from CD4+ T-cells. Such responses can occur within hours of transplantation, or can take days to occur, or can even progress slowly for weeks or months. Depending on the speed and strength of the response, rejections are typically characterised as being hyperacute, acute or chronic.

In hyperacute rejection, antibodies already exist in the host that are specific for antigens expressed by the donor endothelial cells. These antibodies then bind to the surface of the endothelial cells, causing their Fc portions to cluster in close proximity to each other. This in turn causes the activation of complement, and endothelial damage, inflammation and thrombus formation (blood clotting). The smaller vessels become blocked by the complement mediated blood clotting, and blood flow within the grafted tissue becomes too low for the graft to remain viable. The antibodies may pre-exist in the host as ABO-specific antibodies (see below), or be derived from prior exposure to alloantigens from earlier transplants, transfusions or pregnancies.

In acute rejection, the immune response to the grafted tissue develops after several days or weeks, reflecting the time taken for the alloantigens to activate naïve T-cells. In this type of rejection, CD8+ T-cells may cause lysis of donor parenchymal cells (the cells that perform the function of the organ). Alloantibodies directed against endothelial antigens, typically donor MHC molecules, may also contribute to acute rejection.

In chronic rejection, organ function is slowly lost over many months or years as a result of low-grade, chronic immune responses directed against the graft. Often, the arteries of the graft are affected, as the chronic T-cell mediated inflammation causes smooth muscle cell proliferation and eventually narrowing and occlusion of the blood vessels resulting in ischaemic injury to the organ.

Transplant success is enhanced by matching ABO and MHC alleles of donor and recipient

In order to limit the strength of the rejection response, the alleles of the ABO and MHC genes expressed by the donor must match as closely as possible the alleles of the ABO and MHC genes expressed by the recipient. This is done by carefully testing which variants of each MHC and ABO antigen is expressed by both donor and recipient using monoclonal antibodies and other techniques (termed tissue typing). The ABO blood group antigens must be precisely matched to prevent hyperacute rejection caused by pre-existing natural antibodies expressed by the host directed against blood group antigens (see below). Next, the donor tissue with the least mismatching HLA alleles is chosen for the transplant, since the lower the number of mismatches, the lower the strength of the resulting rejection response.

ABO blood group antigens

One of the most frequently performed types of transplant is the transfer of blood cells from one individual to another. The success of this procedure, termed transfusion, is entirely dependent on providing the recipient with blood from a compatible donor, where compatibility depends particularly on the expression of ABO blood group antigens.

ABO antigens are carbohydrate groups that are covalently attached to many different cell-surface proteins. The enzyme that attaches these groups to the proteins, a glycosyltransferase, is polymorphic in the human population. This means that some individuals express one form of the gene, and others express another form of the gene. All humans are able to synthesis the most basic unit of the antigen, termed the O-antigen. People who carry the ‘O’ allele of the gene cannot add any further sugars to this structure. However, some people may express the ‘A’ variant of the gene that additionally adds an N-acetylgalactosamine residue (A), and others express the ‘B’ variant that adds a galactose residue.

Individuals who are homozygous for the O, A or B alleles (i.e. OO, AA or BB) express proteins modified by O alone, the A antigen, or the B-antigen only, respectively. However, individuals who are heterozygous for A and B (i.e. AB) express both A and B antigens. Individuals carrying these gene variants are said to have the following blood groups: OO (blood group O); AA and AO (blood group A); BB and BO (blood group B); AB (blood group AB). Each of these antigens are abundantly expressed on the surface of red blood cells of the donor and recipient.

Individuals expressing each of these antigens are tolerant to the antigens they express on their own red blood cells. However, the serum of individuals who do not express either the A or B antigens contains natural antibodies which bind to these antigens. These antibodies are thought to arise because the A and B antigens are sugars that are expressed by the commensal intestinal bacteria, and these organisms trigger the development of IgM-secreting natural antibodies targeting these sugars in subjects who do not express these antigens on their own cells.

When blood cells expressing these antigens are transfused to a recipient expressing antibodies that target these antigens, the antibodies bind strongly to the blood cells causing the activation of complement, red blood cell lysis and systemic inflammation and coagulation which can be very severe. For this reason, only blood of compatible donors can be given to recipients, and this compatibility is dependent on the ABO blood group antigens expressed by donor and recipient. For example, individuals with blood group O express antibodies to both A and B, and therefore can only accept blood cells from donors who do not express A or B antigens, i.e. O donors. By contrast, AB recipients express no antibodies to A or B, and can therefore receive blood from any donor.

There are other red blood cell associated antigens that differ within the human population, but most of these have limited potential to cause haemolysis and therefore do not normally require to be carefully matched. An exception is the rhesus D antigen (RhD), which is present in a functional form on the blood cells of some human subjects, but altered or absent from others. The latter individuals are termed rhesus D negative, or RhD-. RhD- mothers can develop antibodies to RhD if their baby is RhD+ and there is some mixing of blood during birth. This is not a problem during the first pregnancy and birth, but can cause problems for subsequent pregnancies. For this reason, RhD antibody (taken from the serum of blood donors) can be administered to the mother shortly after birth to prevent her from generating antibodies against RhD, and therefore reducing the risk of complications to subsequent pregnancies. The blood groups of individuals are often annotated by a compound form of their ABO and RhD status, for example, AB negative or O positive, where positive or negative relates to expression of the RhD antigen.

Notably, ABO and rhesus antigens are also expressed on other cells of the body, and these antigens must also be taken into account when performing solid organ transplants.

Table 14.2: Common ABO blood groups and their associated natural antibodies

![](image_rsrc4E7.jpg)

Haematopoietic stem cell transplantation and acute graft vs host disease

A number of diseases, or clinical interventions such as chemotherapy, may result in a deficiency in blood cell production. This can be treated by allogeneic transplantation of haematopoietic stem cells. Because haematopoietic cells are rapidly rejected by immunocompetent donors, two conditions must be met to enhance success of the graft. First, the donors must be very well matched, ideally expressing identical MHC alleles. Second, the donor should be depleted of their own bone marrow stem cells and immunosuppressed prior to the transplant. In successful grafts, the transplanted stem cells migrate to and repopulate the empty niches in the host bone marrow and begin to produce new blood cells. However, two major problems that can occur after bone marrow transplant are immunosuppression and graft vs host disease.

Patients may demonstrate immunosuppression, in terms of poor immune responses to pathogens, because of the loss of memory T- and B-cells caused by the treatment they received to reduce numbers of their own, endogenous bone marrow cells. Such individuals are effectively naïve once again to these pathogens, and during the time required to develop new memory cells responsive to these pathogens they may be at risk of serious infections.

Graft vs host disease (GVHD) occurs when the organs of the recipient (or host) are attacked by T-cells derived from the graft. In the acute setting, GVHD results in the T-cell mediated killing of host epithelial cells, causing symptoms that can be severe, ranging from diarrhoea and rash to jaundice and intestinal bleeding. Chronic GVHD, however, is characterised by the slow deterioration of function of certain organs. The mechanisms of this response remain unclear, although it is associated with T-cell dependent fibrosis in the tissues.

Immunosuppression may be used to enhance the success of transplantation

Most therapeutic approaches to reducing the risk of organ rejection revolve around the inhibition of T-cell responses to alloantigens. For example, the calcineurin inhibitors cyclosporine and tacrolimus, are commonly administered before and after transplantation, to limit TCR-signalling and the activation of T-cells. Rapamycin is sometimes used to inhibit T-cell proliferation, and mycophenolate motefil is selectively toxic to lymphocytes. Monoclonal antibodies against CD3 and CD25 (the IL-2 receptor α-chain) may also be used to inhibit T-cell activation. Likewise, a CTLA-4-Fc fusion protein has been developed that binds to B7 and inhibits co-stimulatory signals, resulting in reduced T-cell activation. Anti-inflammatory drugs, particularly corticosteroids, may also help to reduce the damage caused by the influx of inflammatory cells into the graft tissue. All of these drugs are currently used to prevent allograft rejection, or are in late stage clinical trials for this purpose, and dramatically reduce the incidence of graft rejection. However, their use over the longer term is associated with increased risk of infection arising from the immunosuppression.

Summary

1) Immunological tolerance is the lack of adaptive responses to self-antigens and broadly speaking consists of two major mechanisms: central tolerance and peripheral tolerance

2) Central tolerance is the deletion, suppression of function, or receptor editing (B-cells only) of self-reactive lymphocytes that occurs in the generative lymphoid organs, i.e. bone marrow and thymus, resulting in the functional deletion or inactivation of the majority of potentially self-reactive naïve T- and B-cells

3) Peripheral tolerance may cause self-reactive B-cells or T-cells that have escaped central tolerance to die by apoptosis, differentiate into an unresponsive state called anergy, or be functionally suppressed by other cells

4) Most CD4+ T-cells that respond to self antigen undergo apoptosis in the thymus, although some become regulatory T-cells that serve to inhibit immune responses (termed suppression) through the secretion of anti-inflammatory and immunosuppressive cytokines, particularly IL-10 and TGF-β

5) Regulatory T-cells (T-regs) generated in the thymus are called natural T-regs, and those that are generated in the periphery are termed inducible T-regs

6) Commonly expressed markers of CD4+ regulatory T-cells are the transcription factor FOXP3 and the IL-2 receptor α chain, CD25

7) Anergy is a long lasting lack of capacity to mediate effector functions that may be triggered in naïve T-cells encountering antigen in the absence of co-stimulators, such as B-7

8) B-cells that express antibodies that bind tightly to self antigens may re-organise their BCR genes in an attempt to create a receptor that does not bind self-antigen in a process called receptor editing

9) B-cells that fail to develop an antibody that does not bind to self antigen undergo apoptosis and are deleted in the bone marrow

10) B-cells may also become anergic should they encounter antigen in the absence of T-cell help

11) Autoimmunity is caused by the failure of tolerance and the generation of adaptive responses to self antigens, which may be T-cell or antibody mediated

12) Genetic factors, particularly carriage of certain MHC alleles, combine with environmental factors to modify the risk of developing autoimmune diseases

13) The breakdown of tolerance to self-antigens may be triggered by exposure to pathogens expressing a molecule very similar to a host molecule, termed molecular mimicry, or by bystander activation of lymphocytes, where self antigens happen to be presented in an environment of strong co-stimulators driven by other (typically infectious) stimuli

14) Rheumatoid arthritis, characterised by chronic inflammation of the joints, is driven by Th1 and Th17 responses to self antigens and often responds well to treatment with anti-TNF-α

15) Systemic lupus erythematosus is most frequently characterised by skin rashes and is caused by the formation of antibodies to self DNA or other nuclear antigens resulting in immune complex deposition and complement activation in the tissues

16) Multiple sclerosis is driven by a Th1 and Th17 cell response to myelin proteins in the central nervous system that cause damage to nerve sheaths, resulting in muscle weakness

17) Type 1 diabetes mellitus is an insulin deficiency caused by autoimmune destruction of insulin-secreting β-cells in the pancreas

18) An allograft refers to the tissue transplanted from donor to recipient, when each is a member of the same species but not genetically identical

19) When immune responses are triggered by alloantigens (i.e. antigens expressed by the graft cells but not the host cells), the transplanted organ can be damaged by the immune response, termed rejection

20) MHC proteins expressed by graft cells are the major alloantigen recognised by the host because they are highly polymorphic in the population

21) MHC proteins may stimulate host T-cells by the direct presentation pathway, or by the indirect presentation pathway, where host APCs ingest, process and present donor MHC peptides to host T-cells

22) Graft rejection can be hyperacute, occurring within hours if the host expresses pre-existing antibodies to donor antigens, acute, occurring within days to weeks should effector T-cells develop in response to alloantigens, or chronic, where organ function is lost slowly over several months, most frequently because of chronic inflammatory responses in the blood vessels resulting in vessel narrowing and ischaemia

23) The ABO blood group antigens are major determinants of the success of red blood cell transfusions, and recipient and donor ABO type must be appropriately matched for successful transfusion

24) When haematopoietic cells are transplanted, often called a bone marrow transplant, there is a risk that the transplanted T-cells will attack the host tissues in a process called graft vs host disease

25) The success of transplantation is markedly improved by the use of immunosuppressive drugs, such as cyclosporine and tacrolimus

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autoimmunity>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transplantation_immunology>

Schwartz RH. Historical overview of immunological tolerance. Cold Spring Harb Perspect Biol 4:a006908 (2012) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3312674/>

Matsumoto M, et al. Which model better fits the role of aire in the establishment of self-tolerance: the transcription model or the maturation model? Front Immunol 4:210 (2013) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3717480/>

---

# Chapter 15: Mucosal and cutaneous immunity

Most frequently, invading pathogens gain access to the tissues of the body through the epithelial barriers of the skin, gastrointestinal tract and respiratory tract. Each of these organs presents a very large surface area for microbial colonisation, and therefore millions of potential points of entry for pathogens. One of the greatest challenges faced by the immune system is the need to ensure adequate defence of these large surfaces. More challenging still, the immune system is required to tolerate the hundreds of different species of commensal microbes which live on our skin and in our intestines and cause us no harm, since such responses could cause severe damage to these organs. To meet these challenges, specialised forms of immunity, involving specific cellular and anatomical adaptations, have evolved to protect these different tissues. Because the gastrointestinal tract is faced with some of the most difficult challenges in immunity, we will begin with a discussion of immunity in the gut.

The immune system of the gut must tolerate an expansive commensal microbiota

We are born as a single species - Homo sapiens. However, from the moment we enter the world, our outer epithelial surfaces are colonised by hundreds of different species of microbe. The gut, in particular, is colonised rapidly by an array of bacterial species that specialise in growth within this niche. The vast majority of these organisms are harmless, and many confer some benefit on the host, such as through the production of vitamins or the ability to digest certain nutrients that we otherwise cannot utilise. These organisms are collectively referred to as the commensal microbiota.

Remarkably, the adult human body contains ~10 trillion human cells, but as many as 100 trillion bacterial cells, prompting some to point out that we are each in fact not merely one species, but a co-operative community of hundreds of different species working together. The majority of these bacteria live in our intestines, but are not uniformly distributed throughout the gut, as the density of bacterial colonisation varies greatly in different gut locations. For example, in the mouth, bacterial densities may reach 107 to 109 organisms per ml (often given as colony forming units, or CFU, per ml). However, in the stomach, where the acidic conditions are toxic for all but the hardiest of organisms, bacterial numbers range from 100 to 103 CFU/ ml. The conditions in the small intestine, however, are more amenable to bacterial growth and bacterial load increases gradually from 100 \- 104 CFU/ml in the duodenum and the jejunum to 100 \- 105 CFU/ml in the proximal ileum, and reaches 105 \- 108 CFU/ml only in the most distal portion of the terminal ileum in a transition zone before the caecum. Bacterial density remains relatively low in the small intestine because peristalsis ensures that food transits quickly (i.e. within several hours), through the small intestine. By contrast, the human large intestine is one of the most densely populated environments on earth, with bacterial density reaching as high as 1010 to 1011 CFU/ml. This is because undigested food and other nutrients remain in the large intestine for a long time, and the environment within the lumen of the large intestine is specifically maintained by the host to be conducive for the growth of certain species of fermentative bacteria which help to digest the foods that we are unable to digest in the small intestine.

However, every time we eat we face the risk of ingesting a pathogen. Such pathogens include many different species of virus, bacteria or parasite, and many of these organisms are able to cause damage to the intestine, resulting in disease. Some pathogens may also use the gastrointestinal wall as a point of entry into the body.

The first major challenge faced by the immune system in the gut is therefore distinguishing between the ‘friendly’ commensal organisms that are constantly present but which are of no threat as long as they remain in the lumen, and the ‘hostile’ organisms that may cause disease. This balance must be carefully weighed, because if an immune response is developed against the commensals of the gut, debilitating chronic inflammation and tissue damage can occur. On the other hand, if the host does not respond with sufficient rigour to intestinal pathogens, they will become easily parasitized and may suffer from similar tissue damage and malnutrition attributed to the infection.

Innate immune defences in the gastrointestinal tract

The principle mechanism of innate immunity in the gut is the continuous layer of epithelial cells that form the primary barrier to invasion by microbes. These cells bind tightly to each other through the expression of tight junctions, which are formed by proteins such as zonula-occludins-1 and claudin. These structures help to ensure that there are no gaps between the cells to allow the entry of pathogens or large antigens into the body.

In addition to forming this barrier, certain specialised forms of epithelial cell contribute to other aspects of innate immunity in the gut. Intestinal epithelial cells differentiate from a common stem cell precursor, which divides in the intestinal crypts, into several different specialised forms, including (i) enteroabsorptive cells, which absorb nutrients; (ii) paneth cells, which secrete anti-microbial molecules, (iii) goblet cells, which secret mucous and (iv) M-cells, the function of will be discussed below (Figure 15.1).

![](image_rsrc4E8.jpg)

Mucous is constantly secreted by goblet cells and this serves as a protective layer to prevent bacterial contact with the underlying epithelial cells. This layer is formed of two parts: a thin section that is composed of membrane-bound mucin proteins expressed on the surface of the epithelial cells, called the glycocalyx, and on top of this, a thicker section that forms a hydrated gel composed of soluble secreted glycoproteins including other types of mucin that are not attached to the epithelial cell membrane. Examples of membrane-bound mucins include MUC1, MUC12, MUC13 and MUC17. Examples of soluble, secreted mucins include MUC2, MUC5 and MUC6. Together, these two layers may extend for ~1 mm from the epithelial surface and serve to trap bacteria and flush them out with peristalsis. Inflammatory signalling can lead to marked increases in mucin production, which enhances gut barrier function.

The next major innate immune defence against infection in the gut is the production of anti-microbial peptides. Some of these, such as cathelicidin LL-37, are secreted continuously by absorptive epithelial cells. Others are produced by specialised Paneth cells, that exist in the base of the crypts and store large quantities of various anti-microbial peptides within cytoplasmic granules. Paneth cells release these anti-microbial peptides at a low level continuously to maintain sterility of the crypt, since this is where the sensitive epithelial stem cells reside. However, during infection, paneth cells can degranulate more rapidly, thereby secreting large quantities of anti-microbial peptides to enhance defence against invading microbes. In man, the major anti-microbial peptides secreted by Paneth cells are the α-defensins. These are produced as inactive precursors that are activated by proteases, (which are also released by the Paneth cells), to yield peptides that insert into the membranes of microbes (but not mammalian cells) and cause their lysis.

Some of the key pattern recognition receptors described in earlier chapters are also expressed in the gut. For example, epithelial cells express the peptidoglycan sensors NOD-1 and NOD-2. These cytosolic proteins respond only to bacteria that have invaded the epithelial cell, an event commonly associated with pathogenic but not commensal bacteria. Intestinal epithelial cells also express various Toll-like receptors (TLRs), such as TLR5 and TLR9. However, unlike in most other cells of the body, the stimulation of TLR-signalling by PAMPs in intestinal epithelial cells does not trigger inflammation. Instead, a programme is induced that results in enhanced barrier function, including the upregulation of tight junction protein expression, and increased survival and proliferation of the epithelial cells. There is evidence that some TLRs, such as TLR5, are expressed on the basolateral surface of intestinal epithelial cells, but not the apical surface. This localised TLR expression means the cells are likely to respond only to bacteria that breach the epithelial cell layer, thereby preventing unnecessary responses to commensals which remain in the lumen.

Further innate defences of the gastrointestinal tract include the low pH of the stomach and the constant peristalsis of the small intestine, which limits the potential of pathogens to remain in one place for long enough to adhere to epithelial cells and invade the underlying tissues.

Adaptive immune defences in the gastrointestinal tract

As mentioned in the introduction, the adaptive immune system of the gut faces quite distinct challenges from most other tissues of the body, and therefore behaves in many ways that are quite different from immunity in other parts of the body. Generally speaking, however, the adaptive responses that appear to play the most critical roles in maintaining gut homeostasis include the transport of IgA into the gut lumen, the preferential polarisation of effector T-cells to the Th17 phenotype and the continued presence of large numbers of CD4+ regulatory T-cells.

GALT and MLN

In earlier chapters, we described the structure of the lymph node (Chapter 3), and outlined the mechanisms by which this encapsulated structure shepherds B-cells, T-cells and antigen presenting cells together to generate effective immune responses. The gut also contains lymphatic vessels, which drain the loosely structured tissue immediately beneath the epithelial barrier, termed the lamina propria, into the mesenteric lymph nodes (MLN). However, the gut also contains other structures, termed gut-associated lymphoid tissues, or GALT, where T-cells, B-cells and APCs can meet to generate immune responses. Similar structures are present in other mucosal tissues, and are generically referred to as mucosal-associated lymphoid tissues (MALT). Unlike lymph nodes, these structures are not encapsulated and are composed of less well defined collections of lymphocytes and APCs. Some of these cellular aggregates are large and diffuse, others are quite small and similar to the follicles found in lymph nodes. One particular form of GALT, termed Peyer’s patches, are anatomically and functionally quite similar to the follicles found in lymph nodes (Figure 15.1). These large collections of cells contain germinal centres, consisting of B-cells, follicular helper T-cells, follicular DCs and macrophages. Peyer’s patches exist beneath M-cells (described below), and are most numerous in the ileum. Unlike lymph nodes, Peyer’s patches do not receive antigen from draining lymphatic vessels, but from other routes as we will now describe.

Antigen sampling of the gut lumen contents is achieved by M-cells and DCs

Antigens from the gut lumen are delivered to APCs in the GALT, including Peyer’s patches, by three major mechanisms: (i) transcytosis of antigen through overlying M-cells, (ii) capture of antigen from the lumen by antigen-sampling DCs and (iii) capture of antigen that has breached the epithelial barrier by other DCs.

Microfold cells, (or M-cells), are so called because they lack the long microvilli expressed by most other types of intestinal epithelial cell. These cells are adapted to internalise bacteria, or other large antigen-bearing structures, from the gut lumen by phagocytosis or endocytosis. To enhance this process, they are covered by a thin glycocalyx and express various receptors, such as lectins, that bind to bacterial surface molecules. Unlike macrophages or DCs, M-cells do not extensively digest or degrade their ingested material. Instead, the material is transported through the cell, and is passed out relatively unaltered by exocytosis through the basolateral surface. In this manner, intact, often living, microbes are transported from the lumen of the bowel into the lamina propria. These cells are chiefly found overlying Peyer’s patches and other GALT. APCs of these tissues, particularly DCs, are then able to ingest the material, and process it for presentation to lymphocytes.

Luminal antigens may also be captured by so-called antigen-capture, or effector dendritic cells. These cells, which are CD11b+ and CX3CR1+, reside within the lamina propria just beneath the epithelial cells. They extend long cytoplasmic extensions, called dendrites, that penetrate the tight junctions between the epithelial cells and enter the luminal space. Once in the lumen, these processes capture antigens. The DC then retracts its process, apparently causing no damage to the integrity of the epithelial layer, and migrates to a mesenteric lymph node to present its antigens to T-cells.

Another type of DC present in the lamina propria, termed regulatory dendritic cells, do not extend processes into the lumen but rather capture antigen that has already traversed the epithelial barrier by other means (such as at points of damage or weakness). These cells are CD103+ CX3CR1-, and tend to polarise naïve T-cells towards the regulatory T-cell phenotype, as discussed below.

Gut lymphocyte homing

When lymphocytes are activated in MLN or Peyer’s patches, they are programmed to become effector cells that preferentially home from the blood back to the intestinal lamina propria. This is achieved largely by the presence of retinoic acid, which is produced by DCs in these structures. Thymic stromal lymphopoietin, which is present in GALT, triggers DCs to express the enzyme retinaldehyde dehydrogenase, which converts dietary vitamin A to retinoic acid. This molecule causes activated lymphocytes to express the chemokine receptor CCR9, and the integrin α4β7. The lamina propria post-capillary venule endothelial cells express the adhesion molecule MadCAM-1, which binds α4β7. The chemokine CCL25, which is the ligand for CCR9, is also expressed in the lamina propria. By these two mechanisms, lymphocytes that are activated in the gut tend to home back to the gut to perform their effector functions. These lymphocytes eventually settle and secrete their products, such as cytokines or IgA, into the lamina propria. Because the lamina propria extends throughout the gut, effector cells generated in a single location may end up being distributed throughout the length of the gut, and not just at the site of the original infection.

IgA antibody is produced in the lamina propria and secreted into the gut lumen

One of the major defences against colonisation or invasion of the gut wall by pathogens is IgA. These antibodies tend to form dimers, with a linker peptide called the J-chain covalently bound to the ends of two Fc portions. IgA antibodies are highly resistant to degradation by digestive enzymes, and for this reason are well-adapted for use in the gut.

Most IgA is produced by plasma cells that reside in the GALT, because they were generated in the gut and home to this location for the reasons outlined in the above section. Naïve B-cells that are activated in the gut tend to class-switch to IgA production by both T-dependent and T-independent mechanisms. In T-dependent responses, the combination of CD40:CD40L signalling derived from direct contact with an activated helper T-cell, and TGF-β released from local DCs, promotes IgA class switching. In T-independent responses, the stimulation of lamina propria DCs by TLR ligands can lead to production of APRIL and BAFF. These mediators, in conjunction with TGF-β, which is generally present in the lamina propria, promote IgA class switching in B-cells even in the absence of T-cell help. It is thought that retinoic acid also enhances the switch to IgA production. This critical role of TGF-β in specialising immune responses for use in the gut is a common theme that will recur in the following sections.

IgA produced in the lamina propria is transported into the gut lumen by the process of transcytosis (Figure 15.2). This is mediated by binding of the J-chain, which is linked to IgA molecules synthesised in the gut, by the poly-Ig receptor expressed on the basal surface (i.e. the underside) of intestinal epithelial cells. The epithelial cells then endocytose the poly-Ig receptor, containing its IgA cargo, from the basolateral side of the cell, through internal vesicles, and onto the apical (luminal) surface of the cell. Here, proteases act on the poly-Ig receptor to cleave off the extracellular domain. This domain, now referred to as the secretory component, remains bound to the IgA molecule. The complex of IgA, J-chain and secretory component then enters the lumen to bind to microbes to limit their adhesion to epithelial cells. In addition to IgA, a small amount of IgM is also transported across intestinal epithelial cells by the same mechanisms.

![](image_rsrc4E9.jpg)

T-lymphocytes play key roles in maintaining the balance of immune function in the gut

T-lymphocytes are highly abundant in the gut, and play key roles both in the induction of effector immune responses to pathogens and also in the maintenance of tolerance to the commensal microbiota. Major subsets of T-cells present in the gut include the CD4+ helper T-cells and regulatory T-cells of the lamina propria, and intraepithelial lymphocytes, which nestle between the epithelial cells at the interface between the tissues and the gut lumen.

The most abundant subset of CD4+ helper T-cell in the gut is Th17. This is because the molecules present in the lamina propria and GALT tend to favour the polarisation of naïve T-cells to the Th17 subset when they are first activated. Th17 cells play key roles in the maintenance of gut barrier integrity. They secrete the cytokines IL-17 and IL-22, which are detected by epithelial cells and thereby cause upregulation of genes involved in barrier enhancement, such as β-defensins and mucins. It is thought that the combined presence of IL-6 produced during innate immune responses, and TGF-β which is constitutively present in the gut, promotes the formation of Th17 cells in the GALT (Figure 15.3).

![](image_rsrc4EA.jpg)

By contrast, infection with intestinal worms (helminths) triggers a strong Th2 response. Th2 effector cells that home back to the gut after activation and expansion may then enhance defence against worms by secreting cytokines such as IL-4 and IL-13. These cytokines promote mucous secretion, and the smooth muscle cell contraction required to stimulated gut motility. Both of these responses help to eliminate worms from the intestinal lumen.

Intraepithelial T-cells tend to be CD8+ in man, but are unusual in that they express receptors of low sequence diversity. For this reason, it is thought that these cells respond to relatively conserved molecules derived from pathogens or the commensal microbiota. Their precise contribution to gut immunity currently remains unclear.

Mechanisms of prevention of inflammatory responses to commensal microbiota

One of the key challenges faced by the gut immune system is the requirement to tolerate the vast numbers of microbes, PAMPs and food-borne antigens that are separated from the underlying tissues of the gut by the one-cell thick layer of the intestinal epithelium. If inflammatory responses were generated to these bacteria, PAMPs or antigens, the resulting chronic inflammation would result in severe damage to the delicate tissues of the gut.

To avoid such inappropriate responses, a number of mechanisms are in place to restrain immune responses in the gut, thereby maintaining the lamina propria in a non-inflammed state for most of the time. The first of these mechanisms is the large abundance of FOXP3+ regulatory T-cells in the lamina propria of the gut. These cells are abundant in the gut because the activation of naïve T-cells by CD103+ regulatory DCs, present in the lamina propria, together with signals from TGF-β and retinoic acid which are also abundant in this anatomical location, tends to result in their polarisation to the FOXP3+ regulatory T-cell subset. As described in earlier chapters, these cells secrete cytokines, such as TGF-β and IL-10 which dampen inflammatory signalling and the responses of effector lymphocytes. Indeed, IL-10 and TGF-β are understood to play essential roles in the prevention of gut inflammation to commensal microbiota, as deficiency in these cytokines can promote chronic inflammatory bowel diseases. It is thought that the preferential induction of T-regs when there is an absence of innate immune ‘danger’ signals plays a role in so-called oral tolerance. This is observed when protein antigens are given in large doses by the oral route in the absence of co-stimulator-inducing molecules, such as PAMPs. After such treatment, the host is often unable to generate effective adaptive responses to these antigens in the peripheral tissues.

A second mechanism by which inflammation is limited in the gut is the carefully partitioned expression of pattern recognition receptors (such as TLRs), in intestinal cells. For example, TLR5 is expressed on the basolateral surface, but not the apical surface of the epithelial cell. This means that the receptor can only be activated when bacterial flagellin breaches the barrier. NOD1 and NOD2 are expressed in the cytosol of intestinal epithelial cells, and only respond to bacteria that penetrate and enter these cells. Thus, when the commensal bacteria are in their normal location (i.e. in the lumen and not invading the gut wall), there is limited PRR-signalling and no cue for inflammation.

A third mechanism to reduce potential for inflammation is the low-level of expression of PRRs by intestinal macrophages and DCs. Fourth, the signalling pathways downstream of PRRs are specialised within intestinal epithelial cells to result not in inflammatory cytokine production, but in mediators of barrier enhancement, such as the secretion of mucin, antimicrobial peptides, survival, proliferation and enhanced tight junction expression. Likewise, TLR-signalling in intestinal DCs and macrophages tends to be blunted by the expression of inhibitory signalling molecules, and these cells preferentially secrete IL-10 rather than pro-inflammatory cytokines, when encountering PAMPs.

Inflammatory bowel diseases can result from dysregulation of innate or adaptive immunity

Because of the large abundance of bacteria, PAMPs and antigens in the gut, the typical result of breakdown of any of the mechanisms described in the above section is chronic inflammation of the gut wall. Such diseases are referred to as inflammatory bowel diseases (IBD). Two of the most common forms of IBD in man are Crohn’s disease and ulcerative colitis.

Crohn’s disease causes inflammatory lesions to form most frequently in the terminal ileum, but it may also affect any other part of the gastro-intestinal tract. Genetic evidence suggests that it is most frequently caused by defects in the innate immune defences of intestinal epithelial cells, such as NOD2. The resulting chronic translocation of microbes, thought to be mainly commensal derived, into the underlying tissues is the cause of a relapsing-remitting inflammation driven in large part by Th1 cells.

Ulcerative colitis affects the rectum most commonly, but may also spread upwards with time to affect other parts of the colon. Unlike Crohn’s disease which penetrates deep into the gut wall, lesions of ulcerative colitis affect only the mucosa. Mouse models of ulcerative colitis suggest that regulatory T-cells, IL-10 and TGF-β play key roles in preventing colonic inflammation. However, the mechanisms of the disease in man remain poorly understood.

Innate immunity in the respiratory system

Immune defences of the mucosal surfaces of the respiratory system face many of the same challenges posed by the gut, including the large surface area and frequent exposure to environmental microbes and antigens. However, the microbial density of the airway surfaces is much lower than found in the gut. Bacterial density in the respiratory tract is highest in the upper regions, and reduces with depth into the airways to the point where the alveoli are typically sterile. Nevertheless, many of the mechanisms of immune control as described for the gut in the previous sections are also active in the respiratory system.

With respect to innate immunity, the primary defence against infection is the epithelial cell barrier which, like the gut epithelium, maintains its resistance to penetration by the expression of tight junctions between the cells. Respiratory epithelial cells also express mucins, and anti-microbial peptides, such as defensins and cathelicidins. Members of the collectin family, including surfactant protein A and surfactant protein D, are secreted onto the surface of the respiratory epithelium. These proteins bind to carbohydrate PAMPs on micro-organisms, including viruses and bacteria, resulting in their inactivation or clearance. One of the key means of retaining a low microbial burden (of pathogens and commensals) in the lung is the constant upward flow of mucous, which traps inhaled microbes and eventually delivers them into the oesophagus. This flow of mucous depends on the beating of cilia, expressed by epithelial cells, and their damage, such as observed in heavy smokers, can lead to increased frequency of respiratory infections. Interestingly, while most macrophages in the body are strongly phagocytic and secrete pro-inflammatory cytokines in response to PAMPs, alveolar macrophages are adapted to be much less phagocytic, less prone to inflammatory cytokine production and tend to secrete the anti-inflammatory cytokines IL-10 and TGF-β.

Adaptive immunity in the respiratory system

As in the gut, the secretion of IgA onto the surface of the respiratory epithelium plays a key role in defence against infection. This is achieved by the same mechanism of transcytosis using the poly-Ig receptor expressed by epithelial cells as described above. IgA-secreting plasma cells of the respiratory system home to this location by expression of the chemokine receptor CCR10, which detects the chemokine CCL28 that is abundant in the respiratory lamina propria. Some IgG is also secreted into the airways, particularly in the upper parts of the respiratory tract, and this is mediated by FcRn. Mast cells are abundant in the lung, and the presence of IgE specific for inhaled allergens can cause symptoms of hay fever and asthma, as will be discussed in more depth in Chapter 17. Also similar to the gut, there are several different populations of DC, including regulatory and effector DCs. For example, CD103+ CD11b- effector DCs sample the contents of the airway spaces by extending long cytoplasmic processes between the airway epithelial cells, and then migrate to the draining lymph nodes, (often the peribronchial and mediastinal lymph nodes), to initiate T-cell responses. These T-cell responses tend to be biased towards the Th2 subset.

Innate immunity in the skin

Although the surface area of the adult human gut is ~300 m2, that of the skin is only ~2 m2, and the microbial density of the skin is also much lower than that of the gut. Nevertheless, some unique challenges are faced by the cutaneous immune system, most notably due to its susceptibility to scratches and cuts that may cause breaches of the outermost protective barriers. The principle physical barrier against invasion is the epidermis, which, unlike the single-cell thick gut and respiratory epithelium, is composed of multiple layers of specialised epithelial cells called keratinocytes. These cells proliferate continuously, and as the outermost layer of cells mature, they undergo apoptosis to leave behind long-lasting deposits of tough keratin protein that form a resilient outer barrier (Figure 15.4). Keratinocytes express a wide range of TLR and NLR pattern recognition receptors and are able to initiate immune responses through the secretion of pro-inflammatory cytokines, such as IL-1β, IL-18 and TNF-α. They also secrete various anti-microbial molecules, including defensins and cathelicidins.

![](image_rsrc4EB.jpg)

Adaptive immunity in the skin

The epidermis (which refers to the outer layer of skin above the basement membrane), is permeated by extensive cytoplasmic projections of a specialised type of DC called Langerhans cells. These cells express the C-type lectin receptor langerin, and numerous pattern recognition receptors. When these Langerhans cells, or other types of DC which are also present in the skin, are activated by exposure to PAMPs, they become mobile and migrate to the draining lymph nodes to present antigen to T-cells. Effector T-cells stimulated by such DCs are then predisposed to home back to the skin by expression of the chemokine receptors CCR4 and CCR10 (which detect the chemokines CCL17 and CCL27 in the skin), and the cutaneous lymphocyte antigen (CLA), which binds to E-selectin expressed by endothelial cells. This skin-homing property appears to be imparted on T-cells by the combined actions of vitamin D and ultraviolet light from sunlight on cutaneous DCs. These factors together result in the generation of di-hydroxy vitamin D3 (1,25(OH)2D3) by DCs that modifies T-cell CCR expression and hence their homing properties. The majority of the T-cells in the skin are present in the dermis layer, beneath the basement membrane, but some intra-epithelial lymphocytes with receptors of limited diversity are also present in the epidermis. Th1, Th2, Th17 and Treg subsets are all found in the skin.

Summary

1) The most frequent point of entry for pathogens into the body are the epithelial barriers of the skin, gastrointestinal tract and respiratory tract

2) Specialised forms of immunity are present in each of these locations to deal with the unique challenges faced by each surface

3) The human gut is colonised by ~100 trillion commensal bacteria, which are either harmless or confer a benefit to us, and these organisms are tolerated by the immune system

4) Despite this, the gut must remain able to induce vigorous immune responses to intestinal pathogens

5) A major mechanism of innate immunity in the gut, respiratory system and skin is the physical barrier created by the continuous layer of epithelial cells that bind tightly to each other through the expression of tight junction protein complexes

6) Mucous secretion by gut and respiratory epithelial cells traps microbes and then, through peristalsis in the gut and the actions of cilia in the lung, the mucous is flushed out to eventually expel these microbes

7) A major defence against infection in the skin, respiratory system and gut is the secretion of anti-microbial peptides, such as cathelicidins, α-defensins and β-defensins, by epithelial cells

8) In addition to the mesenteric lymph nodes, adaptive responses in the gut may be initiated in loose, unencapsulated collections of APCs and lymphocytes termed gut associated lymphoid tissues (GALT)

9) Peyer’s patches are a form of GALT that are structurally and functionally similar to the follicles of lymph nodes, and are present mainly in the ileum

10) Antigen sampling of the contents of the gut lumen is achieved by microfold (M)-cells, which transcytose intact antigens into the lamina propria, effector dendritic cells, which project dendrites between intestinal epithelial cells into the lumen then migrate to lymph nodes to initiate effector T-cell responses, and regulatory dendritic cells, which capture antigens in the lamina propria and tend to stimulate regulatory T-cell development

11) Lymphocytes generated in GALT or MLN tend to home back to the gut by expression of the chemokine receptor CCR9, and the integrin α4β7, which are induced by retinoic acid converted from dietary vitamin A by intestinal DCs

12) T-lymphocytes play key roles in the balance between immune responses and immune regulation and the limitation of inflammatory responses in the gut

13) The activation of naïve T-cells by antigen in the gut tends normally to lead to the generation of immunosuppressive FOXP3+ regulatory T-cells, but during infections, Th17 cells tend to be generated, due to the combined presence of IL-6 and TGF-β in the gut

14) The Th17 cytokines IL-17 and IL-22 promote epithelial barrier function through induction of epithelial cell survival, proliferation, mucous production and defensin secretion

15) The major humoral defence in the gut and respiratory system is the secretion of dimeric IgA into the lumen

16) IgA produced by lamina-propria B-cells is dimeric and covalently bound to the J-chain, which is recognised by the poly-Ig receptor expressed on the basal surface of intestinal epithelial cells

17) IgA is then transcytosed by the epithelial cells and released into the lumen

18) Immune responses to commensal bacteria, PAMPs and food antigens are suppressed by various mechanisms to ensure that inflammation is not initiated in the healthy gut

19) Such mechanisms include the large number of regulatory T-cells and regulatory DCs in the lamina propria, the carefully sequestered expression of pattern recognition receptors, such as TLRs and NLRs in intestinal cells, and the induction of barrier enhancement programmes rather than inflammatory cytokine production in PAMP-stimulated epithelial cells

20) Lymphocytes activated in the skin or respiratory system preferentially home back to these organs by the expression of specific chemokine receptors and adhesion molecules
