# Chapter 16: Immune responses to major pathogens

According to World Health Organisation statistics, infectious diseases were responsible for approximately 25% of all deaths in 2016. Of these, some of the largest killers were: Infections of the lower respiratory tract (accounting for 5.3% of all deaths), diarrheal diseases (2.4%), tuberculosis (2.3%), HIV/AIDS (1.8%) and malaria (0.8%). The majority of these deaths occur in the developing world, due to limited access to effective sanitation, nutrition and healthcare. In addition to the premature mortality caused by infectious disease, many more people are affected by morbidity and disability resulting from infection. In this chapter, we will discuss how the human immune system responds to key examples of the most commonly encountered types of pathogen, and how these responses can be countered by pathogens.

Influenza

Infections of the lower respiratory tract, most commonly bronchitis or pneumonia, are currently the leading cause of premature death caused by infection. It is most frequently caused by bacterial infection in immunosuppressed hosts - which can often be precipitated by a preceding viral infection. As one of the most common viral respiratory infections is influenza, and because this virus has been responsible for many serious pandemics, we will begin with a discussion of the host immune response to this pathogen.

Influenza is an RNA virus of the Orthomyxoviridae family that is spread by airborne aerosols arising from coughing, sneezing and contact with contaminated surfaces. Common symptoms of infection include fatigue, weakness, fever, chills, muscle pains and headache. Influenza is responsible for ~250,000 deaths each year, but this number can increase dramatically during pandemics, when particularly virulent strains emerge. For example, the ‘Spanish flu’ of 1918 killed ~25 million people.

New strains of influenza arise continually via two major mechanisms. First, because the viral genome is composed of 8 separate RNA molecules, and because influenza that infects man may also infect pigs and birds, there is potential for co-infection of one animal with two or more influenza viruses that may normally be adapted for transmission in different species. During these events, reassortment of the viral genome can occur, and new variants are formed which may be more or less virulent than the parental strains. This process is referred to as antigenic shift. Second, the viral enzymes used to copy the influenza RNA genome during replication are relatively error prone, and point mutations occur in viral antigens over time. This less dramatic alteration to the viral strain is referred to as antigenic drift. The two major antigens of influenza virus are called neuraminidase and haemagglutinin, and the variants of these antigens are numbered (e.g. H1N1) to refer to the specific antigen types that are associated with a particular strain of influenza.

As with all viruses the host has not previously encountered, the early immune response to influenza is dependent on innate immunity. In particular, the viral RNA molecules are PAMPs that are recognised by RNA-specific pattern-recognition receptors, such as TLR3, TLR7 and TLR8, and also MDA-5 and RIG-I. When triggered, these receptors stimulate the activation of transcription factors, including IRF3, that lead to the production of the anti-viral type I interferons IFN-α and IFN-β. Together, these cytokines trigger anti-viral gene expression in both the infected cell and its near neighbours. Plasmacytoid dendritic cells additionally express IRF7, which when activated by viral RNA leads to secretion of large quantities of IFN-α. This in turn promotes systemic induction of an anti-viral state. NK-cells also detect infected cells that express NK-activating receptors from an early stage and kill them. By these two major mechanisms, the innate immune-mediated anti-viral response limits the spread of infection, giving time for the adaptive immune response to generate the antibodies and CTLs required to eradicate the infection.

Within 4 to 5 days after infection, CD8+ CTLs specific for viral peptides are generated. These CTLs kill cells presenting viral peptides on MHC-I molecules, thereby removing reservoirs of infection. A day or two later, titres of antibodies specific for the surface antigens of the virus begin to rise in the circulation. These antibodies bind to the antigens used by the virus to attach to host receptors and enter cells, and cause complement activation, thereby preventing their access to uninfected cells and neutralising extracellular viruses. The combined actions of virus-specific antibody and CTLs eventually lead to the clearance of both intracellular and extracellular viruses.

A challenge faced by the immune response to influenza is that typically, at least once per year, a new strain of influenza arises in the community that is sufficiently different from previous strains to evade the memory antibody and CTL responses developed against previous influenza infections. This is why vaccines for influenza must be given annually, each based on the most current circulating strains of the virus. However, immunological memory to one strain of influenza often imparts partial protection to new strains, such that the symptoms of the infection are less severe.

HIV

The human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) is the causative agent of the acquired immuno-deficiency syndrome (AIDS). This disease is characterised by severe immunosuppression, resulting in profound susceptibility to infections that are often harmless to healthy individuals. HIV is transmitted by sexual contact, blood transfer associated with needle re-use or from mother to baby at birth. There is currently no effective vaccine for HIV, which currently results in the death of ~2 million people per year, mostly in the developing world.

HIV is a retrovirus, which means that it possesses an RNA based genome that is converted to DNA, which then inserts into the host cell genome during infection. Remarkably, this highly virulent virus contains only 9 genes: gag, pol, vif, vpr, tat, rev, vpu, env and nef. Together, these genes code for all the proteins required to copy the genome, build the viral capsid and express the receptors it uses to bind to target host cells. Of most relevance to immunity, the env gene gives rise to two protein products, the gp120 and gp41 receptors, which form heterodimers on the surface of the virus. These receptors bind to CD4 molecules expressed by certain host cells, resulting in a conformational change that allows the viral proteins to bind a co-receptor, which is most frequently either the chemokine receptor CXCR4 or CCR5, although other co-receptors have also been described. This specific co-receptor requirement means that HIV tends to infect helper T-cells, macrophages and dendritic cells. Certain strains of the virus often preferentially infect one of these cell types, for example, those that utilise CCR5 tend to infect macrophages, and those that utilise CXCR4 tend to infect T-cells.

Once the virus has tethered to a cell, the gp120/gp41 protein complex enables the membranes of the virus and the cell to fuse, and this allows the viral RNA to enter the cytosol. This RNA is rapidly converted to DNA using the viral enzyme reverse transcriptase. The DNA enters the nucleus of the cell and integrates into the genome, where it is referred to as a provirus. Here, the virus can express its genes immediately, or instead remain in a latent, inactive form for days to years before being reactivated by stimuli such as inflammatory cytokines. In either case, the transcription of viral genes present in the host DNA leads to production of the proteins required to release thousands of viruses from the infected cell, and these are then released to infect other cells in the body.

In the two weeks or so required to generate a CD8+ CTL response following first infection of naïve hosts with HIV, the virus multiplies rapidly, infecting and killing many CD4+ T-cells. This results in a transient malaise that subsides when the CTL response eventually reduces the viral load significantly. An antibody response is also triggered to viral coat proteins, and by ~10-12 weeks, the number of viruses in the circulation is much lower than during the first phase of the infection. However, while such responses would be sufficient to eradicate a normal virus, HIV is different in three major ways that make it effectively impossible to entirely eliminate.

The first of these is the fact that it infects the very same cells, CD4+ helper T-cells, that are required to help generate effector and memory CD8+ CTLs, and high affinity antibodies of the correct isotype.

Second, the reverse transcriptase enzyme used by HIV to copy its genome is particularly prone to errors. In fact, it is estimated that every single virus that is produced contains on average at least one nucleotide that is different from the parental strain. For this reason, HIV is extremely adept at generating so-called ‘escape mutants’. Such escape mutants are generated when the immune response is finally able to clear one strain from the body. This leaves open a niche, which mutant versions of the virus quickly fill, as their genomes are now so different from the parental strain, they express antigens that are sufficiently different to evade both CD8+ CTL responses and antibody responses to coat proteins, and they proliferate dramatically.

Third, because the pro-virus can remain inside long-lived cells (such as memory T-cells), for years before being activated to synthesise new viral particles, it is effectively invisible to the immune system so long as it remains in these cells. This latent reservoir of HIV infection is practically impossible for the immune system alone to eradicate.

The net result of these three key viral mechanisms of survival is a slow war of attrition. The host immune system eventually responds to and crushes new strain after new strain, only for escape mutants to emerge each time. This phase can last for years, often with no outward symptoms other than a slow reduction in circulating CD4+ T-cell counts.

Eventually, should the CD4+ T-cell count dip below ~200 per mm3 of blood, the host begins to become increasingly susceptible to infections with other pathogens. This phase of the infection is called AIDS, and today can only be treated by anti-retroviral drugs, including nucleoside analogues, and inhibitors of key HIV enzymes, such as the protease and integrase. These drugs contain but do not cure the disease. The high cost of these drugs is another reason for the desperate need for an effective vaccine for HIV. However, developing vaccines specific for this pathogen is complicated by the extremely high rate of mutation of the virus.

Cholera

Diarrheal diseases are responsible for the deaths of over 1 million children under the age of 5 each year. These infections are caused mainly by bacterial and viral infections, the most common of which in children is rotavirus. However, the disease cholera, which is an infection of the small intestine caused by the Gram-negative bacterium Vibrio cholerae, is particularly dangerous. Transmission of the disease occurs through food and water that has been contaminated with faecal bacteria. The disease is easily prevented by sanitation, and treated by oral rehydration with water and electrolytes. Nevertheless, as a result of limited sanitation and healthcare provision in less developed nations, this bacterium is responsible for ~100,000 premature deaths per year.

Vibrio cholerae is a Gram-negative rod-shaped bacteria that is motile by means of a single flagellum. The virulence of the organism is derived mainly from the expression of its toxin - termed cholera toxin - that attacks the enterocytes of the small intestine. This toxin is composed of five B subunits and one A subunit. The B-subunit binds to specific receptors expressed by the enterocytes. This binding permits the injection of the target cell with the A subunit, which causes excessive generation of cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP). This in turn causes enterocytes to lose their capacity to regulate flux of Na+, Cl-, HCO3- ions and water, resulting in massive loss of fluids through the intestinal wall. By this means, the bacterium receives nutrients and a means of propagation.

Perhaps surprisingly, the tissue damage caused by this response is not associated with extensive inflammation in the gut mucosa. This is partly due to the fact that the organism is non-invasive and does not penetrate the gut wall. However, effective immunity begins with the chemical barrier of stomach acid, which destroys a large proportion of those organisms that are ingested. To clear the organisms that colonise the intestine, it is thought that a secretory IgA response is required. These antibodies often target the O-polysaccharide of the organism’s endotoxin, or the B-subunit of the toxin. This is typically developed by plasma cells in the GALT, which, via the circulation, home back to populate most of the gut. Here, they secrete V. cholerae-specific IgA, which prevents it from binding to the gut wall and neutralise the effects of the toxin, resulting in clearance of the organism.

Vaccination to cholera has moderate effectiveness, and generally the vaccines consist of heat-killed V. cholerae cells.

Tuberculosis

Tuberculosis (TB) is a disease mainly caused by the intracellular bacterium Mycobacterium tuberculosis, which is spread by aerosols arising from coughs and sneezes. Most often affecting the lungs, infection can be asymptomatic in immunocompetent individuals, or can become an active disease with mortality approaching 50% if untreated. Today, around 90% of infections are contained by the host immune system and are therefore asymptomatic. However, it is estimated that one third of the world’s population carries the infection, resulting in ~1.5 million associated deaths annually.

The earliest phase of infection is the inhalation of the bacteria. The innate immune defences of mucous and cilia likely trap and expel most of the inoculum. However, those organisms that reach the alveoli may be recognised by alveolar macrophages and phagocytosed by these cells. PRRs expressed by the macrophages recognise PAMPs present in the bacteria, and trigger production of IL-12 and IL-15, which promotes the release of IFN-γ by NK-cells. As described in earlier chapters, IFN-γ signalling enhances the killing capacity of the macrophage through the induction of anti-microbial reactive oxygen species and lysosomal enzymes. However, while this would result in the destruction of most bacteria, M. tuberculosis is particularly resistant to killing, partly because of its waxy outer coating but mainly because it prevents the fusion of phagosomes with lysosomes. By remaining within the endosome of the macrophage, they escape lysosomal killing and replicate utilising host cell metabolites as their nutrients for growth.

Some time later, both CD8+ and CD4+ T-cell responses are generated to mycobacterial peptides presented on MHC-I and MHC-II of the infected cells. The CD4+ T-cells differentiate into Th1 type cells (due partly to the locally elevated levels of IL-12), and these in turn enhance macrophage activation by secreting further IFN-γ and providing signals through CD40:CD40L interactions. Together, these responses are generally able to halt the further spread of the infection. However, the organism remains resistant to killing and the bacteria enter a dormant state of very slow replication and low metabolic activity which persists for many years. This long-term exposure to unresolved pathogen stimulus results in the formation of a granuloma. Such structures are formed mainly of macrophages surrounded by lymphocytes and fibroblasts. The local tissue is heavily remodelled to form a barrier around the site of infection. Together, these responses are an attempt by the body to wall off the infected tissues and limit further dissemination of the microbe. Immunocompetent hosts can maintain this barrier for many years, and during this phase, the infection is asymptomatic. However, breakdown of the barrier function of the granuloma can occur if the host becomes immunosuppressed. In these cases, the pathogen may be reactivated, resulting in further tissue damage and the formation of additional scar tissue, which can damage the affected organ.

A vaccine is available for TB, termed Bacillus Calmette-Guerin (BCG), which is a live attenuated vaccine, derived from an avirulent strain of the related organism Mycobacterium bovis. Active infections are treated by antibiotics. However, the same properties that enable the bacteria to survive within endosomes also result in a requirement to treat infections with multiple antibiotics for several months to clear the infection.

Malaria

Malaria is caused by single celled eukaryotic micro-organisms of the genus Plasmodium, the most significant of which in man are P. falciparum and P. vivax. The disease infects ~200 million people per year and is responsible for ~1.2 million deaths annually. Infection is acquired through bites from mosquitos which have recently fed on the blood of an infected individual. Like many other parasites, Plasmodium species have a complex life-cycle consisting of various cellular forms which infect different host cells and species at different points in their development.

Shortly after being delivered into the host bloodstream by a mosquito, the sporozoite form of the parasite travels through the blood to the liver, where it invades hepatocyte cells. Within these cells, it may hide from the immune system (a common strategy of many different parasites), while it reproduces to form hundreds of merozoites, which eventually rupture the hepatocyte and they are released into the blood. Because these merozoites are now exposed to the immune system, they quickly bind to and enter circulating red blood cells. The parasites grow and divide rapidly within the red blood cell, eventually giving rise to 8 to 24 new parasites, which burst from the blood cell and escape into the plasma to reinfect other red blood cells.

The main purpose of residence within red blood cells is to protect the parasites from detection or attack by the host immune system. Red blood cells are ideal for this purpose since they do not express MHC-I and antibodies cannot penetrate the red blood cell membrane. However, infected red blood cells are eventually cleared by the spleen. To avoid this, P. falciparum expresses its own proteins on the surface of the red blood cell which cause binding to blood vessels, potentially leading to dangerous blocking of blood vessels. However, because these proteins, including one called P. falciparum erythrocyte membrane protein-1 (PfEMP1), are on the surface of the red blood cell, this makes them suitable targets for generation of an antibody response, and indeed this does occur. To counter this, the plasmodium genome contains ~60 different variants of PfEMP1, which are expressed one at a time in order to evade the antibody immune response. In other words, when the host antibody response is eventually developed to strains expressing one variant of PfEMP1, the parasites within this clone are killed, but other clones arise within the host which express an alternative form of PfEMP1 which is not detected by these antibodies. Thus, the parasite is able to grow without being cleared for a further two weeks or so before a new antibody response is generated to the new antigen. In this way, the parasite can escape from the immune response time after time, resulting in periodic episodes of high blood parasite levels and fever. This process of sequential expression of many different major antigens is termed antigenic variation, and is a key strategy employed by numerous pathogens to escape host adaptive immune responses.

Other than the antibody response to the parasite, it is thought that malarial PAMPs may be detected by PRRs, such as TLRs, when the parasite is in transit between red blood cells. There is also evidence to suggest that CD4+ helper T-cells promote immunity to malaria, although the mechanisms of this protection remain unclear. Nevertheless, after many years of exposure to different strains of malaria, it is possible to develop immunity to infection. This immunity is not comprehensive and likely involves the development of dozens of different memory B-cell clones, reflecting the difficulty of generating effective immunity to an organism capable of antigenic variation. For these reasons, the development of an effective vaccine for malaria is extremely challenging and has yet to be successful.

Pertussis

Pertussis, commonly referred to as whooping cough, is caused by the Gram-negative bacterium Bordetella pertussis. The disease can be very serious in children, and is responsible for ~290,000 deaths per year.

Ordinarily, the response to infections with extracellular Gram-negative organisms is straightforward. Innate barriers such as mucous and ciliary action would trap and expel the organism. Complement would then bind and opsonise or lyse the organism. Innate immune detection of PAMPs such as lipopolysaccharide by TLR4 and CpG DNA by TLR9 would result in inflammation and DC activation. Mature DCs would then present peptide of the organisms to generate Th17 cells, which specialise in the clearance of extracellular bacteria and fungi, and high affinity IgG antibodies. Typically, these responses lead to rapid clearance of the organism.

However, infection with B. pertussis, which occurs in the lungs, is not straightforward and can persist for months before being cleared. This is partly because the organism is able to counter the actions of cilia on mucous by secreting a toxin that paralyses the cilia, and thereby enables the organism to colonise the epithelial surfaces and alveolar spaces of the lung. Through means that remain unclear (but are apparently independent of lipopolysaccharide), the organism is able to resist attack by complement. Finally, the organism expresses a toxin called the pertussis toxin, which prevents the interaction of G proteins with G protein-coupled receptors on the cell membrane. This is particularly damaging to chemokine receptor signalling, which is dependent on G-protein signalling, and results in impaired recruitment of monocytes and neutrophils. Eventually, the disease also results in lymphocytosis and can lead to suppression of immune responses to other organisms. Thus, B. pertussis is an example of a Gram-negative organism that would ordinarily be cleared easily by the host immune system, but persists by the expression of counter-defensive toxins, termed virulence factors.

Vaccines against B. pertussis are widely used and are highly effective. Most pertussis vaccines employ heat-killed organisms complexed with the adjuvant aluminium hydroxide, and result in long-lasting protective antibody responses.

Ascaris

Helminths, or parasitic worms, are among the most common parasites of man. Although rarely life-threatening, infection with these organisms is very common and has a great impact on health. One of the most common helminths, Ascaris lumbricoides, currently inhabits the gut of over 1 billion people. These worms are similar in size to garden earth worms, but can only survive in the adult form within a human host. The lifecycle of the worm is as follows: Eggs which are ingested with contaminated food penetrate the gut mucosa of the duodenum and enter the bloodstream. From here, they migrate to the lungs, where they enter the airspace to be expelled upwards in the trachea by mucous. From here, they are ingested for the second time, but in this now more mature form they are able to colonise the gut lumen where they may live for several years. Although their impact on health is limited compared to other pathogens, they may cause malnutrition and stunted physical and mental development of infected individuals.

To date, the details of the host response to A. lumbricoides remain unclear. However, it has been observed that peripheral T-cells of infected individuals are skewed towards the Th2 subset, and that these cells secrete more IL-4 and IL-5 than T-cells of uninfected individuals. Infected subjects also demonstrate mast cell hyperplasia, eosinophilia and elevated levels of circulating IgE. Some evidence suggests that immunomodulators released by or induced by A. lumbricoides may lead to increased risk of infection by other organisms.

Summary

1) The majority of deaths caused by infectious disease in man are caused by a relatively small number of pathogens, most notably the human immunodeficiency virus (HIV, the causative agent of AIDS), Mycobacterium tuberculosis (the causative agent of tuberculosis), and Plasmodium species (the causative agents of malaria)

2) Prevalent strains of the influenza virus express different neuraminidase (N) and haemagglutinin (H) antigens, which are altered with time by the processes of antigenic drift and antigenic shift

3) Innate responses to the influenza virus include RNA-specific pattern-recognition receptors, such as TLRs 3, 7 & 8, leading to activation of the transcription factors IRF3 and IRF7 which promote type I interferon production and induction of the antiviral state in neighbouring cells

4) Natural killer (NK) cells kill many virally infected cells and begin doing so as soon as the infection starts, but this response is not sufficient to clear the infection

5) Viral peptides expressed on MHC-I induce the generation of CD8+ CTL effector cells, which more effectively kill virally infected cells, while antibody raised against viral surface antigens neutralises viruses, thus leading to clearance of the virus

6) Lymphocyte memory to one strain of influenza is sometimes, although not always, sufficient to confer substantial protection against other strains of influenza

7) The human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) infects macrophages and CD4+ T-cells through the use of two receptors, CD4 and one of several possible chemokine receptors, of which the most commonly targeted by the virus are CXCR4 and CCR5

8) The infected host generates antibody responses to the viral coat proteins, most notably gp120 and gp41, which are used by the virus to bind cellular receptors and enter the cell, and these dramatically reduce viral titres

9) Viral titres soon rise again because the genome copying enzyme used by HIV is highly prone to errors, causing frequent mutations, some which lead to novel antigens that are able to evade the current antibody response

10) The HIV-infected host may be asymptomatic for many years while this process continues, but should the circulating CD4+ T-helper cell count fall too low, a state of severe immunosuppression called AIDS may ensue

11) Efforts to create an effective vaccine for HIV are hindered by the rapid antigen variation of the virus

12) Cholera is a diarrheal disease caused by the Gram-negative bacterium Vibrio cholerae, which is transmitted through ingestion of contaminated food and water

13) Bacterial toxins attack the host enterocytes, leading to impaired ion flux and fluid loss

14) Immune responses to V. cholerae are largely mediated by secretory IgA

15) Mycobacterium tuberculosis infects and resides within the endosomes of alveolar macrophages, which do not fuse with lysosomes because of molecules released by the bacteria

16) Strong innate immune signalling induced by mycobacterial PAMPs triggers a robust Th1 response, resulting in enhanced macrophage activation, although this is still not sufficient to clear the infection

17) A granuloma formed of macrophages, dead cells, and lymphocytes is formed, which effectively walls off the infection from the rest of the body, and thus the infection may remain asymptomatic in carriers for many years

18) Immunosuppression may result in loss of granuloma barrier potential and reactivation of the dormant M. tuberculosis infection

19) Malaria is caused mainly by the eukaryotic single-cell parasites P. falciparum and P. vivax in man, which are transmitted to hosts by mosquito bites

20) Plasmodium parasites evade the immune system by hiding within hepatocytes (liver cells) initially, and later in their life cycle, red blood cells

21) An antibody response develops to parasite antigens expressed on the surface of red blood cells, such as PfEMP1, and these are effective at reducing circulating parasite numbers

22) To counter the antibody response, P. falciparum sequentially switches expression of the PfEMP1 gene to another form, of which there are 60 available in its genome, thus expressing a new antigen that escapes the current antibody response, in a process termed antigenic variation

23) Pertussis, or whooping cough, is caused by the Gram-negative bacterium Bordetella pertussis, which is detected by diverse PRRs, including TLR4 and TLR9

24) Host responses to B. pertussis are inhibited by bacterial expression of the pertussis toxin, which impairs G-protein signalling and limits chemotactic recruitment of immune cells

25) Ascaris lumbricoides is a helminth, or parasitic worm, that colonises the intestine of ~1 billion people today and causes serious health problems but rarely causes morality

26) Immune responses to A. lumbricoides are typically Th2 mediated, involving IL-4, IL-5, IgE and mast cell activation

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bordetella_pertussis>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiv>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascaris>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaria>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cholera>

# Chapter 17: Hypersensitivities and allergy

Most immune responses do not result in serious or lasting damage to the tissues. However, when tissue damage or other disorders are triggered by immune responses, these conditions are termed hypersensitivity diseases, or hypersensitivities. Such responses may be triggered by pathogens, or by triggers which should not normally induce a robust immune response, such as the commensal microbiota, self antigens (autoimmunity) or environmental antigens. Hypersensitivities are categorised into 4 major types, the first three being dependent on antibody and the fourth being dependent on T-cells (Figure 17.1). As repeated type I hypersensitivity responses, referred to as allergy or atopy, is the most common immune disorder today, affecting up to one in 5 individuals in the Western world, this chapter will focus in particular on the mechanisms of allergy.

![](image_rsrc4EC.jpg)

Type I hypersensitivity, also called immediate hypersensitivity, is mediated by IgE

Type I hypersensitivity, also called immediate hypersensitivity, is mediated by IgE antibodies formed as a result of excessive Th2 polarisation. These responses occur when mast cells are triggered to degranulate in response to the detection of antigen by IgE. Mast cells express FcεRI, which has a very high affinity for IgE. This means that the mast cell surface constitutively displays hundreds of different IgE molecules bound to FcεRI molecules. In most healthy (non-atopic) individuals, the IgE antibodies bound to the mast cell are specific for many different types of antigen. However, in atopic, or sensitised individuals, the proportion of IgE specific for a particular antigen may be high in the circulation, and also on the surface of the mast cell. This means that exposure to antigen is likely to lead to strong cross-linking of mast cell IgE/FcεRI complexes, resulting in a powerful degranulation response. Repeated episodes of such responses are referred to as allergy or atopy, and we will return to the topic of immediate hypersensitivity and these diseases later in the chapter.

Type II hypersensitivity is mediated by IgG or IgM antibodies targeting self antigens

Occasionally, despite all the mechanisms present in the host to prevent their formation (see Chapter 11), antibodies may be generated that bind to self antigens. When antibody binds to self-antigens, inflammation and tissue damage can occur. If the target is an immobilised tissue antigen, such as may be expressed on a host cell or as part of the extracellular matrix, this response is referred to as type II hypersensitivity. This type of hypersensitivity is mediated by IgG or IgM antibodies and is typically restricted to the tissue where the target antigen is expressed.

There are three main mechanisms by which antibody binding to self antigens can promote disease. First, cells may be damaged by complement activation caused by cross-linking of antibody on the cell surface, and complement degradation products may lead to the recruitment and activation of inflammatory cells which secrete tissue damaging agents, such as proteases and reactive oxygen species. Second, cells may be opsonised by antibodies binding to their surface and therefore targeted for phagocytosis. Third, the binding of cellular receptors by antibodies may inhibit, or in some cases even trigger, their activation, resulting in impaired regulation of various physiological pathways. Two examples of the latter form of impairment include Grave’s disease and myasthenia gravis. In Grave’s disease, antibodies bind to and inappropriately activate the thyroid stimulating hormone (TSH) receptor, resulting in excessive production of thyroid hormones (referred to as hyperthyroidism). In myasthenia gravis, antibodies inhibit the binding of acetylcholine to the acetylcholine receptor, thus impeding the transmission of signals from nerve endings to muscle fibres resulting in weakness.

Type III hypersensitivity is mediated by antigen-antibody complexes

Type III hypersensitivity occurs when large quantities of antigen, which may be of either self or non-self origin, gain access to the circulation in an individual expressing IgG or IgM antibodies specific for that antigen. The resulting binding of antigen by these antibodies leads to the formation of circulating immune complexes. Such complexes are generated at a low level during most infections, and as part of some normal body processes. Under these conditions, immune complexes are cleared quickly from the circulation by the liver and spleen. However, when the rate of their formation exceeds the capacity for their clearance from the circulation, they begin to deposit on the blood vessel walls or within specific tissues. The close proximity of Fc portions induced by immune complexes results in complement activation and the recruitment of neutrophils and other inflammatory cells. These recruited cells may then be activated by the cross-linked Fc portions, and also by the products of complement activation, resulting in an inflammatory response and the production of mediators that may damage the tissues.

Some tissues appear to be more prone to immune complex deposition than others. These include the glomeruli of the kidneys, small arteries and synovial joints. The localised immune complex mediated inflammatory response to injected antigen is called the Arthus reaction. The autoimmune disease systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE, described more fully in Chapter 14) is driven by immune complexes formed between autoantibodies and host cell nuclear antigens, resulting in nephritis, vasculitis and arthritis.

Type IV hypersensitivity is mediated by CD4+ or CD8+ T-lymphocytes

In contrast to hypersensitivity types I-III, which are mediated by antibodies, type IV hypersensitivity is mediated by T-lymphocytes. Most frequently, these responses are generated by cytokines released from activated CD4+ helper T-cells, particularly of the Th1 and Th17 subsets. Occasionally, CD8+ T-cell responses may also promote a type IV hypersensitivity response. In both cases, exposure of the host to a protein or peptide to which effector T-cells have already been generated may result in induction of inflammatory responses at the site of challenge.

Tissue injury arising from CD4+ Th-cell dependent responses arises mainly from production of IL-17 by activated Th17 cells, or Th1 cell derived IFN-γ promoting the activation of macrophages and production of other pro-inflammatory cytokines. In both cases, monocyte and neutrophil-mediated inflammation is promoted, and the proteases and reactive oxygen species released from the activated phagocytes cause damage to the tissues. It is believed that CD4+ T-cell dependent type IV hypersensitivity plays a key role in many autoimmune diseases, including psoriasis, rheumatoid arthritis and multiple sclerosis. Contact sensitivity, the chronic form of which is called eczema, is the response of sensitised individuals to exposure of the skin to certain chemicals, and this is also thought to be mainly T-cell dependent. CD8+ T-cell dependent type IV hypersensitivity responses tend to be directed towards microbial antigens, causing damage to associated tissues.

A key aspect of T-cell dependent hypersensitivity that sets it apart from the other hypersensitivities is that there is often a long lag phase (typically 24 to 48 hours) between exposure to the antigen and the resulting inflammatory response. This is called delayed type hypersensitivity (DTH). DTH responses can result in the deposition of fibrin in the tissues that, if chronically induced, can lead to fibrosis of the tissue and organ dysfunction.

Allergy is caused by repeated type I hypersensitivity reactions

As described above, type I hypersensitivity refers to inappropriate inflammatory responses arising from IgE-dependent activation of mast cells. This response is typically divided into two phases: the immediate hypersensitivity reaction, which occurs within minutes of antigen exposure, and the late phase-reaction, which develops several hours later.

The immediate response is characterised by rapid dilation of blood vessels, increased fluid recruitment into the tissues, and contraction of bronchial and intestinal smooth muscle. By contrast, the late phase reaction is characterised by Th2-cytokine dependent inflammatory responses, resulting in the recruitment of eosinophils.

When individuals repeatedly suffer type I hypersensitivity responses, this is termed allergy or atopy. The environmental antigens which trigger these responses and which are bound by IgE are termed allergens. Allergic responses can resolve quickly after exposure to antigen leaving little tissue damage. However, repeated immediate hypersensitivity reactions within a tissue can result in tissue remodelling and organ dysfunction (as seen, for example, in asthma).

Approximately one in five individuals in industrialised nations experience some form of atopy. This leads to the question of what benefit the host may gain from having such a potentially damaging form of immunity? The answer, believed by many immunologists, is that the activation of mast cells and eosinophils by IgE confers a substantial benefit on the host by promoting defence against helminths. For example, mast cell activation promotes mucous secretion and peristalsis, both of which expedite the expulsion of worms. Furthermore, activated eosinophils are effective at inhibiting the growth of and colonisation by a number of different helminths.

It is thought that both environmental exposure to certain antigens and genetic predisposition to allergy is required for atopic responses to develop. Atopy often runs in families and the risk of developing atopy is associated with common variants in a number of different genes, particularly those involved in Th2 cytokine production and signalling.

Allergy is driven by Th2 cytokines, IgE and mast cell activation

Individuals predisposed to allergy tend to generate strong Th2 responses to certain antigens, and produce high levels of antibody of the IgE isotype in response to these antigens (called sensitisation). The IgE produced in this response binds to FcεRI expressed on the surface of mast cells. When the antigen is encountered subsequently, this causes the IgE molecules on the mast cell to cluster close together, which causes clustering of FcεRI receptors and leads to mast cell activation and degranulation. The activation of the mast cell is central to the development of allergic responses, as the mediators released from these cells, including biogenic amines, cytokines and inflammatory lipids, promote both the early smooth muscle contraction and vascular response, and the later inflammatory responses. The consequences of these responses can range from the benign, including rashes and runny nose, through to bronchial constriction, diarrhoea and anaphylactic shock.

Allergens cause the activation of Th2 cells and class-switching to IgE production

When exposed to a new antigen, most healthy individuals generate antibodies that are mainly of the IgG and IgM isotypes, which are specific for that antigen. However, for reasons that remain unclear, atopic individuals tend to produce IgE more frequently and more abundantly than do non-atopic individuals. The antigens which trigger atopic responses are termed allergens, and these may reflect either protein antigens, or other molecules which modify proteins to form neo-antigens. Common sources of allergens include pollen, house dust mites and animal hairs. Less commonly, some individuals may become allergic to small molecules, such as penicillin, through covalent modification of proteins (i.e. forming hapten-carrier conjugates).

It is thought that two key factors drive environmental antigens to become allergens. First, they should be frequently encountered. Second, the antigens should not be associated with any stimulus, such as bacterial or viral PAMPs, that promote induction of either Th1 or Th17 responses, since these suppress Th2 responses. It has been proposed that the default pathway of T-cell activation, in the absence PAMP-derived stimuli, may be towards the Th2 subset, and that by repeated exposure to non-threatening antigens, Th2 responses to these antigens may therefore arise.

Once Th2 effector cells have been generated, two of the cytokines they produce, namely IL-4 and IL-13, promote B-cell class-switching to the IgE isotype. IL-13 also stimulates the production of mucous (a feature of some allergic responses). IL-5, also secreted by activated Th2 cells, promotes the recruitment and activation of eosinophils, which may contribute to tissue damage. This IgE then circulates to coat the surfaces of mast cells which exist throughout the body, but are most numerous just beneath the epithelial surfaces (these being common points for helminth invasion).

Key features of mast cells, basophils and eosinophils

Although mast cells play the dominant role in immediate hypersensitivity responses, basophils and eosinophils also contribute to these reactions. Each of these three cell types contain large intracellular reserves of mediators, which are retained within cytoplasmic granules. The contents of these granules, and the mediators which are released, differs between each type of cell. Each cell type therefore has a distinct role to play in the allergic response (Table 17.1), as will now be discussed.

Table 17.1: Comparison of key characteristics of mast cells, basophils and eosinophils

![](image_rsrc4ED.jpg)

Mast cell activation in allergy

Immature mast cells exit the bone marrow but do not mature until they have entered the peripheral tissues. At least two subsets of mast cells appear to exist: those that reside in the mucosal tissues and those that are found in the connective tissues, differentiated by subtle differences in the types of mediator they release and T-cell dependence.

Mast cell activation in allergy occurs because atopic individuals express higher levels of IgE specific for particular antigens, than do non-atopic individuals. In other words, non-atopic individuals may express circulating antibodies of other isotypes that are specific to a particular allergen, but the proportion of their circulating IgE antibodies which are specific for this antigen is low. By contrast, in atopic individuals who are sensitised to the same allergen, a much higher proportion of their circulating IgE is specific for that antigen. This means that while the mast cells of the non-atopic individual are coated in highly diverse IgE antibodies which are specific for a wide variety of different antigens, the IgE antibodies that coat the mast cells of the atopic individual are heavily biased towards particular antigens, i.e. the allergens to which they are sensitive. It follows that when both individuals are exposed to the same allergen, there is limited potential for cross-linking of mast cell FcεRI in the non-atopic individuals, while the same allergen causes extensive cross-linking of FcεRI in sensitised individuals, resulting in activation of the mast cell. These activated mast cells degranulate and secrete cytokines which then potentiate the immediate and late phase reactions of type I hypersensitivity.

Certain other stimuli are also able to promote mast cell activation, such as the complement activation products C3a, C4a and C5a, neuropeptides, polybasic compounds and exposure to cold temperatures, but the relevance of these pathways of mast cell activation to allergy remains unclear.

FcεRI cross-linking leads to mast cell activation

When IgE is cross-linked by polyvalent antigen, the FcεRI on the mast cell surface is also cross-linked. This results in the clustering of FcεRI intracellular domains and the recruitment of signalling adaptors. The FcεRI receptor complex consists of one α-chain, one β-chain and two γ-chains. The α-chain serves to bind the Fc portion of IgE antibodies with high affinity. The β- and γ-chains contain ITAM domains that enable tyrosine kinase dependent signalling. Syk tyrosine kinase binds to and is then activated by the ITAMs of the γ-chains. This leads to activation of phospholipase C-γ (PLC-γ), which catalyses the release of inositol triphosphate (IP3) and diacylglycerol (DAG) from the membrane phospholipid phosphatidylinositol. The released IP3 causes intracellular calcium ion (Ca2+) concentrations to rise, and the DAG promotes activation of a major signalling mediator termed protein kinase C (PKC). Activated PKC acts on various pathways resulting in the breakdown of the actin cytoskeleton beneath the cell membrane, which allows granules to move towards the plasma membrane.

In resting mast cells, the membranes surrounding the intracellular granules do not fuse with the plasma membrane, because inhibitory proteins prevent them from doing so. This inhibition is lost during mast cell activation, and the granule membranes fuse with the plasma membrane with the assistance of SNARE (soluble N-ethylmaleimide-sensitive factor attachment protein receptors) protein complexes, which are also activated by the increased levels of cytosolic Ca2+. The combined actions of Ca2+ and MAP-kinase signalling results in the activation of phospholipase A-2, which liberates fatty acids, including arachidonic acid, from membrane phospholipids. Key transcription factors activated by the above pathways include NF-κB, NFAT and AP-1. Together, these transcription factors co-ordinate with others on the chromosomal DNA to upregulate transcription of cytokine genes associated with mast cell activation.

Together, these signalling pathways generate the intracellular responses required for the three main functions of activated mast cells: degranulation, lipid mediator synthesis and cytokine production (Figure 17.2).

![](image_rsrc4EE.jpg)

Key mediators produced by activated mast cells

The key mediators produced by activated mast cells are biogenic amines, proteases and other enzymes, prostaglandin D2, leukotrienes and cytokines.

Of the biogenic amines released from activated human mast cells, histamine has by far the greatest impact. This small, soluble molecule serves a remarkable variety of functions in the body, acting on diverse cell types and inducing diverse responses, depending on the type of histamine receptor (H1, H2 or H3) expressed on each type of cell. However, in the context of mast cell activation, the most relevant actions of histamine are the induction of vascular leakage (allowing plasma to enter into and swell the tissues), blood vessel dilation (increasing local blood flow), increased peristalsis and bronchospasm. The effects of histamine are short-lived, because the molecule has a short half-life once secreted into the extracellular space.

Other major mediators released from mast cell granules include the proteases tryptase and chymase. These proteases break down various components of the extracellular matrix, and therefore can result in tissue damage when released in excess. The proteoglycans heparin and chondroitin sulphate, which bind to and act as slow-release agents for the proteases, are also released from mast cell granules.

Activated mast cells also secrete two key classes of bioactive compounds via mechanisms that are independent of granule contents or degranulation, namely lipid mediators and cytokines.

As mentioned above, the activation of PLA2 results in the liberation of free arachidonic acid into the cell. This substrate is acted upon by the enzymes cyclo-oxygenase and lipoxygenase to yield prostaglandins and leukotrienes. The major prostaglandin produced by mast cells is PGD2, which promotes neutrophilic inflammation and bronchoconstriction. Major leukotrienes produced by mast cells include LTC4, LTD4 and LTE3, which promote bronchoconstriction. Another key lipid mediator produced by mast cells is platelet activating factor (PAF), which promotes vasodilation, plasma leakage and bronchoconstriction.

While the above mediators may be released within minutes of mast cell activation, most mast cell cytokines require de novo transcription of messenger RNA and synthesis of protein. The cytokine response therefore takes somewhat longer, typically several hours, to reach its maximum. Key cytokines produced by activated mast cells include the Th2 cytokines IL-4, IL-5 and IL-13, and also inflammatory cytokines, such as IL-1, IL-6 and TNF-α. These cytokines promote the inflammatory events that occur during the late phase reaction, as described in previous sections.

Basophil activation in allergy

Basophils can be considered to be circulating granulocytes with functional responses that are similar to mast cells in many respects. For example, like mast cells, they express FcεRI and can therefore be activated by cross-linked IgE. The granules they contain, and also the mediators they produce, are also very similar to those produced by mast cells. However, unlike mast cells, they are not normally resident in the tissues. It is thought that they may be recruited into tissues affected by certain types of inflammation, and therefore may contribute to the response to allergen and immediate hypersensitivity reactions.

Eosinophil recruitment in allergy

As mentioned in earlier chapters, different stimuli may result in different types of inflammation. In particular, the inflammation that occurs during the late-phase response several hours after mast cell activation results in the recruitment of a high proportion of eosinophils into the affected tissues. This eosinophilic inflammation is driven by the upregulation of specific adhesion molecules that preferentially bind eosinophils by the actions of Th2 cytokines on nearby endothelial cells. The chemokine eotaxin (CCL11), complement C5a, PAF and certain leukotrienes produced during mast cell activation are also chemoattractants for eosinophils and enhance their recruitment.

Following recruitment, eosinophils may be activated by IL-5 (released by mast cells), causing them to release their granule contents. Eosinophil granules are quite different from the granules of mast cells and basophils, as they contain a number of proteins and peptides, such as eosinophil cationic protein, major basic protein and eosinophil peroxidase, which are particularly toxic to helminths. Anti-helminth defence is therefore considered to be the major function of eosinophils in host immunity. However, these molecules are also capable of causing damage to host tissues, and eosinophilic inflammation is characterised by specific tissue remodelling events that may ultimately limit organ function (see discussion of asthma, below). Although eosinophils express surface FcεRI, it is not clear whether or not IgE cross-linking also contributes to their activation, since they lack the β-chain thought to be required for FcεRI signalling. In addition to degranulation, activated eosinophils secrete cytokines and lipid mediators that are similar to those released by activated mast cells.

Eosinophils are easily distinguished by the use of acidic histological dyes, such as eosin, which intensely stain the granules of these cells but not the granules of mast cells or basophils (which bind basic dyes). Since eosinophils are quite rare in healthy tissues, but are highly abundant in tissues affected by mast cell activation or Th2 responses, the observation of eosinophil-rich infiltrates is a useful marker of the late-phase reaction of type I hypersensitivity.

Characteristics of the immediate reaction

The immediate reaction can be used to test whether or not individuals may be sensitised to particular allergens. In this procedure, a small amount of allergen is injected into the skin of the test subject. If the subject is sensitised, that is, if they express high levels of IgE antibodies specific for that allergen, an immediate reaction will occur in the skin. Within minutes after challenge with allergen, characteristic changes occur in the blood vessels around the site of injection that are obvious on the skin surface. In particular, as the blood vessels dilate and become leaky, a central region around the site of injection becomes swollen with plasma exudate, and raises up as a shallow dome above the rest of the surrounding skin. This is referred to as the wheal reaction (Figure 17.3). Increased blood flow to the region then results in the formation of a red ring that surrounds this dome. This is referred to as the flare reaction. The extent of the wheal and flare response is related to the individual’s sensitivity to the tested allergen, and is dependent on both IgE and mast cell activation. Because the agents responsible for promoting the wheal and flare reaction (most notably histamine, prostaglandins and PAF) have short half-lives in the tissues, these visible changes to the skin typically subside within an hour.

![](image_rsrc4EF.jpg)

Characteristics of the late-phase reaction

Several hours after the immediate reaction to allergen, a later phase of cellular recruitment is observed, which is termed the late-phase reaction. This is driven by specific cytokines, leukotrienes and chemokines produced by activated mast cells that have delayed kinetics and longer lasting effects than the mediators responsible for the immediate reaction. Unlike TLR-stimulant or Th1-cytokine driven inflammation in which macrophages and Th1 cells preferentially accumulate, the late-phase reaction is characterised by a high proportion of eosinophils, and also by Th2 lymphocytes and neutrophils. This phase may last for a day or so before the inflammatory infiltrate subsides.

Allergic rhinitis

The tissues affected by immediate hypersensitivity responses are generally those which are exposed to the allergen. Because mast cells are abundant in the skin and in the respiratory and intestinal mucosae, and since these tissues are likely to be exposed to environmental allergens, they are common sites of allergic inflammation. For example, individuals sensitised to airborne allergens frequently develop allergic rhinitis. When this allergen is derived from pollen, the response is called hay fever, although technically no fever is actually induced. The response is typified by swelling of the nasal mucosa, excessive production of mucous and localised eosinophilic inflammation. Often, the eyes may also be affected, becoming swollen and itchy. Because histamine produced by activated mast cells is central to the pathology, symptoms are often controlled well by anti-histamine drugs. Interestingly, a much higher frequency of allergies, including allergic rhinitis, is observed in developed nations. This has led some to propose that the limited exposure of inhabitants of such countries to severe infections or chronic worm infestation somehow predisposes to increased risk of developing allergies. This notion is commonly referred to as the hygiene hypothesis.

Asthma

Asthma is a chronic inflammatory disease of the lungs, characterised by episodes of reversible airway narrowing leading to breathing difficulties, bronchial eosinophilic inflammation and hyper-reactivity of bronchial smooth muscle cells to bronchoconstrictors. The disease reflects an accumulation of tissue damage caused by repeated bouts of mast-cell activation and hypersensitivity responses. The smooth muscle cell layer surrounding the bronchial airways becomes much thicker (hypertrophied) than observed in healthy subjects, and these cells are more sensitive to bronchoconstrictors, such as leukotrienes and other agents released from activated mast cells, eosinophils and basophils. Goblet cells also proliferate, and are activated to produce excessive quantities of thick mucus that may lead to further breathing difficulties. Interestingly, not all asthma cases are thought to arise as a result of repeated exposure to allergens. Mast cells appear to be activated by other stimuli, such as drugs or cold temperatures, in approximately 30% of asthma cases. Once the disease has become established, mast cell activation may no longer play a central role in the disease. Current therapies for asthma include inhaled steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, and activators of the cAMP pathway, such as β2-adrenergic agents.

Anaphylactic shock

In rare cases, an individual may be exposed to an allergen that gains access to the circulation. The allergen may be delivered to the blood by means of an insect sting, or absorption across the intestinal tract. If this individual has high titres of IgE antibodies specific for that allergen, there is a danger of anaphylactic shock. In this condition, mast cells are activated systemically by the circulating antigen. This causes vascular dilation and plasma leakage into the tissues throughout the body, resulting in very low blood pressure (shock). Large quantities of mucous may be secreted into the airways, and airway smooth muscle contraction caused by mast cell derived mediators may further cause difficulty with breathing. It is not clear why certain allergens, such as bee venom and peanut antigens, are more commonly able to promote this type of response than other allergens. Current therapies for anaphylactic shock include epinephrine and anti-histamines.

Summary

1) Inappropriate, or excessive, immune reactions that cause damage to host tissues are called hypersensitivity disorders

2) Type I hypersensitivity (or immediate hypersensitivity) is mediated by the activation of mast cells and basophils caused by the cross-linking of IgE in response to polyvalent antigens referred to as allergens

3) Type II hypersensitivity is the inflammatory response to self antigens, or inappropriate modulation of host signalling pathways, mediated by autoreactive IgG or IgM antibodies

4) Type III hypersensitivity is the inflammatory response to immune complexes formed of antibody and antigen, which tend to deposit in the arteries, joints and kidneys

5) Type IV hypersensitivity (delayed type hypersensitivity) refers to the tissue damage caused by CD4+ or CD8+ T-cell responses to either self antigens or to microbial antigens

6) Allergy, also called atopy, is the condition caused by repeated bouts of type I hypersensitivity reactions, and is driven principally by activation of mast cells by allergen-specific IgE

7) Atopic individuals, meaning those individuals at high risk of developing allergies, tend to generate high levels of IgE antibodies to particular antigens, to which they are then sensitised

8) The development of IgE is dependent on Th2 cell responses, which promote B-cell class-switching to the IgE isotype

9) Mast cells and basophils express FcεRI, which binds the Fc portion of IgE and cross-linking of this receptor causes degranulation, lipid mediator production and cytokine synthesis

10) The granules of mast cells and basophils are similar, containing histamine, proteases, Th2 cytokines and platelet activating factor

11) The products of mast cell activation cause an immediate response, occurring within minutes of exposure to allergen, and a late-phase response which begins several hours later

12) The immediate response is driven mainly by histamine and PAF, and in skin results in a characteristic wheal and flare reaction which can aid the diagnosis of allergies

13) The late phase response is driven mainly by leukotrienes and cytokines, which lead to the recruitment of eosinophils, and also some neutrophils and Th2 cells

14) The contents of eosinophil granules differ substantially from those of mast cells and basophils, as they appear to be particularly toxic to helminths

15) Allergies are more common in the developed world than in the developing world, and the hygiene hypothesis proposes that this may be due to low exposure to infections and worms in developed countries

16) Asthma is characterised by eosinophilic inflammation of the bronchioles, and hyper-reactivity of bronchial smooth muscle cells to bronchoconstrictors, resulting in episodic airway narrowing and breathing difficulties

17) Anaphylactic shock is caused by systemic mast cell activation caused by allergens which gain access to the circulation

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypersensitivities>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allergy>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mast_cell>

Galli SJ, Tsai M. IgE and mast cells in allergic disease. Nat Med 18:693-704 (2012) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3597223/>
