# Chapter 18: Tumour immunology

Cancer occurs when cells lose their ability to maintain control of the cell cycle, and proliferate too quickly. This loss of control arises as a result of damage to the chromosomal DNA, which if not repaired properly gives rise to mutations. The vast majority of these mutations are harmless, having little or no impact on control of the cell cycle. However, damage to a small number of genes can cause cells to lose control of proliferation, grow faster, outcompete their neighbours, mutate faster and eventually become a mass of transformed cells, otherwise referred to as a tumour.

Mutations appear in the DNA of human cells every time they divide, and also when they are exposed to naturally occurring agents that damage DNA, such as reactive oxygen species and background radiation. Thus, as we age, the burden of mutations accumulates in every cell type in the body. Single cell sequencing studies have shown that, even in healthy tissues, cells often contain mutations within genes that were previously thought to be sufficient to cause cancer. It is therefore a remarkable feat of biology that somehow, for the majority of our lives, these mutated cells are kept at bay. In this chapter, we will focus on how the immune system recognises tumour cells, how it kills them, and how tumours often fight back.

Evidence that the immune system is involved in defence against tumours

The idea that the immune system may play a role in defence against cancer is not new. In the late 1800s, the surgeon William Coley noticed that some of his patients with inoperable tumours achieved complete remission, after suffering an infection with the bacterium Streptococcus pyogenes. Inspired by this observation, he prepared extracts of this and similar bacteria, and injected them into his patients. Although many of his patients suffered terribly from the side effects of systemic immune responses to the bacterial preparations, his treatment nevertheless doubled the survival rate from some forms of tumour.

Evidence that the immune system is likely to underpin this success came in the 1950s, when researchers studied a type of soft tissue tumour, called sarcoma. These tumours can be induced in mice by painting their skin with a chemical (called a mutagen) that induces mutations in cells. It was found that if a tumour was surgically excised from one mouse, and transplanted into another, genetically identical mouse (to avoid issues of non-specific rejection), the tumour grew and the mouse receiving the tumour graft died.

However, if they returned the tumour back into the same mouse they had originally taken it from some time later, it was rejected, and the tumour shrank. The first mouse had become resistant to its own tumour. However, it was not resistant to tumours which had first developed in other mice. Later studies showed that the transplantation of CD8+ T-cells alone from the donor (resistant) mouse was sufficient to confer resistance to tumours from the same mouse in transplant recipients. Together, these experiments proved that CD8+ T-cell responses are generated in response to tumours, and that these cells have the capacity to kill tumour cells if their TCRs recognise antigens of the tumour they enter. Further experiments then showed that mice with intact immune systems survived for much longer after transplantation with various types of tumour than mice genetically deficient in various immune functions, particularly those relating to T-cells.

Together, these observations have given rise to the idea that cells of the immune system are constantly patrolling the body, seeking and destroying cells that may have developed excessive mutations, and are at risk of forming tumours. This concept is referred to as immune surveillance. In support of this notion, human patients with compromised immune systems are seen to be at increased risk of developing certain forms of tumour. For example, a meta-analysis (where data from many separate studies are combined) has shown that patients with HIV infection (who have impaired helper T-cell function), or who have received an organ transplant (who are typically treated with immunosuppressive drugs) are at higher risk of developing 20 of 28 different types of cancer examined. Interestingly, many of these different types of tumour are not thought to be triggered by infectious agents, suggesting that the human immune system has the capacity to recognise, and presumably kill, tumour cells directly.

Some viruses can trigger tumour formation

The most common triggers of DNA damage are errors that occur during the copying of chromosomes prior to cell division, exposure to chemical mutagens (for example, from diet or smoking), ultraviolet light, and radiation from the environment. However, some infectious organisms are also able to increase the risk of formation of certain tumours. For example, infection with certain viruses increases the likelihood of mutations arising in their target cells, and this can in turn increase the risk of cancer. Examples include the human papilloma virus (HPV), which is associated with cervical cancer, and the Epstein Barr virus (EBV), which is associated with B-cell lymphoma and nasopharyngeal carcinoma. Long-term infection with the bacterium Helicobacter pylori, which infects the stomach and can in some cases lead to ulcer formation, increases the risk of stomach cancer. Thus, by helping to prevent or clear infections by such organisms, the risk of cancer associated with these organisms is indirectly reduced by the immune system. This concept is the basis of the HPV vaccination now used successfully to reduce risk of cervical cancer.

Mutated cells can begin to express tumour antigens

As discussed in earlier chapters, the circulating T-cell repertoire has been edited to remove those which are responsive to the host’s own antigens. However, the mutations that occur in cancer cells can result in the expression of either proteins that would not normally be expressed on those cells, or proteins with altered amino acid sequences. Such altered proteins are called tumour antigens.

Broadly speaking there are two different types of tumour antigen. Those antigens which are expressed only on tumour cells, but not on normal cells, are called tumour specific antigens. Those which are expressed at high levels on tumour cells, but also at low levels on some normal cells under certain conditions, are called tumour associated antigens. Both can be targets for the immune response.

The human genome comprises 3 billion base pairs of DNA, of which only about 1% codes for the 20,000 or so proteins required to build human cells. Because mutations occur more or less at random throughout the genome, this means that the majority of mutations a cell acquires throughout life will not affect the sequence of proteins. Those that do, however, can result in proteins with different amino acid sequences. If these changes are present within peptides that are presented on host MHC-I molecules, there is a chance the new antigen will be recognised as foreign by CD8+ T-cells. Alternatively, if APCs phagocytose material from dead tumour cells, the internal antigens from the tumour cells may be expressed on MHC-II of the APC, so potentially activating CD4+ T-cells to respond to those peptides. Some tumours are more antigenic than others, with melanomas being particularly rich in both antigens, and CD8+ T-cells reactive for those antigens.

Most of the mutations that arise in tumour cells occur in the regions between genes. Many of these regions control the rate of expression of proteins, which means that mutations can also result in the expression of genes which are not normally expressed in that type of cell. For example, some proteins are expressed only during development in the foetus, and switched off before birth. When they are aberrantly reactivated and appear on tumour cells, they are referred to as oncofoetal antigens. The two best characterised such antigens are carcinoembryonic antigen (CEA) and alpha-fetoprotein (AFP). Although these antigens do not appear to be major targets of the immune response to tumours, they can be useful as markers for the detection of tumour cells in the laboratory.

Some other proteins are expressed normally only in the testis, but can be aberrantly switched on in tumour cells. So-called testis antigens can be seen as foreign, even if they are not mutated, since the testes are an immune privileged site, which means antigens expressed here are generally not seen by the immune system, so tolerance to these antigens may not have been generated.

Innate immune responses to tumours

All of the cells of the body possess intrinsic mechanisms to detect when their burden of mutations has become excessive and likely to pose a risk of cancer to the host. When such damage is sensed, these mechanisms trigger the cell to undergo apoptosis. It is when these in-built safety checks fail, (often as a result of mutations in the genes that sense or respond to DNA damage), that tumours can begin to develop.

The next line of early defence falls to two key cell types of the innate immune system: Natural killer cells and macrophages. NK cells continually patrol the body, ‘feeling’ the surfaces of the cells they pass by. Cells which are stressed, or behaving abnormally, often express receptors on their surface (such as MIC-A, MIC-B and ULB) which are ligands for the NKG2D activating receptor on NK cells. In the absence of suppressive signals, the NK cell responds to these warning signals by killing the potentially malignant cell. Tumour cells also frequently turn off expression of MHC-I, which could otherwise present internal tumour antigens to passing CD8+ T-cells, to avoid being killed by these cells. NK cells recognise this as a trick frequently employed by virally infected and tumour cells to evade detection, so eliminate them on this basis too. The cytokines IFN-γ and IL-12 increase the activity of NK-cells, which means that their capacity to kill tumour cells is enhanced if Th1 helper T-cells and M1 macrophages are also active in the tumour.

In this way, macrophages polarised in the M1 direction can help to promote anti-tumour immunity. TNF-α released by these cells can also induce thrombosis in local tumour blood vessels, causing it to shrink (hence name ‘tumour necrosis factor’). It is thought that macrophages also possess the capacity to phagocytose rogue cells, even if they haven’t yet undergone apoptosis. Many cancer cells express the CD47 marker on their surface, which serves as a “don’t eat me” signal to prevent phagocytosis by macrophages. Experiments have shown that blocking this marker with antibodies restores the ability of macrophages to phagocytose some cancer cells.

However, as discussed later, not all types of macrophage promote defence against tumours. In other states of polarisation or differentiation they are frequently seen to support tumour growth and survival.

Acquired immune responses to tumours

As mentioned above, a tumour is a chaotic mass of competing clones of rogue cells, each carrying their own private mutations. Inevitably, some of the mutations lead to slight changes in the sequence of proteins expressed inside the tumour cells. These may be revealed to cytotoxic T-cells by the presentation of peptides from these proteins on MHC-I.

However, there are several difficulties in generating T-cells with the capacity to kill tumour cells. First, most tumour cells are not professional APCs. This means that peptides are typically presented on tumour cells in the absence of a co-stimulatory signal. Therefore, in order for T-cell responses to be generated to these peptides, cross-presentation by DCs may be required. For this to happen, dead tumour cells should drain to a lymph node, be taken up there by DCs, and ideally some form of danger signal (it is not clear what this may be in sterile tissues) should also be present to help activate the DC. Nevertheless, despite all of these potential hurdles, studies have shown that CTLs specific for tumour antigens are often present in the lymphocytes isolated from patient’s tumours.

Evidence suggests that, in the early years of tumour development at least, CD8+ T-cells often arise that can kill tumour cells. Their killing potential is enhanced in the presence of IFN-γ released by Th1 cells, which may also be generated in response to tumour antigens. A further benefit of IFN-γ is that it helps to increase expression of MHC-I on the tumour cell surface, so making them more visible to the immune system.

The roles played by antibodies in tumour defence are less clear. Although it has been shown through the use of therapeutic (i.e. engineered) antibodies that the binding of antibody to tumour-antigens can label tumour cells for killing by complement, NK-cells (via ADCC) or macrophages (by phagocytosis), evidence supporting a role for B-cells and antibodies in natural defence against tumours is still somewhat lacking.

Most tumours evolve defences against the host immune system

If tumours were fixed targets, accumulating no further mutations over time, the combination of M1 macrophages, NK-cells, CD8+ and Th1 lymphocytes, would probably be sufficient to clear most tumours. However, as tumours grow and evolve, further errors in the DNA replication and error checking machinery typically occur, leading to step-wise increases in the rate of mutation. This means that eventually, if not cleared in time, the functions of almost any of the 20,000 genes available in the human genome become unlocked and available to at least some of the cells in the mass. As there are billions of cells in even a modestly sized tumour, it becomes statistically very likely that at least some of the cells in the mass will eventually gain the ability to express proteins involved in immune function.

Over time, those cells that increase expression of molecules that impair anti-tumour immune defences will gain a selective advantage, and outcompete their neighbours, which may still be held back or killed by the immune response. As time progresses, further means of switching off immune functions are discovered by random mutation and natural selection until eventually, the internal environment of the tumour, called the tumour micro-environment, becomes highly immunosuppressive.

There are four major mechanisms by which tumours evade the host immune system. They are (i) immunoediting to evade killing by CTLs, (ii) release of immunosuppressive molecules, (iii) expression of immune checkpoint proteins, and (iv) the recruitment of immunosuppressive cells into the tumour.

Immune evasion via immunoediting

As mentioned above, the number of mutations in protein-coding genes, and their associated altered peptides with potential for recognition by cytotoxic T-cells (called neoantigens), increases with time as tumours develop. It is thought that the forces of natural selection are powerfully at work in early tumours. Those tumour cells which are easily recognised by T-cells are quickly destroyed. However, rare mutants that lack expression of the neoantigen escape detection, and survive. As their neighbours are killed, the less visible clones take over the mass. This process repeats over time, until eventually the only tumour cells that survive are those harbouring mutations that do not result in peptides that trigger T-cell responses. This process of losing immunogenic clones over time, resulting eventually in a tumour largely lacking in appropriate T-cell antigens, is called immunoediting.

Immune evasion via secretion of immunosuppressive molecules

All of the cells in the body contain numerous genes with the capacity to suppress immune responses. However, in most cells, and for most of the time, these genes are switched off. A very common means by which tumours escape immune clearance is the progressive appearance for mutations which enable tumour cells to switch on expression of such immunosuppressive genes.

Two of the most potent examples include the cytokines IL-10 and TGF-β, which efficiently inhibit the anti-tumour responses of CD8+ T-cells, NK-cells and M1 macrophages. However, many other molecules from diverse cellular pathways, including enzymes and lipids, such as arginase and prostaglandin E2, can also be co-opted by tumours to help switch off the immune response. The concentrations of such molecules are highest within the tumour itself (the tumour microenvironment), and their abundance often profoundly limits the effectiveness of immune responses within the tumour. However, if tumours become very large, sufficient quantities of these immunosuppressive molecules may leak into the circulation, and compromise the ability of the patient to combat infection.

Immune evasion via recruitment of immune suppressing cells

Another strategy commonly employed by tumours to escape immune clearance is to co-opt certain cells of the immune system to exert anti-inflammatory effects on their behalf. This is achieved by the secretion of molecules that help to recruit and retain the types of immune cell that normally serve to either switch off or redirect the immune response (Figure 18.1).

![](image_rsrc4EG.jpg)

For example, regulatory T-cells are often recruited to growing tumours, and their release of IL-10 and TGF-β helps to reinforce the immunosuppressive environment. CD4 helper T-cells can also be polarised towards the Th2 subset, releasing cytokines which help to push macrophages away from M1 polarisation, and towards the more tolerant M2 polarisation. M2 macrophages polarised in this way are also recruited into and retained within the tumour, since their secreted products help to limit the anti-tumour activity of Th1 T-cells and M1 macrophages. These recruited macrophages are called tumour associated macrophages (TAMs).

Another cell type that is commonly recruited to tumours and lymph nodes of patients is the myeloid derived suppressor cell (MDSC). These are immature cells recruited from the bone marrow and polarised by the tumour microenvironment into an unusual, immunosuppressive phenotype. They secrete IL-10, and the enzyme indolamine 2,3-dioxygenase, which degrades the tryptophan T-cells need to proliferate. They also promote both Th2 and M2 polarisation, so further enhancing the immunosuppressive environment of the tumour.

Immune evasion via checkpoint proteins

In earlier chapters, we discussed the fact that the recognition of antigen on MHC by a T-cell is by itself not sufficient to cause activation of the T-cell. A second (or co-stimulatory) activating signal, must be received from the APC at the same time as presentation of peptide on MHC. But this process of T-cell activation needs to be carefully regulated. If it is activated too readily, immune responses could be directed towards self-antigens, leading to auto-immunity. There are therefore many other pairs of receptors and ligands that interact while the APC and T-cell are in contact. Some of these pairings promote T-cell activation, while others inhibit it. In this way, the question of whether the T-cell is activated or not depends in large part on the balance of signals it receives from these co-stimulatory and inhibitory signals from either the APC, or the MHC-I expressing target cell (Figure 18.2).

![](image_rsrc4EH.jpg)

The receptors which serve to inhibit the co-stimulatory signals during T-cell activation are called checkpoint proteins. Two of the best studied examples are PD-1 (programmed cell death protein-1) and CTLA-4 (cytotoxic T-lymphocyte-associated protein 4). The counterpart ligand for PD-1 is PD-L1, and CTLA-4 binds to the B7 proteins B7-1 (CD80) and B7.2 (CD86). Notably, B7.1 and B7.2 are also ligands for the activating receptor CD28.

When these receptors bind to their counterpart ligands on APCs or target cells, the T-cell response is switched off. Tumour cells frequently develop mutations which allow them to switch on the expression of checkpoint proteins, so enabling them to limit or even switch off T-cell responses. Thus, although CD8+ T-cells frequently arise in patients which are capable of detecting tumour antigens and killing tumour cells, many are switched off by checkpoint proteins expressed by the tumour.

It should be noted that there are many other inhibitory receptors on the surface of T-cells, and all are available for tumour cells to discover and exploit. These include, for example (with their counterpart expressed on the surface of APCs shown in brackets): TIM-3 (for Galectin-9), TIGIT (for CD155) and VISTA (receptor yet to be identified).

Another approach taken by tumours to evade the initial priming of T-cell responses is to simply reduce expression of MHC and activating co-stimulatory molecules. Examples of activating receptors on the surface of T-cells, include (with their counterpart expressed on the surface of APCs shown in brackets): CD28 (for the B7 proteins CD86 and CD80), CD40L (for CD40) OX40 (for OX40L), GITR (for GITRL), CD27 (for CD70), 4-1BB (for 4-1BBL), HVEM (for LIGHT), and ICOS (for ICOSL).

Some of the checkpoint proteins are also able to inhibit the killing capacity of NK-cells. While they are mostly expressed as transmembrane molecules on the surface of the tumour cell, they can also be expressed as secreted, soluble domains, which block T-cell activities more broadly within the tumour mass.

Summary

1) All cells accumulate mutations in their genomic DNA as they divide, some of which may lead to the loss of control of cell division, and the formation of a tumour

2) Evidence suggests that immune cells continually patrol the body, removing or otherwise preventing from growing, rogue mutated cells, in a process called immune surveillance

3) Some viruses, such as the human papilloma virus, can increase the risk of tumour-forming mutations occurring in the cells they infect

4) Vaccination against such viruses can reduce the risk of cancer developing in the target tissue of that virus (for example, HPV for cervical cancer)

5) Some mutations in tumour cells may alter the expression level, or amino acid sequence, of some of the proteins they express - such proteins are termed tumour antigens

6) Tumour antigens which are expressed only on tumour cells, but not on normal cells, are called tumour specific antigens

7) Tumour antigens which are expressed at high levels on tumour cells, but also at low levels on some normal cells, are called tumour associated antigens

8) Tumour antigens which are expressed normally only before birth are called oncofoetal antigens

9) Tumour cells may express high levels of ligands of NK-cell activating receptors, or low levels of NK-cell inhibitor receptors, such that some tumour cells may be killed by NK cells

10) Cytotoxic T-cells specific for tumour antigens frequently appear in patients, but do not always clear the tumour

11) Most tumours eventually discover mechanisms of turning off the immune response through natural selection, enabling them to continue to grow

12) The four major mechanisms by which tumours evade the immune system are (i) immunoediting to evade killing by CTLs, (ii) release of immunosuppressive molecules, (iii) expression of immune checkpoint proteins and (iv) recruitment of immunosuppressive cells into the tumour

13) Immunoediting is the process by which tumour cells expressing antigens which strongly activate T-cells are killed, leaving those tumour cells which do not express those antigens to survive and grow

14) Tumour cells often express the immunosuppressive cytokines IL-10 or TGF-β, which effectively turn off anti-tumour immune responses

15) Specific cell types, such as regulatory T-cells, T-helper type 2 cells, M2 macrophages, and myeloid derived suppressor cells, are often recruited to tumours to help secrete immunosuppressive cytokines

16) Tumour cells often express proteins, such as PD-L1, which stimulate the checkpoint machinery in T-cells, so preventing the T-cells from being activated within the tumour

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cancer_immunology>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immune_checkpoint>

Binnewies M, et al. Understanding the tumor immune microenvironment (TIME) for effective therapy. Nat Med 24:541-50 (2018) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5998822/>

---

# Chapter 19: Tumour immunotherapies

In recent decades, three major approaches have been used to treat cancer: surgery, radiotherapy and chemotherapy. Although these approaches can often be curative if the tumour is caught early, more aggressive tumours, and particularly those which have spread (metastasised) to multiple sites around the body, are much harder to treat. There is therefore an urgent need for a fourth way of treating cancer. Immunotherapy - the harnessing of the molecules and cells of the human immune system to target cancer cells - is that fourth way, and it promises to revolutionise the outlook for even those cancers which are currently the most difficult to treat. At the time of writing, in the light of some remarkable advances, research into the potential of immunotherapies for cancer has become the fastest area of growth not only in oncology, but also in the entire pharmaceutical industry. In this chapter, we will explore some of the latest approaches to restoring, reawakening and refocussing the power of the immune system to detect and kill cancer cells.

Why new therapies for cancer are required

For many patients, the conventional approaches to cancer treatment often result in complete remission or cure. Surgery, in particular, is often curative if the tumour has not spread and every tumour cell can be excised in one mass. Sometimes, the mass is located in an area that is inaccessible for surgery, or too close to sensitive structures to risk a surgical approach. In these cases, radiotherapy, which is the use of beams of radiation, typically X-rays, gamma rays or protons, can be used to target the tumour.

However, in many cases, patients report symptoms after the tumour has spread, which makes surgery and radiotherapy less viable options. Chemotherapy is currently the mainstay of therapy in these circumstances. The principle of chemotherapy is the use of drugs which are more toxic to tumour cells (which divide quickly), than to healthy cells (which divide less frequently). Thus, a narrow range of specific concentrations - termed the therapeutic window - exists, within which a cytotoxic drug can be used to selectively kill tumour cells without killing too many normal cells. Often, tumours respond well to the initial course of chemotherapy, shrinking, sometimes disappearing, and the patient enters remission.

However, it is now understood that the genetic makeup of cells within a tumour mass is not homogeneous. Every cell in the mass has its own particular suite of mutations - some of which switch on genes that either neutralise chemotherapy drugs, or pump them back out of the cell. Thus, while the first round of chemotherapy often kills the sensitive tumour cells, and the bulk of the mass is killed, those that harbour mutations that make them resistant to the first drug or drugs used survive. Once their sensitive neighbours have been eliminated, they may eventually grow to refill the empty niche. But as the mass is now comprised only of resistant cells, the chemotherapy drugs that worked the first time around will not be effective against the new growth. This emergence of resistance to chemotherapy drugs is a common cause of treatment failure.

The conventional approaches to cancer therapy also present several other limitations. Surgery carries the risk of complications, such as loss of function of nerves or organs. The off-target effects of radiotherapy can lead to nausea, immune dysfunction and skin burns. And because certain cells - such as stem cells in the gut, hair follicles and bone marrow - divide quickly in health, they can be killed by chemotherapy drugs, leading to symptoms such as hair loss, nausea and increased risk of infections.

Together, these issues of drug resistance and unacceptable side effects are the main arguments for the urgent requirement for new approaches to cancer treatment.

Anti-tumour immunotherapy

As discussed in the previous chapter, the earliest steps towards tumour immunotherapies were taken in the late 1800s when the physician William Coley injected extracts of bacteria into patients with inoperable sarcomas. The results were anything but perfect - many of his patients experienced terrible side effects associated with induction of the systemic inflammatory response syndrome (see Chapter 5), which occasionally proved fatal. Nevertheless, complete remission was achieved in about 10% of the treated patients, so proving for the first time that it is possible to prime the normal human immune system in such a way that it gains the capacity to kill tumours.

Today, we have a much better understanding of how tumour cells interact with the cells and molecules of the human immune system, and it seems likely that Coley’s success was due to the priming of M1 macrophages, Th1 and CD8+ T-cell responses by the PAMPs present in his bacterial mixtures. This has inspired more recent attempts to “reawaken” anti-tumour immune responses in similar ways, using more carefully controlled approaches to cell activation.

In particular, the aim of immunotherapy is to either stimulate host responses to tumours, or to introduce exogenous antibodies or cell therapies by passive transfer with the capacity to selectively target and kill tumour cells.

Tumour vaccination

In earlier chapters, we discussed how responses to protein antigens require their presentation on mature (activated) dendritic cells for the generation of effective T-cell responses. The presentation of peptides on non-professional APCs, or non-activated DCs, (i.e. without co-stimulatory signals), can lead to anergy, and lack of response against potential tumour antigens. Thus, early attempts to reawaken the immune response to tumours focussed on the concept of vaccination, which is a well-practised means of educating the immune system through the use of adjuvants to activate dendritic cells while they present target antigens.

One of the earliest such approaches arose from the observation that cancer was less frequently observed in tuberculosis patients than in the general population. The organism used as the basis of the Bacillus Calmette-Guerin (BCG) vaccine for tuberculosis, Mycobacterium bovis, was found to be able to infect bladder epithelial cells if administered to the interior of the bladder. This treatment was then shown to improve survival from bladder cancer, and has been used for this type of tumour for almost 40 years, remaining one of the most effective treatments for this condition. It is thought that the success lies in the capacity of the mycobacterial PAMPs to stimulate DC activation, and the presentation of tumour antigens to T-cells with strong co-stimulatory signals.

More recent approaches to enabling the presentation of tumour antigens on activated DCs have focussed on more specific approaches. For example, tumour antigens can be extracted from biopsies of tumours taken from patients, mixed with powerful adjuvants, then injected back into patients - this approach has been trialled for melanoma and colon cancer. More advanced approaches to DC activation will be discussed in the following section on cell therapy.

There is another, less direct, means by which vaccination is already being used to reduce risk of cancer. As some viruses, such as HPV, can increase the risk of cancer developing in certain tissues, vaccination against such viruses to prevent infection reduces the risk of the tumour forming in the first place. For example, the HPV vaccine is now used to reduce risk of cervical cancer in women, and HBV vaccination reduces risk of liver cancer.

Antibodies against growth factor receptors

Although it is thought that the majority of tumour cell killing is mediated by T-cells, NK-cells and macrophages during the normal immune response, there is much current interest in the use of therapeutic antibodies to treat cancer. For example, some forms of breast cancer rely on signalling through a growth factor receptor called human epidermal growth factor receptor 2 (HER2), for their ability to proliferate. A monoclonal antibody has been developed to target this antigen, so blocking the signal used by the tumour cells to grow and divide. Called Herceptin (or trastuzumab), the drug increases survival in patients with HER2+ breast or stomach tumours.

Blocking antibodies have also been developed to target the epidermal growth factor receptor (EGFR) for treatment of colorectal tumours, and antibodies blocking the function of the angiogenic cytokine vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) have been trialled to reduce the formation of blood vessels in tumours.

The above approaches aim to block tumour cell functions, rather than killing them directly. However, more recently developed antibodies are being used to stimulate the direct killing of tumour cells by antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC). The clustering of Fc domains on the surface of a cell expressing the target antigen will be recognised by NK-cells, causing them to degranulate and kill the cell. Complement will also be activated near the cell surface, further damaging it and labelling it for disposal. Macrophages can then more easily phagocytose the tumour cells, labelled both by antibody and complement.

This approach has been shown to work well for B-cell lymphomas and leukaemias, in which the majority of the tumour cells express the surface antigen CD20. Monoclonal antibodies targeting this antigen, such as ofatumumab, are approved for the treatment of B-cell malignancies. Notably, only B-cells, and no other cells in the body, express this antigen, which means that only B-cells are targeted by the therapy. This is a major advantage of antibody therapy compared to drug-based chemotherapy approaches, which are much less specific for tumour cells and so typically present more serious side effects.

Cytokines as treatments for tumours

Early studies into potential immunotherapies explored a very simple means of activating immune cells, namely the injection of T-cell and NK-cell activating cytokines directly into the patient. IL-2 injections are approved for the treatment of melanoma and renal cell carcinoma, since the growth of these tumours has been shown to be slowed by such treatment. IFN-α, typically released during viral infections and thought to be responsible for some autoimmune conditions, is a potent activator of both NK-cell activity, and MHC-I expression. Its use has been approved for the treatment of malignant melanoma, some lymphomas and leukaemias. Also, while not used to kill tumour cells directly, the cytokine GM-CSF is sometimes administered after chemotherapy to promote the recovery of bone marrow and the restoration of blood cell production.

Checkpoint inhibitors

The strength of every immune response depends on the balance of both activating and inhibitory signals. The immune system has, in effect, one foot on the brake, and one foot on the accelerator at all times. This might seem like an unnecessary complexity, but it serves the two goals of (i) preventing inappropriate, potentially damaging, responses to host or innocuous antigens and (ii) enabling finer control of responses such that they are proportionate to the threat.

Checkpoint proteins are receptors that serve to regulate T-cell activation when it is in contact with an APC. These signals help the T-cell to decide whether to trigger a response or not. Some checkpoints provide co-stimulatory signals. Others provide inhibitory signals. The balance of the strength of these two signals determines whether, and to what extent, the T-cell is activated.

As mentioned in the previous chapter, tumour cells frequently express checkpoint proteins that limit the T-cell response. These pairings can be blocked with monoclonal antibodies used as drugs to interfere with the signal. In other words, they aim to “release the brakes” on the immune system, by blocking these natural safety valves. Such drugs are called checkpoint inhibitors, and they have had a profound impact on cancer therapy (Figure 19.1).

![](image_rsrc4EJ.jpg)

The first attempts to reverse the immunosuppressive effects of checkpoint proteins in tumour therapy began with trials of anti-CTLA4 in 2000. It was the first checkpoint inhibitor to be approved for therapeutic use (for melanoma in 2011). Since then, antibodies targeting two other checkpoints - PD-1 and PD-L1 - have been approved for use in cancer therapy. For reasons that remain unclear, checkpoint inhibitors appear to be particularly well-suited to the treatment of metastatic melanoma. Average survival for this condition was under 1 year on conventional therapies, but is greater than 3 years in patients receiving a combination of anti-CTLA4 and anti-PD-1 therapy. Other types of cancer have also shown promise in checkpoint therapy, including kidney, colorectal, head and neck cancers.

The antibodies mentioned above, targeting CTLA-4, PD-1 and PD-L1 are all blocking antibodies. That is, they serve to prevent the interaction of these proteins with their partners by binding to them and getting in the way. However, because many receptors are activated by dimerisation within the membrane, it is also possible to stimulate signalling of some receptors through the use of antibodies. By binding two receptors of the same type close together, this occasionally causes them to signal. Thus, some monoclonal antibodies have been developed which activate the co-stimulatory receptors on T-cells. So-called agonistic antibodies have been developed to target OX40, GITR and CD28. Research in this area has slowed recently, following a clinical trial where an antibody used to stimulate CD28 caused severe inflammatory responses in healthy volunteers receiving low doses in early tests for safety.

Although most interest is currently in targeting CTLA-4, PD-1 and PD-L1, it should be noted that there are many other potential targets for either blocking, or activating, therapeutic antibodies in the context of checkpoint and co-stimulatory proteins. Table 19.1 lists some of the checkpoint targets currently in late stage research, or clinical trials. This is not a comprehensive list - at least 50 other checkpoint or related genes are thought to be potential contributors to tumour immune evasion.

Table 19.1 - List of checkpoint proteins and their ligands

Inhibitory pairings| | Activating (co-stimulatory) pairings ---|---|--- On APC| On T-cell| | On APC| On T-cell PD-L1| PD-1| | CD86 / CD80| CD28 PD-L2| PD-1| | CD40| CD40L CD86/80| CTLA4| | CD70| CD27 CD155| TIGIT| | 4-1BBL| 4-1BB Galectin-9| TIM-3| | OX40L| OX40 ? not clear| VISTA| | GITRL| GITR HVEM| BTLA| | ICOSL| ICOS Abbreviations for Table 19.1: 4-1BB(L) tumor necrosis factor receptor 9, CD137 (ligand); BTLA, B- and T-lymphocyte attenuator; GITR(L), glucocorticoid-induced TNFR family-related protein (ligand); HVEM, herpes virus-entry mediator; ICOS(L), inducible T-cell costimulatory (ligand); OX40(L), tumor necrosis factor receptor superfamily, member 4, CD134 (ligand); TIGIT, T-cell immunoreceptor with Ig and ITIM domains; TIM-3, T-cell Ig mucin domain-containing 3; VISTA, V-domain Ig suppressor of T-cell activation.

Cell therapy

The approaches taken to stimulate anti-tumour immunity discussed above are based on the use of relatively simple molecules, such as adjuvants and monoclonal antibodies. However, it is also possible to extract living immune cells from patients’ blood, or even tumour samples, stimulate or otherwise modify the cells in some way in vitro, then re-infuse them back into the patient. By using the patient’s own cells, there are no issues of graft rejection or graft vs host disease. This approach, called adoptive cell therapy, has been trialled for DCs, NK-cells and T-cells, in some cases with very promising results.

The dendritic cell approach is an extension of the vaccination strategies discussed earlier, with the same aim of enhancing the activation status of DCs presenting tumour antigens to T-cells. Monocytes are isolated from the patient’s own blood, and matured in vitro through exposure to specific cytokines such that they differentiate into dendritic cells. These DCs can then be activated by treatment with other cytokines and exposed to specific antigens of interest in vitro, before being returned by injection into the patient. The activated DCs migrate to lymph nodes, where they activate T-cell responses directed against the tumour antigen of interest.

This approach is the basis of Sipuleucel-T, the first DC vaccine approved by the FDA for the treatment of cancer. In this therapy, DCs are exposed to the cytokine granulocyte-macrophage colony stimulating factor (GM-CSF), and the target antigen prostatic acid phosphatase, which is highly expressed in most prostate cancer cells. The treatment was shown to extend the survival time for patients with metastatic prostate cancer, and was approved by the FDA in 2011 for this condition. However, the improvement in survival is not dramatic, perhaps because the antigen-specific T-cells arising from treatment are switched off by the immunosuppressive environment of the TME.

Other treatments have aimed to reawaken the latent power of NK cells, which have intrinsic capacity to recognise and kill at least some forms of tumour cell. Here, specific antibodies are used to capture and purify NK cells from the blood of the patient, before activating them in culture by exposing them to activating cytokines, then infusing the activated cells back into the patient. At the time of writing, such NK-cell therapies remain in clinical trials, and have not yet been approved for widespread use.

Many researchers believe that T-cells have the greatest capacity for stimulating anti-tumour responses, since CD8+ T-cells can kill tumour cells expressing specific tumour antigens, and Th1 T-cells can help to skew the TME towards a less immunosuppressive environment. Early approaches to the activation of tumour-specific T-cells focussed on isolating T-cells from tumours or tumour-draining lymph nodes of patients, treating them with IL-2 in vitro to expand their numbers, and then returning them to the host. The hypothesis was that this process may amplify the number of tumour specific T-cells, and thereby help to shift the balance towards tumour rejection and clearance. However, this approach has not yielded great success. It was realised that this may be at least partly because many or most of the expanded T-cells do not target tumour antigens.

Chimaeric antigen receptor (CAR) T-cells

The short-comings of this approach led researchers to consider totally new approaches to adoptive cell therapy, with the aim of expanding and activating only those T-cells targeting specific tumour antigens. It was realised that one way of achieving this may be to genetically engineer a patient’s T-cells to express a receptor targeting the tumour antigen of interest. While promising, this approach faced the issue that a TCR from one person may not recognise a tumour antigen on MHC of a different person (since different individuals express different versions of MHC molecules, as explained in Chapter 9). Moreover, tumour cells often downregulate MHC expression, which renders their internal antigens invisible to T-cell detection. What was needed was some way of using antibodies, which bind directly to the target of interest and do not require presentation on MHC. A key breakthrough occurred in the field when it was discovered that the signalling domain of a TCR can be genetically fused with the antigen-binding domain of a monoclonal antibody, to create a hybrid, or chimaeric receptor, that is still able to activate T-cells (Figure 19.2). Such receptors possess the benefits of both systems - the ability to bind antigens without MHC from the antibody part, and the ability to activate T-cells, from the TCR part. T-cells genetically engineered to express these chimaeric antigen receptors (CARs) are called CAR T-cells.

![](image_rsrc4EK.jpg)

The process of CAR T-cell production begins with the extraction of T-cells from the patient’s own blood. These cells are then genetically engineered in the laboratory, typically using a virus, to express a CAR which has been engineered to target a single, cell-surface antigen of interest that marks the surface of the tumour cell. The synthetic gene that codes for the CAR comprises two main parts: the intracellular domain of the zeta chain of the TCR complex, and an antigen-binding antibody fragment called scFv (see Chapter 21) in the extracellular space, joined together by a transmembrane domain and a flexible linker (Figure 19.2). The modified T-cells are then injected back into the patient, where they begin to patrol the tissues for cells expressing the antigen they have been programmed to detect.

If a CD8+ CAR T-cells makes contact with a cell expressing the antigen of interest, the antibody domain of its CAR will bind to that antigen, so causing the CARs of that T-cell to cluster closely together in the cell membrane. Just as regular TCRs become activated by binding to their target peptides presented on MHC, so the binding of the CAR antibody domain of its target antigen causes clustering of the CAR’s TCR zeta chains inside the T-cell. In this way, the same signalling events are triggered inside the CAR T-cell that occur during normal TCR activation. Thus, host cells lacking the target antigen are ignored, but those expressing the tumour antigen are killed. The CAR T-cell itself becomes further activated by this process, proliferates, and continues to seek further tumour cells to kill.

At the time of writing (2019), two CAR T-cell therapies have been approved for clinical use. Both target the CD19 antigen, which is a cell-surface marker expressed by B-cells. Crucially, the CD19 antigen is not expressed by other cell types in the body, which means only B-cells are killed by this therapy. By targeting CD19 in this way, the CAR T-cells are able to reduce circulating B-cell numbers dramatically, so controlling the effects of the excessive proliferation of mutant B-cell clones that underpins B-cell lymphoma and most cases of acute lymphoblastic leukaemia (ALL).

Both CAR T-cell therapies have yielded some remarkable rates of remission for B-cell lymphoma, and particularly for ALL. These successes have inspired much excitement in the field, and hundreds of trials of CAR T-cells for other types of tumour. While these trials are yet to result in approved treatments for other types of tumour, the results so far suggest that first generation CAR T-cells appear to work well in the blood, but less well in solid tumours. It is thought that this may be because the immunosuppressive TME that tends to evolve in solid tumours deactivates the CAR T-cells when they arrive in the mass.

Clearly, the potential of engineered T-cells is at a very early stage, and there are doubtless many breakthroughs to come. However, it is also obvious that the first-generation CAR T-cells in use today present a number of limitations. It is presently a very expensive treatment, and commercial mass production is proving challenging. Some patients relapse with the appearance of tumour cells which have mutated to no longer express the target antigen, so becoming immune to the first CAR T-cell treatment. And relatively frequently, the excessive activation of CAR T-cells results in expression of such large quantities of cytokines that a dangerous ‘cytokine storm’ is triggered, similar in symptoms to septic shock.

Immune-related adverse events (IRAEs)

One of the dangers of artificially enhancing the killing power of the immune system is the potential to trigger damage to normal tissues. When efforts to enhance anti-tumour immunity in patients results in effects such as excessive cytokine release, systemic inflammation or autoimmunity, these events are referred to as immune-related adverse events (IRAEs).

Most information on IRAEs has come from the study of side effects in patients treated with immune checkpoint inhibitors. While ‘releasing the brake’ on T-cell function by the use of such therapies is well tolerated in some patients, IRAEs occur in up to 50% of those receiving anti-PD1 or -PDL1 antibodies, and up to 70% of patients treated with anti-CTLA4.

Organs commonly affected by IRAEs include the lungs (pneumonitis), skin (rashes, vitiligo and pruritus), liver (hepatitis), endocrine system (pituitary, thyroid, adrenals and pancreas), and the digestive tract (colitis). Because the expression profiles and functions of PD-1 and CTLA-4 differ, so the profiles of IRAEs resulting from each type of checkpoint inhibitor are also different. For example, thyroiditis and pneumonitis are more common following anti-PD1/PDL1 treatment, while colitis and diarrhoea most commonly occur after treatment with anti-CTLA4.

The symptoms of IRAEs typically do not present immediately, but rather several weeks to months after treatment, and can range from relatively benign to severe. As more trials focus on the use of combination therapies, in which antibodies against both PD-1 and CTLA-4, and occasionally other checkpoint proteins are given together, so the frequency and severity of IRAEs also increases.

The principal causes of IRAEs are currently unclear. However, recent evidence suggests that the inhibition of checkpoint proteins results in a breakdown of tolerance, increasing the risk of autoimmunity to normal (non-tumour) host antigens, mediated by both T-cells and antibodies. These results are consistent with the observation that mice genetically deficient in various checkpoint proteins frequently develop autoimmune conditions.

Left untreated, the systemic inflammation, autoimmunity or cytokine storm could lead to shock and/or permanent damage to organ systems. Thus, prompt action is required to limit immune function in these cases. The mainstay of treatment in these circumstances is the use of high-dose corticosteroids to dampen systemic immune function, and stopping checkpoint inhibitor therapy. Should these interventions fail to control symptoms, monoclonal antibodies targeting the inflammatory cytokine TNF-α (such as infliximab) can also be given.

Future developments in immunotherapy

It is not normally within the remit of a textbook to comment on advances in science or technology which have not yet fully matured. However, given the unusually rapid progress being witnessed currently in the field of immunotherapy, and the fact that this technology is likely to become a mainstay of cancer therapy in the near future, it may be worth commenting on some of the approaches currently being explored in this field at the time of writing, which are likely to be of significance in the coming years, and present key opportunities for study, research and jobs for early career immunologists.

As mentioned above, in addition to PD-1, PD-L1 and CTLA-4, there are many other potential checkpoint molecules employed by tumours to evade immune clearance. Examples showing particular promise in early laboratory and clinical studies include VISTA, LAG-3, TIGIT and TIM-3. It is also increasingly recognised that tumours frequently switch on numerous different checkpoint inhibitors at once, and combinations of various antibodies targeting these are currently being explored. Some groups are exploring the possibility of using DNA or mRNA sequence information taken from the tumours of individual patients, to identify which checkpoint proteins are expressed by those tumours, such that personalised medicines can be prescribed to maximise potential for restoration of anti-tumour immune responses in a specific patient.

CAR T-cells are a relatively new addition to the field, having been only approved for clinical use in 2017. However, it is already recognised that they have enormous potential for improvement. The CAR T-cells available in the clinic today are activated only by the clustering of the zeta domain, and target only one antigen. Next generation CAR T-cells are being engineered to contain multiple co-activator domains in the cytosolic component of their TCR chains, including, for example, from the cytosolic domains of CD28 and 4-1BB. These domains provide a co-stimulatory signal to support the TCR zeta chain signalling, so helping to fully activate the cells in the presence of tumour antigen.

Another key limitation of current CAR T-cell therapies is that a small number of cells within a tumour may lose expression of the target antigen. This makes them invisible to CAR T-cells, and the tumour can relapse and regrow. In principle, this issue can be mitigated by targeting more than one tumour antigen. From a statistical point of view, while it is likely that some cells in the mass may switch off expression of one surface antigen, it is less likely that they will lose two at the same time, and much less likely that they may lose three or more. For this reason, next generation CAR T-cells are being developed which express multiple CARs, each targeting a different tumour antigen. This approach has the potential to reduce the risk of relapse through antigen loss by escape mutants significantly.

Today’s CAR T-cells also work poorly within solid tumours. Some researchers believe that this is due to the immunosuppressive effects of the tumour micro-environment (TME). Efforts are underway to engineer T-cells to be unresponsive to the immunosuppressive cytokines of the TME - such as by genetically silencing the receptors for IL-10 or TGF-β. Other approaches include engineering the cells to secrete cytokines, such as IL-7, that promote the survival and activation of the cells, or the chemokine receptor CCR2, which helps them to find and migrate into the tumour mass. T-cells modified in this way are referred to as ‘armoured CAR T-cells’.

Another limitation of CAR T-cells is that they are presently derived from the patient’s own blood. This requires the shipping of a patient’s lymphocytes to a central facility, their bespoke engineered there, and shipping back to the patient. This process is presently time consuming and very expensive (typically hundreds of thousands of dollars per patient). Several groups are working towards lowering these costs by engineering NK cells to express CARs. The premise is that so-called CAR NK cells will have the potential to be more easily engineered to work effectively in a wide variety of different individuals. As CAR T-cells are engineered by the addition of a modified TCR to the patient’s existing T-cells, these cells still retain their original TCR in addition to the engineered one. This means that T-cells from one donor cannot be administered to anyone else, as there is a risk that the TCRs of the donor could recognise antigens on the recipient’s tissue as foreign, and attack them in a graft vs host disease manner. By preparing a library of pre-engineered CAR NK-cells, each targeting common tumour antigens, it may be possible to offer antigen-specific cell therapies in an ‘off the shelf’ manner for any patient with tumours expressing those antigens, so lowering the costs and complexity of CAR cell therapy.

Of course, it is very difficult to predict where this rapidly evolving field may go. New discoveries are emerging at remarkable pace, and each promises to improve the specificity and efficacy of anti-tumour immunotherapies. For immunologists and patients alike, immunotherapy offers a very exciting future.

Summary

1) The conventional approaches to cancer treatment are surgery, chemotherapy and radiotherapy, but these are not always entirely effective

2) Immunotherapy refers to the use of elements of the immune system to target and kill tumours

3) Immune responses to tumour cells can be improved through the use of vaccines containing tumour antigens, but these have not met great clinical success

4) Antibodies targeting growth factor receptors, such as the human epidermal growth factor receptor 2 (HER2), can be given to slow the growth of tumours which depend on those pathways for proliferation

5) Antibodies targeting cell surface antigens can be used to reduce numbers of specific cell types, such as through the targeting of CD20 on B-cells for the treatment of B-cell lymphomas

6) Therapeutic administration of recombinant cytokines, such as IL-2 and IFN-α has been attempted to promote anti-tumour immune responses, but these have met limited success

7) Checkpoint proteins are receptors that, when triggered by binding their ligand, inhibit the activation of T-cells

8) Tumour cells often evolve mutations which allow them to express checkpoint proteins, as this helps them to evade the immune response

9) Checkpoint inhibitors are therapeutic antibodies that target checkpoint proteins, most notably PD-1, PD-L1 and CTLA-4

10) A common side effect of checkpoint inhibitor therapy is the immune-related adverse event (IRAE), which frequently involves either systemic inflammation or autoimmunity

11) Adoptive cell therapy is the therapeutic administration of preparations of cells which have been typically taken from a patient, then expanded, modified or otherwise engineered in vitro, then reinfused back into the same patient

12) Efforts to clear tumours through the in vitro activation and reinfusion of NK-cells or T-cells with natural, un-modified receptors has not shown great promise

13) Chimaeric antigen receptor (CAR) T-cells harbour a genetically engineered receptor comprising the cytosolic signalling domain of the TCR zeta chain, genetically fused to an extracellular antibody variable domain targeting a tumour antigen

14) CAR T-cells are prepared by harvesting T-cells from a patient, genetically engineering them in vitro to bestow the ability to target a tumour antigen, and reinfusing them back into the same patient

15) CAR T-cells do not require presentation of tumour antigen on MHC molecules, since their synthetic receptor uses an antibody domain to bind directly to a target antigen

16) CAR T-cells can kill target cells expressing the antigen for which their CAR is specific, using mechanisms similar to those of cytotoxic T-cells

17) CAR T-cells currently approved for clinical use target the B-cell antigen CD19, and are used to treat B-cell lymphoma and acute lymphoblastic leukaemia

18) CAR T-cells seem to work well in blood, but are presently less effective for the treatment of solid tumours

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cancer_immunotherapy>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ofatumumab>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Checkpoint_inhibitor>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chimeric_antigen_receptor_T_cell>

Zhang H, Chen J. Current status and future directions of cancer immunotherapy. J Cancer 9:1773-81 (2018) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5968765/>

Yu Y, Cui J. Present and future of cancer immunotherapy: A tumor microenvironmental perspective. Oncol Lett 16:4105-13 (2018) <https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6126324/>
