# Chapter 20: Methods in immunology

Many of the techniques that have been developed to study immunological processes have been found to be immensely useful in other areas of biomedical research. In particular, the discovery that antibodies can bind with high affinity and specificity for particular antigens has led to the development of a wide range of assays based on the use of antibodies. In this chapter, we will discuss some of the major techniques which are used both in immunology research, and in wider biomedical studies.

Monoclonal and polyclonal antibodies

Many of the most commonly used techniques in immunology research, and indeed biomedical research generally, depend on the capacity of antibodies to bind with very high affinity and specificity to target antigens of interest. This property enables antibodies to be used for such purposes as: (i) the detection and quantification of specific antigens (ii) the identification and isolation of specific cell types, (iii) the diagnosis of disease (iv), the inhibition or activation of specific receptors, and (v) the purification of antigens from complex mixtures of other molecules. Generally speaking, there are two main formats of antibody that may be used for these purposes: polyclonal and monoclonal, as will now be discussed.

Polyclonal antibodies are generated by vaccinating a vertebrate animal with the antigen to which specific antibodies are desired. Several weeks later, antibodies specific for that antigen appear in the circulation of the immunised animal. Blood is then drawn from the animal, and allowed to clot so that the serum may be collected. The serum contains antibodies which are specific for the antigen in question. However, the antibodies which arise from this process are a mixture of the many different antibodies generated by the immune response to that antigen, which may involve responses from several different B-cell clones. This occurs because protein antigens typically present multiple distinct epitopes on their surface, and these can be targeted by a range of different antibodies arising from different B-cell clones. Thus, antibodies with many different V-regions are produced that each bind to different parts of the same target antigen. A polyclonal antibody preparation therefore comprises of a mixture of different antibodies derived from several distinct B-cell clones.

By contrast, monoclonal antibodies are solutions containing only one type of antibody, with every antibody molecule in the solution bearing exactly the same amino acid sequence and epitope specificity. The major benefits of polyclonal antibodies are that they are generally faster and cheaper to produce than monoclonal antibodies. However, the properties of polyclonal antibodies are less reproducible between batches, since the relative amounts of each type of antibody in the mixture can vary greatly between individual animals, even if they were immunised in exactly the same way. The preparation of monoclonal antibodies takes more time and effort, but the result is a product which behaves much more consistently from batch to batch. Another common problem in assay development is that antibodies can sometimes bind to antigens in a mixture, other than the target of interest. This so-called “off-target” binding can confound the results of tests, and is more commonly encountered with polyclonal antibodies than monoclonal antibodies. Nevertheless, there are many applications for which polyclonal antibodies are entirely sufficient. The methods by which both types of antibody are produced will be discussed in Chapter 21.

Functional antibodies

Monoclonal antibodies have found a wide range of uses as inhibitors or activators of specific biological pathways. When they are useful for these purposes, they are referred to as functional antibodies. For example, in many cell biology experiments it is useful to be able to inhibit the function of a specific cell-surface receptor. Often this cannot be achieved by drugs, but antibodies can sometimes be generated which are exquisitely specific for individual subclasses of receptor and can be used to block responses to examine the role of a particular receptor in an experiment. Occasionally, the cross-linking of receptors caused by some antibodies leads to receptor activation, and this can also be used experimentally. In experiments using mice, it is sometimes helpful to delete specific cell populations. This can be achieved by injecting the mice with antibodies specific for markers uniquely expressed on the surface of those cells, causing them to be targeted by complement and deleted by ADCC.

Functional antibodies have also been found to have great utility in medicine. For example, antibodies specific for TNF-α, marketed as drugs such as infliximab and etanercept, are used to treat rheumatoid arthritis and Crohn’s disease. Likewise, B-cell depletion using antibody specific for the B-cell marker CD20 can be beneficial in some autoimmune diseases, such as rheumatoid arthritis and multiple sclerosis.

Enzyme linked immunosorbent assays (ELISA)

One of the most commonly used methods involving antibodies is the quantification of specific antigens within a mixture containing many other molecules. Most frequently, this is achieved using enzyme linked immunosorbent assays (ELISAs), of which the most common form is the sandwich ELISA (Figure 20.1).

![](image_rsrc4EM.jpg)

In this process, the plastic wells of a multi-well microtitre plate are coated with an antibody that is specific for one epitope of the target antigen. This is referred to as the capture antibody. The spaces between the antibody molecules are then blocked by adding a solution containing non-specific, unrelated proteins, such as albumin. The wells are then washed several times to remove unbound antibody and the mixture containing antigen is then added. Over the course of an hour or so, the antigen binds firmly to the capture antibody which itself remains firmly bound to the plastic on the bottom of the well. The wells are then washed again several times and a solution containing a different antibody is then added. This antibody binds to a different, non-overlapping epitope on the same target antigen and is therefore referred to as the detection antibody. The wells are then washed again to remove the unbound detection antibodies, which are usually conjugated directly to an enzyme, such as horse-radish peroxidase, that causes the colour of a chemical substrate to change.

The amount of detection antibody that remains bound to the antigen is proportional to the amount of antigen in the sample. This is measured by adding a chemical substrate which changes colour at a rate proportional to the amount of enzyme in the well. At the end of the assay, the colour changes in each well can be measured using a plate reading spectrophotometer, often after adding a solution that halts further progress of the reaction. In some variants of the assay, the detection antibody is biotinylated (i.e. tagged with a molecule termed biotin), and the enzyme is conjugated to a molecule called streptavidin, which binds very firmly to biotin. Because several biotin molecules may bind to a single Fc domain, this helps to amplify the response and improves the sensitivity of the assay. There are also ‘non-sandwich’ variants of the assay (direct binding ELISAs) which bind antigen directly to the plate at the first step, thereby avoiding the requirement for a capture antibody.

In most ELISA experiments, several wells of the plate are filled with a standardised form of the antigen at specific concentrations. These are called standards, and the absorbance readings from these wells are used to create a chart called a ‘standard curve’, which is a plot of absorbance vs concentration. By comparing the colour change in the wells containing samples with those of the standards, the concentration of the antigen in the original mixture can be calculated. ELISAs are often used diagnostically in medicine to quantify the levels of specific antigens or antibodies in patient samples.

Immunoprecipitation

Molecular and cell biology research commonly requires the purification of a specific protein from a mixture of proteins, such as serum. This can be achieved by immunoprecipitation. In this technique, antibody specific for the target protein is bound firmly to a small inert support, such as plastic or agarose beads. A suspension or slurry of these beads is then added to the protein mixture of interest and allowed to incubate for some time to allow the antibody to bind to the target protein. Subsequently, the samples are centrifuged to pellet the beads, which are solid and quickly sink to the bottom of the tube. The overlying supernatant, which contains the unbound proteins, is removed and discarded. The pelleted beads are then resuspended in another solution which either denatures the antibodies, or otherwise disrupts their binding to the target protein, so releasing it into the solution for further experimental analyses. A common method of attaching antibody to beads is to first coat the beads with staphylococcal protein A or protein G. These two proteins bind the Fc portion of IgG antibodies with high affinity, and so may be used to bind capture antibody to the beads. Whole cells may also be purified from mixtures of different cell types using a similar approach, with the adaptation that the beads to which the antibodies are conjugated are magnetic (termed magnetic separation).

Immunoblotting, or Western blotting

Detecting or quantifying the levels of a specific protein within a cell is commonly required. For technical reasons, ELISAs are often not suitable for this purpose. The alternative is to use immunoblotting, also called Western blotting (Figure 20.2). In this method, a cell culture, tissue sample or other complex mixture of proteins, is lysed in a buffer containing sodium dodecyl sulphate (SDS). This detergent denatures the proteins in the sample, causing them to unwind to an unfolded form. The SDS molecules also binds loosely to the protein, conferring a negative charge that is proportional to the size of the protein. The SDS-treated proteins in the samples may then be separated according to their size using polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis (PAGE). When a voltage is applied to the gel, the proteins migrate through it at a rate indirectly proportional to their size, which means that smaller proteins migrate further than larger proteins within a given length of time. Once the proteins have been separated, a membrane composed of a substance that binds tightly to proteins, such as nitrocellulose, is overlaid on top of the gel. A voltage is applied once again, and the proteins migrate under the influence of this electrical field out of the gel and onto the membrane, where they are trapped.

This membrane is then blocked with non-specific proteins, such as bovine serum albumin or milk proteins. This helps to prevent non-specific antibody binding to the membrane during later steps of the experiment. Next, the membrane is ‘probed’ by the addition of an antibody specific for the protein of interest. This antibody, which can be either polyclonal or monoclonal, is referred to as the primary antibody. Next, the membrane is washed and a detection antibody is applied. This so-called secondary antibody binds to the Fc portion of the primary antibody. For this reason, the secondary antibody is always an antibody that specifically binds to Fc of the species in which the primary antibody was raised. For example, if the primary antibody is a mouse IgG, the secondary antibody must bind the Fc portion of mouse IgG, and is usually derived from another species (since it is difficult to make mice generate antibodies to their own proteins, for reasons discussed in earlier chapters). Often, the secondary antibody has an enzyme covalently attached to it for detection purposes.

After the secondary antibody has bound, the membrane is washed again and unbound antibody is removed. At this stage, a chemiluminescent substrate may be applied which gives off a faint glow of light in the presence of enzyme attached to the detection antibody. Because the enzyme is located where the secondary antibody has bound to the primary antibody, which in turn has bound to the antigen, the location of this light signal can be used to identify and quantify the presence of the protein in the sample. In years gone by, this light was revealed by exposure to photographic film in a dark room, but sensitive digital cameras are more commonly used today. Alternatively, a secondary antibody tagged with a fluorescent label can be used to locate and quantify the protein of interest. One of the key points to consider in Western blotting is that some antibodies which bind well to intact antigens often do not bind well to denatured proteins and may perform poorly in immunoblot experiments, even if they function well in other applications.

![](image_rsrc4EN.jpg)

Flow cytometry and antibody-mediated purification of cells

In previous chapters, we have made frequent reference to the utility of cell surface markers that can be used to identify particular types of cell. These markers are often labelled by their ‘cluster of differentiation’, or CD number. For example, CD3 is a good marker of T-lymphocytes, CD14 of monocytes and CD19 of B-lymphocytes. The expression levels of these, and other cell surface markers can be measured using a technique called flow cytometry (Figure 20.3).

This technique employs a specialised device called a flow cytometer, which uses a focused beam of laser light to illuminate individual cells as they pass through the beam, and monitor the light that is emitted from these cells. The process generally begins with the labelling of the cells with one or more antibodies specific for particular cell markers that have been labelled by the covalent addition of a fluorophore. A suspension of the labelled cells is then prepared in a buffered liquid, which is passed through a series of tubes in such a manner that eventually a very fine stream of liquid is ejected from a tight nozzle within the machine. The stream is formed in such a way that the cells contained within the liquid are caused to enter the stream in single file, i.e. one at a time.

Within the machine, a laser, producing light of a fixed wavelength (e.g. 488 nm), illuminates the stream and the cells pass through this beam of light one at a time. Some of the light passes through the cell (this is called forward scatter) and some of the light is bounced sideways by organelles within the cell (this is called side scatter). The machine quantifies the amount of light being sent in each direction. The amount of forward scatter gives an indication of the cell size, and the amount of side scatter gives an indication of the granularity of the cell. Often, researchers create charts that plot forward scatter against side scatter to identify certain populations or cell types within a mixture of cells (Figure 20.3B). The measurement of forward and side scatter does not depend on fluorescence and therefore this function does not require antibody labelling.

The measurement of cellular antigens, or markers, is achieved by detecting the specific wavelength of light that is emitted by the fluorophore attached to the antibody in question. For example, a commonly used tag called FITC absorbs light efficiently at 488 nm (the colour produced by a helium laser), and emits well at 520 nm. Dozens of other fluorophores are available that fluoresce at different wavelengths, and therefore give rise to different colours that can be monitored separately. Modern flow cytometers can measure at least 3 of these different colours simultaneously, which means that several different cellular markers can be monitored at once using such machines, if different coloured antibodies are chosen for each marker. This allows researchers to finely distinguish subsets of cells that otherwise appear very similar by forward and side scatter alone. For example, the CD14loCD16hi and CD14hiCD16- monocyte populations are readily distinguished using multi-colour flow cytometry (Figure 20.3C).

In addition to measuring CD markers, other cellular antigens can be detected, and are often used as markers of cell death, proliferation or activation. For example, the DNA-binding dye propidium iodide can be used to quantify the amount of DNA within a cell, and thereby which stage of the cell-cycle it is in. As propidium iodide is unable to enter healthy cells with intact membranes, it can also be used as a marker of cell viability. Fluorescently tagged Annexin-V, which binds to exposed phosphatidyl serine, is a useful marker of apoptotic cells. Cells can also be permeabilised with mild detergents to create pores in the membrane which allow antibodies to enter during staining. These antibodies can then stain cell signalling intermediates or cytokines, for example, which give an indication of the state of activation of the cell.

In most flow cytometers, the cells that have passed through the laser are discarded to waste. However, a useful adaptation of flow cytometry has been developed which enables users to isolate and purify specific cell types from a mixture of different cells. This process, called fluorescence activated cell sorting (FACS), relies on the same staining of specific markers using fluorescently labelled antibodies as described above. However, rather than discarding the cells that have passed through the detector, electrical fields are used to deflect the stream containing individual cells into different collection vessels. Cells of interest may then be collected in one vessel, and contaminating cell types in another.

![](image_rsrc4EP.jpg)

Immunohistochemistry

While flow cytometry is an excellent tool for the measurement of marker expression on cells in suspension, it is often useful to compare the levels of expression of markers in intact tissues. This often gives an idea of where and how the cells are interacting within the tissue itself. The method used to achieve this is called immunohistochemistry. In this process, a sample of tissue (for example, taken from a biopsy) is first embedded within a waxy substance to preserve its structure. Then, very thin sections of this tissue are created using a microtome. The tissue sections are then allowed to adhere to glass microscope slides and stained in different ways. Frequently, histological stains, such as haematoxylin and eosin, are applied to such slides to visualise the morphology of the different cell types present in the tissue sample.

However, to gain an impression of the localisation or levels of expression of specific antigens, labelled antibodies must be used. In this case, the antibodies may be either enzyme conjugated or fluorescently labelled. Often, a primary antibody is used to bind the antigen and a secondary antibody (that binds to the primary antibody, as described above) is used to generate a signal for detection. The most commonly used enzyme conjugated secondary antibodies yield a brown colour that indicates the presence of antigen, and this can be measured using an ordinary light microscope. Less commonly, fluorescently labelled antibodies may be used and detected using a fluorescence microscope. As different fluorophores can be targeted to different antigens, several colours can be imaged simultaneously to reveal the relative locations of different antigens within the same sample. DNA binding dyes, such as DAPI or Hoechst, are useful indicators of the locations of the nuclei. Modern fluorescent microscopes can use laser scanning of the tissue to reveal the locations of antigens at high resolution in 3 dimensions, and this is referred to as confocal microscopy. Immunohistochemistry is a valuable and frequently used tool in the diagnosis of different diseases.

Antigen-specific T-cell assays

The above methods all rely on the use of antibodies. However, immunology research often requires examination of T-cell function. Two of the most frequently used assays of T-cell function are the T-cell proliferation assay and adoptive transfer of T-cells in experimental animals.

In the T-cell proliferation assay, a culture containing both antigen presenting cells and a population of T-cells, for example obtained from the peripheral blood of a human volunteer, is cultured ex vivo in a tissue culture vessel. The specific antigen of interest is then added to the culture together with thymidine which has been labelled with radioactive tritium (3H). As the T-cells proliferate in response to the antigen, they incorporate thymidine into their DNA. At the end of the experiment, the quantity of radiolabel present in the T-cells is measured by scintillation (a process where radioactive decay causes flashes of light to occur in a liquid called scintillant). The amount of incorporated radiolabel is proportional to the amount of cell division that occurred in response to the antigen. In this way, the frequency of T-lymphocytes which are responsive to a specific antigen can be quantified from within a mixed population of T-cells.

Another method that is commonly used to investigate T-cell function is adoptive transfer. In this technique, T-cells from an animal, which may have been given specific experimental treatments, or even a T-cell based cell line, are injected into a second animal, usually of the same species, to observe the effects of the transplanted T-cells on immune function in the recipient animal. A commonly used reagent in this type of experiment is the ovalbumin protein (often called simply OVA), which is a major protein found in chickens’ eggs. A number of T-cell lines have been developed which express TCRs that are specific for individual peptides derived from ovalbumin. Usefully, these cells-lines are transformed, which means they can be cultured and expanded in vitro, but also survive in vivo. Commonly used examples include the cell line B3Z (which is a CD8+ T-cell line) and OT-II (which is a CD4+ T-cell line). Both of these cell lines are specific for peptides derived from the OVA protein presented on particular mouse MHC molecules. Adoptive transfer of these cell-lines into experimental mice can be used to examine in detail the presentation pathways and resulting immune responses to ovalbumin administered under various conditions.

Summary

1) Much research in biomedical science is reliant on the capacity of antibodies to bind with high affinity and specificity to target antigens of interest

2) Functional antibodies are those that bind to a target molecule to activate or inhibit a biological pathway of interest

3) A polyclonal antibody preparation is a mixture of different antibodies derived from several distinct B-cell clones

4) A monoclonal antibody preparation contains only one type of antibody, derived from just one B-cell clone, such that every antibody molecule in the solution has exactly the same amino acid sequence and epitope specificity

5) Soluble antigens may be quantified using enzyme linked immunosorbent assays (ELISAs), the most common form of which is the sandwich ELISA

6) Sandwich ELISAs use one plastic-bound antibody to capture antigen, and a second, soluble antibody to detect antigen

7) Immunoprecipitation is a technique that uses antibodies to purify specific proteins from a complex mixture of proteins

8) Immunoblotting (also called Western blotting) uses antibodies to detect proteins which have been separated by electrophoresis on a polyacrylamide gel

9) Flow cytometry uses fluorescently labelled antibodies to quantify the level of expression of antigens on individual cells, as they are flowed within a thin stream of fluid past a laser light source

10) Immunohistochemistry uses antibodies to detect the presence of antigens in sections of tissues or cultured cells

11) Immortalised T-cell lines expressing a TCR specific for peptides from the chicken egg ovalbumin protein (OVA) can be used in adoptive transfer experiments to explore how T-cells are activated in vivo, most commonly in mice

Suggested further reading

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ELISA>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_blot>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flow_cytometry>

<https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monoclonal_antibody>

---

# Chapter 21: Industrial immunology

At the time of writing (2019), the global market for antibody based technologies, for use in research, industry and medicine, is estimated to be at least $100 billion per year. This figure is anticipated to grow rapidly in the coming years as new advances in antibodies, and other technologies related to the human immune system, are adopted more widely in clinics and research laboratories. There is therefore much demand for graduates to be trained in the principles of industrial immunology - which can be broadly defined as the development and manufacture of research reagents and medicines based on technologies inspired by immunity. This chapter will focus on some of the key techniques employed presently, and in the near future, in this rapidly expanding new industry.

Polyclonal antibody production

The capacity of antibodies to bind with high affinity and specificity to target antigens of interest is immensely useful in biomedical research, medicine and industry. This has led to a great need for methods of production of antibodies which are specific for proteins under investigation. Generally speaking, there are two main formats of antibody that may be useful for these purposes: polyclonal and monoclonal. As described in the previous chapter, polyclonal antibodies derive from a mixture of B-cell clones targeting multiple epitopes on the target antigen, and monoclonal antibodies derive from a single B-cell clone, targeting only one specific epitope.

In order to generate polyclonal antibodies, a vertebrate animal is injected with the antigen to which specific antibodies are desired, together with an adjuvant to enhance the immunogenicity of the preparation. Several weeks later, antibodies specific for that antigen appear in the circulation of the immunised animal. Blood is then drawn from the animal, and allowed to clot so that the serum may be collected. This serum contains antibodies which are specific for the antigen in question.

There are numerous factors to consider when choosing which species of animal to use. When small amounts of antibody are required, such as for a limited set of experiments, mice are generally chosen on the basis of cost. When a larger pool of the same batch of antibody is required to support a larger number of experiments, rabbits are often chosen, on the basis of their larger size, longer life span and ease of bleeding. When yet larger quantities are required, such as for secondary antibodies for use in ELISA or flow cytometry which may have a market size large enough to support many hundreds of customers, larger animals, such as goats, sheep or horses may be used. Of course, the larger the animal, the greater the costs involved in breeding and husbandry. A further major consideration is the phylogenetic distance between the animal to be immunised, and the organism from which the target antigen was isolated. This is because tolerance prevents the formation of high affinity antibodies to antigens which are very similar to host antigens. For example, it is very difficult to raise high affinity antibodies to mouse antigens in mice. It might also be difficult to generate antibodies to some mouse antigens using rabbits, since they are quite closely related (from an evolutionary perspective) to mice. In these instances, greater success is generally obtained by choosing an animal which is genetically very distant from the organism that gave rise to the antigen. For example, useful antibodies to sheep antigens might not be raised in goats, but success will likely be achieved using chickens.

The adjuvant used to increase the immunogenicity of the antigen is typically Freund's adjuvant, but other types of adjuvant, such as aluminium salts, can also be used. The amount of antigen given must also be carefully considered, since too much or too little antigen can lead to suppression, sensitization, tolerance, or other unwanted immunomodulation. The antigen should also be as pure as possible, since antibodies are often generated to even trace (e.g. <1%) quantities of impurities that occasionally prove to be immunodominant. A series of carefully timed booster shots may also be given, to promote affinity maturation and the production of higher titres of antibody.

After the serum has been collected from immunised animals, it may be used directly, or passed through columns containing covalently bound target antigen. In the latter process, called affinity purification, only the antibody that binds to the antigen remains on the column, and the non-specific antibodies are washed away. Changing the buffer conditions then releases the antibodies that were bound to antigen on the column, and they may then be eluted and collected. This process helps to remove non-specific antibodies which may bind to other targets in subsequent experiments.

The main benefit of polyclonal antibody production is that a useful antiserum can often be obtained within a short time (4 to 8 weeks) for relatively little financial outlay. However, the main limitations are the batch-to-batch variability, arising from differences in the immune responses of individual animals, and the increased likelihood of binding to off-target antigens.

Monoclonal antibody production

For many experiments and technical applications, it is preferable to use antibodies which are specific for only one epitope of one antigen. In other words, it is often desirable to use a reagent consisting of antibodies which all express the same V-region sequence. Such antibodies are referred to as monoclonal antibodies, as they are derived from a single B-cell clone.

The monoclonal format is also the first choice for antibodies to be used clinically to treat disease. The first monoclonal antibody to be approved for clinical use targeted CD3 to prevent kidney transplant rejection, in 1986. Since then, dozens of monoclonal antibodies have been approved to treat a range of diseases, including chronic inflammatory diseases, infectious diseases, cardiovascular disease, and, most recently, cancer. Large pharmaceutical companies are expressing ever more interest in the use of monoclonal antibodies for therapy, as their success rate in progressing from clinical trials to approval is so far about twice that of chemical (small molecule) drugs. As the commonly used shorthand term for monoclonal antibody is ‘mAb’, most antibody-based drugs are given a name ending with this suffix. For example, checkpoint inhibitors targeting CTLA4 include ipilimumab and tremelimumab.

mAb production typically begins in the same way as for polyclonal antibodies - that is, by the immunisation of a vertebrate animal. After receiving several booster shots to promote affinity maturation (typically with intervals of 14 to 28 days), the animal is sacrificed. B-cells are then harvested from lymphoid tissues, usually the spleen, and taken to a laboratory for further processing.

Because the B-cell clones that arise from this process are incapable of dividing indefinitely in laboratory culture, they must first be modified to enable such growth. The B-cells are made to join, or ‘fuse’, with a transformed cell-line which grows indefinitely in vitro. Either chemical or viral means can be used to achieve this fusion, which causes the plasma membranes of adjacent cells to join and become contiguous (Figure 21.1). These hybrid cells, called hybridomas, now have two nuclei - one which allows them to divide continually (from the transformed cell-line), and the other which enables the secretion of specific antibodies (from the B-cell).

![](image_rsrc4ER.jpg)

The product of the fusion process yields many thousands of hybridoma cells, each expressing an antibody with a specific V-sequence. To separate out those which bind to the antigen of interest, the suspension is first diluted to a very low cell density, and this mixture is then plated in 96-well tissue culture plates, such that on average just one hybridoma cell is seeded per individual well. Over several days, these individual cells grow to form separate miniature colonies, each expressing just one type of antibody.

The supernatants can then be collected from many hundreds of these tiny cultures, and screened using assays such as ELISA to identify those cultures which express antibody targeting the antigen of interest. Once a useful clone has been identified, it can be grown up in larger tissue culture vessels and propagated further to generate sufficient antibody for several laboratory experiments (Figure 21.1).

However, for commercial use, it is often necessary to produce larger quantities of mAbs than is feasible by small-scale laboratory culture. In these instances, suspensions containing the B-cell hybridoma of interest are injected into the abdominal cavity of the same species and strain of animal which was first used for the immunisation. As the hybridoma is essentially a tumour cell, it grows rapidly inside its new host, reaching a much higher mass than can be achieved by tissue culture alone. The abdominal cavity of the treated animal swells with a fluid called ascites as the tumour grows. The ascites contains high concentrations of the antibody of interest, and is harvested by syringe, potentially on several occasions. A strain of mouse called BALB/c is most commonly used for this process, because one of the best cell-lines available for hybridoma formation originated in a BALB/c mouse. By using BALB/c mice for both immunisation and ascites production, immunological rejection of the hybridoma cells can be avoided.

The ascites method was the preferred approach to mAb production for many years. However, there are a number of drawbacks and ethical issues which have led to a decline in its popularity. The first is the fact that animals must be sacrificed for mAb production. The second is the issue of the pain and distress caused by the induction of tumours in the abdomen, and the fluid harvesting process. The third is the fact that ascites fluid can contain other biologically active molecules which may interfere with sensitive assays. It also takes considerably longer (often 3-6 months) to produce mAbs using this method, compared to polyclonal antibody production.

However, a more fundamental problem exists with the use of mice to generate mAbs for therapeutic use in humans. Early clinical trials of mouse-derived monoclonal antibodies revealed that while the antibodies often worked well in the early stages of treatment, they soon lost effectiveness in some patients. It was eventually discovered that this was due to the immune system of the patient generating antibodies which targeted the mouse mAbs, and neutralised their activity. This occurs because the amino acid sequence of the mouse antibody framework is sufficiently different from that of the human equivalent that it can give rise to T-cell epitopes, and eventually anti-mouse antibodies, in the human recipients. Once these antibodies formed, the therapy was no longer effective. Thus, new approaches were required to solve the problem of rejection of mouse-derived monoclonals.

Humanisation of antibodies

Early attempts to circumvent the problem of immune responses to mouse antibodies focussed on genetic engineering of the gene coding for the mouse antibody in such a way that the mouse constant regions were replaced with human constant region equivalents. These so-called chimaeric antibodies reduced the problem of rejection significantly, and, usefully, their human Fc domains were also more efficient at inducing effector responses. However, it soon became clear that the more of the framework that could be replaced with the human equivalent, the less rejection occurred. With modern genetic techniques, it is now possible to replace the entire framework with human sequence, leaving the CDRs as the only parts remaining that derive from the original mouse antibody. These so-called humanised antibodies have been found to be much less prone to rejection than their non-humanised equivalents.

Fully humanising a mouse antibody is, however, still quite difficult and time consuming. Complex genetic engineering is required, and the framework sometimes needs to be tweaked to accommodate the mouse CDRs. To circumvent this issue, new strains of mice have been created by genetic engineering in which their entire original (mouse) immunoglobulin locus has been replaced with the human equivalent. In these mice, the antibodies that form during immune responses are fully human, because they are prepared entirely from the rearrangement of human genes and gene segments. But because tolerance has been developed to mouse antigens during development, they are still capable of generating antibodies that bind human antigens. As we shall see later, bacteria can also be manipulated to produce fully human antibodies, and mAbs produced by each of these approaches have been approved for the treatment of various human diseases.

Antibody fragments commonly used in industry

The antibody Fc domain is the part of the molecule that exerts effector functions, such as complement activation, or labelling cells for killing or phagocytosis. However, there are some applications where the Fc domain is a hindrance to performance, and smaller, cut-down versions of antibodies are preferred. Early attempts to prepare such fragments used protease enzymes to cleave intact antibodies into their constituent parts. For example, the protease papain was found to cleave at the hinge region, yielding two components: a Fab component, which comprises the antigen binding part of the molecule and an Fc component, which comprises a disulphide linked dimer of the heavy chain CH2 and CH3 domains (see Figure 21.2). The origins of these terms are explained in more detail in Chapter 8.

![](image_rsrc4ES.jpg)

A different protease, pepsin, breaks down the heavy chain constant region until as far as the hinge region, but no further. The antigen-binding fragments that are produced in this instance are a bit larger than the Fab fragments produced by treatment with papain, as they include a small part of the heavy chain hinge region, and are called Fab’ to distinguish them from Fab (‘ is pronounced prime in this context). Because the hinge region contains the disulphide bonds that keep the two heavy chains covalently attached to each other, Fab’ is also formed as a dimer, referred to as (Fab’)2. The cleavage of these disulphide bonds using chemical treatments yields Fab’ monomers.

Molecular biology techniques can also be used to splice the genes for the heavy and light gene variable regions together to produce a new, synthetic gene that codes for both halves of the antigen binding domain (i.e. one VH and one VL domain) in one single protein. This format is called the single-chain variable fragment, or scFv, and it has proven to be enormously useful for diverse applications in molecular immunology. ScFv fragments lack the constant regions found in naturally occurring antibodies, and their two variable domains are joined by a flexible polypeptide linker between the C-terminal of one chain and the N-terminal of the other. Each variable domain (one from the light and one from the heavy chain) consists of three hypervariable CDRs interspersed between the more conserved framework regions, which are essential components of the scFv, allowing it to fold properly and present the CDRs in the correct orientation.

Antibody fragments are preferred for some applications simply on the basis of their size. While an intact IgG antibody has a molecular weight of around 150 kDa, the (Fab’)2 molecule has a molecular weight of 110 kDa, Fab’ of 55 kDa, Fab of 50 kDa and ScFv of 27 kDa. This is potentially of use in the development of antibodies for tissue staining, or in therapeutic use, since smaller molecules diffuse more readily than larger molecules into tissues, such as a tumour microenvironment.

However, the most commonly exploited advantage of Fc-lacking fragments is their potential to reduce background staining in experiments where antibodies are used to monitor the expression of specific antigens of interest on cells, such flow cytometry and immunohistochemistry (see Chapter 20). Some cells, particularly dendritic cells, NK-cells, monocytes, macrophages and B-cells, express high levels of Fc-receptors. When secondary antibodies are used which contain Fc regions, they may bind to these receptors, and a false positive staining may be observed which confounds detection of the antigen of interest. For this reason, Fab-like fragments are often preferred as secondary antibodies in this application, since they do not bind non-specifically to FcRs.

Phage display for antibody discovery

As mentioned above, the major limitations of traditional approaches to monoclonal antibody production are the time, cost and ethical implications of using animals. These issues are largely circumvented by an in vitro method of antibody discovery called phage display.

In this approach, the genes for millions of different antibodies are extracted from a large number of B-cells obtained from a living animal (typically from the blood of a human volunteer). The mRNA is extracted from the B-cells and converted to complementary DNA (cDNA) using the enzyme reverse transcriptase. The VH and VL gene sequences are then amplified from the B-cell cDNA using the polymerase chain reaction (PCR). Degenerate primers are used to amplify the greatest possible variety of VH and VL genes by targeting the more conserved sequences surrounding the V regions. The DNA library that results from this process typically contains many millions of different VH and VL sequences, corresponding to the millions of different antibodies that were expressed by the B-cells in the sample.

Genetic techniques are then used to join the VH and VL sequences into a cloning vector, typically in the scFv format. Next, a type of virus that infects bacteria, called a bacteriophage, or phage, is engineered to accommodate and express the scFv gene collection. This is achieved by cloning the scFv gene in such a way that it becomes part of a coat protein (typically PIII) used to assemble the phage. Because this vector contains genes from both bacterial plasmids, and bacteriophages, it is referred to as a phagemid. The type of phage most commonly used for this purpose is the filamentous phage M13, and the bacterial host used to propagate it, is Escherichia coli.

Electroporation is then used to insert the phagemid vectors into E. coli cells. As the phagemid vector alone does not contain sufficient genes to code for a complete phage, these are provided by inserting the DNA coding for replication defective “helper phages”, at the same time. Soon, billions of new phage particles are released from the bacteria, each expressing a different scFv on their surface as part of one of the coat proteins. This collection of phages, each expressing a different scFv, is referred to as a phage display library.

The next step is identifying those scFv molecules which are capable of binding to the antigen of interest. This is achieved by adhering the antigen to a solid surface, such as a plastic dish, or plastic beads. A solution of many billions of phage particles is then layered onto the antigen. Those phage expressing scFv that matches the antigen bind firmly to it. A washing step is then performed to remove all those phage (the vast majority) which do not bind to antigen. The phage that do bind are then eluted from the antigen by protease treatment, or by changing the pH. This new phage population can then be used to infect more E. coli cells and amplified to vast numbers once again. By this stage, the new phage suspension is heavily enriched in phages which bind to the antigen of interest. This process, called biopanning, can be repeated several times to maximise the potential to find antibodies with the highest affinity for antigen (Figure 21.3).

![](image_rsrc4ET.jpg)

Finally, the phage DNA is isolated from a bacterial culture and sequenced. This reveals the sequence of the scFv responsible for the binding, which in turn can be used to generate recombinant antibodies. The production of such antibodies does not occur in vivo, but rather in large cultures of mammalian cell-lines, such as CHO cells or HEK-293 cells, which are emerging as workhorses of the protein expression industry. Mammalian, rather than bacterial, hosts are used to express the protein, because they are capable of producing antibody chains with the correct folding, glycosylation and disulphide bonds required for use as therapeutics. Proteins expressed in mammalian cells are also much less prone to contamination with bacterial PAMPs.

The phage display approach has many key advantages over earlier antibody discovery technologies. The most obvious is the lack of requirement to sacrifice animals for antibody production (either in spleen harvesting or ascites production). It is also possible to produce fully human antibodies, as the phagemid library can be prepared from human B-cells. Once the library has been created, it can be easily maintained indefinitely by growth in E. coli, and the original preparation can, theoretically at least, give rise to many millions of different antibodies for many different targets and projects.

The timescale of antibody discovery is also much faster using phage display. Some groups have reported the generation of monoclonal antibodies to a target antigen in under one week. Phage display is also very useful for the development of antibodies against targets which are either toxic, or weakly immunogenic, in animals, and fail to give rise to a strong humoral response. Because the phage particles are tiny, trillions may be propagated in just one millilitre of culture. This means that even small laboratories have the potential to screen libraries of many millions of different antibody specificities using the technique.

The first monoclonal antibody derived from phage display was approved for clinical use in 2002. Adalimumab, a TNF-α neutralising mAb, is used to treat rheumatoid arthritis and Crohn’s disease, and has become one of the most commercially successful mAbs of all time.

Properties of effective phage display libraries

The success of any phage display programme depends critically on the diversity of the VH and VL gene sequences represented in the library. The greater the diversity, the greater the chance of finding a ‘jackpot’ antibody with high binding affinity to a target antigen. This diversity depends in turn largely on the source of the B-cells used to prepare the mRNA for phagemid construction.

One of the best ways to maximise diversity in human antibody libraries is to simply isolate mRNA from as many people from different genetic backgrounds as possible. There is great diversity in the human population at the immune loci, and the greater the ethnic diversity of the populations sampled, the greater the chance of isolating rare (and potentially valuable) VH and VL sequences for inclusion in the library. Many antibody companies invest much time and effort in seeking volunteers of rare ethnicities to maximise diversity in this way.

Another means of enhancing diversity is to prepare libraries using synthetically constructed VH and VL genes. In this approach, the framework regions are kept relatively constant, and the CDRs are assembled from oligonucleotides synthesised with random sequences. In theory, this approach has the potential to give rise to even greater diversity than that present in an animal’s own V-region repertoire. However, in practice only the CDR3 loop is subjected to random synthesis, because it is easier from a technical point of view, and most of the contact with antigen is achieved by this loop. The major limitation of this approach is that some of the CDRs that arise from the essentially random process may contain T-cell epitopes, i.e. peptides which could be presented on the MHC molecules of some people. These could in turn increase the risk of rejection by the patient. Such epitopes are generally not present in V region genes from the human population, because they have been removed over the millennia by natural selection.

Another aspect to consider in the choice of library for a phage display project is whether antibodies targeting self-antigens are required. This is often the case for clinical applications, where neutralising antibodies for cytokines or receptors are required. Because the B-cells of healthy human volunteers represent only those clones which have escaped negative selection, they should not express any antibodies with high affinity for self antigen. Thus, self-reactive antibodies cannot be generated from libraries based on B-cells from healthy human volunteers. B-cells from other animals, such as mice, may of course express antibodies targeting human antigens, but they would then need to be fully humanised by complex genetic techniques before they can be used clinically. Alternatively, a phage display library can be prepared from B-cells isolated from the humanised mouse model. Such a library would contain only human sequences, so avoiding the T-cell epitope problem, while also retaining the potential to yield antibodies that bind well to human antigens, since B-cells reactive to human antigens would not be deleted in mice.

Bispecific antibodies

One of the most useful properties of naturally occurring antibodies is that are multivalent - i.e. they have at least two antigen binding sites linked together on the same molecule. This enables the clumping of particles bearing multiple target antigens, such as bacteria and viruses, which in itself helps to slow the course of infection.

However, there are many applications in which it would be beneficial to have an antibody with two different antigen binding sites on one molecule. Such molecules do not exist naturally, but can be prepared synthetically, and are referred to as bispecific antibodies. The major advantage of bispecific antibodies over their natural equivalents is their inherent capacity to bring together two different antigens, on any two targets, into close proximity.

Early experiments using chemical linkage of fragments from naturally occurring antibodies proved the utility of such molecules. Today, however, they are more commonly made in synthetic form using gene synthesis techniques, for expression as a single protein. There are dozens of different formats of bispecific antibody, each prepared from different combinations of gene segments from full IgG molecules, Fab or scFv fragments, and paired with diverse linking mechanisms to offer a variety of potential orientations. However, the most commonly studied form is a single protein comprising two scFv antigen-binding units linked through a flexible peptide connector.

To date, most research on bispecific antibodies has focussed on their potential to promote anti-tumour responses in immunotherapy. A common approach is to pair an scFv for a tumour antigen at one end, with an scFv for CD3 at the other. In this way, a T-cell is both brought into close proximity with a tumour cell, and activated by the clustering of CD3. These bispecific T-cell engager molecules are referred to as BiTEs. BiTEs have already shown promise in the clinic, as blinatumomab, which comprises an anti-CD19 scFv linked to an anti-CD3 scFv, has been approved for the treatment of B-cell precursor acute lymphoblastic leukaemia (ALL).

Tandem scFv molecules can also help to retarget NK cells towards tumour cells, by fusing a CD16-binding scFv with an scFv targeting a tumour antigen. Such bispecific killer cell engagers (BiKEs) are also showing promise in early stage clinical studies.

More inventive variants of the bispecific binding format are also being explored. For example, it is now possible to fuse a TCR capable of recognising MHC-presented tumour antigen, with a CD3-binding scFv. Such bispecific molecules (called ImmTACs) are being researched for their capacity to promote T-cell anti-tumour responses against internal (cytosolic) tumour antigens.

Although immuno-oncology has fuelled most of the recent interest in bispecific antibodies, progress is also being made in exploring their potential to treat other diseases, including autoimmunity, inflammatory diseases, neurodegeneration and infections.

Engineered antigen-binding molecules based on novel scaffolds

Progress in simplifying and miniaturising recombinant antibodies took a notable step forward when it was discovered that camels and llamas express antibodies that lack light chains and consist of heavy chains only. Antibodies from these species have only 3 CDRs, rather than the conventional 6, yet are fully functional and afford good binding affinity to antigens. Single chain binding molecules based on this principle have been commercialised in the form of nanobodies, which comprise a single variable domain (VH) and two constant domains (CH2, CH3).

This approach, and the others listed above, have taken the antibody scaffold and gene structures from the natural world, and modified them slightly to produce improved versions of conventional immunoglobulin frameworks. However, several companies are going one step further to explore alternative frameworks for antigen-binding molecules. The rationale is that while the natural immunoglobulin framework is excellent in many respects, it has some limitations, such as its large size, requirement for production in mammalian cells (due requirement for disulphide bond formation) and propensity to denature under adverse conditions of high temperature or low pH.

By choosing completely different proteins as scaffolds to support the CDR loops that bind antigen, this research aims to develop binding molecules which escape these limitations. For example, fynomers, offered by the firm Creative Biolabs, are based on the human protein Fyn kinase. This protein has nothing to do with the immune system, but has been chosen as a more suitable framework as it offers a particularly stable structure that remains intact up to 70oC. Unlike conventional antibody frameworks, it contains no disulphide bonds, which means it can be expressed easily in bacteria, allowing rapid large scale production. Fynomers present two flexible loops on the surface of a 7 kDa single chain molecule, which serve as CDRs.

Another company, Avacta Life Sciences, has developed two different non-classical scaffolds that they call affimers. These binding agents are based either on the human protein stefin A, or the plant protein cystatin A, both of which are highly stable and lack disulphide bonds, so are easily produced in bacterial systems. Affimers have two CDRs, each 9 amino acids in length. They are compact (10-12 kDa in size), and the human origin of the stefin A scaffold is expected to enable the development of molecules suitable for clinical use. Both affimers and fynomers can be screened using phage display techniques to support the rapid discovery of novel antigen binding molecules.

Summary

1) Industrial immunology can be broadly defined as the development and manufacture of research reagents and medicines based on technologies inspired by immunity

2) Polyclonal antibodies are prepared from the serum of animals which have been immunised with the target antigen of interest in combination with an adjuvant

3) Affinity purification is the process used to purify those antibodies which bind to the antigen of interest from other antibodies present in serum

4) Polyclonal antibodies are relatively inexpensive to produce, but are limited by issues of batch to batch variation, and the ethical implications of using animals for their production

5) Monoclonal antibodies are often prepared from hybridoma cell-lines, which are prepared by fusing B-cells from an immunised animal with a transformed cell-line

6) Monoclonal antibodies offer more reproducible and reliable antigen-binding characteristics than polyclonal antibodies, but are limited by their increased cost compared to polyclonal antibodies, and the ethical implications of the requirement to sacrifice the animal for recovery of splenic B-cells, and the growth of the hybridoma cell-line within the abdominal cavity of other animals

7) Antibodies from non-human species can be humanised by genetic splicing techniques, such that the Fc domain and the bulk of the framework amino acid sequence becomes human, and only the CDRs derive from the original species

8) Humanised antibodies offer reduced risk of rejection when used therapeutically

9) Antibody fragments lacking the Fc domain which are commonly used in research include the Fab, Fab’ and (Fab’)2 fragments, each differing in the amount of heavy chain remaining after protease digestion

10) The single-chain variable fragment (scFv) is a very useful synthetic version of the antigen-binding fragment of an antibody

11) ScFv molecules are prepared using genetic techniques to join the variable domains of the light and heavy chains of an antibody of interest via a short polypeptide linker, resulting in a single polypeptide chain which retains full antigen-binding capacity

12) Phage display is a method which uses libraries of bacteriophages genetically engineered to express billions of different antibody fragments on their surface

13) Phage display libraries are prepared by cloning the cDNA coding for the VH and VL genes from millions of B-cells, typically isolated from human blood, into expression vectors which enable their presentation as ScFv molecules on the surface of the phage particles

14) Suspensions of phage libraries are washed over antigen of interest in a process called biopanning

15) Phage particles which bind well to antigen are eluted by changing the buffer conditions, and then amplified by reinfecting E. coli bacteria

16) The amino acid sequences of scFvs that bind well to antigen can then be identified by sequencing the phagemid vector that is present within the phage particle

17) Antibodies prepared by phage display are typically produced by overexpression in mammalian cell-lines, such as CHO cells or HEK-293 cells

18) The major benefits of antibody generation by phage display are that there is no requirement to use animals, it is faster and cheaper than conventional approaches, and it can yield fully human antibodies without requirement for the complex process of humanisation

19) The main limitation of phage display libraries prepared from human B-cells is that it is often difficult to discover antibodies which bind well to human antigens

20) A key contributor to success in phage display programmes is the extent of diversity of the VH and VL gene sequences represented in the library

21) Phage display library diversity can be maximised by collecting B-cell samples from ethnically diverse human populations

22) Bispecific antibodies are prepared by genetically splicing two different antigen-binding domains into one protein

23) Bispecific T-cell engager molecules (BiTEs) are bispecific antibodies with an scFv targeting a tumour antigen joined to an scFv targeting CD3, resulting in a molecule which can help T-cells to bind and kill tumour cells

24) Most recombinant antibodies are synthesised using the natural immunoglobulin framework, but some other proteins, unrelated to the immune system, can also be used as suitable frameworks to support the proper positioning of CDR loops

25) Examples of such non-immunoglobulin framework binding molecules include fynomers and affimers

# Appendix II: Properties of key cytokines  
  
Below are listed the major features of the key cytokines most likely to be discussed at undergraduate level. It is worth committing to memory the key properties of these cytokines.

Cytokine| Key properties ---|--- IFN-α| There are 13 human IFN-α subtypes, each of which promotes induction of an anti-viral state in almost any type of host cell by binding to the type-I interferon receptor (IFNAR). IFN-α is produced mainly by plasmacytoid dendritic cells in response to the detection of viral PAMPs, such as stimulants of TLR7, TLR8 or TLR9. IFN-β| There is only one type of IFN-β which may be produced by diverse cell types in response to infection with viruses or intracellular bacteria. IFN-β induces an anti-viral response in target cells as described for IFN-α via the same type-I interferon receptor. IFN-γ| IFN-γ, or type-II interferon, is produced by activated T-cells of the Th1 phenotype and also by activated NK-cells. It is detected by the IFN-γ-receptor and promotes responses required for the elimination of viruses and other intracellular pathogens. For example, IFN-γ increases the expression of MHC on antigen-presenting cells, promotes class switching to IgG1 and enhances the activation state of macrophages, enabling them to more efficiently kill phagocytosed microbes. IL-1| IL-1α and IL-1β are highly pro-inflammatory cytokines produced by macrophages in response to stimulation of innate immune receptors, such as by PAMPs. They are detected by the IL-1 receptor and induce NF-κB signalling in target cells. IL-1β is produced initially as an inactive precursor which may be cleaved to yield the active form by the protease caspase-1, which is derived from inflammasome activation by diverse PAMPs or indicators of cell-stress. IL-2| Secreted by activated T-cells to promote the growth and proliferation of T-cells in an autocrine fashion (i.e. it acts on the same T-cell that produced the IL-2) or paracrine fashion (i.e. it acts on other nearby T-cells). IL-4| Released by activated Th2-polarised T-cells and mast cells, IL-4 promotes class-switching to IgE, the activation of mast cells and eosinophils, peristalsis and M2 macrophage polarisation to aid defence against helminths. IL-4 also inhibits the polarisation of T-cells to the Th1 phenotype. IL-5| Released by activated Th2-polarised T-cells, IL-5 promotes eosinophil production and activation to aid defence against helminths. IL-5 also inhibits the polarisation of T-cells to the Th1 phenotype. IL-6| IL-6 may be produced by diverse cell types, but is released in the largest quantities by activated macrophages in response to pro-inflammatory stimuli (such as TLR-stimulants). IL-6 binds the IL-6-receptor to exert diverse effects, such as the induction of acute phase protein production by hepatocytes. IL-8| IL-8 is a chemokine that may be produced by diverse cell types in response to inflammation or tissue damage, promoting the recruitment of neutrophils and other leukocytes. IL-10| This anti-inflammatory cytokine is produced mainly by regulatory T-cells and activated macrophages, particularly macrophages polarised to the M2 phenotype. IL-10 inhibits both inflammatory responses and T-cell activation. IL-12| Released by macrophages and dendritic cells activated by PAMPs, resulting in the activation of NK-cells and the polarisation of T-cells towards the Th1 phenotype. IL-13| Released by activated Th2-polarised T-cells, IL-13 promotes eosinophil recruitment, peristalsis, mucous formation and M2 macrophage polarisation to aid defence against helminths. IL-13 also inhibits the polarisation of T-cells to the Th1 phenotype. IL-17| Although there are several subtypes of IL-17, the term IL-17 typically refers to IL-17A, which is released by activated Th17 polarised T-cells. IL-17 promotes neutrophilic inflammation to enhance defence against extracellular pathogens and the production of anti-microbial peptides by epithelial cells. IL-22| Produced by activated Th17 T-cells. Acts on epithelial cells to promote their survival and thereby enhances the integrity of the physical barrier at the mucosal surfaces. TGF-β| This anti-inflammatory cytokine is produced mainly by regulatory T-cells and macrophages polarised to the M2 phenotype. TGF-β inhibits both inflammatory responses and T-cell activation, but promotes the production of collagen during wound healing and, in the presence of IL-6, the differentiation of T-cells to the Th17 phenotype. TNF-α| This pro-inflammatory cytokine is produced mainly by macrophages or dendritic cells which have been activated by PAMPs, but may also be produced by T-cells and NK-cells. TNF-α stimulates NF-κB signalling in target cells, which results in the expression of adhesion molecules such as ICAM-1 and E-selectin, by endothelial cells to promote leukocyte recruitment.

---

# Appendix III: Greek letters commonly used in immunology terms  
  
Many of the terms used by immunologists include Greek letters that may not be familiar to all readers. The table below gives examples of their usage and an indication of the correct pronunciation of each of these terms:

Greek letter| Example of use| English| Pronunciation ---|---|---|--- α| TNF-α| alpha| /ˈælfə/ β| IL-1β| beta| /ˈbiːtə/ γ| IFN-γ| gamma| /ˈɡæmə/ δ| TCR δ chain| delta| /ˈdɛltə/ ε| FcεRI| epsilon| /ˈɛpsɨlɒn/ κ| NF-κB| kappa| /ˈkæpə/ λ| λ light chain| lambda| /ˈlæmdə/ μ| μ heavy chain| mu| /ˈmjuː/ φ| Mφ (macrophage)| phi| /ˈfaɪ/ ζ| TCR ζ-chain| zeta| /ˈziːtə/
