# Appendix V: Answers to self-test questions

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 1: Introduction to immunology

Q1: What names are given to the two major arms of the vertebrate immune system?

A1: Innate (or natural) immunity and adaptive (or acquired) immunity.

Q2: What is a PAMP?

A2: A pathogen associated molecular pattern is a highly conserved molecule, or part of a molecule, that is recognised by highly conserved germ-line DNA encoded receptors. PAMPs are generally expressed by microbes, but not mammalian cells, and can therefore be used to detect the presence of infections.

Q3: What are the strengths and weaknesses of innate immunity?

A3: Innate immunity acts very rapidly, requires no past exposure to an organism for defence and is ready to act from the moment of birth. However, innate immunity cannot distinguish the fine detail between different species or strains of organism and the innate immune response to an organism does not improve each time the host is exposed to that organism (i.e. no memory).

Q4: What is an antigen?

A4: An antigen is any molecule that may be recognised and then targeted by the adaptive immune system (i.e. either an antibody response or a T-cell response). Antigens are highly diverse and the responses to them are often very specific.

Q5: What type of cell produces antibody?

A5: B-lymphocytes (B-cells).

Q6: What type of antigen is detected by T-lymphocytes (T-cells)?

A6: Fragments of proteins, termed peptides, presented to the T-cells on MHC molecules by antigen presenting cells.

Q7: List the three major types of T-cell.

A7: Cytotoxic T-cells, helper T-cells (including Th1, Th2, Th17) and regulatory T-cells.

Q8: What is clonal selection?

A8: Clonal selection is the process of amplification (by increased cell division) of numbers of specific clones of B- or T-lymphocytes expressing the best receptor to deal with a particular antigen. Selective proliferation occurs in response to signalling cues that promote the survival and expansion of cells expressing receptors that work well, and cells expressing poor receptors do not proliferate or are deleted.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 2: Introduction to cellular and molecular biology

Q1. What is the name used to describe the process by which one type of cell becomes another type of cell?

A1. Differentiation.

Q2. What is the difference between transcription and translation?

A2: Transcription is the process by which chromosomal DNA is copied by the enzyme RNA polymerase to produce a molecule called messenger RNA (mRNA). Translation is the reading of mRNA by ribosomes to produce protein from constituent amino acids.

Q3: What do transcription factors do?

A3: Transcription factors determine which genes are switched on or off in a cell, and therefore all of the properties and functions of a cell. They mediate this by binding to specific DNA sequences near each gene and promoting or repressing transcription of the gene.

Q4: What is the term used to describe the production of leukocytes by the division of stem cells and the subsequent differentiation of precursors in the bone marrow?

A4: Haematopoiesis.

Q5: What are CD markers?

A5: CD markers, or ‘Cluster of Differentiation’ markers are antigens expressed on the surface of cells that are often specific to particular types of cell and may therefore be used to identify particular cell types within a mixed population.

Q6: What is the phagolysosome?

A6: When particles are ingested by phagocytes through phagocytosis, the first vesicle that is formed to capture the particle is called the phagosome. This vesicle then fuses (or joins) with another vesicle, called a lysosome. The contents of the two vesicles mix and form the phagolysosome. Here, digestive enzymes from the lysosome are used to degrade the ingested particle.

Q7: What separates the contents of the cell cytosol from the extracellular fluid and also from the contents of intracellular vesicles?

A7: Phospholipid bilayers.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 3: Cells and tissues of the immune response

Q1: Which progenitor gives rise to monocytes, neutrophils and dendritic cells?

A1: The common myeloid progenitor.

Q2: Name three major types of cell derived from the common lymphoid progenitor.

A2: T-lymphocytes, B-lymphocytes and NK-cells.

Q3: What is the main purpose of the phagocyte?

A3: To ingest and degrade tissue debris, microbes and other foreign materials.

Q4: What type of cell is by far the most efficient antigen presenting cell?

A4: The conventional dendritic cell, particularly when it is activated by cytokines or PAMPs.

Q5: Name three key CD markers for circulating monocytes, T-lymphocytes and B-lymphocytes.

A5: Monocytes (CD14), T-cells (CD3), B-cells (CD19).

Q6: Name the organs in which T-lymphocytes are (i) produced and (ii) mature.

A6: T-cells are produced in the bone marrow and mature in the thymus.

Q7: Name two primary (generative) lymphoid organs and two types of secondary (peripheral) lymphoid tissue.

A7: Primary - bone marrow and thymus. Secondary - examples include the lymph nodes, gut-associated lymphoid tissue, mucosa-associated lymphoid tissue and spleen.

Q8: What is the term applied to the fluid that percolates slowly between the cells of the tissues, and what is the name of the vessels that collect this fluid to eventually return it to the blood?

A8: Lymph, which travels through lymphatic vessels.

Q9: What are the two main functions of the spleen?

A9: To remove dead red blood cells and to support lymphocyte responses to blood-borne antigens.

Q10: What is the term applied to the aggregates of B-cells found in lymph nodes and spleen?

A10: Follicles.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 4: Innate immunity and pattern recognition

Q1: Describe the key properties of a PAMP.

A1: Pathogen-associated molecular patterns should be highly conserved, not present in host (mammalian) cells, required for microbial survival or at least virulence and present in many different types of a specific group of organisms.

Q2: List some forms of passive innate immunity.

A2: Passive mechanisms of innate immunity include epithelial and chemical barriers, mucous production, low pH in the stomach and anti-microbial enzymes and peptides secreted on external surfaces exposed to microbes.

Q3: List the major types of soluble PRR which are present in plasma.

A3: Complement, pentraxins, collectins, ficolins and natural antibodies.

Q4: List the three major pathways of complement activation and the agents that stimulate induction of these pathways.

A4: The three major pathways of complement activation are the classical pathway (induced by antibodies), the alternative pathway (triggered by spontaneous or PAMP-induced activation of C3 itself), and the lectin pathway (triggered by mannose-binding lectin)

Q5: What is meant by the term ‘opsonisation’.

A5: Phagocytosis of foreign particles, such as microbes, is made much more efficient by labelling the particles with molecules that can be recognised by receptors expressed by phagocytes. These molecules are often antibodies, complement activation products or both.

Q6: List the ligands of human TLRs1-9.

A6: TLR1 (triacyl bacterial lipopeptide, with TLR2), TLR2 (bacterial lipopeptides), TLR3 (double stranded RNA), TLR4 (LPS), TLR5 (flagellin), TLR6 (diacyl lipopeptides), TLR7 (viral nucleic acids), TLR8 (viral nucleic acids), TLR9 (unmethylated CpG DNA).

Q7: Describe the processes involved in inflammasome activation.

A7: NLRs containing a pyrin domain are referred to as NLRPs, and on detection of PAMPs they can trigger the formation of an intracellular complex termed the inflammasome, which activates the protease caspase-1 to cleave pro-IL-1β and pro-IL-18 and yield the active, pro-inflammatory form of these cytokines.

Q8: How do natural killer (NK) cells decide if a cell is infected or stressed, and how are such cells killed by NK-cells?

A8: NK cells kill host cells that express low levels of molecules that stimulate their inhibitory receptors (such as MHC-I), or high levels of molecules that stimulate their activating receptors, by releasing cytotoxic granules onto the target cell which induce apoptosis.

Q9: What is the chief purpose of type I interferon?

A9: Type-I interferons play a key role in the early defence against viral infection. They are induced by the sensing of viral nucleic acids by PRRs and cause both the cell that secreted them and nearby cells to upregulate expression of genes that limit the replication of viruses.

Q10: Can lymphocytes take part in innate immune responses, or do they only initiate adaptive immune responses?

A10: Yes, some lymphocytes appear to play a role in innate immunity. For example B-1 B-cells express antibodies with low receptor diversity that tends to target conserved bacterial molecules. Likewise, certain subsets of T-cell express receptors of low diversity and appear to detect PAMPs rather than antigens.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 5: Inflammation

Q1: What is inflammation?

A1: Inflammation is the process by which plasma constituents and leukocytes from the blood are recruited into the affected tissues.

Q2: How long does the inflammatory response last?

A2: Normally, it is initiated rapidly and tissues then return to normal (through the process of resolution) within several days. If the inflammatory stimulus is not removed, chronic inflammation may ensue which can last for months or years and cause damage to the affected tissues.

Q3: List three major pathways by which inflammation can be triggered:

A3: Inflammation may be triggered by, for example, the detection of PAMPs by macrophages and other cells, the activation of mast cells by allergen or other stimuli, complement activation by PAMPs, immune complex formation.

Q4: What are the four major steps in leukocyte recruitment from the blood into the tissues?

A4: Leukocyte recruitment begins with tethering (to activated endothelial cells), then rolling, then firm adhesion and then diapedesis (transmigration).

Q5: What is meant by the term ‘acute phase protein’, and which is the most commonly measured example?

A5: Acute phase proteins are proteins present in plasma which increase (or occasionally reduce) in concentration after the host is exposed to an inflammatory stimulus. C-reactive protein (CRP) is the most widely measured acute phase protein.

Q6: Which three cytokines are able to promote acute phase reactant production when their concentration in the blood rises?

A6: TNF-α, IL-6, IL-1.

Q7: What is the fate of the neutrophil after recruitment to a site of inflammation?

A7: Neutrophils die by apoptosis shortly after recruitment, and their apoptotic bodies are removed from the tissues by macrophages.

Q8: List two major anti-inflammatory cytokines and the cells that frequently produce them.

A8: IL-10 and TGF-β, both produced by regulatory T-cells and M2-polarised macrophages.

Q9: List two common diseases in which chronic inflammation is involved.

A9: Examples of common chronic inflammatory diseases include rheumatoid arthritis, Crohn’s disease, ulcerative colitis and atherosclerosis.

Q10: List three drugs currently used to reduce inflammation in man, and their cellular targets.

A10: Steroids (such as dexamethasone, which target NF-kB and PLA2), non-steroidal anti-inflammatories (NSAIDs, such as ibuprofen and aspirin, which target COX enzymes) and biologicals, such as antibodies and other recombinant proteins which target inflammatory cytokines such as IL-1β or TNF-α directly.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 6: Overview of adaptive immunity

Q1: Why do lymphocytes need to express so many different receptor variants?

A1: Because, over a lifetime, we may be exposed to millions of different antigens from the millions of different types of microbe we could be exposed to, and it would be impossible to express PRRs to detect each of these using germline encoded genes.

Q2: Why is it that B-cells can secrete a soluble form of their receptor (i.e. antibody), but T-cells cannot?

A2: Soluble antibody is required to diffuse throughout the body to defend against infection in any tissue. The only purpose of the TCR is that it enables T-cells to be activated by peptide bound to MHC on the surface of an antigen presenting cell, and therefore a soluble TCR, which would not be connected to a T-cell, would serve no purpose.

Q3: What happens to lymphocytes that generate a receptor that accidentally targets a host molecule?

A3: They are deleted by apoptosis, or occasionally, some CD4+ T-lymphocytes that detect a host protein become regulatory T-cells.

Q4: What are the major classes of antibody in humans?

A4: IgM, IgD, IgG, IgA, IgE.

Q5: What is the key difference between proteins that are eventually displayed either on MHC-I or MHC-II?

A5: MHC-I presents peptides generated from proteins in the host cell cytosol, such as viral proteins. MHC-II presents peptides generated from extracellular proteins.

Q6: What is the purpose of the CD8+ T-cell?

A6: CD8+ (cytotoxic) T-cells kill target cells expressing non-self peptide to eliminate reservoirs of infection by intracellular pathogens, such as viruses and intracellular bacteria.

Q7: List the three major types of CD4+ helper T-cell, and describe the type of infection they are best suited to defend against.

A7: Th1, Th2, Th17. Th1 cells secrete cytokines that promote defence against intracellular pathogens, Th2 cells promote defence against helminths and Th17 cells promote defence against extracellular bacteria and fungi.

Q8: The vast majority of naïve B-cells and T-cells will not encounter antigen for which they are specific - what happens to them?

A8: They circulate for a while, perhaps several months, but then die by apoptosis to be replaced by new naïve cells from the bone marrow.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 7: Lymphocyte subsets and development

Q1: List three different B-cell subtypes.

A1: B-cell subsets include B-1, marginal zone and follicular B-cells.

Q2: How do lymphocytes generate such diverse receptors during their development?

A2: B-cells and T-cells express an antigen receptor that is generated by the randomised joining together of different sections of DNA from the genome. This process, called genetic recombination, has the capacity to generate receptors with enormous diversity for different antigens.

Q3: What is meant by the term ‘pluripotent’?

A3: Pluripotency means a cell has the capacity to self-renew and also to give rise to progenitors that can differentiate to become a number of different types of cell.

Q4: What is meant by ‘commitment’ in the context of haematopoiesis?

A4: After a developing cell has committed to a certain lineage (such as myeloid or lymphoid, for example), it cannot revert to an earlier form, or subsequently change to a different lineage.

Q5: What happens to developing T-cells or B-cells that fail to produce a working receptor?

A5: T-cells and B-cells that do not successfully form a gene coding for a functional receptor undergo apoptosis.

Q6: During T-cell development, what is meant by the term ‘double positive’?

A6: Developing T-cells initially express both CD4 and CD8 antigens (i.e. they are double positive), but they become single positive (i.e. CD4+CD8- or CD4-CD8+) later, depending on whether their newly formed TCR recognises MHC-II or MHC-I.

Q7: What type of antibody is produced by B-1 B-cells?

A7: B-1 B-cells express receptors of limited diversity termed natural antibodies that tend to target bacterial lipids and polysaccharides and be of the IgA or IgM class.

Q8: What is the main purpose of the CD4+ helper T-cell?

A8: CD4+ helper T-cells control immune responses by expressing cytokines and membrane-bound molecules that ‘help’ B-cells, macrophages, DCs and other types of T-cell elicit immune responses.

Q9: What is the main purpose of the regulatory T-cell?

A9: Regulatory T-cells (Tregs) maintain tolerance to self antigens by inhibiting inflammation and adaptive responses to such antigens through the secretion of cytokines such as IL-10 and TGF-β.

Q10: What is presented on the MHC-like molecule CD1, and which cells detect such molecules?

A10: CD1 presents lipids and other small non-peptide molecules to γδ T-cells and natural killer T-cells (NKT)

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 8: Antibodies and the humoral response

Q1: Which part of the antibody molecule binds to antigen, and which to host cell receptors?

A1: The part of the antibody that binds to antigen is the variable region (V-region), which is at the opposite end of the molecule from the part that interacts with host antibody receptors (Fc region).

Q2: Describe what is meant by the term ‘complementarity determining region’.

A2: Complementarity determining regions, or CDRs, are 3 flexible loops, each ~10 amino acids in length, which protrude from the N-terminal end of each heavy and each light chain. Unlike most of the framework sequence of the antibody molecule, the sequences of these loops are highly variable, and the six loops together form the major part of the antibody surface that recognises and binds to specific antigens.

Q3: Name the two types of antibody light chain that may be produced.

A3: The two types of antibody light chain that may be expressed are kappa (k) and lambda (λ).

Q4: What is the difference between affinity and avidity?

A4: Affinity refers to the strength of an interaction between two molecules, while avidity refers to the strength of the interaction between several pairs of the same molecules working together, such as the 10 variable regions of IgM binding to 10 antigens on a microbial surface.

Q5: What is meant by the term ‘cross reactivity’?

A5: Cross-reactivity refers to the occurrence when one antibody can bind to two or more different antigens.

Q6: How does the binding of antibodies to antigen improve as the immune response develops?

A6: As the humoral immune response progresses, point mutations are introduced into the V-regions of the genes coding for antibodies in some B-cells through a process called somatic hypermutation. The B-cells that bind antigen more effectively are positively selected through affinity maturation, which occurs in the germinal centres of lymph nodes. As the number of B-cells which have undergone somatic hypermutation and affinity maturation increases, so the average affinity of circulating antibodies for the target antigen also increases.

Q7: What is the difference between linear and conformational epitopes?

A7: Linear epitopes represent parts of a protein that comprise a chain of several amino acids all covalently linked together in one contiguous string, while conformational epitopes represent a surface patch of a protein made up of several different amino acid loops, each from different parts of the protein amino acid sequence. Linear epitopes retain antigenicity on denaturation of the protein, whereas this is not always the case with conformational epitopes.

Q8: What is the difference between T-dependent and T-independent antigens?

A8: Antibody responses to T-dependent antigens can only occur with help from helper T-cells. Antibody responses to TI antigens do not require T-cell help. Proteins are T-dependent antigens, and can give rise to high affinity antibodies of different classes and memory B-cells. TI antigens are generally polyvalent antigens, such as polysaccharides, that cross-link B-cell receptors, mostly leading to short-lived plasma cells secreting low-affinity IgM.

Q9: List some key differences between the primary and secondary responses to protein antigens.

A9: The primary antibody response is generated by the activation of naïve B-cells, is slow to develop and is dominated by low affinity antibodies. The secondary antibody response is generated by the activation of memory B-cells and rapidly yields high levels of correctly class-switched, high affinity antibodies.

Q10: What is passive immunisation?

A10: Passive immunity is the transfer of antibodies, often in the form of serum, from one individual to another to provide temporary protection from toxins or certain infectious agents.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 9: Presentation to T-cells

Q1: What is the relationship between CD4, CD8, MHC-I and MHC-II?

A1: CD8+ T-cells can only recognise peptide displayed on MHC-I, and CD4+ T-cells can only recognise peptide displayed on MHC-II.

Q2: Which is more widely expressed on different types of cell, MHC-I or MHC-II?

A2: All nucleated cells express MHC-I, but only a small number of cell types, including dendritic cells, macrophages and B-cells, express MHC-II and are able to present peptide to CD4+ helper T-cells.

Q3: List the six different human HLA molecules that comprise MHC.

A3: Human MHC-I molecules include HLA-A, HLA-B and HLA-C, human MHC-II molecules include HLA-DP, HLA-DQ and HLA-DR.

Q4: Which MHC (I or II) is able to accommodate the longest peptide chain?

A4: Only short peptides of 8-11 amino acids fit in the MHC-I groove, but the groove of MHC-II has open ends and can accommodate larger peptides of 10-30 amino acids.

Q5: What is meant by an ‘anchor residue’?

A5: Peptides presented on MHC are anchored into the binding cleft by 2 or 3 key amino acids, called anchor residues, which are of the correct size, charge and polarity to fit within specific binding pockets located in the floor of the groove.

Q6: Are peptides from self (host) proteins ever presented on MHC-I or MHC-II?

A6: Yes, peptides from host proteins are always present on most of the MHC-I and MHC-II molecules of an antigen presenting cell.

Q7: Which chemokine receptor is upregulated on activated dendritic cells to allow them to migrate to the T-cell zone of lymph nodes?

A7: Dendritic cell activated by PAMPs migrate to the T-cell zone of lymph nodes by upregulation of CCR7.

Q8: Which molecules break down cytosolic proteins and transport peptides into the endoplasmic reticulum?

A8: Cytosolic proteins are broken down into peptides by the proteasome, and these are transported into the lumen of the ER by the TAP protein.

Q9: What is meant by ‘cross-presentation’?

A9: Cross-presentation is the process by which dendritic cells ingest virally infected dead host cells and transport their antigens into the cytosol for processing via the MHC-I pathway.

Q10: What type of antigen is presented to T-cells by the CD1 molecule?

A10: CD1 is a MHC-I-like molecule that binds and presents lipids to NKT-cells.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 10: Genetic rearrangement: the key to adaptive immunity

Q1: What is somatic recombination?

A1: Somatic recombination is the editing and splicing together of small sections of the germline chromosomal DNA selected at random to yield the variable region of BCR and TCR genes

Q2: In which types of cell does somatic recombination occur?

A2: Somatic recombination occurs only in developing T- and B-lymphocytes.

Q3: Do all TCR or BCR chain variable domains contain one V, one D and one J section?

A3: No, the variable domains of TCR α and γ chains, and the Ig κ and λ light chains, are generated by the random joining together of V and J segments (but not D).

Q4: What is the difference between ‘N’ and ‘P’ nucleotides?

A4: In the joining segments of DNA between the V, D and J sections of the recombined TCR or BCR genes, additional nucleotides may be inserted. Those which are complementary to existing DNA are called P nucleotides, and those which are inserted at random are called N nucleotides.

Q5: What happens to the developing lymphocytes that fail to generate a functional receptor?

A5: We carry two copies of each chromosome, so if developing T- or B-cells fail to generate a receptor chain from one chromosome, they can attempt to make a receptor from the second chromosome. If this also fails, they do not receive survival signals and die by apoptosis.

Q6: Explain the process of allelic exclusion.

A6: Allelic exclusion is the process by which the successful generation of a receptor chain gene leads to signalling from the receptor expressed on the membrane that causes recombination and expression from the other chromosome to cease. This ensures that most receptor chain genes (other than TCR-α) are expressed from only one of the two available chromosomes, and therefore the cell expresses receptor specific for only one antigen.

Q7: How does the developing lymphocyte recognise the beginning and ends of each V, D and J segment in the genome?

A7: Each V, D and J segment is marked by recombination signal sequences, which are composed of a conserved 7 base pair sequence (called heptamer), a gap of 12 or 23 nucleotides of any type, and a conserved 9 base sequence (called nonamer).

Q8: Which protein complex recognises these sequences?

A8: The recombination signal sequences are recognised by the V(D)J recombinase complex which is a tetramer made up of the proteins Rag1 and Rag2.

Q9: What is the name of the enzyme that cleaves the hairpin DNA structures which are formed transiently during V(D)J joining?

A9: The Rag1/Rag2 complex causes the formation of hairpins at the ends of the cleaved gene segments, these are then opened up by an enzyme called artemis.

Q10: What happens to B-cells that produce an antibody that binds too tightly to self antigens?

A10: B-cells that generate receptors that bind too strongly to self antigens may undergo B-cell receptor editing, wherein Rag1 and Rag2 are expressed once again and the light chain recombined again in an attempt to generate a receptor that does not bind self antigens. If this process fails to generate a receptor that does not bind strongly to self antigens, the B-cell will be deleted by apoptosis.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 11: B-cell activation

Q1: Why are antibody responses to polysaccharides, glycolipids and nucleic acids generally T-independent (TI)?

A1: Antibody responses to polysaccharides, glycolipids and nucleic acids are T-independent (TI), because these molecules cannot be presented on MHC-II to T-cells.

Q2: What type of antigens do follicular, B-1 and marginal zone B-cells typically respond to?

A2: Follicular B-cells mainly respond to T-dependent antigens (i.e. proteins), and B-1 and marginal zone B-cells mainly respond to T-independent antigens.

Q3: The antibody component of the B-cell receptor complex (BCR) is not by itself sufficient to stimulate cell signalling. Name two key co-receptors involved in translating BCR clustering to intracellular signalling.

A3: Signalling via the BCR requires the co-receptors Igα and Igβ, which possess cytoplasmic signalling domains that allow cellular activation by antigen.

Q4: Name two types of secondary activation signal that stimulate B-cell activation in addition to receptor clustering induced by polyvalent antigen.

A4: B-cells receive additional activation signals from the complement product C3d binding to CR2, from PRR recognition of PAMPs and from cytokines released from nearby cells.

Q5: In what part of the lymph node do activated follicular B-cells meet activated helper T-cells?

A5: Activated follicular B-cells migrate to the outer edge of the lymph node follicle, where they meet recently activated CD4+ T-cells migrating in the opposite direction, and the two cells meet at the follicle boundary.

Q6: What functions are performed in the dark zone and light zone of the B-cell follicle germinal centre?

A6: B-cells proliferate in the dark zone. In the light zone, B-cells encounter antigen, TFH cells and follicular dendritic cells presenting intact antigen.

Q7: Can any class switching occur in the absence of T-cell help?

A7: Although class-switching requires T-cell help in the majority of cases, some limited class switching can occur without T-cell help in the presence of cytokines from activated macrophages and DCs, or TGF-β in the gut mucosa.

Q8: Name the key CD molecule pairing that occurs between activated T-cells and B-cells to induce expression of the class-switching enzyme activation-induced deaminase (AID)?

A8: The expression of activation-induced deaminase (AID) is induced by CD40:CD40L ligation.

Q9: Where do memory and plasma B-cells reside to perform their functions?

A9: Memory B-cells recirculate throughout the body, or remain in the lymph node in which they were generated, for many years. Plasma B-cells migrate to the bone marrow, where they receive survival signals from the local cytokines that allow them to remain alive and secrete class-switched, high affinity protective antibody into the circulation for many years.

Q10: Does somatic hypermutation and affinity maturation occur during TI responses?

A10: Somatic hypermutation and affinity maturation does not occur during TI responses.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 12: T-cell activation and cell-mediated immunity

Q1: What happens to naïve T-cells that do not meet antigen for which they are specific after exiting the thymus?

A1: Naïve T-cells that do not meet their specific antigen within several months die by apoptosis and are replaced with new naïve T-cells from the thymus.

Q2: Which types of cell are able to activate naïve T-cells?

A2: Naïve T-cells can only be activated by mature DCs, which have been activated by ‘danger’ signals such as microbial PAMPs, to express co-stimulatory molecules such as B7.

Q3: Which co-stimulators are recognised by T-cell CD28?

A3: The costimulators B7-1 and B7-2 are recognised by CD28 expressed on the T-cell surface.

Q4: What is meant by the immunological synapse?

A4: The immunological synapse is the region of close proximity between the membranes of the T-cell and the APC, formed by adhesion molecules, MHC-peptide-TCR and co-stimulator-ligand interactions.

Q5: List three key transcription factors required for T-cell activation.

A5: The best examples of key transcription factors required for T-cell activation are AP-1, NF-AT and NF-kB.

Q6: The cytokine IL-2 is secreted by activated T-cells - what purpose does it serve?

A6: The binding of IL-2 to the IL-2 receptor promotes the stimulation of T-cell survival and proliferation, in both an autocrine and paracrine manner.

Q7: How is T-cell commitment to a particular T-helper subset maintained?

A7: Commitment to a particular T-cell subset is maintained by regulatory loops generated by transcription factor expression induced by cytokine signalling, and also by tightly packing the chromatin of cytokine genes associated with other T-cell subsets, thereby switching these genes off.

Q8: What are the signature cytokines of Th1, Th2 and Th17 cells?

A8: Th1 cells express IFN-γ, Th2 cells express IL-4, IL-5 and IL-13, Th17 cells express IL-17A, IL-17F, and IL-22

Q9: What are the signature transcription factors of Th1, Th2 and Th17 cells?

A9: Th1 cells express the transcription factor T-bet, Th2 cells express GATA-3 and Th17 cells express RORγT.

Q10: What types of infection are most efficiently targeted by Th1, Th2 and Th17 cells?

A10: Th1 cells promote defence against intracellular pathogens, Th2 cells promote defence against parasitic worms (helminths) and Th17 cells promote defence against extracellular bacteria and fungi.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 13: Immunological memory and vaccination

Q1: What are effector lymphocytes, and what is their fate after infections are cleared?

A1: Effector lymphocytes perform the functions required to eliminate infections, such as secreting cytokines or killing target cells, and die soon after the infection is cleared.

Q2: What is the purpose of the memory lymphocyte?

A2: Memory cells are derived from activated naïve lymphocytes, and express the same antigen receptor as the initial responding B- or T-cell clone. When activated by exposure to antigen on a subsequent occasion, they proliferate and then differentiate to quickly yield effector cells that are well adapted to clearance of the invading microbe. They may live for many years, often for the lifetime of the host, to confer long-lasting immunity to infection by various pathogens.

Q3: List three molecular markers commonly associated with memory T-lymphocytes.

A3: Memory T-cells tend to express high levels of the IL-7 receptor, anti-apoptotic proteins such as Bcl2, and a version of the CD45 surface protein that lacks the A exon, termed CD45R0.

Q4: What is the difference between central and memory effector T-cells?

A4: Central memory T-cells express CCR7 and are slow to yield responses to antigen, such as the secretion of appropriate cytokines, because they spend some time proliferating first. Effector memory T-cells do not express CCR7 and are able to generate effector responses more quickly than central memory T-cells, since they do not spend much time proliferating before a response is generated.

Q5: What is the purpose of a vaccine?

A5: Vaccines are forms of antigen that can be administered to people or animals to generate immunity without the symptoms of disease normally caused by the pathogen that expresses the antigen. The aim of vaccination is generally to induce long-lived lymphocyte memory cells and a high affinity antibody response.

Q6: What are the differences between live attenuated, inactivated, subunit and conjugate vaccines?

A6: Vaccines may consist of live attenuated organisms (which are alive but made harmless by various treatments), inactivated (killed) organisms, subunits (individual antigens), or conjugates (such as peptide covalently bound to polysaccharide).

Q7: Why are peptides sometimes covalently attached to polysaccharide antigens during the production of vaccines?

A7: Non-protein antigens, such as polysaccharides, are often covalently attached to foreign peptides (called conjugation) because the peptide component allows the activation of T-cells and the generation of T-cell help, which is required for effective B-cell memory and affinity maturation.

Q8: The administration of purified antigens, such as proteins, rarely results in efficient T- or B-cell responses or memory in naïve hosts. Why is this?

A8: The administration of purified antigens alone tends to result in very poor T- or B-cell memory because DCs must be activated by some form of danger signal to present antigen efficiently and express co-stimulatory molecules. This danger signal normally arises from the activation of innate immune receptors, such as Toll-like receptors or complement, by microbial molecules.

Q9: What is an adjuvant?

A9: An adjuvant is a substance that causes activation of receptors of the innate immune system, such as Toll-like receptors or the inflammasome, to enhance the efficacy of vaccines. It is included in vaccines to enhance activation of dendritic cells and thereby promote lymphocyte responses and ultimately immunological memory.

Q10: Give two examples of commonly used adjuvant.

A10: Commonly used adjuvants include bacterial PAMPs, or the inflammasome-stimulant alum.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 14: Immunological tolerance and transplantation

Q1: What are the key differences between central and peripheral tolerance?

A1: Central tolerance is the deletion, suppression of function, or receptor editing (B-cells only) of self-reactive lymphocytes that occurs in the generative lymphoid organs, i.e. bone marrow and thymus, resulting in the functional deletion or inactivation of the majority of potentially self-reactive naïve T- and B-cells. Peripheral tolerance may cause self-reactive B-cells or T-cells that have escaped central tolerance to die by apoptosis, differentiate into an unresponsive state called anergy, or be functionally suppressed by other cells.

Q2: There are two main types of regulatory T-cell, what are they called and where are they formed?

A2: Regulatory T-cells (T-regs) generated in the thymus are called natural T-regs, and those that are generated in the periphery are termed inducible T-regs.

Q3: Name the key transcription factor that is expressed by and controls the function of regulatory T-cells.

A3: FOXP3.

Q4: What term is applied to the failure of tolerance and the generation of adaptive responses to self antigens?

A4: Autoimmunity.

Q5: Of the genetic factors that increase risk of developing autoimmune disorders, which key group of genes are most frequently associated with such risk?

A5: Carriage of certain MHC alleles often modifies the risk of developing autoimmune diseases.

Q6: What is meant by molecular mimicry?

A6: Molecular mimicry occurs when a pathogen expresses an antigen which is very similar to a host antigen. Infection with such pathogens can result in the breakdown of tolerance to self-antigens.

Q7: What type of mediator promotes the inflammation that underpins the disease systemic lupus erythematosus?

A7: Systemic lupus erythematosus is caused by the formation of antibodies to self DNA or other nuclear antigens resulting in immune complex deposition and complement activation in the tissues.

Q8: Which type of cell is the target of autoreactive responses during Type 1 diabetes?

A8: Type 1 diabetes mellitus is caused by autoimmune destruction of insulin-secreting β-cells in the pancreas.

Q9: What is an allograft?

A9: An allograft refers to the tissue transplanted from donor to recipient, when each is a member of the same species but not genetically identical.

Q10: What is meant by the term ‘graft versus host disease’?

A10: When haematopoietic cells are transplanted from a donor into a recipient, the transplanted T-cells from the donor may attack the host tissues. This is termed graft vs host disease.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 15: Mucosal and cutaneous immunity

Q1: Why do we require specialised immune functions at the epithelial surfaces of the body?

A1: Specialised immune function is required at the epithelial surfaces because these are the most frequent point of entry for pathogens into the body, and they are also covered in a commensal microbiota that must be tolerated.

Q2: Name three major types of anti-microbial peptides which are secreted by epithelial cells.

A2: Anti-microbial peptides secreted by epithelial cells include cathelicidins, α-defensins and β-defensins.

Q3: What are Peyer’s patches, and where are they most abundant?

A3: Peyer’s patches are a form of gut-associated lymphoid tissue (GALT) that are not encapsulated but structurally and functionally similar to the follicles of lymph nodes, and are present mainly in the ileum.

Q4: What is the purpose of the microfold (M)-cell?

A4: Antigen sampling of the gut contents is achieved by microfold (M)-cells, which transcytose intact antigens from the gut lumen into the lamina propria.

Q5: Which key chemokine receptors and integrins direct the homing of lymphocytes back to the gut-associated lymphoid tissue?

A5: Lymphocytes generated in GALT or MLN tend to home back to the gut by expression of the chemokine receptor CCR9, and the integrin α4β7, which are induced by retinoic acid converted from dietary vitamin A by intestinal DCs.

Q6: What effect do the Th17 cytokines IL-17 and IL-22 confer on the intestinal epithelial barrier?

A6: The Th17 cytokines IL-17 and IL-22 promote epithelial barrier function through induction of epithelial cell survival, proliferation, mucous production and defensin secretion.

Q7: How is IgA in the lamina propria targeted for transcytosis into the gut lumen?

A7: IgA produced by lamina-propria B-cells is covalently bound to the J-chain, which is recognised by the poly-Ig receptor expressed on the basal surface of intestinal epithelial cells and transcytosed into the lumen by these cells.

Q8: Describe two mechanisms by which immune responses to antigens from food or commensal bacteria are limited.

A8: Mechanisms by which immune responses to antigens from food or commensal bacteria are limited include the large number of regulatory T-cells and regulatory DCs in the lamina propria, the carefully sequestered expression of pattern recognition receptors, such as TLRs and NLRs in intestinal cells, and the induction of barrier enhancement programmes rather than inflammatory cytokine production in PAMP-stimulated epithelial cells.

Q9: What phenotype do naïve T-cells adopt when stimulated by antigen in the healthy (non-inflammed) lamina propria?

A9: The activation of naïve T-cells by antigen in the gut normally leads to the generation of immunosuppressive FOXP3+ regulatory T-cells.

Q10: What is meant by the term ‘oral tolerance’?

A10: Oral tolerance is observed when protein antigens are administered to vertebrates in large doses by the oral route in the absence of co-stimulator-inducing molecules, such as PAMPs. After such treatment, the host is often unable to generate effective adaptive responses to these antigens subsequently in the peripheral tissues.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 16: Immune responses to major pathogens

Q1: Which two proteins are the defining antigens of different strains of the influenza virus?

A1: Strains of the influenza virus express different neuraminidase (N) and haemagglutinin (H) antigens.

Q2: Which two transcription factors mediate most of the innate interferon response to influenza infection?

A2: Innate responses to the influenza virus result in activation of the transcription factors interferon regulatory factor (IRF) 3 and IRF7 which promote type I interferon production and induction of the antiviral state.

Q3: In the context of influenza infection, what is the difference between antigenic drift and antigenic shift?

A3: Antigenic drift is the slow accumulation of point mutations in the neuraminidase and haemagglutinin genes, antigenic shift is the reassortment of different versions of these genes from two or more different viruses that infect a single host organism at the same time.

Q4: Viruses often attempt to evade detection by CD8+ T-cells by causing downregulation of MHC-I expression in their host cell. How is this countered by the innate immune system?

A4: Natural killer (NK) cells detect and kill host cells which have switched off MHC-I expression.

Q5: The human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) infects macrophages and CD4+ T-cells by binding to CD4. Which two chemokine receptors are most frequently also used as co-receptors for HIV entry into these cells?

A5: The most commonly targeted co-receptors by HIV are CXCR4 and CCR5.

Q6: T-cell and antibody responses to HIV antigens are quickly generated after infection by the virus. Why does this not lead to clearance of the virus?

A6: The virus is able to ‘hide’ as a provirus integrated into the chromosomal DNA of long-lived host cells. It also mutates rapidly, such that escape mutants are frequently generated that express antigens which evade the antibody response.

Q7: HIV infection is generally asymptomatic for many years. Why does the infection eventually progress to AIDS?

A7: Eventually, HIV infection causes the number of circulating CD4+ T-lymphocytes to fall below the level required to confer the host with protection against diverse infections by other pathogens. The lack of ability to defend against these infections is responsible for the clinical manifestation of AIDS.

Q8: Why has it been so difficult to develop an effective vaccine for HIV?

A8: Efforts to create an effective vaccine for HIV are hindered by the rapid mutation rate and antigen variation of the virus.

Q9: How does the parasite responsible for malaria, Plasmodium falciparum, evade host antibody responses?

A9: To evade the host antibody response, P. falciparum sequentially switches expression of the gene coding for its major surface antigen, PfEMP1, to another variant, of which there are 60 available in its genome, thus expressing a new antigen that escapes the current antibody response, in a process termed antigenic variation.

Q10: What is a granuloma, and why are these formed during infection of the lung by Mycobacterium tuberculosis?

A10: A granuloma is a tissue structure formed of activated macrophages, dead cells, and lymphocytes which can be formed around a site of chronic infection. In the case of Mycobacterium tuberculosis infection, the granuloma effectively walls off the infection from the rest of the body, and thus the infection may remain asymptomatic in carriers of Mycobacterium tuberculosis for many years.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 17: Hypersensitivities and allergy

Q1: What is meant by the term ‘hypersensitivity disorder’?

A1: Hypersensitivity disorders are inappropriate, or excessive, immune reactions that cause damage to host tissues.

Q2: What is meant by type I hypersensitivity?

A2: Type I hypersensitivity (or immediate hypersensitivity) is mediated by the activation of mast cells and basophils caused by the cross-linking of IgE in response to polyvalent antigens referred to as allergens.

Q3: What is meant by type II hypersensitivity?

A3: Type II hypersensitivity is the inflammatory response to self antigens, or inappropriate modulation of host signalling pathways, mediated by autoreactive IgG or IgM antibodies.

Q4: What is meant by type III hypersensitivity?

A4: Type III hypersensitivity is the inflammatory response to immune complexes formed of antibody and antigen, which tend to deposit in the arteries, joints and kidneys

Q5: What is meant by type IV (delayed type) hypersensitivity?

A5: Type IV hypersensitivity (delayed type hypersensitivity) refers to the tissue damage caused by CD4+ or CD8+ T-cell responses to either self antigens or to microbial antigens

Q6: Why are some people atopic?

A6: Atopic individuals are, for genetic and environmental reasons, at higher than average risk of developing high levels of IgE antibodies to particular antigens, termed allergens, to which they become sensitised.

Q7: Which type of T-helper cell polarisation promotes IgE production and atopic responses?

A7: B-cell class-switching to the IgE isotype is dependent on Th2 cell responses.

Q8: List some of the major mediators contained within mast cell granules.

A8: Examples of mediators contained within mast cell granules include, platelet activating factor (PAF), leukotrienes, histamine, and cytokines.

Q9: Which physiological processes underpin the development of asthma?

A9: Asthma is characterised by eosinophilic inflammation of the bronchioles, and hyper-reactivity of bronchial smooth muscle cells to bronchoconstrictors, resulting in episodic airway narrowing and breathing difficulties.

Q10: What is the term given to the situation where allergens gain access to the circulation and cause systemic activation of mast cells?

A10: Anaphylactic shock.

---

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 18: Tumour immunology

Q1: What evidence supports the notion that the immune system continually seeks and eliminates most transformed cells, for the purpose of preventing cancer?

A1: Humans with impaired immune systems, and mice deficient in certain genes relating to the immune system, have increased rates of cancer occurrence. Transplantation of CD8+ T-cells specific for tumour antigens can also protect mice from specific tumours expressing those antigens.

Q2: List three different infectious organisms which can increase the risk of tumour formation in man.

A2: Human papilloma virus (cervical cancer), Epstein Barr virus (B-cell lymphoma), and Helicobacter pylori (stomach cancer).

Q3: What is the difference between tumour specific antigens and tumour associated antigens?

A3: Tumour specific antigens are expressed only on tumour cells, but not other cells in the body. Tumour associated antigens are expressed at high levels on tumour cells, but also at low levels on some normal cells under certain conditions.

Q4: What is meant by the term ‘oncofoetal antigen’?

A4: Oncofoetal antigens are proteins which are expressed only during development of the foetus, and are normally switched off before birth, but can be reactivated and expressed by tumour cells.

Q5: Name 3 activators of the NK-cell activating receptor NKG2D, which are sometimes expressed on the surface of tumour cells?

A5: MIC-A, MIC-B and ULB.

Q6: What is meant by the term ‘immunoediting’?

A6: Immunoediting refers to the process by which those tumour cells in a mass which express antigens that are easily recognised by T-cells are destroyed, and the rare mutants that lack expression of those antigens escape detection, survive and proliferate to eventually take over the mass and make it less visible to the immune system.

Q7: Name four types of cell which may be recruited by tumours to promote the production of an immunosuppressive tumour microenvironment.

A7: Regulatory T-cells, Th2 helper T-cells, M2 macrophages and myeloid derived suppressor cells.

Q8: What are the two best studied checkpoint proteins on the surface of T-cells, and what are their ligands commonly expressed on tumour cells?

A8: PD-1 (for PD-L1 on the tumour cell), and CTLA-4 (for B7.1 or B7.2 on the tumour cell).

Q9: Which two cytokines are most commonly expressed within tumours to inhibit immune responses to tumour cells?

A9: Interleukin-10 (IL-10) and transforming growth factor beta (TGF-β).

Q10: Which antigen is commonly expressed on tumour cells to prevent their phagocytosis by macrophages?

A10: CD47.

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 19: Tumour immunotherapies

Q1: What was the major limitation of William Coley’s early approach of injecting bacterial preparations into patients for the treatment of cancer?

A1: Although approximately one in ten of the treated patients achieved complete remission, the systemic inflammatory response syndrome was commonly triggered in response to the bacterial PAMPs, and this occasionally proved fatal.

Q2: Explain how tumour vaccination works.

A2: Biopsies of tumours taken from patients can be mixed with powerful adjuvants, then injected back into the donor to help promote immune responses to tumour antigens. Alternatively, dendritic cells can be loaded with tumour antigens and activated in vitro, before return to the patient (e.g. Sipuleucel-T).

Q3: How does the blockbuster drug Herceptin (trastuzumab) help to treat breast cancer?

A3: Herceptin is a monoclonal antibody which blocks the capacity of the HER2 growth factor receptor to signal, so inhibiting the proliferation of tumour cells which rely on that pathway for growth.

Q4: How are monoclonal antibodies used to treat B-cell malignancies?

A4: Monoclonal antibodies, such as ofatumumab, target the CD20 antigen which is expressed by B-cells, but not other cells in the body. Excess B-cells are then removed from the circulation by NK-cells, via the process of antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC).

Q5: Name 3 proteins which are most commonly targeted by therapeutic checkpoint inhibitor antibodies.

A5: CTLA-4, PD-1 and PD-L1.

Q6: Explain how chimaeric antigen receptor (CAR) T-cells are prepared from a patient’s blood and used in the treatment of cancer.

A6: T-cells are isolated from the patient’s blood, and a virus is used to insert genetic material into the T-cells coding for an engineered T-cell receptor (TCR), which is specific for a tumour antigen of interest, such as CD19. The engineered T-cells are amplified by proliferation in vitro, then returned to the patient by injection.

Q7: Outline the genetic structure of the first generation chimaeric antigen receptor, as used to prepare CAR T-cells.

A7: An scFv domain with binding specificity for a tumour antigen of interest is cloned from an antibody, and genetically fused with the TCR zeta chain, resulting in a hybrid molecule which has antigen binding functionality on the extracellular domain, and TCR signalling functionality on the intracellular domain.

Q8: In the context of immunotherapy, what is an ‘immune-related adverse event’?

A8: An ‘immune-related adverse event’ occurs when efforts to promote immune responses to tumours result in excessive cytokine release, systemic inflammation or autoimmunity.

Q9: Which organs are most commonly affected by immune-related adverse events arising from tumour immunotherapy?

A9: The lungs, skin, liver, endocrine system and digestive tract, are the most commonly affected targets of immune-related adverse events.

Q10: What steps can be taken to limit the damage of an immune-related adverse event after it has been triggered?

A10: The immunotherapy treatment should be halted, high-dose corticosteroids can be given, and if this fails to control symptoms, monoclonal antibodies targeting the inflammatory cytokine TNF-α (such as infliximab) can also be given.

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 20: Methods in immunology

Q1: What are the differences between monoclonal and polyclonal antibodies?

A1: Polyclonal antibodies are derived from a mixture of different B-cell clones, each targeting different epitopes on the same antigen. Monoclonal antibodies are derived from a single B-cell clone, targeting a single epitope on one antigen.

Q2: What is a hybridoma?

A2: A hybridoma is a cell formed by the fusion of two different cells, one being a B-cell expressing a particular antibody, and the other a transformed cell-line that allows the joined cell to continue proliferating in long-term culture in vitro.

Q3: What is meant by a functional antibody?

A3: A functional antibody is an antibody which alters a biological process by binding to its target, often inhibiting the function of the target molecule, or occasionally activating receptors (by cross-linking them).

Q4: What is meant by the term ‘humanised’ antibody, and why are these sometimes prepared?

A4: When antibodies developed for therapeutic administration to man are raised in other species, such as mice, they may be rejected as foreign by the human host and therefore are not useful therapeutically. The framework residues of these antibodies can therefore be replaced with human framework residues to create a molecule that is effectively a human antibody with CDRs derived from the donor species. Such humanised antibodies are much less likely to become the target of host adaptive responses.

Q5: What is the purpose of the ‘sandwich ELISA’ technique?

A5: A sandwich ELISA, or enzyme linked immunosorbent assay, is used to quantify antigens within samples containing many different antigens, such as serum, using antibodies specific for the antigen of interest.

Q6: Describe the key stages of the Western blotting technique.

A6: Proteins in a sample are denatured using the detergent SDS, then separated on a polyacrylamide gel by electrophoresis. The gel containing separated proteins is then overlaid onto a protein binding membrane, often made of nitrocellulose, and the proteins are transferred onto this membrane by applying an electrical current across the gel and membrane. The membrane is then blocked with non-specific protein, probed with a primary antibody, washed and then probed with a secondary antibody that binds the Fc portion of the primary antibody. The location of the protein of interest is then revealed by means of tags covalently attached to the secondary antibody that fluoresce or emit light.

Q7: In the context of flow cytometry, what do side scatter and forward scatter indicate, respectively?

A7: Forward scatter gives an indication of cell size and side scatter gives an indication of granularity of the cell.

Q8: Which CD markers are most commonly used to identify monocytes, B-cells and T-cells during flow cytometry of human leukocytes?

A8: Monocytes - CD14, B-cells - CD19, T-cells - CD3.

Q9: Name the reagent most commonly used to label apoptotic cells for flow cytometry.

A9: Fluorescently labelled Annexin-V is often used to label apoptotic cells for flow cytometry.

Q10: How does FACS differ from traditional flow cytometry?

A10: While the cells that pass through a flow cytometer are normally lost to waste during standard flow cytometry, they may be recovered and cultured following FACS, or fluorescence activated cell sorting. Specific types of cell may be targeted for recovery by FACS depending on their expression of particular markers.

# Answers to test questions for Chapter 21: Industrial immunology

Q1: What are the major technical limitations of the use of polyclonal antibodies in experimental research?

A1: The main technical limitations of polyclonal antibodies are the increased level of batch-to-batch variability, which can arise from differences in immune responses of individual animals, and the increased likelihood of binding to off-target antigens, compared to monoclonal antibodies.

Q2: Name two adjuvants that are commonly used in the immunisation of animals for antibody production.

A2: Freund's adjuvant and aluminium salts.

Q3: Which organ is usually taken from immunised animals as a source of B-cells during monoclonal antibody production?

A3: The spleen.

Q4: Why is it helpful to raise antibodies in a species that is evolutionarily, and phylogenetically, distant from the species expressing the target antigen of interest?

A4: It is very difficult to generate antibodies to a target antigen using an animal of the same species as that expressing the target antigen, since tolerance prevents the production of B-cells specific for self-antigens. Therefore other species must be used, and the more evolutionarily distant the animal used to raise antibodies is from the target species, the more likely it is that their own equivalent antigen will be sufficiently different from the antigen of the target species, that antibodies are likely to be present in their B-cell pool which target the antigen of interest.

Q5: What are the ethical implications of monoclonal antibody production using the immunisation of animals and the production of ascites from hybridoma cells?

A5: The immunised animal must be sacrificed to obtain the spleen, and the hybridoma cells are cultivated within the abdominal cavity of another animal, resulting in the production of ascites fluid which causes swelling and is harvested by syringe, all of which can cause pain and distress.

Q6: In what ways are the Fab’ and (Fab’)2 antibody fragments distinct from the Fab antibody fragment?

A6: Compared to Fab, the Fab’ fragment contains additionally a small part of the heavy chain hinge region, and when disulphide bonds form on this region to link two Fab’ molecules together, the resulting dimer is referred to as (Fab’)2.

Q7: In what way is the single-chain variable fragment (scFv) distinct from the Fab fragment?

A7: The Fab fragment is part of a natural antibody, and comprises two separate polypeptide chains - one being the natural light chain, and the other arising from cleavage of the natural heavy chain, both comprising both variable and constant regions. scFv fragments are prepared from an engineered gene, resulting in a single polypeptide chain in which a light chain variable region is joined to a heavy chain variable region using a flexible polypeptide linker.

Q8: List the major steps involved in the discovery of antibodies using the phage display technique.

A8: mRNA is extracted from B-cells, which are typically isolated from human blood, converted into cDNA, and the light and heavy chain variable regions are cloned into a phagemid vector, which allows expression of scFv molecules corresponding to these genes on the surface of bacteriophage particles. Suspensions of these bacteriophages expressing billions of different antibody fragments on their surface are then flowed over the antigen of interest, and those that bind well are recovered by elution in a different buffer. The genetic sequence of the scFv is recovered from the phage particles that bind, allowing monoclonal antibodies to be produced as recombinant proteins in mammalian cell expression systems.

Q9: From an ethical perspective, what are the major benefits of antibody production from phage display techniques compared to conventionally produced monoclonal antibodies?

A9: No animal needs to be sacrificed, and there is no requirement for cultivation of the antibody secreting cells in the abdominal cavity, as is required for ascites production.

Q10: What is the structure and function of a bispecific T-cell engager (BiTE)?

A10: BiTEs are bispecific antibodies which comprise an scFv domain targeting the T-cell CD3 receptor, and a second scFv domain targeting a tumour antigen, joined by a synthetic linker. The function of the BITE is to cause the clustering of CD3 when a T-cell binds to a tumour cell, thus promoting T-cell activation, and potentially improved anti-tumour immune responses.
